# 🤖 database, how humans state guarantees and structure proofs
(authored by agents unless marked 🧑)

304 sources, best-read first, then newest first. Generated from `sources.jsonl`; edit the reviews' notes, not this page.

- Ang-Pirlea-Mathur scenario proofs (2026)
    - title: Scenario Proofs for Verification of Distributed Protocols
    - what: Introduces scenario proofs: regular-expression-like representative executions with ghost variables, guards, phases, and loops; coverage modulo sound semi-commutation plus Hoare-style correctness replaces monolithic inductive-invariant discovery. The semantics has structure, variable, feasibility, and commutativity layers. Coverage is reduced to syntax-directed inclusions J1K⊆JsK and Js·aK⊆JsK; correctness checks only feasible representatives using weakest preconditions and supplied loop invariants; local pairwise soundness of semi-commutation justifies reordering. The Paxos scenario defers negative Promise/Accept actions, orders positive rounds ascending, and proves agreement because each later round sees a quorum intersection with completed earlier rounds. Additional appendixed proofs cover two-phase commit, a decentralized lock (empty commutativity), and two Ivy multisignature-payment variants.
    - number: 6 protocol case studies
    - number: 3 proof obligations: coverage, correctness, semi-commutation soundness
    - number: Paxos positive and negative scenario parts
    - limits: Protocols are modeled as finite-action transition systems with feasible runs; semi-commutation must preserve feasibility and final states and is checked locally. Scenario authors must invent representative expressions, coverage derivations, and loop invariants; iteration still requires user invariants. Results are proof case studies, not an automated prover or runtime evaluation; paper notes page-limited appendix and leaves automation/decision procedures open.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `ang-pirlea-mathur-scenario-proofs-2026`
- Can LLMs Write Correct TLA+ Specifications? Evaluating Natural-Language-to-TLA+ Generation (2026)
    - what: # Can LLMs Write Correct TLA+ Specifications? Evaluating Natural-Language-to-TLA+ Generation (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Bisharat, Ortiz, Spencer, Bhadauria, Wang, Thiruvathukal, Läufer and Abuhamad evaluate generation and completion of TLA+ specifications. The useful contribution is a public task, four prompt designs, checker outcomes, reference-text comparisons and a concrete error taxonomy. The experiment reports 2,600 open-weight runs and 130 proprietary-model runs. Its headline “semantic correctness” means an operational TLC outcome for a finite configuration. It does not establish correspondence to the natural-language requirements or equivalence to a reference behavior. Infrastructure failures, inconsistent counts and released evaluator differences materially limit the proposed ranking of strategies.  The saved arXiv version is 2606.05792v1, dated 4 June 2026. The publisher first page identifies ICSOFT 2026, pages 39–50, DOI 10.5220/0015070400004088 (B1). This record reads the entire 12-page arXiv paper, including all 34 references and 11 tables. All 12 pages were inspected as rendered images. There are no figures, numbered theorems, mathematical proofs, appendix, checklist or separate algorithm listing. The publisher PDF was acquired and its first-page bibliography inspected; no byte identity or complete final-version comparison is claimed.  The FormaLLM source is pinned to b159f5df093e7ed71f4793bba99459b97a2bb23d, dated 2 April 2026. Twenty-four selected plaintext files were read: documentation, split and prompt code, SANY/TLC evaluators and collectors, completion metrics, four pipeline files and three graph helpers. Identical files were checked by bytes and read once. The complete 1,864-entry tree was inventoried, not wholly read. Original/generated specifications, recorded checker outputs, full corpus/splits, run.sh, unselected analysis scripts and cited papers were not read. No author code was executed or imported; no models, checkpoints or serialized datasets were loaded. Source correspondence to the published historical runs remains unknown. quotes.json preserves 43 exact excerpts with source, line, character and section pointers; numeric_tables.json preserves all 11 literal table blocks and reader arithmetic.  ## Task, inputs and experiment  The corpus contains 205 specifications from 98 TLA+ Examples projects, including consensus protocols, concurrency examples and puzzles. Table 1 lists 92 with both comments and configuration, 14 with only configuration, 95 with only comments and four with neither; totals are 106 with configurations and 187 with comments. Thus “205 paired specifications” needs qualification. The paper reports a project-stratified 143/31/31 train/validation/test split, with five test exclusions leaving 26 evaluated specifications (Q1,T1). Training supplies examples; validation tunes prompts/hyperparameters. These are public repository comments rather than new, independently elicited requirements. No contamination or memorization analysis is supplied.  Twenty-five open-weight models use four strategies; five proprietary models use only few-shot prompting. This gives 25×26×4=2,600 plus 5×26=130 runs. Table 2 preserves model aliases, reported token counts and contexts. Its many `latest` aliases, missing Qwen3:4b context cell and unexplained extreme totals such as 1,141.7K for r1:1.5b limit exact model/budget reconstruction. These token summaries are not measured end-to-end time, price or energy. The “eight families” combine architecture families with overlapping categories such as code specialization and proprietary APIs; they are the authors' grouping, not eight independent experimental factors.  Few-shot (FS) supplies three training comment/specification examples and target comments. Progressive (Prog.) supplies three examples plus instructional messages about module structure, variables, operators and temporal properties. Fill-in-middle (FIM) supplies the first and last 30% of the reference and asks for the middle 40%. Half-completion (HC) supplies the first 50%, comments and configuration and asks for the remainder (Q2–Q4). Completion therefore receives substantial correct reference code and tests a different task from full generation. In selected source, full-generation prompts also include the original configuration, while the written generated configuration becomes the preferred checker input (C10–C11).  The paper says SANY passes on “Parsing completed” with a 30-second timeout, then sends passing specifications with configurations to TLC (Q5). Textual measures apply to the generated reference segment: BLEU, ROUGE-L, edit distance, exact match and line accuracy. Source C13 defines line accuracy by matching line subsequences divided by reference line count, character similarity by SequenceMatcher, character edit distance, whitespace-token BLEU and default ROUGE tokenization. These are textual measures; different but equivalent specifications can score poorly, and reference overlap does not establish correct behavior.  ## What the tables report  Table 3 reports SANY FS173/650, HC80/650, Prog162/650 and FIM92/650. TLC is FS0, HC0, Prog56 and FIM0, with the Prog rate 8.6%. These are printed experimental outcomes, not independently rerun results. Table 5 distributes those outcomes across models, and Table 10 across specifications. The strongest listed individual open-model TLC result is DeepSeek r1:8b with14/26=53.8% under Prog; Qwen235b has12, Qwen30b10, GPT-OSS20b6 and Phi-mini4. These individual rates differ from the aggregate 8.6% headline.  Table 6 is essential to the headline's scope: under FS, GPT-5 reports SANY26/26 and TLC7/26; Sonnet4.5 reports24/26 and3/26, Haiku4.5 reports21/26 and3/26, Opus4.1 reports23/26 and1/26, GPT-4o reports20/26 and1/26. Thus “only progressive” applies to the open-weight multi-strategy experiment; it is false as an unqualified statement about all30 models. Likewise 26.6% is an aggregate open-strategy syntax rate, not the maximum individual result. The sentence calling GPT-5's26.9% TLC “comparable to the open-weight FS ceiling” conflicts with the reported open FS0% (Q7,T3,T6).  Tables 7 and9 preserve all per-model completion similarities. Table 3 reports mean BLEU HC.062/FIM.077, ROUGE-L .189/.213, line accuracy10.90%/12.27%, and exact matches0/650 versus1/650. Table 7 instead prints FIM average12.37%; reported dispersions also differ across summary and detailed tables. The one FIM exact match together with zero TLC passes is another reason to inspect infrastructure/configuration before interpreting zero as behavioral failure.  Table 8 reports bad-module-body first errors FS228,HC264,Prog243,FIM290; other parse errors72/227/70/191; unexpected-token errors82/29/65/17. These are not all categories. Its first-error location counts sum to477/570/488/558, matching 650 minus the Table 3 SANY totals. A first detected error does not count all defects. Early/middle/late thirds are the paper's definition; the selected collector instead labels absolute line numbers below10 and below50 (C14), so the published calculation needs a different, identified analysis path.  Table 10 gives examples rather than a simple “distributed is harder” result. YoYoNoPruning has100% SANY for HC/FIM; HDiskSynod has FS32%, Prog20% and FIM48%, contradicting the prose example that it scores0%. BinarySearch has12%FS/24%Prog syntax and8%Prog TLC. The highest listed per-spec Prog TLC rate is16%, shared by EnvironmentController, MultiPaxos, VoucherCancel and YoYoNoPruning. MCDieHarder has zero TLC across strategies. Multiple spelling/wrapping forms are retained in literal rows; no semantic equivalence across similarly named modules is assumed (Q10,T10).  ## Infrastructure confounding and inconsistent totals  Table 3's footnote explicitly says zero TLC rates “reflect failures (i.e., State Directory Conflicts and Module Not Found errors).” Table 4 labels97.2%/99.2%/99.2% of FS/HC/FIM failures as infrastructure and9.8% for Prog. A pipeline unable to load a module has not tested its behavior. Calling invalid configuration a “genuine semantic” failure also does not establish a mismatch between the generated transition system and intended requirements. These failures can be important practical generation defects while remaining distinct from behavioral incorrectness (Q6,T3–T4).  Table 4 is internally incomplete or inconsistent. Its displayed module/configuration rows sum FS177,HC164,FIM161 against totals650 each; an unlisted state-directory category might explain some gaps but is not numerically supplied. For Prog it gives122 TLC failures,455 parse failures and56 passes, totaling633 rather than650. Table 3's162 SANY passes imply488 parse failures. Moreover122+56=178 exceeds162. The record retains these printed claims and cannot reconstruct a valid common denominator from them.  Reader arithmetic over Table 5 gives column totals FS173/0,HC78/0,Prog162/56,FIM92/0. Table 10 implies HC80, matching Table 3 but conflicting with Table 5. Table 5's averages6.92,3.17,6.38,2.08,3.71 differ from row-derived6.92,3.12,6.48,2.24,3.68 for the corresponding nonzero columns. Also80/650=12.31%, whereas Table 3 prints12.2%; Qwen30b's23/26 is88.46%, whereas its best-model cell prints92.3%. No source output logs were inspected to settle which transcription or analysis is wrong.  ## Claims about models, causes and error repair  The observed results support some nonmonotonic size comparisons. They do not support “r1:8b outperforms70b across all strategies”: HC SANY is1 versus3 and FIM3 versus3. The claim that LLaMA1b's12 Prog SANY passes are highest among all LLaMAs conflicts with both3.3 variants'18. “Just three model families” conflicts with the paragraph listing DeepSeek,Qwen,GPT-OSS,Phi and Table 5's Gemma pass. Model training, instruction alignment, context limits, prompt interaction and output processing are not separated by a controlled factorial study (Q6,Q8–Q9,T5).  The authors attribute poor code-model performance to negative transfer, operator substitutions to rendered mathematics and reasoning leakage to training. These are proposed explanations. They did not manipulate training data, measure causal mediation, run controlled fine-tuning ablations or establish statistical significance. “Not noise” is especially strong given their explicit limitation: “We run each model-specification pair once per strategy” (Q14). More heterogeneous tasks do not substitute for repeated measurements of a given model/task condition.  Table 11 and §4.4 give five categories: Unicode operator substitutions, syntax from other languages, reasoning/formatting leakage, length miscalibration and module structure. Semicolon first-error counts are18/11/25/10, while file-presence scans count49/23/82/22; these have different denominators and meanings. Backtick counts are28/10/21/13; think-block counts15/30/not-applicable/28. The paper says QwQ contributes26/26 HC and FIM think blocks. It reports mean output/target length HC3.46× and FIM2.24×, with9.84× as an extreme. These are observed author counts, not a controlled explanation of accuracy (Q11–Q13,T11).  The claimed243+ missing Prog terminators numerically matches Table 8's243 bad-module-body first errors, but that broad parser category is not equivalent to an independently counted missing terminator. The selected generator also appends a terminator. The historical counting path is unresolved. A semicolon-presence scan is not itself a syntax-error test: semicolons can occur within comments, strings or PlusCal text. Similarly a blanket claim about all Unicode needs a tool-version/context boundary. Suggested grammar constraints, retrieval, normalization, fence removal and checker-guided repair are future interventions here. Their correctness and benefit were not measured; indiscriminate character deletion could change intended content (Q13,Q15,C16).  ## Released source qualifications  The primary parse step checks return code0 plus a success substring (C1–C2). Its separate metrics collector computes `parse_success` from that substring and error keywords but never uses it to return status: it returns PASSED whenever its line-number regexes find no errors (C3–C4). By static inspection, a present empty/unrecognized-error log can therefore receive PASSED in that collector. This is a source-level possibility; it is not a measured false-positive rate in the paper.  The selected TLC loops iterate every parsed dictionary entry and ignore `parse_status`, contrary to the paper's stated filtering. They prefer any generated configuration file, even an empty one, before fallback to the corpus; full generation writes one. Hence the checker may assess model-selected properties/configuration rather than an independently fixed behavioral oracle. The success collector accepts “Model checking completed. No error has been found.” before checking exit status. The alternate progressive evaluator instead accepts “The specification is correct”; the two strings are different. TLC subprocess calls have no Python timeout, despite TIMEOUT classification later; clearing `generated/states` without setting a working directory/metadir does not establish isolation of TLC's actual state directory. These observations require run-path/log validation before assigning them to historical results (C5–C7,C11).  The split helper performs unseeded flat `random.shuffle`, not project stratification, and floor slicing205 gives143/30/32 rather than143/31/31. Selected prompts read val.json rather than test.json. Those labels may have been repurposed by uninspected run tooling; this record cannot certify the published test set from the selected code (C8,C15).  The `fifty-fifty` and `fill-in-middle` prompt files are byte-identical and implement30/40/30 completion; their metric files are also identical. This does not reproduce two distinct completion designs. The progressive prompt accumulates instructional messages and makes one `llm.invoke` per target; it does not execute separate checked module/variable/operator generation stages. Its cleaner removes fences and explanatory text, adjusts headers and appends `====`, as do other selected generators. Thus Table 11's FIM cleanup footnote does not establish cleanup unique to FIM. The source/documentation mix should not be silently treated as the exact experimental pipeline (C9,C11–C12,C16).  ## Contribution to this literature effort  The paper is directly relevant to evaluating LLM-written distributed-system specifications. It supplies task definitions, public examples, prompt contrasts and failure cases. Its strongest lesson is methodological: separate parser success, tool execution, checked finite properties and faithfulness to intended behavior. The evidence does not justify treating an8.6% pipeline pass fraction as a measured semantic-equivalence rate, or infrastructure-confounded zero passes as proof that a strategy cannot generate valid behavior.  A reader recommendation is to use fixed independent configurations/properties, inspect explored states and vacuity, compare behaviors or traces against requirements, isolate checker paths, publish per-run logs and exact model versions, repeat runs, and report infrastructure failures separately. The recommendation extends beyond the paper's measured interventions. This study verifies no Verus/Rust implementation and proves no distributed protocol. It evaluates draft TLA+ artifacts rather than an autonomous multi-agent proof process.  Prose consultation is complete; independent review is pending. REVIEW.md records consultation scope and disposition. This is a whole-paper reading with selected static source inspection, not an experimental reproduction.  Fresh independent review found no material conclusion issue; two table-packaging defects were corrected (REVIEW.md).  All11tables/34refs,43literalquotes,16diagnostics;operational-vs-behavioral correctness,infra confounding,count arithmetic,proprietaryresults,source evaluator/split/prompt discrepancies.
    - number: 143 training, 31 validation, 31 test specs; 26 eligible test specs used
    - number: GPT-5 26/26 syntax and 7/26 TLC passes (26.9%)
    - number: 8.6% aggregate open-model TLC success (56/650); DeepSeek r1:8b 14/26
    - number: 2,600 open-model runs plus 130 proprietary runs

- CertiFlash reusable FTL proofs (2026)
    - title: CertiFlash: A Formal Verification Framework for Flash Translation Layers in Computational Solid State Drives
    - what: CertiFlash models FTLs as deterministic state machines with a global invariant covering mapping, isolation, integrity, ownership, and allocation, then packages reusable Rocq proofs so modified FTLs discharge five design-specific hypotheses instead of re-proving the full invariant. The framework proves invariant preservation, stepwise simulation, command precondition soundness, read-after-write correctness, and cross-address non-interference. It identifies five SSD failure surfaces and demonstrates ten reachable attacks when command bundles are unchecked. Four case studies cover new state, reimplemented operations, new operations, and changed invariants; inherited proofs and local obligations are measured in lines.
    - number: Rocq development has 21,884 lines, 432 definitions, and 778 closed theorem statements
    - number: base framework contributes 16,489 inherited lines; four case studies add 27–3,231 lines
    - number: clean build takes 93 seconds on Apple M5 Pro
    - number: ten failures across five attack surfaces are proved reachable when command preconditions are unchecked
    - limits: The reusable certificate assumes the custom FTL projects onto CertiFlash’s FTLState and proves five pure hypotheses over its operations; fields outside the projection are not automatically covered. The threat model treats faulty or malicious commands as possible, and each failure theorem is conditional on its stated hypothesis. Security follows Rocq kernel checking and no admitted axioms in the reported development, but implementation integration remains model/refinement dependent.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `certiflash-reusable-ftl-proofs-2026`

- Consensus as Collapse Policy: Communication Evidence, Horizons, and Prefix Decisions (2026)
    - what: # Consensus evidence and output specifications (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Grospić proposes a common vocabulary for comparing how consensus protocols turn communication evidence into decisions. This is a specification and comparison paper, arXiv:2607.10152v1, 11 July 2026, 48 pages. It expressly claims neither a new impossibility theorem nor a replacement for protocol-specific proofs. Its main product is the decomposition of a decision into evidence extraction, visibility and output. It supplies no implementation, benchmark, checked formalization or LLM evaluation. [Evidence: Q0, page excerpt containing title,abstract and source identity, PDF pp.1–1; Q2, page excerpt containing §1 scope,contributions and non-contributions, PDF pp.3–4; Q20, page excerpt containing §§9–12 related work,discussion,future work and conclusion, PDF pp.26–29; literal passages in quotes.json.]  The paper calls communication evidence with causal and evidential relations “order-2,” and a decided value, log or prefix “order-1.” These are semantic labels rather than type-order levels. An evidence domain H has a preorder ⊑: extending h to h′ preserves existing evidence and may add more. A protocol maps H through legitimacy extraction L to evidence E, through horizon restriction R to visible evidence V, and through a partial decision map C to outputs P. Thus D=C∘R∘L. “Collapse” means emitting that output; “collapse debt” means that the visible evidence still permits different terminal observations. The paper describes ways to repair or defer a decision, but Definition 3.1 gives no separate repair operation or temporal transition relation. No quantitative debt metric is defined despite the word “measures.” [Evidence: Q1, page excerpt containing §1 order terminology, PDF pp.2–2; Q3, page excerpt containing §§2.2–2.3 Definitions2.4–2.6, PDF pp.5–5; Q4, page excerpt containing §2.3 collapse debt, PDF pp.6–6; Q5, page excerpt containing §3 normal form and Definition3.1, PDF pp.6–7; literal passages in quotes.json.]  Definition 2.6 calls p determined when at least one admissible future yields p and all futures yielding any observation yield the same p. Reader assessment: this fits a fixed immutable slot or value, but literal equality is too strong when obs returns an entire growing prefix. A safe execution can expose [a] and later [a,b]; those prefixes differ while remaining compatible. The generic definition needs a fixed slot/height or prefix-membership observation, or an explicit prefix-compatibility relation. The CBC section explicitly interprets equality as ancestor/prefix compatibility, but that qualification is not supplied uniformly in Definition 2.6. This is a conceptual case, not a machine-checked counterexample. [Evidence: Q3, page excerpt containing §§2.2–2.3 Definitions2.4–2.6, PDF pp.5–5; Q14, page excerpt containing §7.6 CBC Casper, PDF pp.16–18; literal passages in quotes.json.]  Existence of one future observation and agreement among defined observations do not establish eventual decision on every fair execution. A continuation may keep C undefined forever. Likewise, a global evidence state and commuting maps alone do not establish that finite local views justify the same output: locality, indistinguishability, admissible executions and sufficient evidence still need formal contracts. These are reader obligations for turning the schema into a distributed safety/liveness theorem. [Evidence: Q3, page excerpt containing §§2.2–2.3 Definitions2.4–2.6, PDF pp.5–5; Q5, page excerpt containing §3 normal form and Definition3.1, PDF pp.6–7; Q7, page excerpt containing §4 complete FLP interpretation, PDF pp.8–9; literal passages in quotes.json.]  Definition 3.2 requires denotation maps for concrete and abstract H/E/V/P carriers to commute with L/R/C. Conditional on those equations, the composite concrete output denotes the specified output. This is an algebraic preservation obligation, not an established refinement of any supplied implementation. It leaves admissibility, distributed transitions, fairness, output compatibility and executable evidence checks to each instance. The paper does not discharge those obligations with a prover or checker. [Evidence: Q6, page excerpt containing Definition3.2 and commuting equations, PDF pp.7–8; Q2, page excerpt containing §1 scope,contributions and non-contributions, PDF pp.3–4; literal passages in quotes.json.]  The FLP discussion retains deterministic processes, reliable asynchronous messages and one possible crash. It explicitly rejects the idea that a richer history disproves FLP; evidence can grow while decision remains impossible to guarantee. Reader qualification: the §4 list of ways to escape FLP includes deferring collapse until richer evidence is visible. Waiting alone under unchanged FLP assumptions supplies no termination guarantee; neither authentication nor intersecting quorums by themselves supplies the missing timing or randomness assumption. The set-agreement/topology comparison is interpretive: it cites (k−1)-resilient possibility and k-failure impossibility, while acknowledging that crash message passing, shared-memory tasks and Byzantine protocols use different models. [Evidence: Q7, page excerpt containing §4 complete FLP interpretation, PDF pp.8–9; Q8, page excerpt containing §§5–6 model differences and impossibility comparison, PDF pp.10–11; literal passages in quotes.json.]  The reference baseline finalizes causally closed communication histories; a linear prefix is an optional later projection. It is a reference object rather than a practical protocol. Lamport's happened-before relation and logical clocks demonstrate a causal carrier and a total-order extension; the paper explicitly says this is not a consensus protocol. Its Byzantine Generals comparison contrasts oral-message recursion with signed reports as different ways to retain evidence before a decision. These are comparative descriptions, not new threshold proofs or independent validations of the referenced systems. [Evidence: Q9, page excerpt containing §7.1 reference baseline, PDF pp.12–12; Q10, page excerpt containing §7.2 Lamport causal ordering, PDF pp.12–12; Q11, page excerpt containing §7.3 Byzantine Generals, PDF pp.12–13; literal passages in quotes.json.]  The Paxos case separates phase-1 recovery from phase-2 majority choice, and Multi-Paxos repeats it across slots. The displayed L map retains only accepted triples (acceptor, ballot, value), while R asks whether an acceptor promised the current ballot and C asks about phase-2 accepts. Reader assessment: the displayed intermediate carrier does not carry all the promise, current-ballot and phase information those functions read. The named sorts are richer than the displayed set; additional evidence fields and phase contracts are necessary before the equations are a self-contained executable specification. [Evidence: Q12, page excerpt containing §7.4 Paxos and Multi-Paxos, PDF pp.13–14; literal passages in quotes.json.]  The Paxos prose also says the highest accepted phase-1 value is the only value whose observation can remain invariant under admissible extension. A minority acceptance alone need not already force a globally chosen value: the normal highest-ballot rule constrains the current proposer's safe choice, whereas a later quorum need not recover a value accepted only by an excluded minority if nothing was chosen. One must distinguish recovery constraints for a particular completed phase 1 from a universal forced-output claim. This is a reader qualification to the abstract wording, not a claim that Paxos is unsafe. [Evidence: Q12, page excerpt containing §7.4 Paxos and Multi-Paxos, PDF pp.13–14; literal passages in quotes.json.]  HotStuff is presented through quorum certificates, locks and a three-chain decision horizon. Two-chain variants move obligations into view-change handling; the paper's point is that chain length is a design choice coupled to evidence rules. The schematic maps leave exact views, certificate validity, lock updates and ancestry premises abstract. They do not prove that shortening a chain alone preserves safety. [Evidence: Q13, page excerpt containing §7.5 HotStuff, PDF pp.15–16; literal passages in quotes.json.]  CBC Casper uses justified messages, an estimator, equivocation evidence and a fault-weight-bounded set of future states. Safety means the future estimator stays compatible with the candidate; the paper explicitly separates this from liveness. Its equivocation formula pairs same-sender messages neither of which depends on the other. Reader ambiguity: if ≺ is strict, a message does not precede itself and the displayed formula needs m1≠m2 to exclude self-pairs; a reflexive dependency convention could instead avoid the issue. The paper does not settle that convention in the formula. This is an ambiguity, not an asserted flaw in the referenced CBC protocol. [Evidence: Q14, page excerpt containing §7.6 CBC Casper, PDF pp.16–18; literal passages in quotes.json.]  Cordial Miners contributes blocklace, ratification, super-ratification, final leaders and ordered fragments selected by the τ walk. The paper reads these as progressively stronger evidence before ordering. The sketch needs the protocol's actual distinct-creator, threshold, wave and validity premises; simply counting blocks is insufficient. The review does not independently verify the cited Cordial Miners proof. [Evidence: Q15, page excerpt containing §7.7 Cordial Miners, PDF pp.18–19; literal passages in quotes.json.]  Mysticeti-C reads support, skip and implicit certificate patterns from an uncertified slot DAG. A short direct horizon can commit, skip or remain undecided; a later committed anchor supplies an indirect horizon. Ordered output stops at the first undecided slot. The displayed output carrier includes undecided and C returns it as an ordinary result, while the generic specification makes C undefined when no terminal decision exists. The prose later says undecided is not a terminal prefix decision. A formalization therefore needs a tagged intermediate classification followed by a partial terminal decision, or an equivalent explicit reconciliation. [Evidence: Q16, page excerpt containing §7.8 Mysticeti-C, PDF pp.20–21; Q17, page excerpt containing §7.9 worked Mysticeti-C slot, PDF pp.21–22; literal passages in quotes.json.]  The worked Mysticeti-C case uses a symbolic slot s and later anchor evidence. It gives no concrete n/f thresholds, fully enumerated DAG execution, checked certificate derivation or machine-checked proof. Negative skip evidence is relative to a sufficient committed-anchor horizon; absence of a certificate in an arbitrary finite asynchronous cut is not itself a safe skip rule. The example illustrates the vocabulary and explicitly disclaims an additional protocol proof. [Evidence: Q17, page excerpt containing §7.9 worked Mysticeti-C slot, PDF pp.21–22; literal passages in quotes.json.]  The synthesis compares what evidence is retained, compressed or hidden, what a decision horizon inspects and what happens when it is insufficient. Its communication discussion distinguishes messages, bytes, payload size, amortization and retransmissions. Full gossip can use O(n²) messages for n blocks per round, while amortized per-block accounting can be O(n); HotStuff uses O(n) votes and leader dissemination with compact certificates. The paper also cites O(n²) Bracha reliable-broadcast messages per broadcast and a cubic-cost tradeoff attributed to Mysticeti-C; these are cited complexity claims, not measurements made here. Reader arithmetic: assuming each receiver obtains n independent block payloads, a relayed DAG can change message counts but cannot make their total payload bytes constant; see reader-arithmetic.json. No new linear-message DAG protocol, lower bound or measured performance ranking is established. [Evidence: Q18, page excerpt containing §§8.1–8.3 comparison dimensions, PDF pp.22–24; Q19, page excerpt containing §8.4 communication units and compression, PDF pp.24–25; literal passages in quotes.json.]  Appendix A.1 sketches HoneyBadgerBFT as encrypted reliable broadcast, binary-agreement-based asynchronous common subset, and threshold decryption. Randomness helps progress. Its displayed evidence set abstracts over the binary-agreement and decryption inputs later stages require. Appendix A.2 sketches Raft using terms, matching indices and majority commitment. Its triple “follower i stores index j in term T” leaves entry term versus storage/current term ambiguous; the current-term filter needs that distinction, eligible voter counts including the leader as appropriate, log matching and election premises. Returning an index also needs decoding to the declared committed-prefix carrier. These are gaps in the comparison sketch, not observed implementation defects. [Evidence: Q22, page excerpt containing AppendixA.1 randomized agreement and HoneyBadgerBFT, PDF pp.29–30; Q23, page excerpt containing AppendixA.2 Raft, PDF pp.30–31; literal passages in quotes.json.]  Appendices A.3–A.6 cover PBFT prepared/commit evidence and view changes, Stellar quorum slices and blocking sets, Tendermint prevote/precommit locks, and Streamlet notarized-chain patterns. Each names carriers and decision horizons while leaving protocol-specific validity and intersection conditions schematic. In particular, Stellar needs the relevant intact-set/quorum-intersection assumptions, and the phase/round rules cannot be inferred from quorum size alone. These entries are descriptions rather than standalone correctness proofs. [Evidence: Q24, page excerpt containing AppendixA.3 PBFT, PDF pp.32–32; Q25, page excerpt containing AppendixA.4 Stellar, PDF pp.33–33; Q26, page excerpt containing AppendixA.5 Tendermint, PDF pp.34–34; Q27, page excerpt containing AppendixA.6 Streamlet, PDF pp.34–35; Q21, page excerpt containing AppendixA scope disclaimer, PDF pp.29–29; literal passages in quotes.json.]  Appendices A.7–A.11 cover Algorand committee eligibility, Ouroboros stable chain prefixes, GRANDPA weighted prevote/precommit ancestry, Casper FFG checkpoint links/slashing, and Snowman/Avalanche sampling confidence. Random committee selection, common prefix and sampling confidence require explicit bad-event probabilities or conditional admissibility before they imply the generic universal-future invariant. The paper acknowledges probabilistic families but gives no unified ε-bound or failure-event formalization. GRANDPA and FFG also leave the detailed validity/sufficiency predicates abstract. [Evidence: Q28, page excerpt containing AppendixA.7 Algorand, PDF pp.35–36; Q29, page excerpt containing AppendixA.8 Ouroboros, PDF pp.36–37; Q30, page excerpt containing AppendixA.9 GRANDPA, PDF pp.37–38; Q31, page excerpt containing AppendixA.10 CasperFFG, PDF pp.38–39; Q32, page excerpt containing AppendixA.11 Snowman and Avalanche, PDF pp.39–40; literal passages in quotes.json.]  Appendices A.12–A.16 cover Jolteon/Ditto optimistic and fallback certificates, DAG-Rider reliable-broadcast waves with a random leader, Hashgraph gossip ancestry and virtual fame voting, Aleph units/forks/coin-based ordering, and Narwhal/Tusk/Bullshark availability versus ordering. The comparison distinguishes randomized asynchronous ordering from deterministic ordering under partial synchrony. It does not supply new proofs for the listed protocols. The 38 references are a bibliography, not 38 independently evaluated systems. [Evidence: Q33, page excerpt containing AppendixA.12 Jolteon and Ditto, PDF pp.40–41; Q34, page excerpt containing AppendixA.13 DAG-Rider, PDF pp.41–42; Q35, page excerpt containing AppendixA.14 Hashgraph, PDF pp.42–43; Q36, page excerpt containing AppendixA.15 Aleph, PDF pp.43–44; Q37, page excerpt containing AppendixA.16 Narwhal,Tusk and Bullshark, PDF pp.44–45; Q38, page excerpt containing complete38-item bibliography, PDF pp.45–48; literal passages in quotes.json.]  For this knowledge base, the useful result is an explicit checklist: name evidence, validity, visibility, admissible futures, output compatibility and concrete-to-abstract maps before accepting a consensus specification. The prefix, progress, missing-evidence and undecided cases are concrete checks for LLM-generated specifications. A TLA+ model could add local views, temporal progress and refinement obligations; Verus could verify evidence validation and decision invariants in a Rust implementation. These are reader proposals. The paper supplies no TLA+, Verus, Rust implementation or agent/specification/proof benchmark. [Evidence: Q5, page excerpt containing §3 normal form and Definition3.1, PDF pp.6–7; Q6, page excerpt containing Definition3.2 and commuting equations, PDF pp.7–8; Q20, page excerpt containing §§9–12 related work,discussion,future work and conclusion, PDF pp.26–29; literal passages in quotes.json.]  The introduction gives a concrete Byzantine quorum example: n=3f+1 validators and quorums of 2f+1 intersect in at least f+1 validators, including at least one correct validator when at most f are faulty. Weighted, flexible and federated models require their own intersection assumptions. [Evidence: RQ3, §1 Introduction, PDF p.2; exact passage in quotes.json.]
    - limits: Preordered evidence states and admissible futures; legitimacy L, horizon R and partial output C. Fault, timing, randomness and quorum models remain protocol-specific. The paper’s standard BFT example uses n=3f+1 and quorums of 2f+1, intersecting in at least f+1 validators. Whole-prefix equality versus prefix compatibility; possible versus eventual observation; local-view and admissibility contracts; missing promise/phase evidence in displayed Paxos maps; undecided classification versus partial terminal output; probabilistic failure-event conditions; schematic Raft entry-term, voter and output-index premises. No TLA+, Verus, Rust or LLM benchmark.

- Guidelines for Producing Concise LNT Models, Illustrated with Formal Models of the Algorand Consensus Protocol (2026)
    - what: # Whole-paper read: Guidelines for Producing Concise LNT Models, Illustrated with Formal Models of the Algorand Consensus Protocol (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Hubert Garavel. MARS2026, EPTCS443,pp43–83,doi10.4204/EPTCS.443.5;arXiv2604.05006,41pages. The paper refactors a finite LNT model of Algorand's BBA* phase and reports equivalence/model checks. Its strongest directly measured result is model compaction with strong bisimilarity checked for two four-node configurations. It supplies a detailed transformation sequence, printed model code and property scripts. It does not establish correctness of the deployed Algorand implementation or arbitrary-size Byzantine consensus. [Q0,Q1,Q2,Q10,Q11]  ## What changes, and what is preserved  The original Urbino model has967LNT lines. Application-specific changes produce U0 with769lines. These changes include new observations, merged/split events, a different attacker and a fair-coin correction; the paper explicitly says many “do not preserve bisimilarity properties.” Thus equivalence of U0–U4 does not imply equivalence with the original967-line model. [Q2,Q11,Q14]  Four generic transformations then produce U1–U4: extract shared modules, replace duplicated control branches with data parameters, merge common sequences, and replace mutually recursive tail calls with assignments and explicit loops. Strong bisimilarity means corresponding states can match every labelled transition in both directions. Authors report BCG_CMP checks for A4,0(four honest nodes) and A2,2(two honest/two malicious), with incremental checks after individual changes. They give structural/algebraic explanations, not a mechanized theorem covering every use of every transformation. The common-suffix choice law is explicitly qualified as failing for common prefixes. [Q3,Q4,Q5,Q6,Q7]  The §2.2 table gives total/nonblank-noncomment lines: U0=769/705,U1=607/552,U2=320/282,U3=288/250,U4=250/212. U0→U4 therefore removes67.49%of total lines and69.93%of substantive lines, ratios3.076and3.325 respectively; these are reader arithmetic. The original967→250comparison also includes semantic changes. Readability and reduced maintenance effort are the author's qualitative judgments; there is no reader study or maintenance-cost experiment. [Q3,Q11]  State counts illustrate that fewer source lines need not produce fewer unminimized states. For the honest-node/malicious-node/A4,0/A2,2 LTSs, U0 has1740/3190/24230/42509states; U1 is identical; U2 has1761/3190/24599/42509;U3 has1681/3046/20665/34679;U4 has1723/3130/20905/35059. After strong-bisimulation minimization every version has558/840/12059/19486states. Identical minimized counts alone would not prove bisimilarity; the reported equivalence checks supply that evidence. No runtime or memory benchmark is given. [Q3]  ## Concrete transformation cases  U1 extracts duplicated node/counter code and splits data definitions into types/constants/channels. U2 merges honest, malicious and disguised behaviors using a MORALITY parameter; node IDs≤H identify honest nodes. U3 factors common suffixes/prefixes and reduces NODE's process calls22→9. Its final branch-factorization example is acknowledged as harder to understand despite being shorter. U4 exposes an inner three-step coin loop inside an outer block-processing loop, replacing12recursive calls across node/counter code with3loops. [Q4,Q5,Q6,Q7]  The loop discovery uses pairs of control location and step residue, rather than control locations alone. The initial random-bit process had combined graded-consensus abstraction with a later fair coin; separating these roles restores the nested-loop structure. The earlier probability P_H used for the later coin is corrected to0.5 among the specific changes before the equivalence-preserving sequence. The counter independently accepts propagated/self-propagated bits, increments K0/K1 and resets them after TALLY. AnnexB prints U2/U3/U4 node code and recursive/imperative counter variants, enabling direct comparison. [Q7,Q14,Q15,Q16]  ## What is verified  Visual checking first hides irrelevant events and minimizes by branching bisimulation, which allows matching behavior across internal steps. Four-node models have roughly12000–20000strongly minimized states,43000–69000transitions and131labels. SYNC-only slicing exposes alternating BEGIN/END barriers. The block interface exposes proposal bits0/1 and commit-proposed/commit-empty events: for H≥T the observed model allows acceptance or rejection; for H<T the illustrated attacker systematically rejects bit1 proposals. Earlier pre-v5models have visible deadlock traces. These are observations of the stated abstract models and attacker, not an arbitrary-fault resilience theorem. [Q8]  Equivalence checks compare sliced systems with small hand-written expected processes: TALLY events form a cyclic four-dimensional hypercube; SELF_PROPAGATE has a more involved loop with optional per-node propagation. Hidden bit/counter values are renamed toX, so those checks establish event-pattern behavior after abstraction, not correctness of discarded values. SET_BIT slices remain difficult to inspect, motivating value-passing MCL formulas. [Q9,Q10]  AnnexC reports16named property scripts checked on both U4configurations: P1deadlock absence;P2SYNC alternation;P3aTALLY counter sum≤4 andP3bhypercube;P4a/bPROPAGATE patterns;P5a/bSELF_PROPAGATE patterns;P6a/bselection P_IN/P_OUTpatterns;P7a/bP_ZERO/P_ONEpatterns;P8ablock-interface classification;P9a/b/cper-node SET_BITinitialization and step progression. The latter formulas require an eventual next bit-setting event or, in P9b, a new proposal, without intervening conflicting events. They are local sequencing checks. The conclusion leaves formulas connecting all nodes' votes to the expected consensus decision as future work. The printed P9cdescription is grammatically incomplete, while its formula explicitly targets SET_BIT(ID,0,any). [Q17,Q18,Q19,Q20]  The models have infinite loops. Absence of deadlocks does not itself prove eventual block commitment. The paper does not report probabilistic model checking or almost-sure termination; probability values occur in observable choice labels, and probabilistic verification is proposed as future work. The evaluated equivalences and MCL results should not be turned into a quantitative probability-of-failure guarantee. Larger configurations of6,8,10nodes are mentioned as explored compositionally, without this paper providing comparable property outcomes or resource measurements for them. [Q10,Q11,Q15,Q16]  ## Model assumptions and limits  AnnexA fixes node population, honesty and equal stake; omits money/rewards, cryptography and leader election; abstracts graded consensus by a probability; reduces step numbers modulo3; and synchronizes every voting phase with global BEGIN/ENDbarriers. Committees are independently selected possibilities ranging from0toNmembers rather than fixed sizeC. The attacker does not fork: it manipulates votes to reject selected proposed blocks. U0 replaces shared BOYCOTT synchronization with independent malicious behavior triggered by proposal bit1. Therefore the model covers one censorship-style strategy, not all Byzantine behavior or dynamic corruption. [Q12,Q13,Q14]  Two documented interpretation choices are fresh committees per step rather than per round, and a persistent per-node bit across steps. Printed constants use N=4,C=3,T=2,H=2(or4),P_V=C/N,and P_H=p²(1+p−p²),p=H/N. The main process synchronizes nodes on proposals, propagation, barriers and both commit events. Reader observation: agreement at those shared commit events is constrained by the model composition; the paper's checks do not independently establish all implementation behaviors fit that composition. Relaxing synchrony and moving from historical BBA* to deployed ABFT remain future work. [Q12,Q13,Q15,Q16,Q11]  For this collection, the paper contributes a concrete workflow for simplifying distributed-system specifications while checking equivalence and targeted temporal properties. Its relevance to LLM-generated specifications is my inference: proposed cleanup should retain an explicit preservation target, and success on self-chosen event slices should not substitute for the intended protocol guarantee. The paper has no Verus, Rust, TLA+ or LLM experiment.  ## Read and evidence scope  Read all41pages, §§1–8,29reference entries and AnnexesA–C, including all32specific changes,9model-code subsections,16property scripts and auxiliary processes. Inspected all5figures, the full §2.2table and dense formula/code renderings; complete extracted text supplied the remaining material. No external author artifact was linked or acquired beyond the full code printed in the paper. Official arXiv and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. Publisher PDF differs in66bytes but yields identical raw extracted text; hashes and comparison saved. No author code, compiler, model checker, solver or generated LTS was executed or loaded. All reported tool results remain attributed to the author.
    - limits: boundedfixedsynchronousmodel;restrictedattacker;abstractGC/crypto;equivalenceamongrefactoringsnotoriginal/deployedprotocol
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.05006)
    - record id: `guidelines-for-producing-concise-2026`

- Lean4Agent FormalAgentLib and LeanEvolve (2026)
    - title: Lean4Agent: Formal Modeling and Verification for Agent Workflow and Trajectory
    - what: Presents Lean4Agent, a Lean4 dependent-type framework with FormalAgentLib for structural, semantic and execution-trajectory verification of LLM-agent workflows, plus LeanEvolve for verification-guided workflow refinement. Workflows are typed heterogeneous graphs of read/write variables, natural-language instructions and execution types; trajectories record pre/post states and reasoning/tool traces. Layer 1 checks node reachability, edge validity and read/write consistency. Layer 2 translates explicit and implicit variables into decidable predicates and graph-level information-flow/context predicates, proving precondition entailment under an LLMExec local-correctness assumption. Layer 3 checks concrete trajectories using Lean propositions, Python validators and LLM-as-judge, localizing failed steps. LeanEvolve feeds diagnostics and optional test feedback to an LLM for targeted workflow edits, with a pure-LLM exploratory mode. Experiments generate 40 workflows, compare verification-passing/failing workflows across five LLMs on 50 hard SWE-Bench-Verified tasks and 100 ELAIP-Bench questions, and study graph-predicate and pure-LLM ablations.
    - number: Layer-2 passing workflows improve SWE task rate by 14.80 percentage points (95% CI 10.00–19.60)
    - number: ELAIP passing workflows improve by 9.07 points (95% CI 5.66–13.07)
    - number: LeanEvolve adds 7.47 points on SWE, raising 56.93% to 64.40%
    - number: formal-guided evolution solves 7.00 points more initially failed ELAIP cases than pure-LLM evolve
    - limits: Layer-2 soundness depends on LLMExec: each step satisfying predicates is assumed to produce postconditions; this abstracts away failures of long-horizon black-box LLM behavior. Custom predicates may have only name existence and natural-language explanations, and trajectory checks can rely on an LLM judge. Evaluation uses small random subsets (50 SWE and 100 ELAIP), 40 generated workflows and contemporary model versions, so generalization and causal effects are limited. The paper is a preprint and reports no mechanized verification of the LLM internals or distributed-system protocols.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06523); [notes: ## Lean4Agent (2026)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `lean4agent-formalagentlib-and-leanevolve-2026`

- Mechanized JavaScript Regex Semantics (2026)
    - title: Formal Verification for JavaScript Regular Expressions: A Proven Mechanized Semantics and Its Applications (Extended Version)
    - what: Defines and mechanizes a complete backtracking-tree semantics for modern JavaScript regular expressions, proves equivalence to an embedding of ECMAScript semantics, and formally verifies the PikeVM matching algorithm. The semantics records all possible matches and priority rather than only the top match. Rocq proofs establish contextual-equivalence rewrites and a chain from regex semantics through PikeTree to compiled PikeVM bytecode; the paper proves PikeVM correctness against the ECMAScript specification and examines optimizer rewrites, including counterexamples.
    - number: Regex-equivalence proof development exceeds 6,000 lines of Rocq
    - number: All definitions and results are mechanized in Rocq
    - number: Theorem 16 proves PikeVM correctness; no runtime benchmark table is reported
    - limits: The mechanization targets the specified modern JavaScript regex feature set and leaves several extensions (for example additional engine features and termination/complexity questions) as future work. Contextual equivalence is intentionally stronger or more directional than simple observational equivalence in some cases; performance benchmarking is not the paper’s focus. Section 7 explicitly leaves verification of a concrete implementation and a proof of linear step complexity to future work.

- Move higher-order MVP (2026)
    - title: Formal Verification of Imperative First-Class Functions in Move
    - what: Extends the Move Prover to imperative first-class functions that can be passed, stored in structs and persisted in global storage. Behavioral predicates (requires/aborts/ensures) and state labels specify function-value effects and intermediate memory snapshots; SMT dispatch splits known callees and uses predicates for unknown closures. The verifier supports mutable references/global writes under Move’s alias-free resource model, two-state predicates and quantified state labels. Weakest-precondition inference over Move bytecode generates behavioral specifications for function-valued code, with validation tests covering closures, stored dispatch and state transitions. Static separation of Move memory avoids separation-logic encodings for these calls.
    - number: Closure suite: 19 test modules, about 240 verified functions and 66 expected-error diagnoses
    - number: Tests complete under 5 minutes on GitHub runners; default 40 seconds per verification condition
    - number: Move version 2.4 and MVP Aptos commit pinned in paper
    - number: No larger open benchmark yet; higher-order Move shipped late 2025
    - limits: Function-value dispatch currently enumerates all closure variants reaching a call site, so open or dynamically loaded modules may not scale. State labels are individually bound; sequences of labels and fold-like higher-order iteration remain future work. Nonlinear arithmetic may require user proof hints. Evaluation is a test suite, not a broad benchmark, and Move-specific resource/alias assumptions differ from Rust.

- Scimitar proof decomposition (2026)
    - title: Interactive Safety Verification of Distributed Protocols by Inductive Proof Decomposition
    - what: Introduces inductive proof decomposition and the Scimitar tool for interactively constructing compositional safety proofs. An inductive proof graph decomposes lemmas by protocol actions; counterexample traces are localized and variable slicing hides irrelevant state. Scimitar combines TLC and Apalache to generate counterexamples to inductive proof obligations and TLAPS to validate final proof graphs. The user works backward from a target property, adding support edges and local lemmas; static slicing presents only variables relevant to each node. Five TLA+ protocols were proved, including industrial-scale AsyncRaft with asynchronous messaging and fine-grained local state. The authors report the first invariant effort at that Raft complexity and inspect proof-graph structure as a correctness insight.
    - number: 5 protocol specifications
    - number: AsyncRaft: 12 variables, 11 actions, 638 spec LOC/920 proof LOC, 39 lemmas, median slice 5 variables (0.42)
    - number: AsyncRaft inductive invariant developed in approximately 3 human-weeks
    - number: Other reported proof-development burdens: 1–2 human-months for smaller invariants
    - limits: TLC/Apalache counterexamples use finite parameter bounds and do not themselves prove arbitrary-size invariants; TLAPS validation is required for the final proof. Human guidance remains necessary to add lemmas and edges. Evaluation is on five TLA+ specifications and one implementation; generality to other formalisms is untested. AsyncRaft development took human effort despite automation.

- three-layer agent-skill capability verification (2026)
    - title: Methods for Formal Verification of Agent Skills: Three Layers Toward a Mechanically Checkable Capability-Containment Proof
    - what: Defines two-sided semantics for agent skills—deterministic script-side and nondeterministic LLM-side—and composes three verification layers: static capability analysis, refinement-typed tool-call envelopes, and SMT-bounded checking of runtime traces. A proof-carrying skill bundle is rechecked at bootstrap. Separates script and LLM projections of traces, states containment against declared capabilities, proves soundness and incompleteness for each method, and gives a composition theorem whose sole residual is the LLM’s refusal-to-act surface handled by the runtime biconditional. The implementation includes JavaScript modules, signed evidence files, a bootstrap checker, and a worked SKILL.md example.
    - number: 53-test unit suite and end-to-end CLI (§Abstract, §8.4)
    - number: All 53 tests pass (§8.4)
    - number: Layer 3 takes a few seconds per skill at Kmax=100; bootstrap re-check runs in milliseconds (§9.6)
    - number: Implementation ships as zero-runtime-dependency JavaScript modules (§Abstract)
    - limits: The paper explicitly does not verify model weights, training data, or decoding stochasticity; the LLM is treated as an adversarial nondeterministic oracle constrained by typed dispatch. Method C is bounded by transaction-buffer horizon Kmax, while methods A/B cover only their respective script and dispatch surfaces. Soundness depends on the underlying analyzers, type system, SMT encoding, and runtime biconditional.

- Towards System-Oriented Formal Verification of Local-First Access Control (2026)
    - what: # Whole-paper read: Towards System-Oriented Formal Verification of Local-First Access Control (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Florian Jacob, Johanna Stuber and Hannes Hartenstein; arXiv2604.23560v1,26April2026,PaPoC2026-related manuscript,8PDFpages. The contribution is a capability-based authorization design and three deliberately restricted Verus implementations. The paper explicitly says its full §3target has not yet been met. Its results concern simplified authorization/query functions assuming a valid eventually consistent chronicle; they do not verify Matrix, Keyhive, a cryptographic hash implementation, or a deployed replication stack. [Q0,Q1,Q3,Q10,Q13]  ## Threat model and intended semantics  A local-first entity acts on its own replica and exchanges events asynchronously. The threat model permits any strict subset of entities to be Byzantine, f/n<1, including selective omission, equivocation and backdating. Events are assumed authenticated and integrity-protected. The underlying hash chronicle is assumed Byzantine fault-tolerant, append-only and eventually consistent; collision-resistant identifiers fix an event's causal past. This is the input contract for the authorization layer, rather than a new proof of the replication or cryptographic layer. Eventual dissemination requires the stated chronicle/anti-entropy assumptions; it does not follow merely from having one honest participant. [Q2,Q4,Q9]  The mathematical design uses four event kinds:create,grant,revoke,assign. A grant specifies subject, recipient and operation capability; a use presents the grant's identifier; revocation references that identifier. Events combine an invocation and a downward-closed set of precursor identifiers. The printed identifier contract idealizes collision resistance as equality of identifiers iff equality of events. A valid group has one creation event, no event concurrent with creation, and valid timestamps. Logging appends an event after the current history; joining takes set union. The name query returns the latest authorized assign events, allowing multiple concurrent names. These are the intended §3semantics, not all jointly verified features. [Q5,Q6]  Authorization requires a matching authorized grant in the event's causal past, no matching authorized revocation in its past or concurrent history, and, for a revoke, a referenced grant in its past. Creation and setup precursors are authorized by definition. Extending revocations to concurrent uses prevents an attacker from bypassing a revocation by backdating a new use. It also makes authorization non-monotonic: new information can invalidate an earlier result. [Q6,Q7]  The paper distinguishes authorization based only on immutable precursors (“precursive”) from authorization based on the available group including concurrent events (“discursive”). A subtle example is revocationa concurrent with useb, followed by revocationc concurrent witha but afterb: revokinga can reauthorizeb even thoughc followsb. Concurrency is not transitive. Thus a property's dependence cannot safely be inferred just by looking at direct time order. This motivates distinguishing what may be stored/broadcast from what currently affects queries. [Q7]  The three stated security invariants are:precursor-based authorization is unchanged by adding events, while current authorization can change through authorized revocations; a changed query result requires an authorized newly added event; and an invocation unauthorized now remains unauthorized when backdated. These are specifications of the intended behavior. The paper's explanation that the full Spec.2supports them is not itself a mechanized proof of all Spec.2features. [Q8]  ## What the Verus case study reports  The three verified restrictions separate the difficult features. First, assign capabilities are fixed before creation and cannot later change. Second, the creator may grant assign rights after creation, but nobody may revoke. Third, assign rights are initially granted and the creator may revoke them, but new grants are not introduced afterward. The third case permits concurrent assign/revoke conflicts; it avoids mutually revoking equally privileged entities. Supporting both grants and revocations in one algorithm, post-creation delegation, query/membership capabilities, self-revocation, public joining, sparse replication and group changeover are future work. [Q10,Q11,Q12]  Specification functions describe pure mathematical behavior; executable functions have contracts linking their results to it. Figure4illustrates a log contract accepting exactly the events authorized by the specification, with an explicit fresh-identifier availability precondition. It is a shortened code example with ellipses, not the complete executable implementation. Authors report total and pure queries, precursor authorization as a group type invariant, and the restricted constructions supporting current authorization/revocation safety. If correct replicas eventually hold the same chronicle, deterministic total queries give the same answer. That conditional convergence argument does not prove dissemination, bounded latency, or settlement in a perpetually changing history. [Q9,Q10,Q11]  Totality has a material trust boundary. Authors report modifying vstd to add no_unwind contracts, assuming standard-library functions terminate and allocation/randomness system calls do not panic. They also add the assumption that cloning a HashSet preserves its elements, conditional on Rust std correctness. Ghost specifications and proofs impose no runtime checks according to the paper; the paper provides no throughput, memory or latency experiment demonstrating a numerical runtime saving. Its “zero runtime cost” refers to erasing verification annotations, not to a proof that the algorithms execute for free. [Q9,Q11]  The evaluation is a proof-of-concept experience report. It supplies no complete table of proof obligations, solver times, source/proof line counts, hardware measurements, test-population results or comparative performance study. The artifact README says verification “should complete in a hand full of seconds”; this is an informal expected duration, not a saved timing measurement. A practical difficulty was replacing unsupported iterator combinators with for loops and proving their invariants; authors call this among the most time-consuming parts. Their claim that Verus is accessible/future-proof is an author judgment, not a measured user-study result. [Q11,Q13,S0]  ## Static artifact comparison  The paper-linked repository was retrieved at commit3d6bdaf63c192ada9039f4f61127744465827ed0. Its README identifies Verus release0.2026.01.23.1650a05. The Makefile invokes Verus on main.rs, which imports separate modules for the chronicle, three authorization variants, types, helpers and assumed specifications. I inspected the whole Rust source tree. The HashSetclone assumption is explicit; the only external_body in these sources wraps printing. No assume/admit proof escape appeared beyond that declared assume_specification and imported library axioms. This describes these project files, not the full trusted Verus/vstd closure. [S0,S1,S2,S3,S4,S5,S6,S7,S8,S9]  The source makes further simplifications concrete. EntityID/EventID areu64, and Chronicle::find_free_id scans from0 for a locally unused identifier under an explicit availability precondition. Events contain the full predecessor set. There is no cryptographic hashing/signature verification, replica transport or join operation in these project modules. Grant/Revoke operations target an entity's SetName authorization, without the full Spec.2grant-identifier/delegation structure. The immutable case consults setup_grants; the monotonic case permits creator grants; the non-monotonic case treats all entities as initially able to SetName and disables that right when a matching revocation appears in the relevant history. A type invariant requires all stored events to be causally authorized. name's executable result must equal its specification's latest concurrently authorized SetName values. These contracts support the stated restricted authorization layer; they do not implement the complete hash-chronicle data type. [S1,S3,S4,S5,S6,S7]  main.rs supplies four hand-written scenarios with proof assertions: reject Dave's unauthorized assignment and retain two concurrent names; reject an unauthorized grant and a use concurrent with its grant; accept a causally valid backdated assignment but exclude it from current name results; and observe{name1}→{name1,name2}→{name1}when a concurrent revocation arrives. I read these scenarios without executing or re-verifying them. Some chronicle clones are executable statements labelled “for verification purposes only”; the general proof-erasure statement should not be read as a measured guarantee that this entire example has no verification-related executable work. [S8]  The paper's reported no_unwind experiment cannot be reconstructed from this snapshot alone: no no_unwind annotation or modified-vstd patch occurs in the acquired project files. The Nix/Cargo metadata point to external Verus/vstd sources, which I did not inspect. Thus the paper's conditional totality claim remains attributed to the authors; absence in the project snapshot does not establish that their separate experiment failed. The README's expected fast verification was not reproduced. [Q9,S0,S1,S8,S9]  ## Contribution and read scope  This paper directly adds a verified-Rust authorization case study to the distributed-systems collection. It isolates the trust placed in replication, cryptographic identity, library specifications and resource-related behavior. Its potential use in evaluating proof-writing agents is my inference: assess the exact restricted capability policy and trusted library additions before calling a whole system verified. The paper has no TLA+ or LLM experiment. [Q9,Q10,Q11,Q13]  Read all8PDFpages,37references,all4figures,both complete mathematical specification boxes and all3security-invariant formulas; inspected every page render. The final PDFpage contains all37references. Read all9Rust source files,README,Makefile,Cargo.toml,flake.nix and relevant lock metadata;15static text files acquired withSHA256/Gitblobidentities. External Verus/vstd dependencies and repository scenario images were not inspected. Official arXiv and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. No author execution, compiler, solver or prover run.
    - limits: validauthenticatedeventualchronicle;3simplifiedauthorizationvariants;modifiedvstd/librarytrust;fullcombinedpolicyfuturework
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23560)

- ACL2 dynamic-stake DAG BFT nonforking (2025)
    - title: Formal Verification of Blockchain Nonforking in DAG-Based BFT Consensus with Dynamic Stake
    - what: Formalizes a DAG-based BFT consensus protocol whose validator set and stake can change at every block, and machine-checks blockchain nonforking in ACL2 under a less-than-one-third faulty-stake condition for every active committee. The labeled transition system models validators, certificates, DAGs, network messages, rounds, dynamic bonding/unbonding, lookback active committees, anchor elections/commitments and block generation. Bonding changes become active only after a fixed lookback, resolving the DAG/blockchain circular dependency. The proof establishes interdependent invariants for committee agreement, certificate and DAG nonequivocation, previous quorums, anchor presence/voters/paths/nonforking, and blockchain nonforking. Anchor nonforking uses voter/quorum stake intersection; certificate nonequivocation uses signer quorum intersection, and both are proved together because each depends on the other through committee agreement. The ACL2 development has about 20,000 lines and verifies in under one minute on an Apple M3 Max (under two minutes single-core), applying to arbitrarily long executions and arbitrarily large states. The model intentionally includes deadlocking and other executions beyond an implementation, so safety transfers to implementations as subsets but liveness is not established.
    - number: faulty stake < one third of each active committee
    - number: lookback lkbk≥1; committee can change completely every block
    - number: about 20,000 ACL2 lines
    - number: under 1 minute on Apple M3 Max 16 cores; under 2 minutes single core
    - limits: Correct validators follow the abstract protocol and faulty validators are Byzantine; each active committee must have faulty stake at most fstk(w)<nstk(w)/3, with quorum stake qstk=nstk−fstk. Signatures/addresses are idealized and cryptographic details are omitted. The model is nondeterministic and a superset of implementation executions; validators can advance without certificates and deadlock, and the authors explicitly note that proving liveness would require augmenting the model. Only blockchain nonforking and related invariants are proved; performance, synchronization, transaction validity, fairness and implementation behavior are not evaluated. The ACL2 state-transition system and nonforking predicate are trusted modeling boundaries.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `acl2-dynamic-stake-dag-bft-nonforking-2025`
