- Astrogator (2025)
    - title: Towards Formal Verification of LLM-Generated Code from Natural Language Prompts
    - what: Introduces Astrogator, a verifier for LLM-generated Ansible programs. A user-readable Formal Query Language gives a precise, reviewable specification of natural-language intent; a State Calculus models stateful system effects; a symbolic interpreter and unifier compare generated playbook behavior with the query and report extra assumptions/actions. The formalization separates user intent U from an approved but possibly under-specified query semantics and explicitly defines the unresolved gap p∖s for user review. The State Calculus models attributes, nested elements, stateful functions, reads, writes, conditionals, cases and symbolic loops; unknown reads branch over possibilities, and symbolic lists are approximated by one representative element. Ansible modules are specified in a small module-definition language, while a hand-curated knowledge base maps high-level descriptions such as apache server or zsh configuration file to OS-specific packages and paths. On 1,260 generated playbooks from six LLMs, Astrogator accepts most test-correct programs and rejects most incorrect ones, but under-specified queries permit incorrect programs unless users review reported assumptions.
    - number: 21 benchmarks
    - number: 1,260 generated programs
    - number: 334 correct (26.5%)
    - number: 277/334 accepted (82.9%)
    - limits: The formalizer cannot always infer intent from ambiguous natural language, so the user must approve the formal query. The query is intentionally incomplete and the evaluation accepts all programs satisfying it without user review of p∖s. The Ansible compiler does not support all features, especially shell commands, blocks and handlers; symbolic-loop handling is imprecise and arbitrary loops are future work. Ground truth for 1,260 programs is based on tests across Debian 12.11, Ubuntu 24.04.2 and RHEL 9.6 VMs, not a proof of real-world correctness. Knowledge-base single-valued choices cause false rejections when multiple implementations are valid.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.13290); [notes: ## Astrogator, checking LLM Ansible code against a readable query (202](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `astrogator-2025`
- CCF smart casual verification (2025)
    - title: Smart Casual Verification of the Confidential Consortium Framework (CCF)
    - what: Industrial experience applying TLA+ specification/model checking, weighted simulation and implementation trace validation to the existing C++ Confidential Consortium Framework. Separate node-consensus and client-history specifications are aligned with production protocol details and incorporated in CI. The paper reports six consensus bugs found/fixed and a clarified read-only consistency limitation, plus tooling/effort and state-exploration measurements. Reconstructs all six safety/liveness cases and the unsuccessful first fix, distinguishes trace compatibility from universal implementation refinement, records granularity alignment via composition/stuttering and incomplete-state reconstruction. Saves all scale/coverage rows, effort figures, counterexample sizes and issue pointers. Preserves TEE/model trust, bounded exploration and non-linearizable read-only semantics; no all-executions C++ correctness theorem is claimed.
    - number: Six subtle bugs found, five concerning safety and one liveness
    - number: 400 LoC Trace spec; 88 commits to it and 107 changes to the consensus spec; implementation 63 kLoC C++
    - number: six subtle bugs found
    - number: 63 kLoC in C++ (§2)
    - limits: §§1–2: CCF runs application logic inside trusted execution environments while treating operators, host OS/hypervisor, network and storage as corruptible. This paper checks distributed protocol behavior under modeled failures, not hardware/firmware isolation, enclave security, cryptography, all 63k C++ LOC, or arbitrary Byzantine behavior of the protected consensus code. Production use in Azure Confidential Ledger is context, not evidence that every production behavior is covered. §§1.1,4,8: Smart casual verification combines formal specification, finite model checking/simulation and tests/trace checks. Consensus spec is unbounded but exhaustive checking restricts terms, requests and reconfiguration sequences. No TLAPS safety/liveness proof for all sizes or universal code-refinement proof is reported. Most CI exploration relies on bounded simulations because sufficiently realistic exhaustive exploration is too expensive. §2: CCF returns transaction results before replication; clients separately query Pending/Committed/Invalid status. A returned result may later be invalidated. Committed read-write transactions are strictly serializable; committed read-only transactions are serializable and may be stale. Pending branches offer fork-like consistency rather than arbitrary results. Broad prose about all committed transactions being linearizable needs the explicit read-only exception. §2: Transaction IDs are lexicographic term/index pairs. If two transactions commit, ID ordering agrees with execution ordering. Ancestor Commit says committed <t,i> implies same-term <t,j>, j≤i, committed. This concerns transaction identity/status, not a proof that any stale read immediately observes the newest committed write. §2.1: CCF customizes Raft with signed Merkle-root transactions, messaging without RPC correspondence, optimistic sentIndex advances, express catch-up, CheckQuorum, bootstrapping/reconfiguration and retirement/ProposeVote. A transaction is committed only after a subsequent signature is committed. The actual combined protocol needs verification; citing the base Raft proof does not establish these extensions. §2.1 reader qualification: Early description says an AE-ACK carries the last transaction in the follower log, while the Inaccurate AE-ACK bug/fix later constrains that index to the end of the received AE. The historical/protocol-description distinction must be retained rather than treating the early sentence as the final safe response rule. §§2.1,4: Reconfiguration needs majorities in each active configuration, rather than a majority of their union. A removed node remains retiring until retirement information is safely committed for future leaders. CheckQuorum steps down an isolated leader, but absent delivery/scheduling assumptions this is not a theorem that all executions make progress. §4: Consensus has 17 actions/13 variables, 12 local-state mappings plus network. Parameterization includes every nonempty initial node subset with any member as initial leader. Locally initiated timeout/signature/candidacy/step-down choices impose no clock synchrony. LogInv compares pairwise committed-log prefixes; AppendOnlyProp prevents committed-prefix rollback over time. Another 27 properties include MonoLogInv, requiring term increases only after signatures. §4: Simulation explores behaviors to bounded depth under a time quota; authors manually downweight failures to promote forward progress. Q-learning was implemented in TLC but no state-hash feature set beat manual weighting. More progress-rich traces may help find bugs but do not constitute exhaustive failure coverage or proof of liveness. §5: Client model uses append-only history and logBranches, independent of node count. History includes read-only/read-write requests/responses and status responses, but omits status requests/Pending replies. A simple shared value appends transaction identifiers so conflicts and observations are visible. It is a deliberately selected application workload, not a proved reduction of all arbitrary CCF application semantics. §5: A new branch can be any prefix of an existing branch containing the last committed transaction. Requests may execute at any still-active leader/term. PrevCommittedInv checks same-term committed ancestor statuses; ObservedRoInv demands that a subsequently committed read-only response observe a read-write transaction whose response preceded its request. The latter expectation is falsified and documented, not a proved CCF invariant. §6.1: Scenario driver serializes consensus execution deterministically and mocks governance/networking/other components, injecting loss/reordering/partition/delay. Original 13 scenarios and about 1.6k unit-test/2k Python end-to-end LOC precede the effort; Table 1 gives later exact scope counts. Initial fuzzing was abandoned for insufficient interesting coverage. Driver/mock accuracy is a boundary, as demonstrated by retirement bugs. §6.1: Driver uses a global logical ordering clock; distributed Lamport/vector alternatives are suggested but not implemented here. Fifteen added logging statements capture stable states at message and role-change points, immediately after a global lock for state changes. Constant-space values such as log lengths are recorded, not full log entries. Production logging compiles out, so the checks use instrumented test executions rather than continuous production monitoring. §6.1/§6.5: Traces initially remove/deduplicate bootstrapping events because early consensus spec omits that phase; later specification is expanded to model bootstrapping more faithfully. These describe successive coverage stages. A successful preprocessed trace validates only its retained observations and modeling assumptions, not excluded initialization behavior. §6.2: Trace spec checks existence of a spec behavior consistent with observed events/values: T∩S is nonempty. Nondeterministically reconstructed hidden state is existential. A successful finite trace check is evidence for those observations, not proof that all completions, all execution traces or unlogged state transitions meet the spec. If logging or mappings omit a decisive distinction, an incorrect implementation could remain compatible. §6.2: Send actions constrain current state, invoke high-level action and assert matching successor messages. Network changes from set to multiset to represent duplicate/resend occurrences. Trace network abstractions also explore ordering/duplication/loss guarantees. Such abstraction choices affect what is observable and accepted; they are not runtime network guarantees inferred from traces. §6.2.1 Listing 5: UpdateTerm·HandleAppendEntriesReq composes two model actions into one implementation receive. IsFault·Next admits unlogged network faults before a step; send-response events may stutter high-level variables when already modeled by another action. Composition hides intermediate states and stuttering changes observation granularity, so correctness depends on the mapping retaining semantically important events. Merely making a mismatch disappear is not an independent proof. §6.2.2–6.3: Start with a happy-path trace, add many assertions and cross-references, debug source and formulas. Invalid traces may indicate bogus logging, incorrect mapping, specification error or implementation bug; failure lacks an ordinary model-checking counterexample. Longest matching prefixes, unreachable-state graphs, failing subformulas and an unsatisfied breakpoint support diagnosis. §6.4: DFS stops after finding one compatible behavior, avoiding BFS enumeration of alternatives; a consistency trace goes from about one hour to under a second. This is an author example without trace-size distribution or controlled speedup suite, and does not mean all traces validate in under a second. Existential acceptance is essential to that optimization. §6.5: Logging/driver enhancements took about one day. Consensus alignment/tooling took two engineer-months over four months, including TLC composition/DFS/debug support, discrepancy diagnosis and abstraction design. Writing about 400 lines of Trace was minor; Table 1 counts 369. Trace saw 88 commits and consensus spec 107 changes. This is observed project effort, not a controlled productivity study. §6.5: Client consistency validation uses REST observations without source instrumentation, reconstructs other clients’ hidden transactions from observed IDs, and took about one engineer-week over two weeks, mainly by CCF experts. Smaller spec, previous experience and reused TLC changes confound a simple comparison with initial consensus effort. §7 Table 1: Approximate state counts/rates are measured on Azure DC8s v3. A log line is largely equivalent to a model action, according to authors, not a formal equality of semantic coverage. Different abstraction sizes, bounds, durations and distinct/generated-state counting prevent treating orders-of-magnitude state counts as a calibrated defect-detection probability. §7: Six serious consensus bugs comprise five safety issues and one liveness issue, found by combined modeling, source scrutiny, trace mismatch, simulation and functional/end-to-end reproduction. They are not six independent discoveries by model checking alone. Read-only non-linearizability is an additional documented consistency ambiguity and accepted behavior, not a seventh fixed consensus bug. §§7–8: Authors report all six fixed before user impact/customer reports; software had already been deployed. One-node-at-a-time operational reconfiguration masked the election bug, and absence of an incident from the first bad commit fix was attributed to chance. No external incident audit, current repository verification or independent bug reproduction was done in this reading. §§8–9: Paper chooses model checking for developer accessibility rather than deductive proof, despite compute cost. AI-assisted proof search is future possibility with a reference to neural theorem proving, not an experiment using LLM agents. Related-work sentence says IronFleet and Verdi both proved Raft; reader qualification: IronFleet uses a Paxos-based replicated state machine, so do not propagate that comparison as fact.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- ChonkyBFT (2025)
    - title: ChonkyBFT: Consensus Protocol of ZKsync
    - what: Presents ChonkyBFT, a partially synchronous committee BFT protocol for ZKsync with one voting round, single-slot finality, quadratic communication, and n≥5f+1. Safety, validity, and partial-synchrony progress are proved by quorum and timeout lemmas. The Quint specification and Apalache symbolic checking found pseudocode type/definition errors and attack paths; an 18-lemma inductive invariant was checked for bounded parameters. A deployment evaluation measures failure-free throughput, latency, resource use, and replication lag.
    - limits: At most f Byzantine replicas with n≥5f+1; authenticated reliable point-to-point channels, secure signatures/hashes, and partial synchrony after GST. Liveness additionally assumes block synchrony, periodic retransmission, bounded clock drift, and eventual correct leaders. Formal Apalache checks restrict to six replicas, at most one fault, three views, bounded message/QC sets, so they are model-level evidence. Performance tests are failure-free and exclude block synchronization.

- LAW (2025)
    - title: The LAW theorem: Local Reads and Linearizable Asynchronous Replication
    - what: The paper proves that no replicated register can have all three of these: a local read (a read with no messages), linearizability, and a guarantee that operations finish despite async delays and one crash. It designs almost-local reads (batched read validation) in Hermes, ZAB and Raft variants.
    - number: Raft-ALR 2.6x baseline throughput at 2% writes, 1.8x at 5%; up to 156 million requests/s
    - number: ZAB-ALR within 4% of ZAB at 1% writes; Hermes-ALR within 11% at 1% writes
    - number: 5 CloudLab servers, one million keys; reads batched up to 128
    - limits: A human wrote a mathematical proof about a mathematical register (deterministic servers, one active operation each, at most one crash). The correctness of the read algorithms is argued in prose. The Raft baseline relaxes linearizability, so the performance comparison is an upper bound. There is no LLM, no code proof and no person-hours.

- LLM human-readable PFV proofs (2025)
    - title: Towards LLM-based Generation of Human-Readable Proofs in Polynomial Formal Verification
    - what: Argues for using LLMs to draft human-readable explanations of polynomial formal-verification proofs, illustrated with induction and BDD size examples, while insisting that a formal reasoning engine must validate every generated claim. The paper places PFV in a resource-bounded setting for adders/multipliers and describes induction prompts for two-variable and three-variable monomial sums and totally symmetric Boolean functions, where BDD size is linear or quadratic under stated structures. It deliberately probes a multiplication-function answer: ChatGPT first gives a misleading variable-ordering claim, then the author identifies the complexity error, showing why LLM prose cannot be trusted as proof. Proposed future directions include retrieval-augmented proof context, chain-of-thought and feedback from formal tools, and application to modern data structures.
    - number: adders over 1,000 bits cited as PFV case
    - number: f2/f3 induction examples
    - number: O(n²) symmetric-function BDD bound
    - limits: This is an invited position paper with examples, not a validated LLM benchmark. ChatGPT 4o outputs can hallucinate and even the paper's displayed answer contains an empirically plausible but complexity-theoretically incorrect claim about multiplication BDDs; all results require BDD/PFV engine checking. Examples concern well-studied Boolean functions and do not establish general proof synthesis, scalability, or correctness of generated text. No runtime, success-rate or human-study measurements are reported.

- LLM plan verification with NuSMV (2025)
    - title: Bridging LLM Planning Agents and Formal Methods: A Case Study in Plan Verification
    - what: Introduces an LLM pipeline that translates natural-language plans into NuSMV Kripke structures and LTL properties, then uses model checking to classify plan validity on a simplified PlanBench task. The method maps facts to Boolean variables, natural-language initial conditions to init, action preconditions/effects to guarded transitions, and a stage variable to enforce action sequence; goals become LTL reachability properties such as F(goal). NuSMV bounded model checking labels generated models valid (SAT), invalid (UNSAT) or unknown (parse/model failure). A one-shot GPT-4o/GPT-5 comparison reports formal-verification metrics against direct LLM judgments and analyzes unknown handling, syntax versus semantic fidelity and risk-sensitive precision/recall.
    - number: GPT-5 one-shot: 95.89% accuracy, 99.44% precision, 93.34% recall, 96.30% F1, 6.87% unknown, 47.08 time units
    - number: GPT-4o one-shot: 52.06% accuracy, 59.19% precision, 45.54% recall, 51.48% F1, 34.61% unknown
    - number: direct GPT-5 baseline: 99.59% accuracy/F1 with no unknowns, illustrating comparison mismatch
    - number: direct GPT-4o baseline: 80.37% accuracy, 79.95% F1
    - limits: The benchmark is simplified and reduced to binary validity; it does not explain invalidity reasons or test broad software/circuit settings. Unknown outputs are excluded from headline metrics, so treating them differently changes apparent reliability. Syntactically valid models can still misrepresent plan intent and pass verification; one-shot prompting, GPT-5 low reasoning effort and small qualitative semantic analysis limit generalization.

- $\mathbf{{\textsc {PyCaliper}}}$: Python-Embedded Infrastructure for RTL Verification and Specification Synthesis. (2025)
    - what: # Whole reading: PyCaliper (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  This CAV 2025 paper supplies hardware specification/synthesis infrastructure and two case studies. Its directly relevant LLM result is GPT-4o ordering existing invariant candidates under formal-oracle checks. The record below separates source reports from reader interpretations and preserves scope limits.  Source report: PyCaliper is a Python-embedded domain-specific language (DSL) and infrastructure for register-transfer-level (RTL) hardware specification, verification-backend integration and invariant synthesis. The paper contributes an extensible common specification/oracle interface and two case studies. Its algorithms build on existing incremental invariant synthesis and counterexample-guided inductive synthesis (CEGIS). Reader assessment: no new soundness/completeness theorem, mechanized correctness proof of the framework, or general distributed-system verification result is presented. The framework delegates checks to existing proof engines. [Q1, Q2, Q13]  Source excerpt — §1 Related Work and Figure 1; physical PDF p.3, printed p.410, [paper.raw.txt:96](paper.raw.txt#L96):  ```text PyCaliper on the other hand, is not a veriﬁcation tool in itself: our main contribution is Python-embedded infrastructure that enables programmatic RTL speciﬁcation and interfaces with (SVA and BTOR2)-based veriﬁcation engines. We use these veriﬁers as oracles to implement invariant synthesis techniques such as syntax-guided synthesis via CEGIS [3] and incremental invariant synthesis [11,36] (the latter itself uses an approach based on PDR). ```  Source report: SpecModule/Struct/Group/Logic/LogicArray mirror RTL modules and signals. input, state and output mean global assumptions, state invariants and output assertions. A miter is two copies of the design; eq and conditional eq relate their signals, while inv is a single-copy assertion. The displayed inductive obligation is input(S) ∧ state(S) ∧ T(S,S′) ⇒ state(S′) ∧ output(S′). Reader qualification: this is preservation under the assumed invariant; the formula does not itself show that every reachable initial state satisfies state. The SVA harness includes clock/reset assumptions, but the paper does not provide a separate base-case proof or a complete initialization-semantics argument. Correct results depend on correct assumptions, RTL mapping, translation, solver and oracle handling. [Q3, Q4, Q7, Q8]  Source excerpt — §2.1 Specifications and Figure 2; physical PDF p.4, printed p.411, [paper.raw.txt:141](paper.raw.txt#L141):  ```text Inductive Specification. Inductive speciﬁcations in PyCaliper consist of three components: input, state, and output blocks. These correspond to global assumptions, the state invariants, and output assertions, respectively. In general, the input, state, and output functions can be thought of as predicates over the state of the RTL design. ```  Source examples: Figure 2 gives a parameterized FIFO specification for no stale-data leakage: equal control signals and pushed input data, equal pointers and selected live storage entries, and equal output data when nonempty. These are printed schematic examples with omitted code, not separately measured case studies. Figure 3 uses @unroll(2): assume two chosen input constants at step 0 and assert the adder output at step 1 while reset is deasserted. Reader qualification: the printed Python randomly chooses constants before the bounded check, so that illustration alone does not cover every input pair. It computes sum(vals) % MAX with 32-bit constants, but MAX is undefined in the displayed excerpt; the exact wrap modulus is unspecified. [Q3, Q5]  Source excerpt — §2.1 bounded specifications, Figure 3; §2.2; physical PDF p.6, printed p.413, [paper.raw.txt:197](paper.raw.txt#L197):  ```text Example 1. Figure 3 provides an exam- ple for an Adder module in which the simstep function speciﬁes that the output with two (randomly chosen) inputs a and b equals a + b modulo wrap-around. ```  Source report: elaboration introspects Python objects and resolves hierarchical paths, e.g. a UART tx_fifo.wr_ptr. Parameterized FIFO depth/width can be packaged as a Python library. Section 2.3 presents input-side and output-side implication obligations under a relation R and illustrates FIFO refinement to a Counter. Only synchronous lock-step module transitions are currently supported; stuttering is discussed as a more general form. Reader qualification: the displayed implications alone are not a full transition-simulation proof; initialization and preservation of R are not separately established in this paper. The printed FIFO/Counter relation is preserved in rendered transcriptions without correcting its direct pointer/incdec equalities. [Q5, Q6, Q7]  Source excerpt — §2.3 lock-step restriction; §3.1; Figure 4; physical PDF p.8, printed p.415, [paper.raw.txt:264](paper.raw.txt#L264):  ```text PyCaliper currently supports reﬁnement proofs where both M1 and M2 mod- ules transition synchronously in lock-step. In general, reﬁnement relations may allow one module to stutter, i.e., not transition while the other module tran- sitions. Such stuttering reﬁnement [16,32] can be used to relate two modules that have similar functional behaviors but diﬀerent timing behaviors. For exam- ple, Burch-Dill reﬁnement [33] allows veriﬁcation of a pipelined processor design against its non-pipelined counterpart. ```  Source report: the SVA backend emits a clock/reset harness and a k-step counter, compiles assumptions and assertions, and invokes the licensed Jasper FPV App through TCP/Tcl. Its oracle enables/disables assumptions, covers/checks properties and retrieves traces. The BTOR2 backend uses Yosys lowering with memory flattening, hierarchy removal and technology mapping, retains signal-name correspondence, symbolically unrolls the design and checks bit-vector formulas with Boolector. Other engines/solvers are proposed through interface/operator extensions; the paper does not report an implemented SymbiYosys backend. Reader assessment: the comparison measures two concrete integrations; it does not verify translation correctness or establish equivalence of all engine results independently. [Q7, Q8]  Source excerpt — §3.1 oracle; §3.2 BTOR2; §4.1; Table 3; physical PDF p.9, printed p.416, [paper.raw.txt:306](paper.raw.txt#L306):  ```text PyCaliper currently supports the (licensed) Jasper Formal Property Ver- iﬁcation (Jasper FPV) App [13] as the proof engine. However, we can enable support for other engines (e.g., open-source tools such as SymbiYosys [15]) by implementing the oracle interface. PyCaliper interacts with Jasper FPV via a TCP interface which is used to send Tcl commands (e.g., prove P_asrt for a check queries) and receive responses. ```  Source report: ceqhole searches for a conjunction of conditional equalities selected from a supplied signal list. Its fuel-limited incremental search maintains speculative assumptions and relatively inductive assertions, adds candidates via a strategy, saturates provable assertions, backtracks, and succeeds only when a self-inductive set proves output. Running out of candidates or fuel can fail. ctrlhole searches a bounded-size control-to-dependent-signal lookup table using traces, then checks inductiveness; counterexamples refine the trace set, and unsatisfiable trace constraints increase table size up to a bound. Reader qualification: these are template-restricted searches; unsuccessful bounded search does not show no invariant exists. The two reported experiments do not benchmark ctrlhole/CEGIS. [Q4, Q9, Q10]  Source excerpt — §4.2 strategy/failure; §4.3 CEGIS; Figure 6; physical PDF p.11, printed p.418, [paper.raw.txt:402](paper.raw.txt#L402):  ```text If veriﬁcation fails, the trace counterexample is added to the set of traces, and the process is repeated again (up to a limit). Adding the trace guarantees that the (incorrect) lookup table candidate will not be produced in a subsequent iteration. ```  Rendered source: Figure 5 prints the dive guard |assrts| − |assms| > f*, but the surrounding §4.2 text says fuel bounds |assms| − |assrts| and that candidates are added only with fuel remaining. Reader diagnosis: the operand order and inequality do not match that prose interpretation. Both versions are saved, including the full four-rule transcription and image crop. This is a discrepancy in the paper presentation; author code was not inspected, so the implemented guard and its behavior are unknown. No repaired rule is asserted. [Q9, Q10]  Source excerpt — §4.1 synthesis target; §4.2 incremental synthesis; Figure 5; physical PDF p.10, printed p.417, [paper.raw.txt:364](paper.raw.txt#L364):  ```text The algorithm admits a fuel parameter f∗ that imposes an upper bound on |assms| − |assrts| during the search. Intuitively, a higher f∗ allows for more speculative guessing (with higher coverage), while a lower f∗ forces the algorithm to be more conservative. ```  Source report: domain tags are added to an open-source TAGE indirect-branch predictor so an entry is used only in its creating privilege domain. The stated checked property assumes privileged true targets targ_i < BDRY and an invariant that every PRIV-tagged table target is below BDRY; it asserts prev_mode = PRIV ⇒ targ_o < BDRY. Both backends reportedly verify this using 2-induction. Reader qualification: the formal claim is a privileged-target range property under those assumptions. It does not itself assert full target correctness, independence from all attacker-controlled state, whole-processor security, or complete prevention of every branch-target-injection behavior. No attack experiment, independent proof-certificate check or reproduction was performed here. [Q11, Q12]  Source excerpt — §5.1 IsoTAGE security property and two backends; physical PDF p.12, printed p.419, [paper.raw.txt:428](paper.raw.txt#L428):  ```text We formalize this as a property that says that provided the true branch target in privileged mode (PRIV) is in the valid kernel-code segment (less than a boundary BDRY), the generated prediction is also in the valid kernel-code segment. Formally, we iden- tify the inductive speciﬁcation: ```  Source measurements: Table 4 varies BHT_WIDTH from 4 through 9. SVA total/solve/parse seconds are 4.0/2.6/1.4 at width 4 and 12.7/11.4/1.3 at width 9. BTOR2 is 3.6/2.6/1 at width 4 and 753.9/11.9/742 at width 9; all six rows are saved in evaluation.json. BTOR2 program statements grow 5,465→116,186 and elements 598→14,486. Solving times are comparable in these cases; total BTOR2 time becomes dominated by parsing, and is slightly lower than SVA only at width 4. The authors call the Python-based parser a likely cause; no parser ablation establishes causation. Hardware, software versions, repetitions, run uncertainty and precise timing boundaries are not supplied. These figures support a bottleneck diagnosis for this benchmark family, not a broad solver ranking. [Q11, Q12]  Source excerpt — §5.1 IsoTAGE security property and two backends; physical PDF p.12, printed p.419, [paper.raw.txt:438](paper.raw.txt#L438):  ```text Interestingly, we observe that the solving times (ST) of the BTOR2 backend (using the Boolector solver [12]) is comparable to SVA backend using the Jasper FPV App. However, the parse time (PT) is where BTOR2 backend falls behind. This is likely due to the fact that we currently use a Python-based btor2 parsing library - we plan to report this result and improve the parsing performance. This insightful comparison was possible due to PyCaliper’s compatibility with both veriﬁcation backends. ```  Source report: DAWG dynamically partitions cache ways to prevent cross-domain information leakage, formulated as noninterference. Prior work manually supplied invariants. This experiment synthesizes k out of 8 cache-way invariants for k=2,3,4 as invariant width increases. It compares built-in random ordering against GPT-4o ordering of existing candidates, given current invariants and the partitioning property. The authors report about 80 Python lines and 30 minutes of human effort to add that strategy. Reader qualification: this LLM supplies a search heuristic; it is not reported to invent the desired property, write arbitrary invariants, prove a theorem or produce a verified Rust/TLA+ specification. Backend oracle checks determine acceptance. [Q12]  Source excerpt — §5.1 Table 4; §5.2 DAWG and GPT-4o strategy; Table 5; physical PDF p.13, printed p.420, [paper.raw.txt:467](paper.raw.txt#L467):  ```text We attempt two strategies for selecting the invariant candidate order: random (in-built) and a custom (rudimentary) neural strategy. In the latter, we prompt an LLM (gpt-4o) to provide an order of candidates, providing the current set of invariants and the cache partitioning property as context. Implementing this strategy with PyCaliper required ∼80 lines of Python code and around 30 min of human eﬀort. ```  Source measurements: Table 5 reports means ±σ over 20 runs using the SVA backend and Jasper FPV. For k=2, random/neural verification calls are 16.7±2.3 / 14.3±2.6 and nodes 5.5±0.6 / 4.9±0.9. For k=3, calls are 101.6±47.1 / 54.0±21.8 and nodes 13.3±5.5 / 8.5±3.9. For k=4, calls are 785.7±91.2 / 163±74.2 and nodes 54.3±4.8 / 11.6±8.6. Reader assessment: this shows lower mean search/oracle effort in three configurations on this design. The paper does not report end-to-end wall time, LLM/API cost, exact prompt/model snapshot/temperature, random seeds, fuel setting, success/failure counts or held-out designs. It supplies no statistical significance test or measured generalization claim. These counts cannot establish overall speedup or cost savings after LLM overhead. [Q12]  Source excerpt — §5.1 Table 4; §5.2 DAWG and GPT-4o strategy; Table 5; physical PDF p.13, printed p.420, [paper.raw.txt:484](paper.raw.txt#L484):  ```text We use the SVA-backend with the Jasper FPV App as the veriﬁcation engine. We see that even our very rudimentary neural strategy outperforms the random strat- egy, likely by being able to detect a pattern in the invariants using the provided context. The takeaways from this experiment are twofold. Firstly, PyCaliper allows leveraging industry-scale backends as veriﬁcation oracles in an invariant synthesis task. Secondly, PyCaliper uses the Python ecosystem to enable easy prototyping of new ideas. ```  Reader synthesis: directly useful for LLM-assisted formal-verification evaluation: separate a heuristic candidate proposal/order from a trusted verification oracle, constrain the search language, expose assumptions and search budgets, and distinguish oracle-call count from end-to-end runtime/cost. Its specification reuse, two-trace security properties and assume-guarantee/refinement interfaces are adjacent to distributed-systems proof engineering. No distributed-system, Verus, verified Rust or TLA+ case is evaluated; transfer to those settings is an inference requiring separate implementation and evidence. In particular, its lock-step-only refinement would not by itself cover the stuttering commonly needed when relating systems with differing step granularity. The strongest topic-specific evidence is the measured GPT-4o candidate-ordering experiment, not autonomous specification/proof generation. [Q6, Q7, Q9, Q10, Q12]  Source excerpt — §5.1 Table 4; §5.2 DAWG and GPT-4o strategy; Table 5; physical PDF p.13, printed p.420, [paper.raw.txt:490](paper.raw.txt#L490):  ```text Secondly, PyCaliper uses the Python ecosystem to enable easy prototyping of new ideas. ```  Reading audit: all 18 physical PDF pages (printed 408–425) and all 784 LF of the complete layout extraction were read, including §§1–6, acknowledgments, disclosure, references 1–56 and the license through EOF. All six figures and five tables were inspected in their rendered pages; the remaining pages were viewed in contact sheets after their text was read. No appendix occurs in this PDF; external publisher attachments were not queried. Literal physical-page quotes remain separate from rendered-math/table transcription. PDF metadata duplicates/omits author names, so identity follows the rendered title page. The title page displays artifact-available and reusable badges; no artifact execution or independent evaluation was done here. No author implementation file, scientific dataset/result artifact or checkpoint was inspected/loaded; no formal tool was run. Measurements and proof successes are source-reported. Independent source review and final aggregate acceptance remain for the parent. [Q0, Q1, Q2, Q3, Q4, Q5, Q6, Q7, Q8, Q9, Q10, Q11, Q12, Q13, Q14, Q15, Q16, Q17]  Source excerpt — Open-access license; physical PDF p.18, printed p.425, [paper.raw.txt:683](paper.raw.txt#L683):  ```text Open Access This chapter is licensed under the terms of the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International License ```

- Model Checking and Synthesis for Optimal Use of Knowledge in Consensus Protocols (2025)
    - what: # Model Checking and Synthesis for Optimal Use of Knowledge in Consensus Protocols (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Alpturer, Huang and van der Meyden use MCK to check whether small synchronous consensus protocols decide as soon as their available information permits, and synthesize concrete decision predicates from knowledge-based programs. The contribution is empirical and methodological. They “do not attempt to give a full characterization or a proof” of general optimality (§1, p.3, Q3). The 22-page arXiv 2505.02353v1 is the full version of the PODC 2025 paper, ACM pp.131–140, DOI 10.1145/3732772.3733552 (Q37; saved arXiv metadata). No new parameterized proof or new synthesis engine is presented.  ## Assumptions and specification (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The system has synchronous message rounds and two layers: an information exchange defines observed state, messages and updates; a decision protocol chooses noop or a decision. Failures are crashes or sending omissions. A crashing agent can send an arbitrary subset of its messages in the crash round and then sends nothing; an omission-faulty agent can omit sends but receives messages sent to it (§3, pp.5–6, Q6–Q7). “Byzantine Agreement” names the coordination problem here; the experiments do not evaluate arbitrary malicious message values, asynchronous scheduling or a cryptographic implementation.  The base simultaneous agreement specification requires at most one decision per agent, agreement at the same time among nonfaulty agents, and a decision value initially held by some agent, possibly faulty. The paper explicitly says “termination is not a requirement of this specification,” while reporting additional termination checks in its scripts (§4, p.6, Q8). Eventual agreement permits different decision times (§8, Q20). These distinctions matter when calling a synthesized result correct.  Optimality is relative to the same information exchange and failure model, comparing runs with identical initial preferences and failure patterns (§4, p.7, Q9). The paper distinguishes an optimum protocol, which decides no later than every competitor, from an optimal protocol, which no competitor systematically improves. This is not optimality across arbitrary messaging designs or execution costs. The semantic order as printed compares every agent; this record does not silently narrow its quantifier to nonfaulty agents.  MCK uses finite variables and ordered binary decision diagrams. The chosen clock semantics gives an agent its current observable variables plus the time, rather than perfect recall. Synthesis replaces boolean template variables with local predicates equivalent to knowledge tests (§6, pp.8–9, Q11–Q12). Uniqueness under clock semantics and the optimality characterizations are attributed to prior theory, not proved anew. EBA’s simpler knowledge-based program P0 also requires technical side conditions on information exchange: learning 0 through decision-message chains and suitable possibilities involving nonfaulty agents (§8, pp.11–12, Q20).  ## Concrete cases and results (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Four SBA information exchanges are modeled: FloodSet, FloodSet plus current received-message count, the Count variant plus previous count (“Diff”), and the concrete Dwork–Moses protocol. Two EBA exchanges, Emin and Ebasic, respectively send decision values and additionally send initial 1 messages with a count. The models have parameters n agents, t maximum failures and the decision-value set. The main FloodSet/Count timing table fixes two values (§§7–11, Q13–Q28).  FloodSet retains values seen and broadcasts their union. The naive earliest-knowledge hypothesis t+1 fails at n=3,t=2. Revised Eq 2 uses time=n−1 when t≥n−1, otherwise t+1; model checking and synthesis agree in the instances checked (§7.1, pp.9–10, Q14–Q15). The authors report an all-n proof in companion work [7]; this read does not include that proof (Q17). Adding the current message count exposes the early condition count≤1, meaning only the agent’s own message was received (§7.2, Q16). Diff gave no stronger SBA stopping condition than Count in checked cases, although previous-count memory can help a different EBA specification (§7.3, Q18).  The printed Count formula needs reconciliation at a boundary. Eq 3 uses “time = t” in its t≥n−1 branch (§7.2, p.10, Q16), whereas FloodSet Eq 2 uses time=n−1 (Q14). At n=3,t=3,time=2,count=3, Eq 2 is true and Eq 3 false. Count adds an observation while retaining FloodSet’s values and clock; its stated earliest condition should not require later knowledge on that account. No-crash histories allow count 3 even when t=3 is the failure upper bound. This is a reader-derived discrepancy in the printed conditions, not a replayed MCK counterexample or a refutation of the companion theorem. The benchmark inputs needed to resolve it were not obtained. The source’s formulas are retained unchanged in Q14/Q16 and static-witnesses.json.  There is a second printed EBA ambiguity. Ebasic’s jd update stores 0 on receiving 0 and otherwise ⊥, but its decision 1 rule includes “jd𝑖 = 1” (§9.2, pp.12–13, Q22). Under that update description the jd=1 branch cannot be reached. I have not silently repaired the update or inferred which rule the actual model used.  Appendix A gives a complete FloodSet synthesis example for n=3,t=1,two values. It uses finite time 0..3, communicates while time<2 and has four decision templates for knowledge times 1 and 2 (pp.18–21, Q32–Q35). The reported output makes both time 1 predicates false; at time 2 the predicate for v is values_received[v], with 0 taking priority in the decision code. The authors report that the accompanying formulas all hold (p.22, Q36). State at time m means information after round m; the decision follows in the next modeled round (§7, Q13). This accounts for the appendix’s AX3 completion check.  The appendix checks ordinary and uniform agreement, validity and termination at AX3, and knowledge predicates. Its printed agreement formulas compare values when both agents have decided; they do not explicitly require simultaneous decided flags (Q34). Thus the displayed formulas alone are not a direct check of every SBA clause. The knowledge-based theory may establish simultaneity; no counterexample to the synthesized protocol is claimed. Likewise the finite AX3 termination check is not a general unbounded liveness proof. The appendix uses currently ALIVE agents in knowledge tests, which should not be casually substituted for permanently nonfaulty agents without the cited crash-theory correspondence.  ## Evaluation and limits (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The experiments used one core of a 3.7 GHz six-core Intel Core i5 with 256 KB L2, 9 MB L3 and 32 GB memory. The final run’s timeout was 10 minutes (§10, p.13, Q23). Early exploratory trials sometimes ran 5 hours or 2 days; those are not the table timeout. No MCK build/version, BDD variable ordering, repetition count, variance, raw trial set or memory measurements are supplied. The measured costs are model-checking and synthesis runtime, not consensus latency or deployment throughput.  All three timing tables are transcribed with printed values and converted seconds in numeric-evidence.json: 16 parameter rows in Table 1, 15 in Table 2 and 6 in Table 3, totaling 118 timing cells. TO remains a timeout rather than a measured 600-second completion. Representative results show the steep scaling:  - FloodSet at n=5,t=2: checking 34.635 seconds, synthesis 162.015 seconds; synthesis times out at n=5,t≥3. Checking still finishes at n=6,t=1 in 72.863 seconds, while n=6,t=2 times out (Table 1, Q24) - Count at n=4,t=2: checking 23.328 seconds, synthesis 120.892 seconds; synthesis times out for t=3/4 and both tasks time out at n=5 in the listed rows (Table 1) - Diff at n=3 takes roughly 258–304 seconds depending on failure bound/rounds; Dwork–Moses times out for every listed n=3 case. Both time out at n=4,t=1,one round (Table 2, Q26) - Emin EBA synthesis succeeds through the listed n=3 cases, with crash costs 31.219–178.142 seconds and omission costs 11.371–66.735 seconds. Every n=4,t=1 cell times out. Ebasic succeeds only at n=2 in Table 3 (Q28)  Table 3’s prose says Emin scales to 4 agents before blowup, then names Emin again for the worse count-containing model. The table has no successful 4-agent row, and §9.2 attributes the extra count to Ebasic. This record preserves the cells and flags the prose ambiguity instead of recording 4-agent success or swapping labels without evidence. Receiving/general omissions are reported to have similar successful cases, but no separate timing rows are provided (§11, Q28).  For comparison, the authors report 125 seconds for purely temporal SAT-based SBA checking of Dwork–Moses at n=5,t=4 (§13, p.16, Q30). This checks a different obligation with a different algorithm from the BDD epistemic runs, so it is not a like-for-like solver speedup. They suggest quadratic messages and BDD size explain the difficulty; the paper does not measure a causal ablation. Their conclusion is appropriately limited: “a manual proof of correctness and optimality will” still be needed for all numbers of agents and failures (Q30).  ## Contribution to the requested topic and evidence scope (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  This adds concrete epistemic verification and protocol-synthesis benchmarks for distributed consensus, including examples where stronger local observations permit earlier decisions but make automated analysis harder. It supports a workflow of synthesizing small instances, checking hypotheses on larger finite instances, then proving the general claim manually. That workflow is the authors’ proposal; this paper does not demonstrate LLM agents. There is no Verus or verified Rust experiment. Appendix A calls its nondeterministic transition construct “refinement calculus/TLA action style” (Q33); it does not report TLA+/TLC verification.  For LLM-generated specifications and proofs, I infer evaluation lessons from the record: state the failure and information-exchange scope, declare observation/recall semantics, distinguish agreement from simultaneous agreement, inspect every printed guard and boundary case, separate finite checking from general proofs, and retain timeouts and source/script mismatches. These are reader-derived lessons, not measured LLM results.  Read every one of 1099 layout-text lines, all 22 pages, all 39 references and the complete appendix input/output. Inspected 11 contact sheets and fullsize pages 10,13–15,19–22; the paper has three tables and no figures. The bundle retains 44 literal excerpts with line/character/page anchors, 19 diagnostics, all 37 timing rows and raw hashes. Local/official PDFs are byte-identical. The paper identifies no benchmark archive/repository link. The primary MCK site links a related consensus demonstration. I read its page/loader and all 551 logical lines (550 newline characters) of four static source scripts: FloodSet, optimized FloodSet, Count and Dwork–Moses. They are pinned by content hashes and fetch times; no direct association with the paper’s benchmark version was established. The demo FloodSet/Count scripts read and update w in place, allowing later senders to forward values received earlier in the same round; Appendix A instead snapshots old_w before delivery. Demo FloodSet crash delivery is also selected per value. These differences prevent treating the demos as interchangeable benchmark inputs or evidence against the paper’s reported results (Q38–Q44, D19). No demo Verify action or stored .result output was invoked or loaded. Two MCK subpage links returned 404 and were not retried within five minutes. No author code, compiler, model checker, prover or solver was executed; no serialized scientific/model/results data were loaded. Reported checks and synthesis outputs are statically inspected author results, not independent reproductions.
    - limits: Synchronous rounds, crash or sending omissions, and finite MCK clock semantics using current observations and time. Optimality is relative to a fixed information exchange and failure model. Base SBA omits termination. EBA P0 requires technical information-exchange side conditions. The paper checks finite instances and supplies no all-n proof. Count Eq. 3, the Ebasic jd update and Table 3 prose have unresolved printed discrepancies; the appendix lacks an explicit simultaneity formula. Related static demo models differ from the appendix; the exact benchmark revision and independent reproduction are unavailable.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `model-checking-and-synthesis-2025`
- On Temporal Verification of Stateful P4 Programs (2025)
    - what: Defines P4LTL temporal properties over completed packet processings while retaining register state between packets. Models arbitrary input packets and changing table actions, optionally constrained by symbolic control-plane assumptions. Constructs a Büchi transaction product in which the specification automaton advances only at packet return, reducing violations to fair feasible traces. A proof sketch establishes the abstract correspondence; p4tv implements P4-to-Boogie translation, ghost instrumentation and an Ultimate Automizer extension. Evaluation checks19 temporal tasks on nine programs plus assertion checks/scaling, identifying two naturally occurring property failures and three injected defects. Records exact packet-boundary/old/free-variable semantics, register initialization and control-plane scope, product transitions/acceptance and proof-sketch qualifications, refinement algorithm and implementation trust, all Table2/3 cells, actual versus injected bug distinction, p4v approximation and assertion instrumentation, rapid scaling growth, and source specification/prose inconsistencies including vacuous liveness formula. Separates local P4xos checks from whole Paxos consensus verification.
    - limits: §3: Environment is a serial infinite loop of complete packet processings, persistent registers outside loop, nondeterministic packet fields each iteration and metadata initialized per P4-16. Table applications are nondeterministic action choices. This abstracts a single programmed switch; no proof of interleaved pipeline concurrency, simultaneous register read/modify/write races, multiple switches, queue dynamics or actual hardware refinement is supplied. Packet time is not wall-clock time. §§2/3/7/8: Register initialization is part of each verification configuration. P4NIS failing task9 leaves count unconstrained; successful invariant/assertion examples assume valid/zero initialization or add recovery logic. Persistent arbitrary initial values differ from independently re-randomizing registers every packet. Results under initialization assumptions do not show a real deployment meets those assumptions. §§3.2/4: CPI constrains table keys/actions/parameters, allows symbolic sets of entries and changes between packets. Empty CPI permits every modeled rule. Property checked is assumption implies desired property; it can hold if assumption is impossible. Authors do not report systematic assumption satisfiability/vacuity checks or prove a real controller always enforces CPI. Table2 needs CPI for tasks6,7,8,16 only. §4.1: Predicates are evaluated at packet boundaries: terms normally after processing; old(t) at ingress start after parsing; valid(h) records validity after parsing, hit/apply record occurrence during processing. old(key) and old(para) expressly arbitrary. fwd(t) is egress_spec==t syntactic sugar, so packet-output/architecture correspondence matters. Free variables describe nondeterministic values intended to cover all names; scope/stability must be preserved when translating specifications. §§4/6/7.2: Boundary-only temporal properties can miss transient within-packet violations. Assertion checks use a shadow variable initialized true per packet and capture the assertion at its program point, then check its boundary value. This is an explicit instrumentation workaround; the native P4LTL language still cannot directly observe arbitrary internal events. Correct instrumentation and all relevant visits are part of the trust boundary. §5.1: Product keeps program/specification location pairs, updates specification only on TR(s), uses assume true on other statements, and accepting locations require spec acceptance plus TE/TR boundary. Fair means Büchi acceptance (infinitely recurring accepting states), not automatic network-delivery, scheduler or adversary fairness. Liveness assumptions about infinitely many particular packets are written explicitly in the property. Infinite repeated packet processing is the base execution model. §5.1/Appendix A: Theorem5.1/A.1 is an abstract correspondence with an informal proof sketch, not a mechanized proof of P4 semantics, Boogie translation, instrumenter or executable checker. Projection deletes only true steps imposed by non-return transitions; a return transition labelled true must remain. Appendix switches the named if/only-if directions, and reverse construction retains TE(s)∨TR(s) while operational product advances spec on TR(s) only. A rigorous proof should distinguish statement/boundary indexing and establish infinitely many returns; no concrete tool-unsoundness counterexample claimed. §5.2/6: Algorithm1 reuses counterexample-guided refinement from cited fairness-modulo-theory work: select accepting trace, test feasibility, return genuine trace or remove a generalized set of infeasible traces, finish when none remain. No universal termination/runtime bound or translation correctness theorem provided. Trust includes P4/Boogie modeling, ghost instrumentation, LTL conversion, automata/refinement/feasibility engine and underlying reasoning tools. Code/proofs not run in this reading. §6: Prototype fully supports V1Model but limited TNA/PSA. Authors claim model transferable with more detailed P4 processing; future architecture support is not demonstrated full support. State explosion remains. About10000C++/JavaLOC and selected small benchmark programs do not establish production-scale coverage. §8 pp.228–229: Printed P4NIS liveness consequence is conjunction over ports i of GF(phi_sp -> fwd(i)), under GF(phi_sp). This is weaker than GF(phi_sp AND fwd(i)): infinitely many non-specific packets satisfy every implication regardless of forwarding. Alternating specific/non-specific packets with no fwd(i) satisfies the displayed formula while violating the natural-language goal. This is a semantic observation about the printed specification, not a run of artifact specifications. §4’s earlier liveness example has a different stronger shape. §8: NDN weak-until formula is activated only after a data packet was forwarded; it then forbids repeated data forwarding until a matching forwarded interest. It does not by itself forbid the first unsolicited data forward before that trigger, nor require eventual forwarding because W permits no future interest. Task6’s separate property covers interest-before-data. Universal free hash variable is not a proof of hash collision freedom or data-name authentication. §5.1 Figure5/§7.2 Table3: Figure5 prose says status becomes0 when hdr.cnt<0, while listing/automaton tests>0; record follows code when describing example. Table3 says acceptor stored round strictly less than current round, while adjoining prose uses≤ under zero initialization. Strict< would fail on equal zeros; cannot silently interchange these properties. Printed description/code discrepancies do not establish implementation bugs. §7: Dataset has nine manually selected programs and manually authored properties; artificial bugs inserted in CPI/task8 and code/tasks15,18. Natural failures tasks9,13 are author-reported findings without external maintainer confirmation/production exploit in paper. 14 verified tasks and five counterexamples are outcomes for specified models, not whole programs certified correct. §7.2: Temporal comparison with four prior tools is a support limitation discussion; measured assertion comparison runs bf4 and an approximate p4v implementation from bf4 work because original p4v unavailable. Vera limited user assertions and Aquila unavailable. Spurious counterexamples are relative to chosen persistent-register configuration, not evidence prior tools unsound for their broader nondeterministic model. p4tv is much slower but more precise for these stateful examples. §7.3: Program and specification scaling varied separately on P4NIS ports and acceptor index clauses. Curves grow rapidly; Figure8 about8600s near64ports and Figure9 about24000s near32clauses, approximate visual readings only. Easy false properties return inabout90s for CNT8/16, so falsification speed does not imply comparable proof scaling. No cross-product scalability, latency budget or large production network study. §7: Hardware i5-12400 2.50GHz/32GB; no repeat counts, confidence intervals, error bars or user specification effort measurements given. Main temporal tasks10–1260s; assertion acceptor-safe1701s, so21min maximum applies only Table2. P4xos acceptor/learner predicates are local state/order/vote checks, not an end-to-end Paxos agreement/liveness proof under crash/message-loss/reconfiguration. No Verus/Rust/TLA+ implementation theorem or LLM-agent evaluation. §7.1/Table2 task8: Printed task8 checks property6, data is forwarded only after a corresponding interest. Reported trace receives interest then drops data. A dropped data packet alone cannot violate this only-if safety condition. Preserve author-reported counterexample outcome, but it does not demonstrate violation of the printed property without more trace/specification detail. This inconsistency does not establish tool unsoundness.

- PoWER (2025)
    - title: PoWER Never Corrupts: Tool-Agnostic Verification of Crash Consistency and Corruption Detection
    - what: PoWER encodes crash consistency as a precondition on each storage write: every durable state introduced by a partial write must be permitted by a ghost permission. Prophecy-based storage views and recovery-equivalent writes simplify proofs. A bounded-bit corruption model and an eight-byte corruption-detecting Boolean support atomic data/checksum updates. CapybaraKV in Verus and CapybaraNS in Dafny demonstrate the method, with coarse concurrent KV variants and performance comparisons. Records exact permission quantification, prophecy flush semantics, state versus transition and blanket versus single-use permissions, all four write strategies and concurrency restrictions. Includes correspondence-proof trust boundaries, CRC corruption bounds, Tick-Tock counterexample and CDB protocol, full type/LOC/startup/resource tables, all workload setups and adverse performance cases. Separates conditional detection from arbitrary-media-error recovery and reported proofs from independent proof replay.
    - number: CapybaraKV 5,531 executable / 14,255 spec+proof / 5,244 trusted lines; CapybaraNS 278 / 673 / 414
    - number: verification 54 s single-thread, 23 s eight-thread for KV, 12 s for NS
    - number: Table 2 KV total: 5244 trusted, 14255 spec/proof, 5531 implementation lines; notary 414/673/278
    - limits: Core technique requires Hoare pre/postconditions, ghost variables and quantification over crash states. Each asynchronous write requires all chunk-wise old/new durable outcomes to satisfy its permission; executable annotations erase. Soundness depends on every actual storage mutation using the controlled API, correctness of the storage model and a meaningful allowed-recovery predicate. A permission that allows all states proves no useful crash property. Natural storage semantics distinguish readable last-written bytes from outstanding writes. Atomic persistence chunks can survive independently until flush. The prophecy model instead stores read_state and one prophesized durable_state; write updates all readable bytes and chooses a subset of chunk subwrites for durable state. Flush constrains these two states to agree without changing either in the ghost model. This is a logical modeling device, not a runtime ability to predict crash outcomes. The authors report a Verus proof implementing the prophecy model over a natural model using prophecy variables. The PoWER-to-Crash-Hoare-logic proof in Rocq/Perennial is metalogical and trusts a translation of PoWER semantics, since PoWER was not implemented in Rocq. The Verus correspondence to crash invariants is entirely machine-checked within Verus. Formal theorem bodies are in artifacts; this reading examined paper explanations only. Crash states can be specified through rec(bytes), commonly allowing pre-operation or post-operation abstract state. Transition permissions instead authorize pairs of durable states. Blanket permissions cover arbitrary recovery-equivalent transitions; single-use permissions authorize a mutating transition once and are consumed or invalidated. A blanket permission can manufacture single-use permissions only for its allowed transitions. Initial recoverability and all permission constructions remain proof obligations. Tentative writes change only addresses irrelevant to rec and need no value-specific proof. Committing writes are a single aligned atomic chunk, typically after flushing tentative data, so only old/new durable states matter. Recovery writes rely on idempotent recovery that overwrites all possibly torn bytes on retry. In-place writes can use weaker specifications with many allowed outcomes, but the authors have no verified examples or helper-library support for that category. Atomic PoWER adds durable ghost resources owned by atomic invariants, proof callbacks and completion objects for consumed transition permissions. Completions witness an issued/applied operation even if another thread later changes its key. This extension needs concurrency facilities beyond the minimal core features; a tool-agnostic core does not imply arbitrary concurrent proofs in plain sequential Dafny. Concurrency allows multiple readers, or disjoint statically allocated storage regions with exclusive access during writes. It excludes read/write or write/write overlap within one region because callers must know its current logical state. Sharded wrappers establish linearizable individual create/read/update/delete/list operations, not cross-shard transactions. The reusable inner implementation still supplies a transactional API to wrappers. Corruption is modeled by a fixed device bitmask with at most c potentially faulty physical bits. A zero mask bit reads correctly; a marked bit can return either value independently on each read. The trusted CRC axiom distinguishes byte strings at Hamming distance 1 through c. Current ECMA CRC-64 implementation uses c=1 for arbitrary lengths; the cited c=3 for data shorter than about1 GiB is not exploited. Detection is conditional on that device-wide bound, not a guarantee against arbitrary bursts, malicious rewrites, rollback or unbounded media failure. Reader qualification: integrity proofs must cover corrupted data and checksum bytes, repeated/noncontiguous address mappings, and the chosen CDB codeword distance under the same physical-bit bound. The paper reports verified checking and a trusted CRC library; it does not derive an unrestricted cryptographic collision-resistance guarantee. Detecting a mismatch permits reporting corruption, not necessarily repairing damage or preserving availability. Tick-Tock’s old-checksum/new-data mismatch after a crash is not a small-bit corruption event relative to the old data. CRC(D0) can collide with CRC(D1-prime) even if D1-prime differs by only bounded media corruption from D1, because D0 versus D1-prime may differ arbitrarily. The paper gives a logical counterexample to the algorithm in its model, not an independently reproduced NOVA-Fortis artifact exploit. CDB stores one of two known eight-byte values CRC(0) and CRC(1), with aligned crash atomicity and sufficient corruption separation assumed/proved for the primitive. Two data/CRC slots accompany it. Write the inactive slot and checksum, flush, atomically switch CDB, flush. The selector identifies the committed slot independently of potentially matching stale checksums. This costs extra storage and flushes; it does not remove hardware atomicity assumptions. CapybaraKV values contain a fixed-size item and a list of fixed-size elements, with fixed-size keys. The abstract state has on-abort and on-commit maps. Ordinary operations affect the latter; abort restores it from the former; commit publishes it to the former. A crash during commit may commit or abort, while other uncommitted operations abort. Reads within a transaction see tentative operations, so visible results need not already be durable. Implementation uses main/item/list tables, per-field CRCs, validity CDBs, copy-on-write updates and a physical redo journal. Journal commit writes a CRC of pending entries then flips a CDB; replay installs operations and clears the journal. Volatile key index and free lists rebuild on startup. Correctness depends on recovery traversal, bounds, allocation and CRC checks, not merely the commit selector. Capacity is statically allocated at initialization with maximum key/item/list counts and sizes. No dynamic resizing, partial-value updates or range queries. All keys reside in a volatile Rust HashMap and must be indexed again at startup. Designed target is small records on tens of GiB of dedicated PM in a cloud-storage service. Prototype integration into an Azure Rust system is reported, not evidence of production deployment of all verified variants. Trusted pmcopy bridges Verus and compiler-generated representations. It requires repr(C) and derive(PmCopy), implements PmSafe/PmSized/Clone/PartialEq, generates specifications and static layout assertions checked by Rust. PmSafe includes numeric types, bool and char; composites require safe fields. Casts still require correct layout, valid discriminants/values and prior corruption checks. Compiler assertions caught a u128/i128 layout change; this is a concrete consistency check, not a proof of the macro or compiler. CapybaraNS stores a 64-bit logical timestamp and last hash. Advance increments and atomically persists them using CDB; Sign signs their binding. A trusted C# wrapper supplies CRCs, cryptography and serialization. This paper does not supply a complete distributed-notary protocol, a cryptographic implementation proof, remote attestation argument or availability theorem. Counter overflow handling and full security assumptions require the external specification. PM model permits independent eight-byte chunk reorderings until flush; backend write uses PMDK non-temporal stores or mov followed by clflushopt/clwb, and flush uses sfence. It deliberately overapproximates strongly ordered clflush and same-cache-line hardware ordering, including potential compiler reorderings. Eight-byte alignment/atomicity, backend calls and hardware behavior remain trusted. PoWER can use other models; current model is not a universal storage-device specification. Table 2 counts unverified pmcopy source but not its generated code. KV trusted count includes mock byte-vector PM plus Windows/Linux backends, concurrency and sharding wrappers. Trusted versus proved LOC therefore should not be interpreted as executable core size alone. Reported proof/code ratios are2.6 KV and2.4 NS; LOC is not a measure of specification adequacy. KV design, implementation and verification took about1.5 years by a team of experts and newcomers; headcount and person-time are unspecified. After that experience, porting the PM spec to Dafny took under an hour, porting helper lemmas about an hour, and implementing/verifying NS about nine hours after its spec and C# wrapper were already written. This is author experience, not a controlled productivity comparison. On Linux6.9.3 with i7-11850H eight physical cores and32 GB RAM, KV verifies in54s with one thread or23s with eight; NS takes12s with one. Appendix expects KV1–2min serial or15–40s parallel on other hardware. Paper gives no independent proof replay in this reading and no controlled comparison against alternative verification approaches. Performance setup is a two-socket32-core machine with128 GB RAM and one128 GB Optane DC PMM, Debian Trixie/Linux6.12.10. All KV runs use the sharded variant with one shard per thread. Redis client/server run together but retain communication overhead; RocksDB uses memory-mapped files and non-temporal log appends; Viper preallocates enough capacity except in utilization measurement. Different supported features and durability architectures affect comparison. Microbenchmarks use25M records,64-byte keys,1 KiB values and8-byte list elements. Append adds16 elements per record in random order; read-list returns the entire list; trim removes it. Other operations run once per record. Figure2 uses logarithmic latency axes and95% confidence intervals. KV matches or beats RocksDB but Viper is faster on several operations, including most writes; Redis is slowest. Windows/Azure20 GiB battery-backed DRAM tests run only KV, with up to2× improvement; other systems lack Windows support. YCSB uses15M keys,24-byte keys,1140-byte values and five executions per system. RunE range queries are omitted and RunA/RunB partial updates become full-value updates. X is uniform75% updates,5% read-modify-write and20% reads; Y/Z use50/50 or5/95 read/update with90% accesses to10% records. LoadE is omitted only from Figure4 as similar to LoadA. These modified workloads must not be called an unmodified full YCSB suite. At one thread KV is broadly comparable to Viper and often slightly slower. At16 threads it substantially leads Viper on update/read-heavy run phases but not every load phase; the broad prose claim of outperforming other systems needs this figure-level qualification. Viper is tested on one non-interleaved NVDIMM despite optimizations for interleaved PM. Authors attribute scaling issues partly to CCEH semaphore costs nearly doubling1→16 threads. This is reported profiling attribution, not isolated causal proof. Figure 4 read-heavy B/C/D scale better than write-heavy phases. Approximate 16-thread throughput is RunC 3.5 Mops/s, RunB/RunD 3.0, RunA 1.25, LoadA 0.4, RunF 1.1, RunX 0.9, RunY 1.2, RunZ 0.8 and LoadX 0.4. Values are visual estimates. Hash sharding distributes hot keys across shards, so this does not demonstrate performance under all accesses contending on one shard. Full-instance startup fills each store to its own out-of-space point with64-byte keys/1 KiB values; capacities and record counts can therefore differ. KV/Viper startup is seconds because volatile indexes rebuild, whereas RocksDB is milliseconds. Full Redis startup fails with memory allocation error. Table3 utilization instead uses YCSB LoadA and optimally provisions KV at18 GiB with no further growth. Comparing those startup/resource numbers requires keeping these distinct experiments separate. Artifact branch osdi25-artifact and Zenodo15306499 include implementations, scripts and proof guidance; CHL formalization lives in the Perennial repository rather than being bundled. Exact performance reproduction requires at least128 GB Optane, with128 GB DRAM/32 cores recommended; emulation can check workflow but changes performance. Default artifact experiments are smaller than paper runs. No artifact or external proof was executed here; no LLM, TLA+ model-checking or distributed network liveness experiment appears.
