- Reusable Formal Verification of DAG-based Consensus Protocols (2025)
    - what: # Reusable Formal Verification of DAG-based Consensus Protocols (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Nathalie Bertrand, Pranav Ghorpade, Sasha Rubin, Bernhard Scholz and Pavle Subotić, arXiv2407.02167v2,18March2025. The inventory year2024 identifies the original submission; this read uses the retained2025revision. The authors report TLA+/TLAPS safety proofs for five protocol models by combining three DAG-construction components with two ordering components. The five are DAG-Rider, Cordial Miners, Hashgraph, Eventually Synchronous Bullshark and a minor variant of Aleph. The result concerns Integrity and Total Order; eventual delivery, probabilistic progress and implementation correctness remain outside it (Q0,Q1,Q2,Q12).  ## Reading scope and source identity  Read all21PDFpages, sections1–6 and51reference entries, all three figures including both panels of Figure2, seven listings and numerical Table1. Individually viewed pages6–16, covering the figures, listings, refinement mapping and numerical table. The paper refers to “Appendix ??” but includes no appendix. References were read as bibliography entries, not recursively read papers.  Acquired the paper-linked repository's complete nontruncated tree and22static blobs at9e4c9629eb283c7c34b6ffcf7956bc72a3e26e5d,22December2024, the latest retained commit before this paper revision. This selects a historical version without establishing that it produced the reported timings. Whole reads cover README and the five component Specification.tla files. Proof files and complete-protocol sources are inspected only in explicitly recorded ranges. Acquisition of a file does not imply its whole read. No author code, specification, checker or solver was executed/imported; no serialized experimental data or model was loaded.  ## What is proved and how composition works  A vertex embeds its entire rooted causal history, rather than only an opaque identifier. Thus two processes possessing the same vertex have identical histories for it. A separate anchor-agreement property requires processes that have selected anchors for a frame to select the same anchors. Delivering each frame's previously undelivered causal-history vertices in a fixed deterministic order then prevents duplicate delivery and gives consistent delivery order. A lagging process may deliver a shorter prefix; the proof is not simultaneous equality of local delivery states (Q2,Q3).  The decomposition is three construction models, reliable structured, unreliable structured and unreliable unstructured, plus Global Perfect Coin (GPC) and Virtual Voting (VV) ordering. Reliable construction ensures one agreed vertex per position. Unreliable structured construction allows equivocation and uses ratification to agree on a vertex at a position. GPC ordering separately agrees on leader positions; combining these properties establishes anchor agreement. Hashgraph first converts its unstructured events to a structured witness DAG. Reference consistency constrains witnesses' predecessor references; VV establishes agreement on famous witnesses (Q4,Q6,Q7,Q8).  The full protocol maps constants and new state variables into an INSTANCE of the ordering specification. Each construction action performs an appropriate ordering action or leaves its mapped variables unchanged. Protocol-specific refinement obligations remain necessary: five reusable components do not eliminate the proofs connecting five concrete models to them. DAG-Rider and ES Bullshark share reusable components but differ in wave lengths and commit/leader rules. Aleph is explicitly a variant; neither the paper nor its one-line repository README gives a precise delta against every published Aleph rule (Q4,Q9,C6).  ## Assumptions and abstractions  The system explanation assumes signed identities and fewer than one-third Byzantine processes. Reliable broadcast is weakened to integrity because validity and eventual agreement support liveness. Its model retains the first broadcast for each creator/round; the authors argue a faulty process's chosen delivered message could equivalently have been sent first. This is an abstraction argument to retain in correspondence work, not a separately measured implementation equivalence (Q1,Q5).  Unreliable communication copies a vertex from another local DAG; Byzantine participants can insert arbitrary modeled vertices. Procedures retain input/output behavior while omitting internal loop states. GPC becomes an arbitrary fixed function shared by all processes: randomness, unpredictability and fairness are absent. Agreement of the function's answers suffices for the stated safety argument; it supplies no probability-one termination theorem or implementation of a cryptographic coin (Q5,Q7,Q8).  The retained component models use finite symbolic bounds on waves/rounds or event IDs, with no fixed numerical test instance. Proof claims quantify over their parameters; a single TLC run at one bound would not establish that claim. The record does not independently establish an infinite-execution refinement beyond these bounded model families. The VV component incorporates its reference-consistency premise directly into both the prestate and poststate of Next. Its source proof of that premise therefore depends on the action restriction; a concrete protocol still must justify the mapping (C1–C5).  ## Consequential findings in the retained artifact  The printed self-referential VertexSet listing is explanatory shorthand. ReliableStructuredDAGconstructionSpecification.tla instead constructs vertices recursively by decreasing round with UntilRoundVertex. That resolves the apparent circular-definition concern for this component. However, its received/added-vertex guards access v.edges, while the actual constructed records contain strongedges and weakedges. This is a static field mismatch in the saved model; no checker error or reachable protocol failure was observed (C1).  In the unreliable structured component, Approves(v,u) requires Observes(v,u) and forbids observing every vertex with u's source and round. Taking that quantified vertex to be u yields both Observes(v,u) and its negation. Thus the literal definition admits no approval. With n-f positive under3f<n, ratification requires a nonempty set of approving sources and cannot occur under this reading. Since ratified vertices initialize to Nil, the displayed agreement property can hold without any ratification progress. This is a reader derivation from the retained definitions, not a claim that the real Cordial Miners protocol fails (C2).  The unstructured component's DonotSeeFork(v,u) has the same self-including universal condition. Taking x=u contradicts its Path(v,u) conjunct. Its Sees predicate consequently cannot witness a seen ancestor under the intended reflexive Path interpretation. This is another source-definition concern to resolve before trusting correspondence to actual Hashgraph behavior; the paper excludes liveness and no safety counterexample is established (C3).  VVOrderingProofs.tla contains several named lemma statements without proof text. Most concretely, intInv14 asserts, for integers a,m, that a>=m and m+a>2f imply m>f. The permitted assignment f=1,a=2,m=1 makes both premises true and its conclusion false. The file cites this lemma in its Inv1 preservation proof. The nearby intadd statement has the plausible intended conclusion a>f with strict a>b. This suggests a proof-support defect or mistaken statement in the retained artifact; it does not show a checker accepted a false theorem. The actual preservation goal is a majority-count fact that can be mathematically true even though this cited general lemma is false (C7,C8).  The same proof preamble asserts, without accompanying proof text, that every witness has more than2f predecessor sources in the preceding frame. Combined literally with its positive finite frame range and predecessor-frame condition, that leaves no nonempty witness set: a witness of minimum frame would require an earlier witness. This preamble should not silently become an established domain theorem. A simpler nonvacuity concern appears in the VV specification itself: all witness-DAG cells initially are empty, while adding a witness requires more than2f predecessor sources already present. With f nonnegative and no genesis exception, no first witness can be added under the intended typed interpretation. Whole proof closure and tool handling of unproved declarations have not been replayed. These artifact findings limit independent confirmation of the paper's mechanized-proof claim; they do not refute the high-level decomposition or establish a reachable fork (C5,C7).  ## Evaluation and significance  Table1 reports, in order reliable structured/unreliable structured/unreliable unstructured/GPC/VV: specification lines403/160/230/272/136; invariants6/6/7/10/18; proof lines460/594/554/822/2120; maximum proof depths10/9/8/9/13; maximum degrees7/8/7/7/11; leaf obligations633/895/665/1302/3316; and checking seconds49/68/74/125/651. Environment:2.10GHzCPU,8GBRAM,Windows11,TLAPS1.4.5. These are author-reported component checks, not reader replications or total timings for every full-protocol refinement. No repeated-run variability or controlled comparison appears (Q10).  The authors report approximately14person-months across five people. Their “almost half” proof-effort claim corresponds to reducing an illustrative ten separate construction/ordering components to five reusable ones. There is no controlled person-time baseline or ablation demonstrating a50% labor saving; refinement work and component complexity also differ. A reusable component count is concrete, while a measured causal labor reduction remains unestablished (Q0,Q4,Q10,Q12).  This is directly relevant to reusable TLA+ specifications and distributed-protocol proofs. It has no Verus/Rust artifact or LLM-agent experiment. A reader proposal for evaluating proof agents is to score specification correspondence, nonvacuous progress examples, closure of helper lemmas and refinement obligations separately from reported leaf-obligation throughput. The retained field/predicate/lemma issues make those distinctions concrete without treating static concerns as observed solver results.

- Sync/Async functional distributed verification (2025)
    - title: Functional Reasoning for Distributed Systems with Failures
    - what: Defines twin Rocq-embedded languages Sync and Async to make high-level functional reasoning about asynchronous distributed protocols precise, then proves that compiled Async executions are included in Sync denotations. The authors mechanize the framework and safety proofs for Bosco and Sequential Paxos. Sync is a choreography language whose typed programs are ordered communication steps over role-indexed vectors; its power-set denotation explicitly enumerates message loss, reordering, truncation and arbitrary Byzantine injections. The configuration model separates good, crash-prone and Byzantine nodes and uses a Netwk relation as an executable over-approximation. Finite loop unwinding supports safety proofs for nonterminating protocols. Bosco proofs derive a strong one-step theorem for n>7f and an inductive univalence/agreement invariant for n>3f. SeqPaxos proofs use a weakest precondition UC_D to show once a value is decided, later iterations decide it or abstain when n>2f; the condition is weaker than the protocol’s strongest postcondition and exposes optimization opportunities. Async is a monadic node LTS plus single-use channel LTS; alignment permutes arbitrary interleavings into channel order, and adequacy (Theorem 5.1) follows through aligned traces, big-step semantics and channel-erasure lemmas. The Rocq development is about 2.5k lines and includes endpoint projection/executable extraction.
    - number: Rocq mechanization about 2.5k LoC
    - number: Bosco one-step threshold n>7f; agreement threshold n>3f
    - number: SeqPaxos agreement threshold n>2f
    - number: SimpleVote example n_R=4, f_R=b_R=1
    - limits: Sync excludes branches and loops inside a body, cryptographic primitives, and arbitrary communication order; it therefore targets streamlined communication-closed protocols. Network messages are authenticated, nonduplicated, eventually delivered between correct nodes, and well typed; signatures and parsing are assumed rather than modeled. Byzantine behavior is over-approximated and can inject values it could not know. Safety is proved for finite loop unwindings, not termination/liveness. The Async adequacy theorem is inclusion (compiled outputs are in the denotation), and correctness depends on the stated channel-ordering and synchrony-reduction restrictions.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Trustworthy agents LLM-FM position (2025)
    - title: Position: Trustworthy AI Agents Require the Integration of Large Language Models and Formal Methods
    - what: Argues for bidirectional LLM/formal-method integration: LLMs automate specification and proof work, while model checkers, theorem provers, SMT solvers, testing and runtime monitors constrain and certify LLM actions. The proposed six-stage pipeline starts from natural-language requirements, auto-formalizes them, runs model checking/theorem proving/SMT, tests edge cases, monitors executions and iteratively refines outputs. It sketches a Coq theorem-prover agent and a miniF2F even-number example. Sections 2–5 distinguish failure modes of LLM-only reasoning (hallucination, non-determinism, no traceability), FM-only workflows (specification effort, scalability and unpredictable environments), and hybrid trade-offs. The auto-formalization workflow creates a proof outline with placeholders, delegates algebraic gaps to external tools and revises failed proof steps. The model-checking discussion targets safety/liveness, deadlock and refinement; theorem proving targets machine-checkable proofs; SMT handles satisfiability; testing and monitoring cover behaviors outside proofs. Appendix A gives explicit Coq interaction steps and an Isabelle/HOL formalization attempt, making the proposal concrete but not an implemented benchmark. The implemented PAT prototype repairs GPT-4o car-lock modeling errors using model-checker traces: leavekey must require an open door, and locking receives explicit owner/key guards. Appendix A also gives Z3 meeting scheduling with missing unavailable-day constraints and a Dafny palindrome specification/implementation; these illustrate semantic alignment risks rather than measured success rates.
    - number: six pipeline stages: auto-formalization, model checking, theorem proving, SMT, testing, monitoring
    - number: Coq workflow has four steps from natural language to verified explanation
    - number: Appendix example uses sum of two even numbers
    - limits: This is a position/conceptual paper with case studies, not a controlled empirical evaluation. It assumes formal specifications and verifiers are available and sufficiently expressive; hybrid integration incurs representation mismatch and complexity. Formal methods can miss unmodeled or emergent behavior, while LLM-generated specifications/proofs still require verifier feedback and possibly human refinement. The Coq example is illustrative and does not establish agent success rates.

- Formally Verifying the Safety of Pipelined Moonshot Consensus Protocol (2024)
    - what: # Formally Verifying the Safety of Pipelined Moonshot Consensus Protocol (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  M. Praveen, Raghavendra Ramesh and Isaac Doidge, arXiv:2403.16637v1, March2024; the PDF identifies FMBC2024 in its metadata. The authors report an IVy proof that honest validators' committed chains are prefix-comparable for arbitrary network sizes under an abstract honest-quorum-intersection assumption. This is a safety verification of a high-level model, using190 reported invariants and23 monitors. Liveness is future work; cryptographic checking, executable implementation refinement and transaction validity are outside the demonstrated result (Q1,Q4,Q6,Q8,Q11).  ## Reading and evidence scope  Read all17PDFpages: sections1–7,30bibliography entries, AppendixA, the complete Figure1 protocol pseudocode, the unnumbered proof-dependency diagram, both tables and the appendix IVy listing spanning pages15–17. No numbered theorem/proof, evaluation plot or checklist appears. Individually inspected renders5,9,10,15,16,17 to verify the protocol, numerical table, proof diagram and appendix code. Complete extracted text was read for every page; visual scope is limited to those six renders. References were read as entries, not recursively followed.  Fetched the paper-linked GitHub repository metadata and a complete nontruncated tree. Chose commit1eae7974044d6981b214aa231d4e1c7d6f1bc1b3, the most recent commit affecting suprabft before26March2024. Its date is8February2024. This is a documented historical-source selection, not proof that this exact commit produced every reported check. The current repository has later changes; the record does not substitute current HEAD for the paper-era source. Nine retained files have SHA256 and matching Git-blob identities. Read README, types.ivy, ubd_seq.ivy, network.ivy, moonshot.ivy, quorum_verification.ivy, check_all.sh and Isolates.txt whole. Read selected safety.ivy ranges1–140,703–875,1007–1115,1170–1195 and1230–1309, plus search hits for axioms/assumptions/isolates. This is not a whole1,309-line proof-file audit. Large IVySummary/IVyTrace logs, slides, backup/trial proof files and external tool-library modules were inventoried but not read. No author code, IVy model, checker or SMT solver was executed/imported; no model/checkpoint/serialized experiment data was loaded.  ## Exact property and fault model  Each validator maintains committed blocks and its latest committed block. Safety requires that for any two honest validators one committed blockchain is a prefix of the other. The model may allow one validator to lag arbitrarily far behind another; it need not require equal states at every instant. The retained full_safety definition makes three conditions explicit: the first tip equals or precedes the second; every block of the first set is in the second; and every extra block in the second descends from the first tip. The invariant requires this relation in either direction for honest participants (Q4,C8).  The protocol explanation assumes n=3f+1 and quorums of2f+1, with at most f Byzantine validators. The mechanized abstraction does not prove a cardinality theorem with this arithmetic. It axiomatizes that every pair of quorums shares an honest member, and that an f+1 timeout group has an honest member. For the stated cardinalities, ordinary set counting justifies the honest intersection: two2f+1 subsets of3f+1 participants overlap in at least f+1, exceeding f faulty members. That arithmetic connection is reader reasoning, not a separate IVy run (Q2,Q5,C1).  The paper motivates partially synchronous operation, but the safety network is asynchronous: messages may be lost, duplicated or reordered. Timers are Boolean expiration events that may occur nondeterministically. Neither eventual delivery nor a known time bound is needed for the stated prefix invariant; those abstractions cannot establish eventual commits or latency. The source explicitly says liveness would require fairness for timer execution and additional handling of directly received prepare quorums (Q5,Q6,C5).  Byzantine validators can send arbitrary modeled messages under Byzantine identities, including impersonating other Byzantine validators. They cannot forge an honest identity. Digital signatures and hashes are abstracted: block identity replaces digest computation; global records of honest sent messages validate a certificate's claimed members. These monitors are proof instrumentation, not a deployed trusted coordinator. Their faithful relationship to actual cryptographic validation remains an implementation/refinement obligation (Q5,Q6,C2,C3).  ## Protocol cases  Views have rotating leaders and three voting/proposal paths. A normal proposal extends the block certified in the preceding view. A fallback proposal carries a timeout certificate and extends the block whose included quorum certificate has the highest view. Optimistic proposals let the next leader propose a child immediately after voting, before receiving the current block's certificate. Honest voting guards constrain prior optimistic/normal/fallback votes and timeouts; certificates aggregate votes of one type. An honest validator may vote at most twice in a view under the displayed rules (Q2,Q3).  A validator advances with a quorum or timeout certificate, updates the locked highest-view certificate, and resets its timer. It commits a block when that block and its child are certified in adjacent views, then commits uncommitted ancestors. The one-block-per-network-hop and three-hop finalization claims describe the normal-path design in the cited protocol work; this verification paper supplies no throughput/latency experiment or implementation performance result (Q2,Q3).  The source separates message receipt from processing. A normal proposal must be received from the current leader, contain a constructed block and appropriate certificate, satisfy timer/first-processing conditions and the vote predicate; it may process the certificate first and propose the next optimistic block. Timeout/fallback handlers require embedded certificates to have been processed, explicitly constraining the environment to perform those steps first. This is a modeling convention to retain in any source correspondence argument, rather than evidence that every implementation obeys the ordering (C4,C5).  The eight whole source reads expose additional concrete abstractions. Fresh blocks are chosen from initially present unconstructed objects. Genesis has its own certificate and all participants in its quorum. The optimistic-last-block variable initially uses a separate unused block rather than the paper's genesis digest; a comment calls this a proof-performance shortcut. Honest/block validity invariants are separate from the top safety invariant. These details are not silently normalized to the informal algorithm (C1,C3).  ## How the proof is organized  IVy generates initiation/preservation obligations for inductive invariants and asks Z3 to discharge them. Isolates expose selected supporting properties to control formula size and logical fragments. Passing one isolate while assuming dependencies is only part of a complete verification; those dependencies also need checking. The paper reports completing that process but this read does not replay it (Q5,Q7,Q8).  The principal argument establishes that certified blocks proposed in later rounds descend from the directly committed block. This ordering concerns proposal rounds, not the time a delayed certificate is received. Additional invariants establish ancestry among each honest validator's committed blocks, closure under committed ancestors, and ancestry comparability of different honest tips. These establish prefix comparability. The paper supplies a five-step informal proof outline and a dependency diagram, not the full text of all obligations. The retained source supplies the exact omitted blockchain_prefix formula (Q7,Q8,C8).  The difficult descendant lemma uses induction over rounds. The paper says “The principle of induction is taken to be an axiom” (§4.3,p10). The source includes an explicit parameterized induction schema in quorum_after_ldc_descendant; related predecessor/successor results also use schemas. These are mathematical assumptions/rules requiring an intended natural-number interpretation, not automatically inferred from a bare arbitrary total order. The retained ubd_seq defines immediate successor using the absence of an intermediate element and supplies a nat implementation; no such implementation was executed or proved anew here (Q8,C1,C6,C13).  Transitive closure is not directly definable in first-order logic. A monitor incrementally records ancestors when blocks acquire quorums; supporting invariants establish parent inclusion, transitivity, no divergent ancestors and recovery of the immediate-parent relation from ancestry. The commit update is in safety.ivy rather than simply the core moonshot module: commit_candidate requires local direct commit, descent from the current tip, processed-certificate conditions for ancestors, and maximal eligible round; its after-qc-processing monitor updates the committed set/tip. Thus the exact model being verified includes proof-file actions, not just moonshot.ivy alone (Q8,Q9,C7,C12).  No contradiction or reachable fork was established by this read. Static source inspection is insufficient to certify every isolate dependency, axiom, abstraction or proof result. The paper's categorical statements that verification conclusively rules out design/logic errors should be understood relative to this formal model, stated axioms, tool behavior and intended correspondence, rather than as a theorem about arbitrary implementations or all blockchain security properties (Q4,Q11).  ## Reported evaluation and retained artifact  Table1 reports338lines of Types,110Network,642Moonshot,165Quorum verification and1,309Safety, totaling2,564. These counts exactly match physical line counts of the five retained files at the selected historical pin. The printed ratio of program code to proof is approximately1:3; that partition is descriptive, since files also contain comments, data abstractions and monitors. The authors report190invariants and23monitors, but do not specify a counting convention. A simple line-prefix count finds188invariant declarations in safety.ivy and211across those five files, including other invariants; do not equate that lexical count with semantic proof obligations or silently replace the published number (Q7,C9).  The reported effort is about140person-hours after the protocol specification stabilized,10%modeling and the remainder proofs. Fourteen modeling and126proof hours are reader arithmetic, not a time log. Earlier protocol revisions and proof/specification iterations are discussed but excluded from that clearly stated starting point. No verification wall-clock benchmark, hardware specification, exact IVy/Z3 version, variance, isolate runtime table or controlled cross-tool comparison appears (Q7,Q9,Q10).  The README gives a full-file checking command; check_all.sh instead lists116individual isolate checker invocations separated by date commands. Static shell inspection finds no fail-fast option or aggregate-status check and a final date command. Therefore running the script can return success even if an earlier checker command fails. This is a wrapper-status limitation, not evidence that a particular historical obligation failed or that the README's separate command masks failures. Isolates.txt lists an older basic_safety structure, so it is not used here as a certificate of current full_safety dependency coverage. Unread trace/summary logs are not evidence of successful complete checking in this record (C10,C11).  The authors describe solver stalls, inlining nested subroutine calls, explicit intermediate facts and fine dependency selection. Their concrete ancestry example adds two intermediary ancestor facts so that transitivity becomes easy for Z3. These are useful proof-engineering observations without controlled ablations or measured universal speedups. The claimed lower effort than Coq and preference over TLA+/TLAPS are author experience/opinion, not a matched comparison of equivalent developments (Q1,Q9,Q10).  ## Contribution to the requested topics  This is a directly relevant parameterized Byzantine-consensus safety case study: abstract honest quorum intersection, asynchronous safety despite timer abstraction, certificate-authentication monitors, ancestry instrumentation, round induction and modular solver obligations. It reports human verification work rather than LLM agents. It has no Verus or verified Rust artifact and does not prove a TLA+ model.  For agents writing specifications/proofs, the transfer is a reader proposal: preserve exactly which code and monitor actions define the model; separately check dependencies and axioms; expose small intermediate ancestry facts; retain failures from every checker invocation; and distinguish a proof of an abstract invariant from executable refinement, crypto correctness, liveness and application security. The190-invariant/140-hour development is a potential future study case, not evidence of an agent success rate or measured automation benefit.
    - limits: Honestquorumintersectionandinductionaxioms;unforgeablehonestidentities;Boolean-timer/asynchronoussafetyabstraction;globalcertificate/ancestry/commitmonitors;noimplementation/crypto/liveness/transactionvalidityproof;noauthorcodeexecution
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Modelling the Raft Distributed Consensus Protocol in mCRL2 (2024)
    - what: # Whole read: Modelling the Raft Distributed Consensus Protocol in mCRL2 (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Bora, Pham Duc Minh and Willemse, MARS 2024, EPTCS 399, pp. 7–20; DOI 10.4204/EPTCS.399.4; arXiv 2403.18916v1, submitted 27 March 2024. Read all 14 PDF pages, §§1–5, Figure 1, Table 1, acknowledgement and 14 references, in text and rendered pages. Also read the official MARS mCRL2 model, all nine property files, README and batch script statically. The downloaded paper matches the indexed PDF byte for byte. No author code, model checker, prover or solver was executed. Raw sources, hashes, scope and exact excerpts are retained separately.  The paper contributes an action-based Raft model and modal µ-calculus formulas, with reported model-checking results for eight finite configurations. It does not establish unrestricted Raft correctness. More specifically, the authors write, “All main properties hold true for all configurations of Table 1; verification of each property takes roughly 2-3 times that of generating the state space” (§3, PDF p. 9 / printed p. 15; Q19). Static inspection also finds differences between the printed formulas, artifact formulas and model indexing. These narrow what a successful check can establish; they are not claimed reachable protocol counterexamples.  ## Model and assumptions  The model follows the TLA+ and LNT specifications and focuses on leader election and log replication: “Features such as cluster membership changes and log compaction have not been modelled” (§2, PDF p. 2 / printed p. 8; Q3). Node, Network and Client processes run in parallel; communicating actions and allow restrictions enforce synchronisation. Analysis requires a “fixed, concrete number of servers” (§2, PDF p. 3 / printed p. 9; Q4), rather than a proof quantified over all cluster sizes.  One client sends a bounded sequence of unique natural command IDs, abstracting away command contents and application behavior (Q5). Client-to-cluster communication is synchronous; communication among servers is asynchronous through the network. The network holds a finite set of payloads, permits reordering and limits capacity (Q7). Thus two identical pending payloads collapse to one set element. Reliable mode supplies no scheduler fairness guarantee. Capacity can block sends; the broadcast admission guard uses the number of servers rather than the exact outgoing set size (Q47). These are model restrictions, not a general unbounded or duplicate-preserving network semantics.  The scope description has a tension worth retaining. Section 2.1 says, “we do not analyse the Raft algorithm in the presence of message loss or duplication” (PDF p. 4 / printed p. 10; Q6). Table 1 nevertheless includes one lossy-and-crashing row, and §3 reports all main formulas true for every row (Q18–Q19). The model supplies an optional loss branch (Q8, Q47). The defensible reading is a primarily reliable model with one reported loss experiment; no duplication experiment is reported.  Each server has role, term, log, commit index, vote state, replication indexes and a pending reply. Figure 1 adds a Crashed role to Follower/Candidate/Leader (Q9–Q10). Entries are Command(term, commandID), stored in zero-based mCRL2 lists; the authors explicitly warn that Raft’s original indexing is one-based (Q11). Only leaders accept client commands. Elections and heartbeats use nondeterministic actions; “we cannot analyse real-time requirements, nor the real-time performance of the algorithm” (§2.2, PDF p. 6 / printed p. 12; Q12). There is no sampling of randomized timeout intervals or elapsed-time state.  A candidate votes for itself and sends vote requests to other nodes not already in its voter set (Q13). Stale RPCs are discarded. A received request computes and stores a reply before the later send, allowing a crash between the state update and reply transmission (Q14). The artifact’s resume transition preserves currentTerm, log and votedFor, but resets commitIndex, voterLog, nextIndex, matchIndex and the pending reply, and returns to Follower (Q46). Reply computation itself is atomic: the authors acknowledge it “does not accurately reflect real-world scenarios where the computation of a reply would involve multiple steps” (§5, PDF p. 13 / printed p. 19; Q35).  Leaders send one log entry at a time; the model uses nextIndex backtracking to find a matching prefix (Q15). Majority replication commits only a current-term entry directly; earlier entries are committed indirectly with it (Q16). This is a protocol model without a refinement proof connecting a Rust implementation, application state machine or storage system to these transitions.  ## What the properties check  The action logic cannot inspect process parameters directly. The model therefore adds self-loop actions exposeLeader, exposeLogLeader and exposeLog (Q19, Q46). Safety formulas compare exposed states across arbitrary later execution, rather than merely reading two process parameters in one state.  **Election Safety.** The printed formula forbids a different node from ever later exposing leadership in the same term (Q20); the artifact agrees (Q52). An additional existential formula asks whether a leader can be exposed at all; another asks whether two different node IDs can eventually expose leadership (Q21). The latter is reported true only with at least two terms. These are witness-existence checks, not eventual election on every execution.  The least-fixed-point formula counts possible leader announcements whose ID differs from the most recently stored ID, starting with id=1 and count=0 (Q22, Q53). It does not compare the associated terms or demand globally distinct IDs; a later reuse of an earlier ID can count. It is therefore more precise to describe its actual counter than to call it a proof of MaxTerm distinct leaders or guaranteed succession.  **Log Matching.** The intended statement is that the same index and term imply identical prefixes through that index. The paper’s guard instead tests complete entry equality, log1.index == log2.index, which is a stronger antecedent and makes the standalone requirement weaker (Q23). The artifact repairs this to term(log1.index) == term(log2.index) and checks both list bounds (Q49).  Both versions compare slice(log,1,index+1). The supplied slice helper begins at zero and excludes its finishing index (Q42). It therefore omits position 0, the first actual entry; initial logs contain no dummy element (Q48). Reader-created one-entry logs [Command(1,1)] and [Command(1,2)] have matching terms at position 0, but both compared slices are empty. This separates the intended first-entry equality requirement from the artifact formula. It does not establish that these two logs coexist in a reachable model state.  **Leader Completeness.** After advanceCommitIndex(old,new,term,log), the formula asks that selected entries occur somewhere in every later-term leader’s exposed log (Q24–Q25, Q51). The membership consequent is weaker than retaining each entry at its original index. There is also an indexing discrepancy: it selects log1.index for old < index <= new, while the model’s getTerm translates Raft index to list position index-1 (Q41). With a singleton log and new=1, the formula selects zero-based position 1 rather than 0. No semantic result for out-of-bounds list selection, checker failure or reachable counterexample is asserted here. For commits in the sole elected term of a one-term configuration, there is no later leader term to test; the auxiliary leader/commit existence checks do not make every main formula nonvacuous.  The event itself has a separate static inconsistency. On the successful AppendEntriesResponse branch, advanceCommitIndex computes its new index from the old matchIndex array, whereas the following Node transition computes commitIndex after incorporating the new acknowledgement (Q45). A local valuation with one entry, old matchIndex=[0,0,0] and an acknowledgement of entry 1 from another server yields event new index 0 but state commit index 1 using the supplied count helper (Q43–Q45). Reachability of that valuation was not established. The source expression mismatch is sufficient reason not to equate the event range automatically with all newly committed entries.  **State Machine Safety.** The intended statement forbids two nodes applying different entries at the same committed index. Paper and artifact compare slice(log1,1,commitIndex1) and the corresponding slice of a later other node with commitIndex2 >= commitIndex1 > 0 (Q26, Q50). Under the artifact helper, position 0 is excluded. For commitIndex1=1 both slices are empty, independent of the first entries. Thus a successful check of this formula alone cannot establish agreement on the first committed entry. Again, this is formula coverage established by symbolic values, not a reachable protocol failure.  An existential companion asks whether two different nodes can expose positive commit indexes in sequence, the later one at least as large (Q27). It does not repair prefix coverage or prove every request eventually commits. Append Entries is omitted as a separate checked property because the authors consider it immediate from the modeled leader operations (Q19).  ## Reported evaluation and concrete cases  The authors used manual interactive simulation and safety formulas throughout development to find model bugs (Q19). Table 1 gives eight configurations, all with three nodes and capacity three. The following preserves the reported rounded sizes and approximate generation times; every row is retained in numeric-evidence.json (Q18, §2.2, PDF p. 8 / printed p. 14):  - 2 commands, 1 term, reliable, no crashes: 2.14×10^5 states, about 1 minute - 1 command, 2 terms, reliable, no crashes: 1.17×10^6 states, about 2 minutes - 1 command, 3 terms, reliable, no crashes: 1.32×10^7 states, about 13 minutes - 2 commands, 1 term, reliable, crashes: 1.79×10^7 states, about 5 minutes - 2 commands, 2 terms, reliable, no crashes: 2.25×10^7 states, about 19 minutes - 2 commands, 1 term, lossy, crashes: 5.97×10^7 states, about 105 minutes - 1 command, 2 terms, reliable, crashes: 1.48×10^8 states, about 24 minutes - 1 command, 3 terms, reliable, crashes: 2.38×10^9 states, about 820 minutes  The first seven times use a 2017 Macbook Pro with 16GB; the last uses an unspecified compute server and is explicitly “not directly comparable to the other runtimes reported” (Table 1; Q18). No repetitions, error bars, exact CPU, per-formula times or implementation latency/throughput are supplied. The paper reports roughly 2–3× generation time for each main property using symbolic verification (Q19, technique reference Q38). The base configuration compresses by almost factor 5 under strong bisimilarity; no exact reduced size is given (Q17).  The artifact README names mCRL2 release 202307.1 and gives explicit and symbolic commands (Q56). Its default model has five servers, two requests, one term, capacity five, no loss/crashes, and PRIORITISE=false (Q40). Seven individually guarded Node instances support the small configured sizes (Q48). The batch script substitutes three servers/capacity three, requests 1 or 2, terms 1–3, and both Boolean loss/crash settings, for 24 combinations (Q57). It includes all .mcf files, including timeout/client-command checks (Q54–Q55); neither the script nor their inclusion is a saved result log. Do not infer that every batch combination or every extra formula was successfully checked. The official archive contains source and instructions, not experiment output or an identified experiment commit.  MAJORITY is ceil(NumberOfServers/2) in the artifact (Q40). That is a strict majority for the odd sizes three and five here, but only half for even sizes. No arbitrary-size correctness claim should be inferred from the configurable constant.  Section 4 illustrates how modeling style changes generated state count. A Boolean process with conditionals after the action yields 3 states/6 transitions; moving the choice before the action yields 1 state/2 transitions, strongly bisimilar to the first (Q29–Q30). The tool can then remove an unused process parameter. The example supports a concrete state-generation lesson; it is not a measured uniform threefold Raft reduction. The claim that an early inexperienced model was “orders of magnitude larger than needed” is an author observation without a controlled Raft benchmark (Q29).  ## Relation to the research topic  The direct contribution is a finite distributed-protocol model and action-logic encodings of selected Raft requirements, with bounded symbolic checking and concrete resource measurements. The authors describe the modal formalizations as apparently new while acknowledging the prior TLA+, LNT and Verdi work (Q2, Q33, Q39). This is useful evidence about specification design, state exposure, state-space growth, crash abstraction and model/property correspondence.  The TLA+ comparison is specific. The authors say TLA+ answers stale requests but drops stale responses; their model follows LNT in discarding both. They follow TLA+ when bounding transmitted commit information, and add reboot behavior they say is absent from the LNT model (Q31). These are attributed source discussions, not an independent audit of all upstream artifacts. Section 4’s view that imperative LNT is more appealing to average engineers is an author opinion, not a user study (Q32).  For evaluating LLM agents writing specifications or proofs, the useful checks are reader inferences: validate a formula against its helper definitions, include first/last-index examples, inspect whether instrumentation exposes the actual state transition, distinguish term equality from entry equality, and separate existential witnesses from inevitable progress. This paper supplies concrete reasons these checks matter, but it contains no LLM-agent, Verus, verified-Rust or implementation-refinement evaluation.  The liveness boundary is explicit: “Stronger liveness requirements, asserting that always inevitably a leader must be elected, are simply not true in our model” (§5, PDF p. 13 / printed p. 19; Q34), owing to crashes/loss and bounded terms. More complex configurations, stronger liveness, finer reply computation and real-time verification remain future work (Q33–Q36). Successful bounded checks therefore support the specified finite model and literal formulas, subject to the correspondence issues above. They do not independently establish the stronger prose requirements.  ## Evidence and review  quotes.json contains 57 literal excerpts with file, section, PDF/printed page where applicable, line ranges and character offsets. combined.quote-source.txt contains every excerpt for database validation. source-files.json retains raw hashes; source-scope.json distinguishes whole-paper and static artifact coverage. numeric-evidence.json preserves all table rows, and static-witnesses.json records reader-created separating values. reader-diagnostics.json separates source claims from reader findings. REVIEW.md and validation-final.json track consultation, independent review and mechanical checks.
    - limits: Fixed finite cluster,client commands,terms and finite-set network capacity; one synchronous client and asynchronous interserver RPC; no membership changes/log compaction; nondeterministic untimed timeouts,atomic reply computation,optional crashes/reboot/loss. Eight reported three-node configurations. No duplication/multiplicity or fairness guarantee. Static indexing/formula/event mismatches qualify property coverage.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Synthetic Programming Elicitation for Text-to-Code in Very Low-Resource Programming and Formal Languages (2024)
    - what: SPEAC makes an LLM generate a familiar intermediate language, repairs that output with compiler checks and weighted MAX-SMT, then translates it into a language poorly represented in training data. Eudoxus instantiates this with a subset of Python that describes UCLID5 transition systems. It prunes unsupported syntax, replaces inconsistent syntax-tree nodes with holes, infers repairs from a satisfying model and asks the LLM to fill remaining holes. The evaluation measures compiler acceptance and a single author’s manual semantic scores on 33 textbook tasks. Preserves how the intermediate language was manually designed, exact pruning/constraint/repair contracts and minimality conditions, the traffic-light input ownership mistake, type and timer concerns, every result-table cell and denominator, three dataset roles, baseline and call settings, manual scoring, timing components and limits of the semantic claim. Distinguishes satisfying static constraints from preserving intended behavior or proving the generated transition system.
    - limits: §3–§5: Requires a target language T, familiar parent P, child subset C and compiler f:C→T. Here T is UCLID5, P Python and C an abstract Module subclass with declaration/init/next methods. The language design and syntax-directed translator are supplied by people; the paper does not prove compiler semantic preservation or automatically derive a correct modeling language. §5.1: Synthetic elicitation is a manual lightweight grounded-theory analysis of LLM outputs, not a human-subject study. People label output fragments, group codes into concepts/categories and design C. The object-oriented category is chosen for ease of translation; no experiment establishes universal LLM naturalness or superiority over every alternative child language. §5.1, p.6: GPT-3.5-turbo-0125 and GPT-4-turbo-2024-04-09 each generate ten samples on each of 33 curated regression tasks: 660 direct-UCLID5 attempts, none parses. This is a result for those models/tasks, not a universal inability theorem. Imaginary FormalVerificationLibrary prompts elicit from-scratch Z3, procedural API and object-oriented subclass styles. §5.2/Figure 4: Initial prompt specifies parent, task, unique code block and child description, ending with an affirmative assistant prefix. Repair prompt asks to replace every ?? and repeats the task. The templates are partial; the PDF does not include every concrete benchmark prompt or the complete child grammar. §5.3: Error-tolerant parsing first builds a parent-language AST, then removes constructs outside C. This initial pruning may lose program intent. Static checks are encoded as soft clauses per node; MAX-SMT keeps a satisfiable weighted subset and rejected clauses identify nodes to replace with holes. Hard clauses and algebraic data types constrain legal structure. §5.3, p.7: Minimum node-edit guarantee is conditional on uniform weights and exactly one clause per node. Actual depth-proportional weights favor edits near leaves and explicitly break syntactic minimality guarantees. Largest consistent subtree therefore does not imply globally fewest edits in the implementation or smallest semantic change. §5.4: A satisfying model can infer a hole’s type, e.g. integer from integer-literal assignment; unresolved holes return to the LLM. Satisfying static constraints establishes a well-formed intermediate program, not equivalence to its original candidate, natural-language task satisfaction or a verified safety/liveness property. §3/Figure 1: Compiler-accepted traffic-light output declares pedestrian with var and initializes it false, although the task requires a nondeterministic input from another subsystem. Authors explicitly identify var→input as a semantic correction. Inference for distributed modeling: changing an environment input into locally controlled state can remove executions and make a property misleadingly easy to satisfy. Figure 1 lines 13–15 and Figure 5: The shown state-zero branch resets count to zero immediately before checking count<60 and incrementing it. Under the sequential step semantics suggested by the excerpt, that prevents this branch reaching 60. This is an apparent concern in a partial excerpt, not a confirmed full-model counterexample; the complete generated model and execution were not inspected. Authors discuss the pedestrian mistake, not this timer concern. Figures 5–6: Repairs replace BitVector declarations with int to match integer literal use, and replace Boolean()/False values in declarations with bool. Changing a bounded bitvector to integer changes wraparound/range behavior; static consistency alone does not preserve a bounded-state interpretation. Final snippets also show declarations/init separately and contain ellipses. §3/Figure 5: Figure 5 caption identifies (a) initial output and (b) repair; overview prose reverses these references. Figure 6 likewise contains partial outputs. Preserve the caption/code reading rather than treating the displayed snippets as a complete executable proof artifact. §6: Python implementation uses tree-sitter and Z3 MAX-SMT; local solver machine is a 2.3 GHz quad-core Intel Core i7 with 32 GB RAM, LLMs through OpenAI Python API. Repository is https://github.com/FedericoAureliano/eudoxus. This read covers the PDF, not repository code or a reproduced run. §6/Table 1: Text says allow repair loop five times; caption says limited to five LLM calls per benchmark. Self-repair baseline uses five iterations starting from zero-shot. Exact initial-call versus repair-count convention is not reconciled in the PDF; use the caption’s reported cap without silently adding an initial sixth call. §6: Three datasets have distinct roles: 33 curated developer regression tests with hand-written descriptions for language design; 317 repository regression tests self-summarized by the LLM for fine-tuning; 33 textbook exercises/examples for end-to-end evaluation. Textbooks are Baier/Katoen 2008, Lee/Seshia 2016 and Huth/Ryan 2004. No explicit split-overlap/contamination audit is given. §6: Fine-tunes GPT3t for 284 steps with learning-rate multiplier .16; no GPT4t fine-tune is reported. Baselines include one/three examples, with/without chain of thought, each on two models; two compiler-feedback self-repair variants; and fine-tuning. That is eleven baseline variants plus two Eudoxus variants, thirteen Table 1 rows; prose saying all eleven approaches is a counting slip. Abstract/§6: Abstract mentions retrieval baselines, but the specified baseline is repository few-shot prompting; no operational retrieval pipeline, retriever-quality measurement or retrieval ablation is described. Do not infer a separate implemented RAG system from that phrase. §6.1: Parse is shorthand for passing all compiler checks, including static typing, rather than grammar alone. Semantic assessment is one author’s manual reasoning plus hand-written specifications, performed only for compiled outputs. Scores run from 1 entirely wrong to 5 fully correct; ≥4 means authors believe useful, allowing minor errors. This is not an independently machine-checked end-to-end correctness result. Table 1: GPT3t Eudoxus parses 28/33=84.8%; GPT4t 24/33=72.7%. Combined 52/66=78.8% explains approximately 78% prose. Fine-tuning Table 1 is 2/33=6.1%, inconsistent with introduction 9.1%; preserve the discrepancy. Best GPT4t few-shot parses 4/33=12.1%. Percentages here are derived from printed counts. Table 1: Fully correct score-5 outputs are GPT4t 11/33 and GPT3t 9/33, not 24/33 or 28/33. Useful score≥4 outputs are 19/33 and 14/33 respectively. These derived unconditional denominators differ from the table’s conditional semantic denominators of 24 and 28 compiled outputs. §6.1: Across both Eudoxus variants, 30/33 unique tasks have at least one compiled output; across all eleven baseline variants, only 6/33 do. Eudoxus has 33/52 compiled outputs scoring ≥4, covering 24 unique tasks. Four unique baseline-useful tasks are also useful under Eudoxus. These unions are not a single model’s success rate or 33 verified specifications. §6.1: Four benchmarks hit iteration cap and three suffer implementation syntax/output-extraction/printing bugs. Their scope across model variants is unclear: the two table rows together have fourteen non-compiling outputs. Do not assign the seven reported cases independently to both models or invent an overlap explanation. §6.1, p.10: Reported GPT3t mean repair time .9 s (SD .6), LLM time 7.2 s (SD 4.6); GPT4t repair 1.8 s (SD 2.0), LLM 35.1 s (SD 24.7). These components support that LLM calls dominate reported local repair time. They are not total project compute, API dollars, token counts or a precisely specified aggregate wall-clock distribution. §6.1–§6.2: Authors explicitly caution that few baseline outputs compile, making conclusions about semantic performance difficult. No ablation separates child-language design, pruning, model-based repairs and repeated LLM calls; no broad budget-matched repeated-sampling comparison is reported. §6.2/Checklist: Only one target language, two LLMs and one semantic rater. No inter-rater agreement, semantic-score confidence intervals or statistical-significance claim; checklist item 7 explicitly calls this a case study. Generalization to other languages/models remains open. Checklist items 3–8: No theoretical results claimed. Code/data are said to be in supplemental material/GitHub; not executed here. Checklist says temperature settings are reported, but no numerical temperature is supplied in the PDF’s experimental description. Solver/compiler versions, exact seeds, repeated-run counts and solver timeouts are not disclosed there. Checklist items 10–15: Broader impacts answer No because work targets compiler checks; existing UCLID5 license stated as 3-clause BSD, textbook examples cited, new code/documentation claimed public. Human-subject and IRB questions marked NA. These are disclosure statements, not independent license or artifact verification. Whole paper: No generated distributed protocol, Verus/verified Rust proof, TLA+ specification, termination argument, concurrency refinement proof or validated natural-language specification oracle is evaluated. Formal tools repair syntax/types; UCLID5’s verification capabilities do not by themselves make generated models faithful to the task.

- TLA+ model-checking education (2024)
    - title: WIP: An Engaging Undergraduate Intro to Model Checking in Software Engineering Using TLA+
    - what: Describes an undergraduate/graduate teaching approach that introduces TLA+ and TLC through puzzles, a microwave controller, concurrency, bounded counters, and buffers. Students progressively confront safety, liveness, deadlock, and thread-safety failures. Analyzes model-checking courses, supplies reproducible examples and open-source modules, and uses successive counterexamples to motivate stronger requirements. The microwave case distinguishes safety (door/radiation) from liveness (eventual shutoff), while bounded buffers and counters introduce concurrency.

- TLA+ trace validation (2024)
    - title: Validating Traces of Distributed Programs Against TLA+ Specifications
    - what: Presents an API and TLC-based constrained model-checking workflow that records selected specification-variable updates from Java executions and checks whether the partial trace can be completed to a behavior of a TLA+ specification. The framework traces only chosen variables/events, leaving omitted values existentially open for TLC to reconstruct. It handles stuttering steps and implementation transitions that combine multiple TLA+ actions, and uses shortest BFS counterexamples or constrained DFS for speed. Case studies include Two-Phase Commit, snapshot-isolation key-value store, EWD998 termination detection, two Raft implementations and Microsoft CCF; trace validation found mismatches and exposed a CCF safety bug that became regression tests.
    - number: Counter implementation bug detected in less than one minute with event-only trace for 16 RMs
    - number: Two-Phase Commit 16 RMs event-only trace explores ∞/557k states (BFS/DFS table)
    - number: Key-value 12-agent, 20-key/40-value event-only trace reaches ∞ states at 1-hour timeout
    - number: Five distributed-program case studies all yielded specification/implementation discrepancies
    - limits: Verdicts depend on instrumentation points, trace granularity and the high-level specification. Too-imprecise logs can admit erroneous traces because TLC fills unconstrained values; too much logging causes state explosion. TLC explores finite bounded models and can return no counterexample for an invalid trace when the searched diameter is insufficient. The method detects conformance discrepancies, not a proof that all executions of the implementation satisfy the specification.

- Using Dynamically Layered Definite Releases for Verifying the RefFS File System (2024)
    - what: The RefFS project page links a separate 124-page draft, Progress of Concurrent Objects with Dynamic Dependencies, authored by Mo Zou. Its PDF metadata dates it May24,2024; the project says “A separate paper” illustrates MoLi's metatheory (P1). This is a complete fresh reading of that PODD paper, not an appendix of the OSDI paper and not a new reading of the RefFS implementation. The original RefFS main-paper account remains inherited from formal509. Whether this precise draft proves every rule of the released MoLi framework is not established by the link alone.  PODD makes a thread's progress depend on definite actions, obligations that their owning threads must fulfill once enabled. Actions have state-dependent layers, and dependencies must preserve strict relative order even when numeric layers change. Layers have a total order, not necessarily a well-founded one. Finite dependency sets and decreasing local/stack metrics supply the termination argument. The LIVE-FRM rule extends action/layer context while preserving old ordering, so an acquisition proof can be reused without expanding every nested wait chain. Tokens account for unblocked loop progress and finitely many levels of delaying interference. Theorem4.2 states linearizability and deadlock-freedom; starvation-freedom additionally requires rely and guarantee actions at level0 (S12,S14,S15).  AppendixA supplies the fuller resource/ownership logic, fences, rules and ideal shared-memory semantics. AppendixB first gives judgment simulations with finite-height while stacks: arbitrary-length lexicographic lists would not be well-founded (S31). B.3 expands new while/live-frame cases. B.4 maps these to a common simulation, directly for starvation-freedom and through a lock plus nondeterministically decreasing finite-wait wrapper for deadlock-freedom (S45,S48). B.5 combines thread simulations using finite active-thread dependency sets, scheduler rounds and a lexicographic metric. LemmasB.22–B.30 connect coinductive fair traces to refinement (S54,S57,S58,S66,S69). B.6 defines progress over complete fair traces, permitting abort alternatives, and uses three most-general clients to relate finite refinement to linearizability and fair refinement to linearizability plus starvation/deadlock freedom. The deadlock equivalence wraps the atomic specification; it must not be replaced by the stronger starvation refinement (S72,S74).  AppendixC provides all printed proof outlines for nested TAS/ticket locks, ticket-lock coupling lists, optimistic/lazy TAS lists, and two-list transfers. Ticket queues support starvation-freedom; unfair TAS permits only the weaker deadlock-freedom claim. Lists use local lock obligations, node order and finite traversal metrics. Optimistic/lazy cases retain removed nodes in ghost garbage, not a proved memory-reclamation scheme. Lazy contain is explicitly omitted. Transfers use one mutex to serialize moves, list levels0/1/2 and lexicographic (listlevel,distance-to-tail) layers; their add/remove proofs are referred to earlier lists. The two-list move is a useful dynamic-dependency example, rather than a distributed network/crash model (S87,S90,S94,S102,S109,S116,S122,S124).  Evaluation consists of paper proofs and worked examples; no numerical benchmark, proof-time table or replayed mechanization is supplied here. Section6 explicitly makes mechanization and automation future work (S20). Section5.1 admits that common rely conditions and frame stability can prevent reuse across objects (S17). Assumptions include fair scheduling, an arbitrary fixed finite number of threads, ideal atomic memory actions, safe total abstract methods and the presented non-nested method-call language. No bounded response-time, weak-memory, crash/recovery or implementation-to-binary theorem follows from this reading.  Reader deductions from the literal listings must be kept separate from intended theorems. Figure40's inner ticket loop never increments o; Figure55's elems retains marked values and discards unmarked ones; Figure62's sequential MOVE removes a head before reading the new head. Details and elementary counterexamples are in reader_diagnostics.json, with exact page anchors. These are printed-source defects, not findings about unseen code or a global refutation of PODD. The page also has draft-level omissions: a duplicated strict-comparison branch in the proof on p57, an apparent dependency-set type slip in DefinitionB.12, and an unresolved Fig.?? reference plus omitted scheduler semantics on p58. Underlines/fonts were visually checked before identifying these issues.  Relevance to the requested database is manual liveness proof structure and evaluation of specification/proof agents: track actual fairness and progress strength, stable dependency ordering, finite metrics, abstract-method contracts, omitted cases and intended versus literal specifications. These are reader uses of a concurrency result. This paper does not evaluate Verus, Rust, TLA+, LLM agents or distributed consensus. RefFS/Coq performance and proof counts remain attributed to the inherited OSDI paper, rather than this draft. Records exact WHILE/OBJECT obligations, ticket and abstract-lock metric differences, dynamic-layer/TDep construction, VFS bug cycle and source typo, refcount ownership protocol, non-atomic operation abstraction, conditional theorem and unmechanized metatheory, survey denominators, all LOC cells, workloads, normalized performance limits and graph estimates. - Bosch formal-verification explanation user study (2023)
    - title: A User Study for Evaluation of Formal Verification Results and their Explanation at Bosch
    - what: Studies Bosch automotive engineers’ challenges with formal notations, refinement inconsistencies, and model-checker output, then evaluates a counterexample-explanation approach. A 41-person survey and a one-group pretest/posttest with 13 formal-methods-aware engineers compare raw model-checker output against explanations for airbag and electronic-power-steering cases. The design separates RQ1/RQ2 survey constructs from RQ3 intervention measures (better/quicker understanding, confidence, and value), documents questionnaire scales and execution, and reports task-level correctness rather than only opinions. Six of 13 understood the airbag raw result; 12 of 13 preferred the explanation. For inconsistent specifications, 5 of 13 were fully/partly correct with raw output versus 9 of 13 with explanations. The discussion identifies integration with heterogeneous industrial toolchains as a practical barrier.

- Cirisci Enea Mutluergil QTree (2023)
    - title: Quorum Tree Abstractions of Consensus Protocols
    - what: Introduces Quorum Tree (QTree), a sequential rooted-tree abstraction whose add and commit operations model quorum observations in consensus rounds. It proves safety of QTree and shows Paxos, HotStuff, PBFT, Raft, and Multi-Paxos refine one or more QTree instances via fixed linearization points. QTree nodes carry round, value, parent, and ADDED/GHOST/COMMITTED status. Predicates enforce unique rounds, parent links, trunk extension, and (for single-decree consensus) parent-value preservation. Theorem 1 characterizes correct successful invocation sequences using four checkable properties, including the key prohibition on committing a lower conflicting round after a higher branch is added. Paxos refinement marks PROPOSE as add and DECIDE as commit; safety follows from quorum intersection between vote and later join quorums. HotStuff uses valid PRECOMMIT and DECIDE certificates under 3f+1 Byzantine processes and 2f+1 quorums. PBFT maps each sequence number to an independent QTree. A table relates protocol phases to add/commit quorum phases and exposes quorum optimization opportunities.
    - number: QTree statuses ADDED/GHOST/COMMITTED
    - number: HotStuff N=3f+1 and quorum 2f+1
    - number: four invocation-sequence properties in Theorem 1
    - number: Paxos 2 phases; PBFT 3*; HotStuff 4 phases
    - limits: Refinement proofs assume round-based protocols with identifiable phases, fixed code locations as linearization points, honest processes for Paxos/Raft and cryptographically constrained Byzantine behavior for HotStuff/PBFT. Validity additionally requires client-value broadcast or equivalent checks. QTree is a safety abstraction; the authors do not establish liveness and note protocols without round decomposition (e.g., Texel) may not fit. Multi-decree protocols require independent QTree instances, and removing valueConstraint changes validity interpretation for state-machine replication.

    - what: CLAPS learns policies and reach-avoid supermartingale certificates for edges of a DAG describing a SPECTRL task. A forward pass records the best certified product of edge probabilities and prunes prefixes below the global threshold. It extracts one certified path and uses a controller with memory of the current edge. Additive and multiplicative certificate formulations motivate a stronger model-relative probability bound using a Lipschitz/step-size delay before unsafe reachability. A stochastic nine-room example obtains a33.0% certified complete-path lower bound where direct policy/certificate learning times out. Records the exact certificate inequalities, stronger-bound conditions, full appendix proof arguments and printed gaps, verifier margin/numerical-expectation contract, stateful probability composition, restricted best-path completeness, every experiment/table value and the distinction between a33% lower bound and plotted successful trajectories. Identifies formula/translation/regularity discrepancies without assuming the uninspected implementation uses them.

- Creusot higher-order iterators (2023)
    - title: Specifying and Verifying Higher-order Rust Iterators.
    - what: Defines a first-order framework for specifying Rust iterators, including nondeterministic, potentially infinite and effectful adapters. Iterators are described by a production relation over sequences; prophetic mutable-value semantics and closure contracts handle mutable borrows and Fn/FnMut callbacks. Creusot translates the contracts to Why3 verification conditions. Sections 3–6 define completed and production predicates, refinement of mutable references, closure pre/postconditions, and Map/MapExt adapters; MapExt carries ghost history for stateful effects. Range, IterMut, Skip, Take, Fuse, Map and MapExt plus clients (counter, decuple_range, hillel, knights_tour) are verified. The production relation intentionally permits underspecification such as hash-table order, requiring client properties to hold for all permitted sequences.
    - number: iterator proofs 0.40–1.06 s
    - number: Map and MapExt 7 manual steps each
    - number: client proofs 0.40–1.15 s
    - number: hillel 0.86 s; knights_tour 1.15 s
    - limits: The framework relies on safe Rust ownership/non-aliasing and first-order Why3 reasoning. Standard Map restricts closure preconditions to history-free ones; MapExt adds ghost state. Several proofs need manual quantifier instantiation or sequence lemmas. Filter, DoubleEndedIterator, ExactSizeIterator, lending iterators and automatic closure-contract inference are future work; this is not a distributed-protocol evaluation.

- Evangelou-Oost contextuality (2023)
    - title: Contextuality in distributed systems
    - what: Defines a lattice of distributed-program specifications as subpresheaves of relative execution traces paired with maximal covers of a finite topological variable space. The lattice order captures refinement and parallelism; associated information algebras define local and global consistency, with global consistency related to sequential consistency. The paper constructs relative traces from nondegenerate simplicial cells of products of variable prosets, proves the trace presheaf is flasque and a tuple system, and derives an ordered adjoint information algebra of relations. It characterizes local consistency by flasqueness and global consistency by an inference/join test. A dining-philosophers specification is locally consistent but globally inconsistent: three singleton traces induce a causal loop in chopstick acquisitions. Isabelle/HOL formalizes several constructions and proofs, while an explicit local-computation reformulation reduces join cost by projecting pairwise overlaps.
    - number: finite variable space
    - number: nine information-algebra axioms (I1–I9)
    - number: three-philosopher illustrative context
    - number: two consistency criteria: local and global
    - limits: The presentation assumes a finite variable set and nonempty per-variable prosets; finiteness simplifies the topology and lattice arguments. Relative traces preserve ordering but intentionally discard absolute timing, so the model does not represent synchronized clocks. Global-consistency computation is generally exponential in variables; the dining-philosophers demonstration omits detailed arithmetic of the final inconsistency. The work is mathematical and does not report measurements on deployed distributed systems.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `evangelou-oost-contextuality-2023`
- Lessons for Interactive Theorem Proving Researchers from a Survey of Coq Users (2023)
    - what: Analyzes the Coq Community Survey 2022, an online survey with 466 submitted responses, using descriptive comparisons and multiple-variable regressions. Regressions restrict to 390 recent users, then remove 18 without age, leaving 372 (367 for IDE satisfaction). It reports associations between experience, learner status, software-verification use, location and Coq tools, interfaces, satisfaction and requested improvements. Experience is associated with tool/library/CI/extraction use; software-verification respondents report more ecosystem-tool use and extraction. Results concern associations and a non-random respondent sample, not causal effects or all Coq users. Regressions first restrict to 390 respondents who used Coq in the last year, then exclude 18 without age, leaving 372 (367 for IDE satisfaction), Romano-Wolf adjusted p-values and a 0.1 threshold. Controls include age, gender, operating system, research area, employment and PhD. Robust examples include more than two years associated with CoqIDE use, North America with Emacs use, more than five years with lower favorite-IDE satisfaction and a larger excess of essential technical over community improvements, and software-verification use with new extraction-target requests. The paper explains why raw responses are withheld for privacy while code, generated plots and aggregated/open-text data are supplied. Figure 3 (p.12:13) ranks “Fixing bugs” and “Documentation” first by majority judgment: 62% of 299 answers and 56% of 342 answers, respectively, mark them “Essential”; question-specific denominators differ. These are respondents’ priorities, not measurements of improvement effectiveness.
    - number: {'submitted_responses': 466, 'main_regression_n': 372, 'ide_satisfaction_n': 367, 'experience_over_2_years': '64%', 'experience_over_5_years': '40%', 'software_verification': '67%', 'learners': '17%', 'extraction_use': '45%', 'ci_use': '31%'}
    - limits: Survey recruitment targeted current or former Coq users through community forums and social networks; respondents self-selected and are not a random sample. Raw response data are not shared because of re-identification risk, so the supplied code cannot fully reproduce all analyses. Comparisons with Stack Overflow/Haskell surveys use different bin definitions and populations; Coq-to-ITP generalization is explicitly uncertain.

- Netrix guided consensus testing (2023)
    - title: A Domain Specific Language for Testing Consensus Implementations
    - what: Presents Netrix, a central networking server plus DSL for programmer-guided exploration of consensus implementations. Filters control message drops, delays, modifications and reordering; monitor state machines express safety/bounded-liveness assertions; residual nondeterminism is explored by PCTCP and tests are replayable. Formalizes protocol, monitor and product transition systems and proves monitor expressivity for complete runs. Instrumentation shims intercept RPC/message APIs; filters and key-value context encode semantic waypoints. Case studies cover Tendermint, Raft and BFTSmart across Go and Java, including regression tests and protocol-coverage measures. Filter-distance analysis predicts when PCTCP alone can find reorderings.
    - number: 34 unit tests total: 15 Tendermint, Raft tests, 8 BFTSmart (§5)
    - number: Instrumentation: 600 LOC added to 150k LOC Tendermint; 120/16k Raft; 150/16k BFTSmart
    - number: Tendermint found 4 new deviations; one liveness bug increased consensus time 30s→6min; fixes acknowledged
    - number: Raft tests found 4 documented bugs; PCTCP alone found none of new Tendermint or known Raft bugs
    - limits: Requires instrumenting communication APIs and serialization adapters; tests explore bounded executions (e.g., MaxEvents=1000, depth=10, 30s iterations), not exhaustive correctness. PCTCP cannot introduce Byzantine behavior and rapidly loses probability on deep reorderings. Findings are implementation-specific scenarios; passing tests do not prove safety or liveness. BFTSmart case found no bugs, possibly reflecting prior testing maturity.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Strauss-Mitsch RSS verification (2023)
    - title: Slow Down, Move Over: A Case Study in Formal Verification, Refinement, and Testing of the Responsibility-Sensitive Safety Model for Self-Driving Cars
    - what: Formalizes longitudinal Responsibility-Sensitive Safety (RSS) as hybrid programs in differential dynamic logic, proves collision safety and minimal safe distances in KeYmaera X, proves deterministic controller refinement and verified compilation to Python, then uses formally derived ModelPlex monitors for testing and debugging. The model template makes acceleration, reaction-time, orientation, safe-distance and evolution-domain assumptions explicit. The same-direction proof uses an inductive stopping-distance invariant; opposite-direction safety uses a shared-responsibility invariant. Optimality is established constructively by choosing worst-case accelerations and showing any epsilon-smaller initial separation reaches collision. A deterministic det-HP controller is proved a refinement of the nondeterministic envelope, and structural induction proves Python compilation preserves reachable states. ModelPlex monitors connect runtime states to formal paths: faulty controllers trigger alarms, and collisions without alarms expose modeling flaws. Simulations explore reaction-time parameter effects after proof.
    - number: four RSS safety/optimality theorems
    - number: reaction-time examples rho=1 and rho=6
    - number: same-direction safe-distance invariant
    - number: det-HP to restricted Python compilation
    - limits: Safety is only for the longitudinal two-car kinematic model, with nonnegative directional velocities, bounded accelerations/decelerations, positive reaction time and initial safe separation; gear changes are assumed to occur only when stopped. The continuous dynamics use ideal kinematics and omit richer tire/friction physics. Python correctness theorem assumes Float=R (or interval arithmetic is needed), and compiled code inherits the restricted det-HP syntax. Testing samples scenarios and cannot replace proof; formal proof of the chosen model does not establish real-world fidelity.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `strauss-mitsch-rss-verification-2023`

    - what: Builds three linked TLA+ specifications for ZooKeeper/Zab: an abstract protocol specification, a detailed system specification serving as a super-document, and a test specification that drives explorative implementation testing. The authors reproduce deep ZooKeeper bugs ZK-2845 and ZK-3911 involving accepted-log inconsistency or committed-log loss, and merged the specifications into Apache ZooKeeper.
