- VLSM Faulty Distributed Systems (2023)
    - title: Validating Labelled State Transition and Message Production Systems: A Theory for Modelling Faulty Distributed Systems
    - what: Introduces Validating Labeled State transition and Message production systems (VLSMs): labeled transition systems with validity predicates on transitions and recursively defined valid states/messages. Composition, validators, local/global equivocation evidence, fixed-set and weight-limited state/message-equivocation models, and Byzantine components are defined in one framework. The paper distinguishes constrained executions (arbitrary environment inputs) from valid executions (inputs producible by the VLSM), defines free/constrained composition and induced validators, and proves state/message equivocation trace correspondence under full-node assumptions. It models weight-limited equivocation by annotating states with observed equivocator sets, then defines Byzantine components that can emit authenticated messages arbitrarily. Theorems 7.1/7.2 reduce fixed or weight-bounded Byzantine behavior to equivocation as observed by validators; examples include multiplication VLSMs, UMO/MO/ELMO observers, and explicit counterexamples showing composition constraints can break equivalence.
    - number: All results and examples are formalized and checked in Coq
    - number: Theorem 6.1 gives two-way trace correspondence for fixed equivocators under full-node assumption
    - number: Theorem 7.1 equates non-Byzantine traces under fixed Byzantine and equivocation behavior
    - number: Theorem 7.2 gives the analogous weight-limited reduction
    - limits: Validity can be undecidable in general (Turing-machine example). Equivalence theorems require channel authentication, sent and full-node assumptions for components; arbitrary composition constraints can make state and message equivocation differ. Byzantine components are restricted from forging sender identities. The work is foundational and Coq-checked, not a performance benchmark or deployed protocol.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Agda abstract HotStuff/LibraBFT safety (2022)
    - title: Towards Formal Verification of HotStuff-based Byzantine Fault Tolerant Consensus in Agda: Extended Version
    - what: Defines an implementation-independent Agda model of HotStuff/LibraBFT for one epoch and machine-checks the core safety theorem (two committed blocks cannot conflict) plus CommitsDoNotConflict′, allowing non-participating clients to verify commits from partial record chains. Implementations instantiate EpochConfig and prove honest-peer assumptions once; liveness and cross-epoch reconfiguration remain outside the abstract proof. Abstracts Blocks, quorum certificates, RecordChains, CommitRule and implementation predicate InSys. The bft-assumption requires any two quorums to share an honest member; thmS5 proves committed blocks lie on one tree path modulo an explicit block-ID injectivity failure. The extended property uses RecordChainFrom and Complete ∈QC⇒AllSent to let clients validate only records needed for a commit. The paper refines non-injectivity to collisions among records actually in the system, linking abstract safety to cryptographic hash assumptions.
    - number: Single-epoch abstract model; no benchmark/runtime numbers reported
    - number: Common quorum instantiation: n>3f Byzantine bound and >2n/3 quorum (§3)
    - number: Agda 2.6-era open-source model/proofs; extended version adds proof details over NFM paper
    - limits: Proofs cover a single fixed epoch and 3-chain commit rule; 2-chain DiemBFT option and epoch changes are future work. Safety is conditional on quorum intersection, honest voting/record-membership assumptions and block-ID injectivity (or an explicit collision witness). Liveness is deliberately not meaningful for the abstract model; concrete implementations must prove handlers satisfy assumptions. No runtime performance evaluation is reported; contribution is machine-checked metatheory and open-source Agda code.

- Cosmos DB TLA+ (2022)
    - title: Understanding Inconsistency in Azure Cosmos DB with TLA+
    - what: # Whole-paper read: Understanding Inconsistency in Azure Cosmos DB with TLA+ (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  A. Finn Hackett, Joshua Rowe and Markus Alexander Kuppe; arXiv2210.13661v1,24October2022,11pages. This is a client-facing behavioral model and industrial explanation case study. It reports390lines of core specification developed over2person-months,24non-type properties checked by bounded exploration/simulation, two documentation issues and an abstract explanation of an Azure outage. The service implementation is represented through expert feedback, without automated conformance checking. [Q0,Q1,Q11,Q19]  ## Model and assumptions  The four conceptual variables are an ordered write log, a universally replicated prefix readIndex, a durable majority-replicated prefix commitIndex, and a failover epoch. Their relationship is readIndex≤commitIndex≤Len(log). Replication nondeterministically increases either prefix; data loss truncates only the non-durable suffix and increases epoch. The log abstracts replica/region arrangements rather than enumerating them. The retrieved source additionally carries the configured write level as an unchanged state variable; the paper calls it a constant. [Q3,Q4,Q5,S1]  One write consistency level is configured for a deployment; individual reads may select an allowed weaker level. The model concerns individual single-key operations. Transactions and optimistic concurrency are outside its scope. Bounded staleness models operation counts, omitting the wall-clock bound. VersionBound limits unreplicated entries for all write levels; StalenessBound separately limits non-durable entries when bounded-staleness writes are configured. These abstractions should not be mistaken for a proof covering arbitrary implementation arrangements merely because the model does not explicitly contain servers. [Q3,Q5,Q14]  A write begins by appending its key/value and obtaining an epoch/checkpoint token. It can later succeed or fail. Failure need not delete the value: the request can have taken effect even if its response was lost. Weak reads can see the value while the write remains pending. Successful strong writes require durability. An epoch change invalidates all old session tokens in this model; authors acknowledge that real data loss need not invalidate every token. [Q6,Q7,Q9,Q18]  GeneralRead(key,index,allowDirty) returns the latest mapping at or before index, or a not-found marker if absent. If dirty reads are allowed, it also permits later matching entries. Strong reads use commitIndex without dirty values; bounded-staleness reads use commitIndex with dirty values; session reads use max(token.checkpoint,readIndex) with dirty values and require a current-epoch or null token. The client updates its token with the maximum prior/read checkpoint. Prefix/eventual reads both use readIndex with dirty values. This last equivalence concerns single reads/writes, not transactions. [Q8,Q9,Q10,Q14]  ## Validation and its limits  Authors report10liveness and14safety properties across4distinct levels, excluding basic typing, using exhaustive logs up to length6 and depth-first random traces up to100steps. Examples include durable committed prefixes, read-your-writes, monotonic reads, staleness bounds, token lifetime and eventual write completion. The paper gives no complete per-property run logs, state counts, runtimes or mapping of every property to each exploration bound. These are bounded model checks and sampled traces, not a parameterized proof or implementation test. [Q11]  The pinned artifact gives useful additional scope. MCCosmosDBProps uses two keys, three values, StalenessBound2,VersionBound3,Len(log)<3 and epoch<3. Its configuration labels several temporal checks “very very slow (1hr+)” and three “overloads TLC (out of memory), probably holds”. SimCosmosDBProps has a finite natural-number replacement0..100 and omits some costly properties. These comments are evidence about the retrieved snapshot, not proof that the paper's historical runs failed; neither do they substantiate completion of every stated check. The history specification assumes weak fairness of the disjunction WriteSuccess∨WriteFail; the simulation specification omits that fairness clause. Thus completion claims depend on the specification and bounds actually used. I did not establish an unbounded per-write progress theorem. [S2,S3,S4]  CosmosDBLinearizability maps strong read results to an atomic dictionary view. A commitIndex jump can correspond to multiple dictionary writes in one abstract step. The saved configuration asks for DictSpec under epoch<3 and Len(log)<4. This supports the paper's model-level refinement argument; I did not independently establish a full invocation/response-history linearizability theorem. The old-model refinement goes from the older model to this newer behavioral specification, so old behaviors are included in the new model. The claimed strictness additionally rests on the newer model admitting data loss and dirty reads omitted before. The retrieved mapping fixes two regions/two clients per region/one write region and constrains history length<5. I inspected the mapping, not its old-model dependency closure. [Q12,S5,S6]  The read hierarchy is checked while holding write consistency fixed and considering all session-token choices. Comparing differently configured write levels does not give the same inclusion: strong writes can admit more pending non-durable values than bounded-staleness writes. The four retrieved adjacent read-refinement modules use fixed write configuration and small finite state constraints; no unbounded theorem proof is attached to their THEOREM declarations. [Q13,S7]  ## Concrete cases and industrial results  The first documentation issue is a claim that prefix reads cannot observe A,C or B,A,C after sequential writes A,B,C. A,C is possible simply by interleaving reads around writes. For B,A, use five replicas: A reaches1,2,3;B reaches1,4,5;one read hits5 and another2 before replication catches up. Each write reached a local majority, yet the client seesBthenA. These are allowed service behaviors, not newly demonstrated implementation failures. Authors report the documentation was rewritten. [Q14]  The second issue concerns regional guarantees. Bounded-staleness operations in one region may appear strongly consistent until a region failover loses or exposes older data; they lack global durability. Authors report their argument was confirmed for individual operations, while regional documentation bullet points remained because transactions distinguish prefix and eventual behavior. The paper's section title about regions must be read with that explicit restriction. [Q14]  The outage affected thousands of subscriptions over28days and was noticed after26days. FrontDoor wrote task data, then asked Worker to read it. Session consistency was configured, but the processes used separate/unconfigured session tokens. Their cross-process interaction therefore had only eventual-read guarantees. A routing change exposed the existing semantic problem through latency. A PlusCal model reproduces a missing read before replication; forwarding FrontDoor's token to Worker removes the modeled error according to the authors. The original postmortem author confirmed the explanation made sense. This is an abstract causal explanation and proposed/rechecked model fix, not evidence that the fix was deployed or that every production trace was matched. [Q15,Q16,Q17]  Other cases show why the model has two-step writes: a weaker read can observe an incomplete strong write; a failed write can remain durable; and a bounded-staleness read under strong-write configuration lacks the separate bounded-staleness write throttle. Although the paper says strong consistency has no bound on in-progress requests, the model still has VersionBound on partially replicated entries; completed replication permits more pending requests. These are distinct quantities. Expert review caught an earlier incorrect atomic-write abstraction, which was rewritten and rechecked in2–3days. [Q18,Q19]  ## Printed text versus pinned source  The displayed WriteCanSucceed on paperp4 checks token validity and, for strong writes, token.epoch=epoch. It omits the checkpoint≤commitIndex condition that the immediately following prose says is required. The pinned CosmosDB.tla includes that durability conjunct. Thus the displayed formula and prose disagree; this does not establish that the checked source permits non-durable successful strong writes. [Q7,S1]  The outage text's expected formula is eventually workerValue="workerValue", but Listing1writes"taskValue". The source's temporal property uses eventually workerValue="taskValue" and its terminal-state property checks the same literal. I treat the printed literal as a presentation mismatch and preserve the intended missing-task-value scenario rather than presenting the printed formula as a valid test of that scenario. [Q16,S8]  These comparisons use a retrieved, pinned repository snapshot, not an asserted reconstruction of the exact2022experiment checkout. No author code or proof/checker was run. [S9]  ## Contribution and reading scope  For this database, the contribution is a reusable specification of a distributed dependency's visible behavior and a concrete demonstration that model correctness also depends on choosing faithful abstractions. Its relevance to LLM-written specifications is my inference: evaluation should test behavior the model must permit as well as properties it must forbid, record expert corrections, and distinguish bounded self-consistency from service conformance. The paper has no Verus, Rust or LLM experiment. [Q19,Q20]  Read all11pages,28reference entries, every displayed definition, Listing1,all4figures and TableI; inspected all11page renders across the preceding and current context. Read the complete core/property/read-wrapper/client/linearizability modules, both PlusCal outage modules, both model-check/simulation wrappers and configurations, four read-level refinement modules and their common-pattern constraints, the general-model mapping/configuration, and two repository READMEs.26static files acquired with hashes and Git blob identities. No old general-model dependency closure or broader implementation inspected. Official and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. No author execution/import, solver, compiler, checker or serialized scientific-results loading.
    - number: 2 person-month development
    - number: 10 liveness and 14 safety properties across 4 consistency levels
    - limits: single-keyindividualoperations;countnotwallclock;alltokensinvalidatedonepoch;boundedchecks;expertconformanceonly
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.13661); [notes: P24. Understanding Inconsistency in Azure Cosmos DB with TLA+](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_3_spec_tools_tla_to_code.md)
    - record id: `cosmos-db-tla-2022`
- Event monads in Isabelle (2022)
    - title: Compositional Verification of Interacting Systems Using Event Monads
    - what: Defines event monads, event systems and Hoare rules in Isabelle/HOL, then composes independently verified handlers into correctness results for distributed summation, a German cache-coherence model and real-time partition scheduling. An event monad returns a set of value/state/output-trace triples and a failure flag, supporting nondeterminism, state updates, signal, bind and while loops. Preconditions and postconditions can depend on traces; an append-only trace frame rule preserves prior output, unlike simply treating traces as arbitrary state. Event systems map events to handlers; unhandled events become environment output, while emitted handled events execute recursively in sequence. Separate inductive reachability and guaranteed-termination predicates support partial/total Hoare rules. Pair and indexed-array lifting rules preserve untouched subsystem properties. The MapReduce model proves that alldone implies the accumulated sum is correct. Cache-coherence proves an invariant ensuring exclusive access for arbitrary N clients. The scheduler case proves watchdog representation refinement, scheduler refinement, their product composition and a final tick-level specification. Both no-switch and next-tick/next-window/next-frame switching cases reuse the watchdog proof.
    - number: 8948 total implementation/example lines
    - number: 1131 foundations; 726 MapReduce; 1261 cache coherence
    - number: 2572 watchdog lines reused by both scheduler versions
    - number: 723 no-switch scheduler; 2265 switching scheduler/system
    - limits: Interactions send events without waiting for immediate replies; all output events may be placed at function end. Handlers and the recursive event-dispatch semantics execute sequentially, so this is not a general concurrent interleaving or network-failure model. Unhandled events are delegated to the environment. Termination must be proved for recursive event chains and loops; MapReduce permits a server to skip progress forever and proves correctness conditional on alldone, not eventual completion. RTOS verification is at design level: parts resemble C, but AutoCorres C translation is not used; the concrete doubly-linked watchdog is abstracted to a list/array of relative deadlines. Case studies demonstrate functional correctness/refinement, not measured end-to-end implementation performance, schedulability for arbitrary workloads, or spatial separation of an entire OS.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `event-monads-in-isabelle-2022`

- Hoare calculus for reconfigurable distributed systems (2022)
    - title: Reasoning about Reconfigurations of Distributed Systems
    - what: Develops a Hoare-style calculus for programs that create/delete components and connectors while finite-state component behaviors continue. A separation-logic-inspired configuration logic uses inductive predicates for unbounded networks; havoc invariants capture interactions in unaffected parts, with an assume/rely-guarantee-style proof system. Defines primitive reconfiguration commands new/delete/connect/disconnect, local programs and frame rule, and havoc semantics allowing loose/tight interactions. Extended regular expressions describe interaction sequences; cyclic proofs establish havoc triples and soundness. Examples prove safety of self-adjustable tree architectures. Automation analysis shows configuration-logic entailment is 2EXP-complete under bounded-degree restrictions, while frame inference remains open.
    - number: No runtime benchmark; contribution is calculus and worked proofs
    - number: CL entailment is 2EXP-complete under stated restrictions (§7)
    - number: Havoc proof soundness established by Theorem 2 (§5.3)
    - limits: The main examples use finite-state event behaviors, ideal networks without packet loss or delays, and exogenous coordination; low-level mutex/CAS coordination is abstracted away. CL entailment is undecidable in general and 2EXP-complete only under syntactic and bounded-degree restrictions. Havoc proof rules require distinctive proof trees and cyclic-progress conditions. Frame inference and broadcast interactions are future work; timed/hybrid behaviors are not modeled.

- On the Correctness of Speculative Consensus (2022)
    - what: # Whole-paper read: On the Correctness of Speculative Consensus (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Jelle Hellings, Suyash Gupta, Sajjad Rahnama and Mohammad Sadoghi; arXiv2204.03552v1,7April2022,25pages. This is a detailed paper proof for Proof-of-Execution (PoE), a Byzantine state-machine replication protocol that speculatively executes before committing. Its main contribution is the connection between early client replies, preservation across view changes, eventual consensus and recovery of client results. The proof is mathematical prose and pseudocode, not a machine-checked development. [Q0–Q3,Q5–Q10]  ## System and required conditions  There are fixed replicas R, Byzantine replicas F, and clients C. The paper writes n=|R|,f=|F|,nf=n−f and assumes n>3f, authenticated communication and deterministic transaction execution. Byzantine replicas can coordinate and clients may all be malicious. Most exposition assumes publicly verifiable digital signatures; digest variants need cryptographic hash assumptions, and threshold variants need threshold signatures. This paper defines f from the fault set; it does not separately explain how deployments configure an upper bound used for quorum thresholds. [Q1,Q11–Q14]  Weak consensus means decisions never conflict, while termination and forcing new decisions require periods of reliable communication with some unknown bounded delay. The advertised asynchronous recovery therefore does not give unconditional progress during arbitrary loss or delay. Assumption4.6 bounds proposal size and the number W of prepared but uncommitted rounds. This makes a single finite message-delay estimate applicable to protocol messages. Timeout estimates must eventually grow sufficiently large. The displayed backoff argument also requires a positive initial estimate; multiplying zero cannot make it positive. [Q1,Q6,Q8]  ## Normal execution and recovery  The primary in view v has id=v mod n. A signed client request is proposed in a round. Each honest replica prepares only one proposal for that round/view and broadcasts Prepare. A certificate collects nf matching preparations. Replicas execute speculatively after all preceding transactions have executed and send Inform to the client. The client needs nf identical, view-specific Inform replies as a proof of execution. The familiar f+1 reply threshold for committed execution is insufficient here because speculative executions can be rolled back. Theorem4.1 establishes same-view uniqueness and normal-case service with a good primary, reliable communication and identical execution history. [Q2,Q3]  The printed Theorem4.1 contradiction proof defines T_i=S_i minus Byzantine replicas, but then treats S_i as if all its members were honest and as if those full sender sets were disjoint. Byzantine senders can vote for both proposals. The usual intersection argument applies to T_i, the honest subsets, instead. This is a notation defect in that displayed proof, not a demonstrated counterexample to same-view uniqueness. [Q3]  The four-replica failure examples distinguish omission by a faulty primary, a false accusation by a faulty backup, and network loss. Other replicas can observe the same messages in all three cases. A malicious primary can leave some honest replicas without a proposal while the client still receives a proof of execution using Byzantine replies. Recovery therefore must preserve what a client could already have observed, rather than treating an isolated replica's missing proposal as evidence that no execution occurred. [Q4]  Check-commit is decentralized. An honest replica broadcasts its prepared certificate only after execution, while still in the view, and after all previous rounds have committed. Receiving a valid certificate lets a missing replica prepare and execute. A commit certificate collects nf CheckCommit messages. Theorem4.4 relates a client proof to eventual preparation at every honest replica, relates one honest commit to eventual commits/client proof given the shared prefix, and derives that a commit certificate entails nf−f honest executions plus commitments for earlier rounds. The first two statements assume reliable communication and all honest replicas in the view. Out-of-order proposals are supported; the proved base still commits in order. [Q5]  View changes use three stages. A replica that detects failure rebroadcasts Failure. Receiving f+1 alerts induces an honest replica to join; nf alerts make it stop the old protocols and send ViewState to the next primary. State includes the latest commit certificate and every subsequently executed prepared certificate not rolled back. The next primary collects nf states and broadcasts NewView. Under reliable communication with delay δ, Lemma4.5 synchronizes entry into the proposal stage within2δ; Lemma4.7 delivers a good primary's new-view proposal within4δ of the first such entry. [Q6]  The new ledger retains the committed prefix through LC, then the latest-view prepared request in each round through LP. Missing committed history is queried; speculative requests outside the selected ledger are rolled back; the new primary must repropose selected uncommitted requests before new rounds. Theorem4.10 inducts over views to preserve any request executed by nf−f honest replicas, including every request with a possible client proof or commit certificate. Assumption4.8, uniqueness of the request named by commit certificates for a round, is a temporary proof hypothesis subsequently discharged by Corollary4.11. It is not an additional final assumption. [Q6,Q7]  Lemma4.12 supplies QueryCC/RespondCC recovery of missing committed history. Lemma4.13 and Theorem4.15 use increasing timeout estimates and eventual good primaries to establish resumption once communication is reliable. The worked δ=30ms example starts estimates7,3,5,1ms; three doublings yield56,24,40,8ms, and the four replicas need3,4,3,5 doublings respectively. Remark4.17 suggests a practical cap such as10seconds, but the theorem's unbounded-growth argument only carries to a cap when that cap exceeds actual reliable-network delay. [Q8]  Theorem4.18 combines preservation, recovery and client-request forwarding to prove weak consensus. Honest clients repeatedly send an unanswered request to all replicas; replicas forward it or trigger view changes if it is not served. Suggested defenses against malicious clients include per-client forwarding limits and treating another request by the same client as evidence of a timely proposal. These are stated heuristics, not a quantified load-admission/fairness analysis. Recoverable non-Byzantine crashes require certificates in persistent storage; Remark4.19's recovery claim depends on enough replicas recovering. [Q9]  Consensus alone does not guarantee a client obtains nf identical replies from one view. Example4.20 partitions honest replicas into A,B,C of sizes f,f,1 and alternates unreachable groups across three views. The same request becomes decided, but view-specific replies and commit certificates remain split, so no ordinary proof of execution forms. The repair is InformCC: after an honest replica commits, it can send the view-independent round/result to a retrying client. Now f+1 matching InformCC replies suffice because at least one is honest and committed execution is preserved. Theorem4.21 proves service when communication is reliable. Client request order is not automatically enforced; waiting for the first result or validating per-client counters supplies an extra application policy. [Q10]  ## Complexity, variants and evaluation  Figure1 compares seven protocols/variants; Figure12 gives13 message/certificate rows and three size variants. These are analytical accounting, not measured experiments. Base PoE takes two replica communication rounds before execution and three through check-commit; Linear-PoE takes three and five. The table's C parameter is request size, so expressions containing nC account for payload, not just the number of messages. Off-by-one terms are intentionally omitted in Figure1. Base prepared certificates have size O(nf+C); commit certificates O(nf²+C), because different replicas may attach different prepared certificates. ViewState and NewView additionally depend on W. [Q1,Q11]  Digests make most messages independent of request size but require explicit retrieval of missing request bodies and certificates. Replacing a certificate-carrying CheckCommit with a constant-size message requires f+1 matching senders before querying an honest holder. It does not eliminate the bounded-request-size assumption. Threshold signatures reduce certificates to constant size; by themselves they do not reduce all-to-all message counts. [Q12,Q13]  Linear-PoE sends preparations through the primary. Its decentralized commit replacement rotates aggregator q=round mod n, commits a digest of the last n prepared certificates, and uses a separate n:(f+1) threshold scheme for recovery certificates before n:nf commit certificates. This prevents a fixed faulty aggregator from permanently hiding committed history. The pseudocode waits for commitments through round−n and covers the preceding n−1 rounds with the resulting certificate. Failure detection still broadcasts quadratically. The authors' belief that such communication cannot be eliminated in their recovery setting is not an impossibility proof. The linear variant is explained by construction; it does not receive a separate full formal refinement proof here. [Q14]  The MAC-only variant is a proposed redesign of the two new-view stages by analogy with PBFT; the paper does not print that redesign. Out-of-order processing means proposing future rounds within W while earlier decisions finish. It does not remove the sequential execution dependence or the proved base's in-order commits. [Q15,Q16]  The n=31,C=10KiB,M=256B bandwidth example reports322KiB for the base primary and315KiB with linear aggregation, approximately2percent savings. Direct substitution gives322.5KiB and315.7258KiB, about2.10percent. The primary still transmits the full proposal to every replica. The accompanying “roughly-1/3-th increase in latency” is inconsistent with the stated increase from two to three communication rounds: relative to two, that is50percent. This is a reader arithmetic observation, not an execution measurement. [Q14]  Example5.6 assumes n=31,δ=15ms and1Gbit/s and prints22 sequential versus3028 out-of-order decisions/second. The22 figure follows1/(3δ)=22.22. The3028 figure is inconsistent with the printed bit/byte units: using10kB and256B gives about386.95 decisions/second; using10KiB gives378.51. The value3028.1 is obtained with10KiB if1Gbit/s is treated as10⁹bytes/s. Either consistent byte interpretation still gives a bandwidth-only upper estimate, excluding computation and other bottlenecks. The paper's two-orders-of-magnitude statement is therefore not supported by its stated inputs. [Q16]  Section6 compares PBFT, Zyzzyva, SBFT, HotStuff and MinBFT using communication rounds and simplified costs. It qualifies Zyzzyva/SBFT fast paths, assumes PBFT-like checkpoint costs for SBFT, and identifies trusted hardware/reliable communication for MinBFT. The HotStuff discussion and33 decisions/second example are this paper's account of a particular chained scheduling model, not an independently validated universal bound on HotStuff deployments. Its prediction that PoE outperforms PBFT “in all situations” exceeds what round counts alone establish: computation, rollback/recovery workload, implementations and resource bottlenecks are not measured here. [Q17–Q22]  The introduction/conclusion claim an “analytical and experimental” evaluation. This saved25-page version contains analytical comparisons and hypothetical numeric examples, with no experimental setup, implementation measurements, repeated trials or measured plots. Its earlier EDBT2021 paper is reference18; results from that separate paper are not imported into this record. No proof assistant, model checker, Verus, verified Rust, TLA+ or LLM-agent experiment is reported. [Q0,Q17–Q24]  ## Relevance and reading evidence  For distributed verification, this contributes detailed proof obligations for speculative client observations, view-change preservation, the distinction between agreement and delivered service, and communication/recovery tradeoffs. My inference for proof-agent evaluation is to check eventual-reliability and bounded-window conditions, quorum intersections, temporary proof hypotheses, persistent crash state and units in analytical evaluation separately. A successful local proof script would not by itself establish any of those model-to-implementation connections.  Read the complete25pages,40references,all15numbered figures including pseudocode and both tabular figures,all printed proofs/formulas/examples,and all25page renders. Official and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. No implementation or artifact source was linked as part of this paper's proof/evaluation and none was inspected here. This record does not audit the related ResilientDB implementation or preceding EDBT paper. No author execution/import, compiler/checker/prover/solver run or serialized scientific-result loading.

- SMR synchronizers and PBFT-light liveness (2022)
    - title: Liveness and Latency of Byzantine State-Machine Replication
    - what: Specifies an SMR synchronizer with application-controlled advance calls, supplies an O(n)-view-variable implementation tolerant of pre-GST message loss, and uses its interface to prove PBFT-light atomic-broadcast liveness and derive recovery/good-case latency bounds. The proceedings also describes extensions to periodic PBFT leader rotation and a HotStuff-like variant. Figure 1 separates Monotonicity, Validity, Bounded Entry, Startup and Progress. Figure 3 retains only each sender’s highest WISH and uses the (2f+1)st and (f+1)st order statistics plus periodic retransmission; it enters a view only when these agree. Bounded Entry holds eventually with d=2δ, provided no correct process requests a higher view during the entry interval. PBFT-light uses delivery and recovery timers, increasing both only on expiration. Lemma 6 shows that a correct process with recovery timeout >6δ and delivery timeout >4δ cannot initiate the first advance in a sufficiently late good view. Theorem 7 uses this to rule out executions where only a subset of correct processes increase timeouts indefinitely, then proves every valid broadcast by a correct process reaches all correct processes. Reliable DECISION retransmission also ensures dissemination of any already delivered value. Good-case delivery takes 4δ from a correct broadcaster, corresponding to 3δ from the leader. The analysis distinguishes unknown per-execution δ from a known conservative Δ used only for the latency bounds.
    - number: n=3f+1; quorums 2f+1; f+1 correct advance requests suffice for startup/progress
    - number: O(n) stored view variables; eventual entry skew d=2δ
    - number: good-view timer thresholds: dur_recovery>6δ; dur_delivery>4δ
    - number: Theorem 8 deadline: GST+ρ+max{ρ+δ,6Δ}+4Δ+max{ρ,δ}+7δ under its stated assumptions
    - limits: n=3f+1, at most f Byzantine processes, authenticated point-to-point communication, signatures and collision-resistant hashing. Partial synchrony: unknown GST and δ; before GST messages may be lost/delayed and clocks drift unboundedly; after GST correct-process delays are ≤δ and clocks track real time. Correct clients invoke advance at most once between new_view notifications. Correct broadcasts are valid and unique. PBFT-light uses signatures and omits checkpoints/watermarks; reliable DECISION broadcast requires retransmission omitted from pseudocode. Latency results additionally assume known Δ>δ, timeout caps 6Δ/4Δ, retransmission period ρ and reliable-broadcast latency ≤δ+ρ. Theorem 8 assumes sufficiently large timeouts already at GST and a correct leader(V); Theorem 9 assumes synchronous startup, correct leader(1), timeout bounds and the view-1 optimization. Full synchronizer proof, PBFT safety proof, portions of timer proof, latency proofs, periodic-leader and HotStuff protocol details, and textbook-bug details are explicitly deferred to arXiv:2202.06679, which was not read in this batch. Theoretical proof, not a machine-checked artifact or implementation performance experiment.

- TLA+ verification trifecta (2022)
    - title: Specification and Verification With the TLA+ Trifecta: TLC, Apalache, and TLAPS
    - what: Uses a TLA+ specification of asynchronous termination detection and Safra's ring algorithm to demonstrate complementary verification with TLC explicit-state checking, Apalache bounded/SMT and inductive-invariant checking, and TLAPS theorem proving, including refinement from the algorithm to a high-level state machine. The high-level module specifies active nodes, pending messages, termination detection, safety, liveness under weak fairness, and quiescence. Safra's algorithm adds white/black colors, message counters and a circulating token; termination requires an inactive node 0, no pending messages, white token/node, and zero accumulated counter. TLC finds 4,097 states for N=4,K=3 in the abstract model and 1.3 million (N=3,K=C=3,Q=9) or 219 million (N=4 with same bounds) in the algorithm model, exposing state explosion and a token-color bug. Apalache checks bounded prefixes, proves an inductive invariant for N=100 in 20 seconds, and checks quiescence for N=100 in 11 seconds. Refinement is checked for small N; TLAPS proves type, safety, liveness, and refinement for arbitrary instances, with an invariant proof taking about 230 lines and liveness/refinement about 245 lines. The conclusion recommends TLC first, Apalache for inductive invariants, TLC/random exploration for liveness, then TLAPS for higher confidence.
    - number: 4097 states for high-level TLC N=4,K=3
    - number: 262145 states for high-level N=6,K=3
    - number: 1.3 million states for Safra N=3,K=C=3,Q=9
    - number: 219 million states and about 50 min for Safra N=4
    - limits: The abstract model assumes a nonzero finite N, natural pending-message counters, asynchronous actions and weak fairness only for DetectTermination; stronger fairness is optional but unnecessary for the stated liveness. TLC requires finite bounds on pending messages/counters for exhaustive checking, so finite checks cannot establish arbitrary executions. Apalache handles bounded prefixes and inductive invariants but needs type annotations and human-discovered invariants; it cannot directly handle the fairness temporal property. TLAPS proof effort is manual and backend-dependent. The Safra case is a ring algorithm and does not evaluate throughput, deployment behavior, or other distributed topologies.

- DSS (2021)
    - title: Detectable Sequential Specifications for Recoverable Shared Objects
    - what: Defines detectability by transforming an ordinary sequential object specification into a detectable sequential specification (DSS). Per-process abstract state remembers a prepared operation and its response. prep-op announces the operation, exec-op performs it, and resolve reports whether it took effect and its response; ordinary operations remain available without detection. Section 2 gives the state transformation and four operation axioms. prep-op and resolve are total and idempotent, while exec-op requires the matching prepared operation with no recorded response. An auxiliary parity bit can distinguish repeated identical operations. DSS is combined with a separate concurrency correctness condition and leaves nesting recovery to the application. Section 3 derives a queue from the Michael–Scott/durable queue: tagged per-thread pointers record operation preparation and completion, CAS and helping maintain the linked list, and Appendix A supplies a centralized recovery scan. A variation replaces centralized recovery with individual recovery. Theorem 1 states lock-freedom and strict linearizability, but the article omits the analysis and detailed model. Section 4 measures failure-free queue throughput on real Optane memory.
    - number: up to 20 threads on 20 cores
    - number: 30-second runs, ten runs per point, standard deviation <2% of mean
    - number: non-detectable DSS nearly 3× detectable DSS throughput at low parallelism
    - number: MS up to 1.5× non-detectable DSS at two threads
    - limits: A recovering process must retain its identifier across crashes to query its previous operation; this conflicts with the fresh-identifier model underlying durable linearizability. DSS specifies sequential behavior and requires a separate concurrent correctness condition. The queue uses asynchronous shared memory, persistent memory, volatile caches, explicit flushes and system-wide crashes. Correct nesting is an application responsibility. The correctness theorem is stated rather than proved in this article. Experiments cover failure-free runs, use debug compilation at O0 and sequentially consistent atomics, and do not measure crash recovery latency. The log queue comparator is the authors’ implementation and could be optimized further.

    - title: Metastable failures in distributed systems
    - what: Names a failure pattern in which a trigger (for example a short network outage) pushes a system into a bad state that a feedback loop (for example client retries) keeps going after the trigger is gone.     - number: Link imbalance example went unexplained for more than two years; fix was a single line in the connection-pool policy

- Specifying Message Formats with Contiguity Types (2021)
    - what: Introduces contiguity types, a formal-language layer for self-describing binary messages with records, arrays and guarded alternatives. Defines semantics over byte strings and a matcher computing field slices. Proves termination, substitution reconstruction and soundness in HOL4; completeness is not proved. Extends the matcher and proofs with empty languages and tagged lists. Applies filters/parsers to OpenUxAS LMCP messages. The formalism treats a base contiguity type as a set of strings of fixed width rather than a set of program values. Array lengths and alternatives can read earlier message fields through L-values; partial expression evaluation yields an empty language. The matcher returns an assignment from L-values to byte slices, enabling filters and test generation. Recursive list encodings handle finite nested structures but recursive first-order terms require mutual recursive constructions. The UxAS application adds semantic assertions for GPS ranges, bounded optional arrays and enumerations; filters and parsers were instantiated and tested with the UxAS simulator. The HOL4 model is supplied as supplementary material. Assertions should precede arrays so limits are checked before allocation. First-order terms with nested recursive term lists are a challenge needing more elaborate mutual recursion, rather than an already verified general construction.
    - number: {'application': 'LMCP messages in OpenUxAS; payload and key-value arrays bounded at 8 and 32 in shown example'}
    - limits: Messages are contiguous byte arrays/strings; interpretation functions map leaf byte strings to numbers/booleans. Expression evaluation can fail when a referenced L-value is absent; the presentation omits error-handling details but HOL4 formalization models option failure. Current matcher is interpreted and copies or indexes slices; compilation to CakeML and a correctness proof are future work. Application testing reports successful simulator tests, but no size, speed or fault-detection measurements are given. Matcher soundness and reconstruction are proved; completeness has not been tackled. Tagged-list extension verified, generalized nested-recursive term handling needs further work.

- Config2Spec: Mining Network Specifications from Network Configurations (2020)
    - what: Config2Spec mines every policy in a chosen finite policy language that holds for all link-failure environments allowed by a model. It intersects policies inferred from sampled forwarding states with a candidate set, then uses grouped symbolic control-plane queries to verify remaining policies or produce counterexamples. Two runtime predictors choose between sampling and verification; policy-aware sampling, topology cuts and prefix grouping reduce work. A handwritten argument establishes exactness and termination conditional on exact terminating component tools and correct environment enumeration. Evaluation measures runtime, query reduction and specification size. Records the candidate/specification/verified invariant, universal failure semantics and finite policy-language boundary, complete predictor equations/initialization/smoothing, randomized unseen-environment requirement, dominator/shortest-path inference, SMT query polarity, fixed-link topology reduction, full algorithm/correctness argument, qualified forced-down environment-count concern, all table cells, parser/model edits to Internet2, and numeric discrepancies in summary claims.
    - limits: §§1/2: The mined specification describes behavior already enforced by the configuration, including mistakes or unintended restrictions. It does not discover operator intent or certify that existing behavior is desirable. An independent intended policy remains necessary to identify bugs; mined policies can support regression checking or refactoring only within the chosen semantics and failure scope. §2: Policy language includes reachability(r,p), isolation(r,p), waypoint(r,w,p), loadbalancing(r,p) on at least two paths. All policies means all instantiations of these supported templates, not arbitrary temporal/security/performance specifications. No convergence, transient update, quantitative bandwidth, timing or distributed liveness property is mined. §2: Failure model partitions links into forced-up, forced-down, symbolic; k bounds total down links including forced-down ones. Each concrete environment fixes all links and must satisfy that partition and count. Policy holds only if true in every admissible environment. Need consistent inputs k≥|Ldown|; empty-environment/vacuity handling not developed. Link-failure scope does not imply arbitrary router/software/Byzantine failures. §§3.2/Appendix A: Maintain verified⊆true specification⊆cands. Exact data-plane inference on a genuine admissible sample removes only false universal policies; sound complete control-plane query adds true policies or removes those violated by witness. Equality yields exact result; exhausting all genuine environments permits verified=cands. Proof assumes both tools model same configuration/policy semantics and their own results are correct. Appendix A: Termination relies on finite policy/environment spaces, PickCE always returning a previously unseen admissible environment, and every nonempty verifier query either proving policies or returning a real witness violating at least one. All component calls must terminate. No handling of solver unknown, timeout, parser errors or conflicting tool models is included in this theorem. Random sampling alone does not prove finite bounded runtime. Appendix A Algorithm1 line5 candidate listing error: totalEnvs=Σj=0..k binom(|Lsymbolic|,j), but §2 defines k as total failures including forced-down links. Correct admissible count uses k−|Ldown|. Example one forced-down link, one symbolic link, k1: one admissible environment, listing counts2; exhaustion branch may never trigger and PickCE can be asked for nonexistent unseen environment. Source §7 correctly subtracts forced-down count for topology trimming. This is a printed algorithm concern, not an executed artifact failure; experiments use empty forced sets. Appendix A Algorithm1: No explicit TopoTrim call or moving-average history appears despite §§3/4 implementation description; line25 divides by a set rather than its cardinality in print. Initialization calls verifier ten times and can classify policies before first data-plane sample, even though performance discussion says k1 only DPA. Read listing as schematic; exact code correspondence not proved. §4: Time-per-policy estimator=last runtime/newly classified policies, with infinity on zero DPA progress; remaining-time estimator=per-call time×unseen environments or unclassified policies. DPA selected when either estimator prefers it. Initial DPA times0, ten verifier policy sets initialize verifier averages, last ten calls smooth times. Heuristic choice affects cost, not exactness if underlying invariants/progress hold; no optimal scheduling bound. §5.1: Policy graph edge weight counts unclassified policies using a previous forwarding graph. Weighted random failed-link choice prioritizes many dependencies but rerouting can preserve them. Deterministic heaviest-link selection observed to oscillate; random fallback returns an unseen environment. Need legal fixed-link/count enforcement and finite unseen selection; no guarantee weighted sampler always needs fewer samples. §5.2: Batfish computes forwarding states, one graph per equivalence class of destination prefixes sharing forwarding. Dominator tree includes routers with at least one destination path, yields reachability and mandatory waypoint policies; isolation is inferred when reachability absent. Precise path quantification and treatment of ECMP branches/loops/drops must match verifier. Paper does not prove Batfish/Minesweeper correspondence for all supported policies. §5.2: Load balancing inferred from multiple equal-cost shortest paths. This is path multiplicity, not balanced byte counts, per-flow hashing fairness, disjoint paths or guaranteed throughput. Text says shortest paths in the network after describing forwarding graphs; ACL/route-policy-aware interpretation must be supplied by implementation, not assumed from raw physical connectivity alone. §6: Query formula φnet∧φfailure∧¬(∧policypredicates). UNSAT certifies whole group; SAT witnesses at least one failed policy, not all. Remove only actually violated policies; remaining ones unclassified. Group maximal same-prefix policies, arbitrary prefix choice. Claimed always at least as good follows query-count classification argument, not a wall-clock guarantee because larger conjunction can cost more or time out. §7: TopoTrim removes forced-down links, reduces residual budget k, replaces each forced-up edge with k extra parallel copies, then computes(k+1)-edge-connected components. Lack of required reachability/connectivity prunes reachability/load balancing and waypoint endpoints. This is necessary topology feasibility, not sufficient routing correctness. Requires compatible undirected link-failure model, prefix attachment and nonvacuous waypoint semantics; isolation is not trimmed. §8: Implementation5KPython/Java uses Batfish and Minesweeper extended with waypoint/load-balancing. Feature limits/parser bugs/SMT encoding become trusted boundaries. No mechanically checked framework proof, verified parser or proof of executable implementation. Tool-specific precision is assumed by mathematical argument rather than independently established through differential correctness evaluation. §8: Evaluated NetComplete-synthesized OSPF/BGP configs on three topologies and2/4/8/16prefix path-ordering specs, five config sets/type/topology. Large-network waypoints supplied by operator, simulated with random20%routers. Output completeness therefore relative to selected waypoint domain, not all possible waypoint devices. Not a benchmark of recovering independently authored human requirements. §8/Table2/3: Reported values are averages across generated configurations; fractional policies are averaged counts, not actual fractional policies. Candidates count after first all-symbolic-up sample, not entire template space. Increasing k shrinks true-policy set for same config; the paper does not explain why first-sample candidate averages differ across k. §8.1/Table2 discrepancy: source repeatedly claims OSPF completion within2.7hours, but USCarrier k2 OSPF cell10528.4s≈2.925h. BGP49151s≈13.653h agrees13.7. Source trend k1→2→3 describes averaged rows; time can decrease at more permissive failure bound because trimming eliminates nearly all policies. No monotone-runtime guarantee. §8.2: Baseline comparison only OSPF grids20/25/30routers because baselines do not scale to large topologies; five config sets, k1–3. At k2/3 DPA speedup mean10.2/max41, CPV mean3.8/max8.3. At k1 plain DPA wins by mean24/max37seconds due verifier initialization. Large-topology runtime is not direct large-topology measured baseline speedup. §8.3/Table4: Sampler comparison ten OSPF/BGP config runs per topology,k3; weighted policy-aware generally prunes more, but random transitions earlier in6/30runs due stalled pruning, not necessarily better results. More expensive sampling22.1–358.2ms versus random.5–1.4ms; overall DPA1.4–57.8s. AddedSamples>500 is censored, not precise mean. Candidate percentages use first sample as denominator. §8.3: Query reductions measured against one-query-per-policy baseline at first transition, not runtime speedups. BICS k2 text51.1%trim/41.5%group yields7.4%remaining arithmetically, whereas text/plot give9.6%; 90.3%overall reduction versus9.6%remaining differs rounding/definitions. USCarrier text1.15%trimremaining differs from the plotted trimming result; the text and plot relationship is unexplained. The final0.04%groupremaining is retained as a source claim. §8.4: Internet2 real May2015 configs modified before analysis: remove parsing/logging/anonymization/BFD lines and unsupported community/empty-prefix-list route-map matches. Authors say internal-prefix-only/noexternalpeers makes latter irrelevant, but no independent semantic equivalence proof. >90Klines,10routers/18links is small topology with large config text, not158routerproductiondeployment. §8: VMs32GBRAM/12vcores@2.3GHz. No exact CPUmodel, sourceversions/commits, solverlimit/unknownpolicy, seeds, standarderrors/confidenceintervals or per-run maxruntime provided. Timings and simulation/SMT outcomes measure performance under component assumptions, not tool code correctness. §§1/9: Operator anecdotes motivate need but no controlled user study of usability/specification review, bug detection accuracy or deployment benefit. CEGIS analogy concerns enumerating all valid finite policies with examples/oracles, not a learned or LLM-generated intent specification. No Verus/Rust/TLA+ or LLM agent result.

- FRET (2020)
    - title: Formal Requirements Elicitation with FRET
    - what: FRET lets users write requirements in a restricted English called FRETish. Each requirement has scope, condition, component, timing and response fields. FRET turns them into temporal formulas, explanations and diagrams. It also has a simulator for trying out signals.
    - number: industrial autopilot case; no large quantified benchmark
    - limits: The translation is checked against the language's own semantics, not against what the customer meant. Mapping variables to the model is a separate source of error. New users need help with subtle points of meaning.

    - record id: `fret-2020`
- Giskard specification (2020)
    - title: Specification of the Giskard Consensus Protocol
    - what: The paper gives a rigorous executable-style specification of the Giskard consensus protocol used by PlatON, defining nodes, epochs/views, message formats, local/global block stages, state transitions, and fault behavior. It proves three safety properties: same-height prepare blocks in one view are identical; same-height precommit blocks across views are identical; and same-height committed blocks are identical. The proofs reduce quorum intersections and view-change height relationships to contradictions under the one-third Byzantine bound. The specification is intended as an implementation and formal-verification reference, while liveness is left open.
    - limits: The network is partially synchronous and nodes communicate by authenticated broadcast; at most one third of participating nodes are Byzantine. Epoch/view changes can be asynchronous across nodes, and Byzantine behavior includes proposing multiple same-height blocks or voting without required messages. Safety reasoning focuses on message/block stages and does not establish liveness, performance, implementation correctness, or cryptographic assumptions beyond authenticated signatures and quorum behavior. The protocol has ten-block views and VRF-selected epoch participants as modeled in the specification.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2010.02124)
    - record id: `giskard-specification-2020`
- Igloo refinement bridge (2020)
    - title: Igloo: Soundly Linking Compositional Refinement and Separation Logic for Distributed System Verification
    - what: Introduces Igloo, an Isabelle/HOL framework connecting compositional event-based distributed-system refinement to code verification in expressive separation logics. Sound refinement and trace inclusion let protocol proofs transfer to heterogeneous Java/Python implementations verified by VeriFast and Nagini. The framework proves composition and translation theorems for event systems, I/O specifications and process implementations. Case studies cover leader election, primary-backup replication and two-party authentication, including fail-stop servers, TCP, UDP, cryptography and unbounded component instances. Isabelle formalizes all definitions/results; code-level abstraction functions connect model states to locked heaps and message bytes.
    - number: Three complete case studies
    - number: Primary-backup proof uses nine invariants for backup consistency
    - number: Java and Python implementations verified with VeriFast/Nagini
    - number: All definitions and results mechanized in Isabelle/HOL
    - limits: Correctness depends on explicit environment/fault assumptions (perfect failure detection, channel guarantees, Dolev–Yao abstraction) and trusted library contracts/parsers. Implementations are manually written Java/Python; extraction is not automatic. Security bitstring cryptography is abstracted through a surjective abstraction function and trusted cryptographic libraries.

- Leaderless SMR ROLL limits (2020)
    - title: Leaderless State-Machine Replication: Specification, Properties, Limits
    - what: Defines leaderless state-machine replication using possibly cyclic dependency graphs, separates committing from stabilization/execution, and decomposes decision into dependency discovery plus per-command consensus. Claims ROLL tradeoff 2F+f−1≤n between failure tolerance f and fast-quorum size n−F under Reliability, Optimal Latency, Load Balancing. For protocols on its parameter frontier, every chain length k has some failure-free 2-asynchronous run with a live dependency chain of length k. A five-site Google Cloud experiment illustrates EPaxos tail latency increasing with contention, unlike Mencius; proofs in conference paper are sketches with formal treatment deferred. Records graph phases, stable agreement modulo aborted dependencies, batch execution order, DDS visibility/weak agreement, exact ROLL quantifiers, quorum partition proof, skyline examples and chain induction. Separates empirical tail/correlation observations from existential worst-case chains, nice runs from synchrony, decision from execution, and stated abstract properties from a machine-checked implementation. Replaces earlier record that lacked later sections.
    - limits: Fixed n≥2 message-passing processes, fail-stop rather than Byzantine faults, deterministic replicated state machine and unbounded command/log space. Model extends asynchronous wait-free computation with failure detectors; precise detector admissibility/fairness assumptions are deferred to [22]. Do not read Reliability as solving consensus in pure asynchrony without such an assumption. Clients are not modeled; replicas proxy submissions, and different replicas may submit the same command. Conflicts safely overapproximate semantic noncommutativity, considering both final state and response values; relation is symmetric and nonreflexive. Distinct nonconflicting commands may execute in different orders. Classic logs append immutable slots; Generic SMR uses acyclic partial logs with incoming-prefix closure and compatible upper bounds. Safety properties are distinct from termination and real-time/client-response contracts; no complete client refinement proof printed. Leaderless graph maps each command to pending⊥, aborted⊤, or a committed dependency set; phases pending/abort/commit/stable/execute. Aborted commands are removed from dependency sets permanently. A committed command is stable only when no command in its transitive dependency closure remains pending. Stability agrees on stable dependencies globally; conflicting committed commands must have at least one direct dependency between them, allowing cycles rather than a prechosen total order. Algorithm1 executes the largest still-stable subset of a stable command’s closure, in reachability order between components and canonical total order within a cycle. Already executed dependencies need not execute again. Invariant1 excludes an unexecuted dependency outside an earlier batch; Invariant2 says if conflicting c precedes d within a batch then c is in d’s transitive dependencies. It is not simply arbitrary SCC iteration: batch includes eligible dependency predecessors too. Abort pruning, stable-versus-execute status and canonical order must be made explicit in a formal implementation. Theorem1 states Generic SMR reduces to Leaderless SMR without messages, with proof deferred. Direction is an implementation/refinement claim, not equivalence of arbitrary cyclic dependency states with Generic acyclic logs. Ordering all commuting commands to recover Classic SMR would require extra communication. The common dependency graph itself is not yet an agreed total execution log. Algorithm2 calls DDS.announce(c), returning dependency set D or abort⊤ and fast flag b; slow path invokes per-command consensus, then records and broadcasts outcome. Coordinator or a process suspecting it may submit/recover. A nice run has no failures and a perfect failure detector, so only the coordinator calls that command’s consensus. Leaderless does not mean no coordinator: each command has one, with no single primary ordering all commands. DDS Visibility: two conflicting nonaborted announcements include each other in at least one returned set. Weak Agreement: if one announcement of c returns a nonaborted set with fast=true, every other announcement of c returns a nonaborted set; differences contain only commands whose every announcement aborts. Thus raw dependency sets may differ, but aborted differences are removed before stable agreement. Theorem2 claims Algorithm2 correctness, proof in [22]; consensus, detector, and DDS are assumed abstract services, not implemented or verified here. Examples: rotating coordinators preassign order and always use slow flag; Clock-RSM uses timestamps and majority wait, propagates clocks for stabilization; Mencius aborts unannounced prior slots and needs everybody for fast flag; EPaxos example omits sequence numbers, unions observed conflicts, uses spontaneous agreement, and falls back/recovery to a majority; Caesar timestamps and waits/rejects inconsistent higher-timestamp conflicts. These are explanatory decompositions, not verified full protocol implementations or safety audits of recovery rules. Reliability states every submitted command eventually decided at every correct process when at most f failures. This is decision, not by itself a quantitative execution-latency guarantee. Surrounding prose inconsistently says after f failures no progress or less than f; retain the explicit inclusive property. Stable termination of dependency closures and outcomes of submissions by faulty coordinators require the detailed model. Footnote3 discusses reconfiguration/persistent recovery informally, outside the fixed fail-stop proof. Optimal Latency: in every nice run, announce returns after two message delays with nonaborted D containing only previously announced commands; if no concurrent conflicting command, fast flag true. With contention it may be slow-flagged despite two-delay DDS completion, and complete execution may wait arbitrarily longer. Load Balancing: any quorum Q containing coord(c) with |Q|≥n−F can serve its announcement in a nice run. This is freedom of quorum choice, not an empirical guarantee of equal load or geographic latency. Theorem3 ROLL assumes those properties and an available pair of noncommuting commands. Bound 2F+f−1≤n is necessary, not sufficient to construct a protocol. Together with majority-style bounds f,F≤floor((n−1)/2), skyline means no simultaneous parameter improvement. For fixed f the algebra gives quorum q=n−F≥ceil((n+f−1)/2). Do not substitute arbitrary fast-consensus quorums: ROLL quorums must contain that command’s coordinator. Proof sketch assumes violating inequality, partitions P1,P2 of sizeF−1 and Q of size n−2F+2. CAP premise 2F<n gives two coordinators p1,p2 in Q; fast quorums Q1=P1∪Q−p2 and Q2=P2∪Q−p1 have size n−F. Solo nice runs decide empty dependencies. Concurrent run delays cross messages then crashes Q; integer violation implies |Q|≤f. Survivor cannot distinguish carefully spliced runs, forcing empty dependencies for both conflicting commands, contrary to Visibility/Consistency. Detector-history equivalence and eventual stability are essential deferred proof details; the conference sketch invokes Reliability (defined as decision) to obtain stability without fully showing that bridge. Printed consequence that a plain majority fast quorum tolerates at most one failure is not implied by its displayed inequality. For odd n=2m+1, q=m+1 permits f≤2; for even n=2m, q=m+1 permits f≤3, subject also to f≤floor((n−1)/2). Its own n5(F,f)=(2,2) example gives majority q3 with f2, contradicting the unqualified consequence. This is a printed inference conflict, not a demonstrated implementation or refutation of Theorem3. Frontier examples: n5 only(F,f)=(2,2), n7 (2,3),(3,2); EPaxos attains first n7 tuple, Atlas offers(3,1). Paper calls EPaxos/Alvin optimal for n=2f+1, Atlas for f1 and even n or n3. Fast quorum floor(3n/4) for EPaxos needs integer rounding; nearby f+(f+1)/2 notation is unrounded for even f. Table1 rotating-coordinator quorum0 should not be treated as a legal coordinator-containing fast quorum; protocol lacks Optimal Latency and uses no fast DDS path. Table1 marks Caesar as having Optimal Latency, but §7 explicitly says Caesar lacks it because even contention-free commitment may need two round trips. Keep this internal classification conflict rather than certifying either claim from the unread Caesar paper. Table1 has Clock-RSM and rotating coordinator without Optimal Latency, Mencius with it but nonoptimal frontier; EPaxos/Alvin/Atlas with stated frontier conditions. Historical protocol optimality/current best claims are not checked beyond this2020paper. Chain c1…ck means ci∈deps(ci+1), so dependencies run toward the first element. §5.1 defines live as first command not stable at any process; §5 introductory overview instead says last, which is inconsistent. With pending d and deps(a)={b},deps(b)={a,c,d}, chain dba blocks a; c alone stable. A live finite chain does not assert an infinite run, inevitable starvation, or every execution having a long chain. k-asynchrony bounds overlaps: if any message overlaps both first and last messages of a causal message path, path length≤k. Overlap is real-time interval overlap, not Lamport independence of endpoint events. Formal definition is universal over all messages/paths, unlike introductory prose saying existence of a path. 2-asynchronous is not a fixed bound on wall-clock message latency, delay ratio, or clock skew; nice means failure-free/perfect detector, not lock-step synchronous. Theorem4: for every finite k>0, there exists a 2-asynchronous nice run of any ROLL-optimal protocol with live chain sizek. This is a worst-case existence theorem, not a probability distribution, expected latency, or measured percentile bound. Conference statement does not give a numeric command-latency formula in milliseconds or require every command to suffer. Full proof in [22]; conflict-pattern/termination assumptions belong to that detailed model. Chain sketch splits each announcement into request-send S, intermediate M, reply-return R. Induction constructs complete nice σi with no pending messages, chain ci…c1, and interposed Γprime whose paths have size≤1 and whose participating overlap with Qi is Pi of sizef−1. Alternate quorums and indistinguishable coordinator views force next dependency. Truncating final replies makes the chain live. Figure4 gives n5,k7 and quorums{p1,p2,p3}/{p3,p4,p5} with intersection{p3}. Exact general quorum/induction case analysis deferred; sizek need not mean k different replicas. Experiment: Google Cloud, five sites South Carolina, Finland, Canada, Australia, Taiwan (leader),128 closed-loop clients each, no-op service, synthetic key conflicts atρ=0,2,5,10,30percent for EPaxos; Paxos and Mencius baselines. Latency submission-to-execution at steady state, not commit acknowledgment. Each operation picks common key42 with probabilityρ, unique otherwise; ρ is hot-key choice probability, not directly fraction of conflicting random command pairs (under independent choices that pair probability isρ²). No-op operations commute semantically, so key conflicts are an intentional safe overapproximation. Figure1 CDF shows EPaxos tails increasing with synthetic contention and Mencius lower median; paper reports latency/batch-size correlation>0.7. No named correlation estimator, sample count, duration, repeated-run uncertainty, machine types, protocol versions, or fault injection specified in this conference paper. No exact quantiles tabulated; graph readings must not be invented. Correlation plus worst-case theorem supports a mechanism, not measured causal attribution of every tail sample or general workload performance. Mitigation discussion: pure writes may return at commit or durable f+1 receipt, but footnote5 requires later reads to wait for all preceding writes to preserve linearizability. Claimed read dependency exclusion assumes cited per-read single-process execution scheme, not arbitrary reads. Waiting for prior announcements can still allow chain of sizen by rotating coordinators, stated without proof here. These are tradeoffs, not new verified algorithms. ROLL Optimal Latency/Load Balancing enforce request-reply form with no pending messages at announcement completion. Weaker latency permitting background messages can sidestep the bound in a fully synchronous model by sharing replies among quorum members. Discussion says partially synchronous version retains ROLL and chaining requires more asynchrony. Thus theorem is relative to its formal properties, not a universal impossibility for every system called leaderless or every weaker fast-path definition. Entire supplied17-page paper now read, including previously missing §§4–8. Theorems1–2 proofs and formal versions of ROLL/chaining deferred to arXiv2008.02512. No machine-checked proof, Verus/Rust/TLA+ specification, LLM involvement, artifact audit, experiment rerun, or independent verification of underlying protocol papers. Citation[4] about dependency-set decomposition names an unrelated local-independent-set approximation paper in bibliography; original intended source cannot be recovered from that reference here.
