- Modeling the Raft Distributed Consensus Protocol in LNT (2020)
    - what: # Whole-paper record: Modeling the Raft Distributed Consensus Protocol in LNT (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Hugues Evrard, MARS 2020, EPTCS 316, pp.15–39, DOI 10.4204/EPTCS.316.2; arXiv 2004.13284v1. Read all 25 PDF pages, §§1–5, the 16 references, complete Annex A model and Annex B skeleton/reliable network. Inspected all 25 rendered pages and read the entire released LNT model and README statically. Exact passages, source lines and PDF/printed pages are in quotes.json and combined.quote-source.txt. No author program, compiler, model checker, prover or solver was run.  ## What the paper establishes (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  This is a Raft modeling and experience paper. Its stated goal is “to discuss the process of developing the formal model of a distributed system using LNT” (§1,Q2). The model covers leader election and log replication, separates server and network processes, and illustrates CADP state-space development. It reports a concrete size comparison between reliable and unreliable networks. The complete printed model makes its abstractions inspectable.  The paper does not enumerate checked Raft safety/liveness formulas or report their truth values. It gives no parameterized correctness proof or implementation refinement theorem. The abstract attributes Raft correctness to “a manual proof, based on a TLA+ specification” (Q1), and §1 says that proof was presented by the Raft authors (Q2). Thus the LNT model and CADP availability should not be recorded as a new complete Raft verification result.  The paper reports two discrepancies in the original TLA+ specification: an unintended prime on matchIndex in AdvanceCommitIndex and a missing candidate-to-follower transition after a same-term leader heartbeat (§3.7,Q20). It says the former “made no practical difference” and the latter “did not impact Raft manual proof.” These are the author's assessments, not independently checked proof conclusions. The pinned historical TLA+ source credits Evrard for the apostrophe correction (Q55). That pinned snapshot already contains the candidate stepdown branch (Q54); we did not reconstruct the earlier faulty revision.  ## Assumptions and represented behavior (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The configuration fixes the number of servers and bounds terms and total client interactions, “otherwise the state space would be unbounded” (§3.2,Q9). Printed and released defaults are three servers, three terms and three client interactions. The sole measured comparison instead uses two servers, one term and one interaction. Changing server count also requires changing instantiated processes and the array range (§3.3,Q10; Q41–42). The quorum is the strict majority `(MaxServerID div 2) + 1` (Q41).  The scope is “the core of the protocol, i.e. leader election and log replication”; dynamic membership and log compaction are omitted (§3,Q6). Client actions carry only the leader ID. “We do not need to model client commands, or server responses to clients” (§3.4,Q11). Entries contain only terms (§3.6,Q14; Q44). This cannot establish distinctions between different same-term commands or full service/client response behavior.  The network can reorder, drop a message on send, or deliver while retaining a message for duplication (§3.5,Q12–13; Q43). The declared buffer is a set, despite the name msgBag. Identical pending messages collapse to one element. There is no explicit capacity bound or delivery fairness condition. The reliable alternative removes loss and retain-after-delivery branches, but still permits arbitrary message selection and provides no fairness guarantee (Annex B.2,Q40). Its smaller state space supports development under fewer network behaviors.  Timeouts are nondeterministic alternatives without elapsed time or randomized duration (§3.6.1,Q15; Q45). At the term bound only the election alternative stops; receiving and other alternatives remain possible. A candidate votes for itself directly, reducing self-message actions relative to the compared TLA+ model. Lower-term messages, including requests, are discarded; the author says this is safe, but supplies no equivalence proof (§3.6.2,Q16).  The disrupt branch permits a crash between LNT actions, including midbroadcast (§3.6.4,Q19). It ends in Crash(self), after which the server process terminates; no reboot/recovery transition appears (Q47). This is a process-level crash model, not evidence about interruption at arbitrary implementation instructions. Leaders accept client interactions, append a term entry, and replicate on a later timeout (§3.6.3,Q18). AppendEntries carries zero or one entry, enforced by an assertion (§3.6.2,Q17; Q49).  ## Concrete evaluation and cases (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  For two servers, one term and one client interaction, the unreliable network yields 11,862,015 states and 74,821,042 transitions. The reliable network yields 22,311 states and 108,176 transitions (§4.3,Q23–24). Dividing the supplied counts gives about 531.67 times fewer states and 691.66 times fewer transitions. The paper describes the reductions as “three and two orders of magnitude smaller, respectively.” Preserve its wording alongside the exact counts; the computed ratios are reader arithmetic.  No timing, hardware, tool release, repeated trials, per-property results or experiment logs are reported for this comparison. The archive contains only README.txt and raft.lnt, with no property files or reproduction script. The downloaded source uses different syntax from the print, including alt/select and card/length; exact textual identity or the exact experimental checkout is not established.  A reliable network is a development proxy. The paper says these state spaces “are not relevant for a proper verification” and that an issue found there also appears with the unreliable network (§4.3,Q24). This supports preserving finite bad witnesses under the stated behavioral inclusion, not an unrestricted transfer of liveness or arbitrary formula truth.  Figure 1 is a syntax example: two calculator operations, 1+2=3 and 3×4=12, can occur in either order in a four-state/four-transition graph (PDF p.4/printed p.18,Q8). It is not a Raft performance experiment. There are no tables.  The DLC/Consul comparison is explicitly attributed to §6 of prior thesis [2]. For 1000 commands and three servers the paper cites 2.3 seconds for DLC versus 0.5 second for Consul, and reports prior interprocess TCP communication under half a millisecond (§4.6,Q28–29). Consul batches commands while DLC sends a round for each command, so this is not an isolated measurement of formal-method overhead. The thesis and implementation were not independently evaluated here.  ## Static discrepancies requiring care (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Three local source cases qualify use of the model as a reference specification. All appear both in the visually inspected appendix and released LNT source. These are reader-created symbolic valuations; no reachable execution or protocol safety violation has been established.  - Same-term candidate: reception sets Follower only when `mTerm > currentTerm`; the AppendEntries handler receives no state argument. A Candidate at term 1 processing a valid term 1 empty heartbeat remains Candidate (Annex A.5/A.6.3,Q35–36; released source Q46/Q49). This disagrees with the paper's Raft overview that “other candidates step down to the follower state” (§2.2,Q4), and resembles the upstream omission discussed in §3.7. It does not establish that the earlier TLA+ defect causes a safety failure. - Failed replication response: `nextIndex[mFrom] := max(1 of nat, nextIndex[mFrom])` has no decrement. A local value 3 stays 3 (Annex A.6.4,Q37; Q50). This disagrees with the §2.3 description that the leader retries “with a lower index” (Q5). We have not established a reachable failed replication trace or measured its effect. - Follower commit assignment: `commitIndex := mLeaderCommit` lacks a cap by local log length or a monotonic guard. With a locally empty log, previous index 0, empty entries and leader commit 1, the handler leaves an empty log with commitIndex 1 (Q36/Q49). Reachability is unknown. The pinned TLA+ source also directly assigns the leader index and says an old duplicate request can decrease it (Q54). This may be an inherited abstraction; do not label it a newly discovered LNT safety bug.  The symbolic cases are retained in static-witnesses.json. Their purpose is to distinguish the source's actual expressions from the prose and from unproven invariants, not to substitute for model checking.  ## What this adds to the research topic (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The paper contributes an explicit distributed consensus model, reusable network/node structure, evidence that network assumptions strongly affect state-space size, and a concrete account of translation exposing specification discrepancies. It also explains the gap between a model and implementation: the Distributed LNT Compiler generates C but its synchronization protocol assumes reliable interprocess communication (§4.6,Q28). Discussion of Verdi is attribution to related work, not an implementation correctness result from this paper.  The state-space advice is qualified. Fixed broadcast order is justified informally by arbitrary receive order. The author warns that removed paths may be “irrelevant to a given verification property, but crucial to an other” (§4.4,Q26). Bypassing duplication or outdated messages is proposed as future work requiring “formal verification or proof” (§4.5,Q27), rather than a proved reduction method.  For evaluating LLM-written specifications and proofs, the reader-derived lesson is to inspect the represented state, bounds, message semantics, source/prose agreement, property statements and implementation link separately. The three static cases show why a plausible protocol narrative and a formal-language artifact do not by themselves establish the intended transitions. The paper supplies no Verus, verified Rust or LLM experiment.
    - limits: Fixed finite servers, bounded terms and total client interactions; defaults3/3/3 versus measured2/1/1. Core election/replication only, term-only entries and leader-ID client actions. Finite-set network reorders/drops/duplicates without capacity or fairness guarantee. Nondeterministic untimed timeouts, crash termination without recovery. Informal reductions and source/prose discrepancies qualify reference-model use.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- NetSMC: A Custom Symbolic Model Checker for Stateful Network Verification (2020)
    - what: NetSMC checks temporal policies on models of stateful network functions with persistent tables and one packet traversing the network at a time. It translates a restricted LTL language into CTL and represents sets of network states as existential first-order formulas. Backward preimages and a containment algorithm adapted from database query containment avoid general solver costs. A packet-order argument gives equivalence to interleaved executions for selected policies under an order-preservation condition. Benchmarks compare VMN, a Z3 containment variant, NuSMV and Cubicle; author-run exercises inject configuration mistakes into real network functions. Records exact NF/packet transition semantics, commutation and order-preservation conditions, restricted temporal operators and full appendix proofs, EFO preimage and containment construction, proof/implementation boundaries, printed listing/formula concerns, every Table2 timing and the narrow scopes of speedup, planted faults and model fidelity.
    - limits: §§4/8: A fixed topology and NF rule set; packets contain modeled header/application fields, not arbitrary payload processing. State is a collection of finite key-value maps; tests use specified finite domains, updates assign modeled expressions or increment/decrement by constants, expressions may choose nondeterministically from a domain. No multicast/broadcast, averages, generic computations, clocks/timeouts or direct network failures. Failures require separate enumerated scenarios. Integer/counter bounds and overflow semantics are not developed into an implementation-level finite-state proof. §§4.1/4.3/AppendixA: Exactly one matching NF rule; sequential commands are atomic at NF network-step granularity. A link preserves packet and tables. Drop/Exit permits an arbitrary next packet at arbitrary ingress, retaining all tables. Correct rules/topology/initial tables/model extraction are trusted; sequential packets do not mean tables reset between packets. Nondeterministic backend choice abstracts a concrete policy rather than verifies its implementation. §4.3/AppendixC: Every packet eventually leaves or drops; transient loops allowed, indefinite loops excluded. Unique immutable IDs distinguish packets. Interleaved model buffers unordered sets, not FIFO queues, and proof considers finite closed traces with no unfinished nonterminal packets. No general liveness/fairness or live controller-update verification. AppendixC/Definitions4–5/Lemma1: Non-conflict means swapping two same-NF processing steps preserves both output packets and final table valuation for every relevant initial valuation. A strict total packet order must be respected wherever packets conflict. Adjacent inversion swaps decrease an out-of-order index until packets can be serialized with identical per-packet located-packet traces. Conditions are assumptions on networks; no checker that establishes them for arbitrary input models is evaluated. §5/AppendicesB/D: Policies universally quantify outer variables; basic predicates inspect fields/location/tables. Permitted temporal forms are basic, F basic, within-packet eventual f basic, X(guard→formula), G(guard→formula), and within-packet g(guard→formula). f/g expand to restricted strong-until formulas terminated by Drop/Exit. General until, arbitrary quantifier alternation, generic temporal nesting/past properties excluded. A positive answer for arbitrary policies under serialization does not establish concurrent correctness. §5/AppendixD/Theorem7/Corollaries2,8: Conditional equivalence explicitly covers isolation, tag preservation and tag-based isolation on order-preserving networks. Other policies such as flow affinity/NAT consistency/service chaining are checked under the one-packet model and may miss interleaving bugs. Do not read Table1 sound/conditionally-complete labels as unconditional verification of all concurrent deployments. AppendixD qualified proof-scope concern: Theorem7 syntactically uses basic guards without explicitly excluding table predicates, although Lemma1 preserves only located-packet input/output traces, not every intermediate table valuation while a packet waits. Reordered unrelated processing can alter such valuations. The printed transfer argument therefore needs a restriction to predicates determined by those traces or an additional preservation proof; isolation/tag examples avoid this issue. This is a reading concern, not an executed counterexample against the tool. §6.2: EFO state formulas are disjunctions of conjunctive clauses with membership, inequality, location/field equality and table lookup. Variables are existential and outermost; denotation is a set of concrete packet/table valuations. This is a symbolic representation, not sampling of packet histories. §6.3/Algorithms1–2: Preimage unions NF-rule, link and new-packet predecessors. Commands processed in reverse order; tests conjoined. New-packet transition drops successor field/location constraints but preserves their variable/table relationships, restricts successor ingress and predecessor Drop/Exit. Table updates split equal-index overwritten entry versus an unequal index retaining old value. Helper expression translation F and link routine are omitted; precise implementation behavior is not reconstructed from pseudocode alone. §6.4/Algorithm3: Interpret clauses as conjunctive queries; homomorphism maps variables of candidate containing clause into contained clause. Inequalities require splitting equality patterns into atomic clauses; finite domains need emptiness checks (e.g., singleton variables forcing incompatible table values). Every nonempty atomic clause must map to some disjunct of target. This avoids a naive implication query to Z3, while still potentially expanding many clauses. §6.4/Theorem3 and AppendixE: Authors state exact True iff satisfaction under one-packet semantics. Appendices provide detailed semantics, trace reordering, policy transfer and structural LTL→CTL arguments (Theorem9/Lemmas10–12); no separate complete proof of Algorithm1/2 preimages, Algorithm3 domain splitting/emptiness, or Python refinement appears. Theorem3 is not a machine-checked verified implementation. Algorithm2/p188 printed-listing concerns: loop binds β_j on line14 but line15 passes β_i, and line21 recursive COMPUTECLS call omits expression-environment arguments present in its signature. Figure11/p196 sequence rule leaves c2 as a pending command without giving the corresponding continuation rule in the displayed semantics. Treat as incomplete/inconsistent presentation; no artifact tested. AppendixF/p200 printed-policy concerns: NAT consistency formula contains p without the displayed outer binder (only ∀i), despite closed-formula requirement. Flow affinity shown for S1 needs analogous instances for other servers. Conditional reachability uses G(loc=B→f(loc=A)) from the same arrival state at B, so without distinguishing request arrival from new reply injection it can demand that the current packet return to A; the prose speaks of packets sent afterwards. Qualified formula/specification boundary, not a measured tool failure. §7: Python prototype,20core2.8GHz/128GB server. CPU model/OS/Python/solver versions, source LOC, repeated-run statistics, confidence intervals and memory usage not reported. Alembic-generated NF models and manual POX translation used; authors assert high fidelity, but no formal extraction/refinement theorem or measured model-fidelity error rate is provided. §7.1/Figures6–8: Synthetic host/rule scaling and extended fattree/Ai3/Sprint topology isolation checks; flow-space sliced for both tools. Specific speedups depend on policy/model and baseline. VMN lacks service-chain policy support, so policy-complexity comparison uses only NetSMC/Z3. NuSMV fixed16-entry table and NetSMC without that size constraint are different encodings; Cubicle750× lacks detailed absolute timing/setup in text. §7.2: Red team authors2/3 inject secret deleted/modified rules; blue team author1 diagnoses with NetSMC, then rechecks fixed model. Four described real-NF exercises and nine modeled Table2 scenarios all succeed as reported; no production-discovered fault count, exhaustive fault set, detection rate, false-positive rate or independent user study. All benchmark/tool outcomes here are authors reports, not reproduced.

- VeriBetrKV (2020)
    - title: Storage Systems are Distributed Systems (So Verify Them That Way!)
    - what: VeriBetrKV verifies a single-threaded crash-safe B-epsilon-tree key-value store by combining ordinary Hoare code proofs with TLA-style state-machine refinement of program plus asynchronous disk environment. Reusable crash-safe/cache templates and transposition split journal and tree reasoning. A Dafny linear/shared extension coexists with dynamic frames and a trusted C++ backend; case studies measure reduced proof annotations and competitive but uneven performance. Records disk acknowledgment/corruption/sync contracts, full refinement decomposition and disjoint-region condition, linearity/BoxedLinear trust and borrowing rules, checkpoint/journal architecture, proof-latency discipline and its empirical caveats. Saves complete code/proof tables and printed benchmark values, including contradictory speedup and latency prose. Separates a general asynchronous-system method from an actual multi-node or CPU-concurrent implementation.
    - number: Table 1: 1210 spec, 6373 implementation, 44685 proof lines; the crash-safe map spec is 283 lines
    - limits: §§1–3: Safety and functional correctness only; operations need not terminate. Implementation is single-threaded, with asynchronous I/O/pipelining but no CPU concurrency. Demonstration has one processing node and one disk. Multi-node/multi-disk/heterogeneous generalization is conjectured, and shared-memory thread concurrency is future work. IronFleet has distributed/liveness results, but those are prior work, not VeriBetrKV results. §2.1: User trusts a 283-line top-level dictionary/crash contract, environment/OS/runtime API specifications, build system, modified Dafny/verifier/Z3, C++ backend/compiler/toolchain and hardware behaving as modeled. Smaller specification size may aid review but does not prove the specification matches intent. The linear typechecker changes and BoxedLinear primitives are trusted rather than a separately proved compiler/type-system soundness theorem. §§2.1,3.1: The I/O bus can reorder requests/responses but cannot duplicate or drop them except during crash. The composed StorageIOSystem nondeterministically takes a program step, disk/environment step or whole-system crash. Crash resets volatile program state; acknowledged writes survive, while unacknowledged addresses may contain old, new or corrupt bytes. Arbitrarily many crashes at arbitrary modeled points are covered, including reboot/recovery states. §3.1.1: Disk corruption can occur at any time but cannot produce a valid-checksum block. CRC32C is trusted and referenced by the disk model. Invalid checksum aborts the current query, preserving no-wrong-answer safety at the cost of availability. A malicious disk that fabricates valid checksums, checksum collisions or stale valid blocks violates the assumed model. This is not an unconditional CRC integrity guarantee or fault-tolerant recovery from arbitrary corruption. §§3.1.2,6.2.3: Without crash/corruption the visible state is a dictionary with latest writes. Crash reverts the entire dictionary to a consistent prior snapshot no earlier than the last completed sync. Spec has ephemeral and persistent states; background steps can advance persistence. sync_start/sync_end ensure that at end, persistence includes the state at start or a newer version. Unsynced operations may survive or disappear as part of a consistent snapshot; this is not independent per-key arbitrary rollback or durability of every returned insertion. §3/§6.2: Hoare reasoning verifies synchronous local implementation handlers against the program machine. Environment behavior and crashes are handled by IOSystem refinement. General real-system theorem is conditional on the actual executable, trusted API interactions and environment matching those models. TLA-style refinement is encoded/proved with Dafny, not reported TLC model checking or proof of arbitrary storage behavior. §§3.2,6.2.2: Map→abstract B-epsilon tree with infinite-map nodes→finite pivot-bucket tree separates key semantics from representation. A hypothetical purely in-memory implementation is shown gray and is not an actual second implementation. Generic CrashSafe<T>/BlockCache<T> lifting says a refinement of node/tree contents lifts through a crash-safe cache/disk composition. §6.2.3 Figure 3: Tree IOSystem and JournalIOSystem independently refine CrashSafe<Map> and JournalViewMap. CompositeViewMap applies journal operations to map snapshots. ConcreteCache adds byte marshaling; Disk<byte> refines a partitioned typed disk. Transposition rearranges program/environment components only with an invariant that tree and journal access mutually disjoint disk regions. Their models cannot simply be separated if their effects overlap. §6.2.4: Executable handler invocations refine Next transitions of ConcreteCache; I/O labels are no-op, request or response and must match trusted API actions. Proving code-to-model is insufficient without the enclosing disk composition, while proving abstract crash safety is insufficient without this implementation connection. Recovery does not write disk (§8.1), avoiding a separate write-during-recovery proof burden without excluding repeated crashes. §4: Developer policy stops feature work when verification exceeds 20 seconds, then controls definition visibility, opacity/reveal and solver instantiation profiling. The discipline reduces search burden but cannot decide arbitrary verification, distinguish all timeouts from defects, or guarantee a fixed bound. Measured final passing definitions are not a distribution of failed proof attempts or controlled developer productivity. §5.1: linear means non-aliased mutable values and shared means temporarily borrowed aliased immutable values. Static checking forbids duplication and silent discard and restricts shared values to borrow scope; explicit free is allowed. Functional sequence semantics permit in-place C++ mutation without SMT heap-aliasing reasoning. Ordinary heap references still use dynamic frames, and can be held inside linear structures. §5.1 Figure 2: Trusted BoxedLinear<A> embeds a linear value in a freely aliased heap object. Take requires Has() and sets it false; Give requires no stored value. Borrow is restricted to pure functional callers so an imperative method cannot remove ownership during the borrow. SMT proofs establish Has() where ownership is not syntactically obvious. Thus the method combines type safety with proof obligations rather than enforcing all aliasing rules in a simple Rust-like typechecker. §5.2: Backend maps immutable datatypes to structs, heap arrays/classes to reference-counted objects and immutable sequences to shared storage plus offset/length. Linear sequences use in-place operations and explicit deallocation without reference counts. Mathematical integer executable code is rejected; refined machine integers must fit C++ types. Compiler and runtime correctness are trusted. §§6.1.1–6.1.2: Uses 2 MiB nodes on HDD, 128 KiB on SSD, fanout 8. Serialized lower nodes avoid conversion, while the root uses a mutable search tree for single insertions. A ghost per-index lseq_has sequence permits taking disjoint subsets of linear children during splits. Claims about Rust needing unsafe/Option are the paper authors’ comparison, not a general impossibility theorem about all safe Rust implementations. §6.1.3: LRU BlockCache tracks inter-block references and garbage collection. Persistent/frozen/ephemeral copy-on-write trees represent last completed checkpoint, checkpoint in flight and live updates. Logical-to-physical indirection uses linear-probing hash table; example 1 TiB disk requires 24 MiB table. Two alternating counter-tagged superblocks select the latest checkpoint. Correctness depends on modeled write completion and checksum semantics. §§6.1.3–6.1.4: Checkpoint writes dirty nodes, then indirection table, then a superblock. At checkpoint start dirty shared nodes become write-back-before-editing, preserving the frozen snapshot. A node write must be issued before modification but need not complete before modification; crash can recover the old checkpoint. Logical journal records each insertion; sync persists journal instead of repeatedly writing large nodes. Checkpoint reclaims journal space. §7.1: Table 1 implementation proof/executable ratio is about 4:1; including refinement proofs gives 7:1. These annotation counts are a proxy for final proof burden, not person-time, maintenance cost or specification adequacy. Authors observed no runtime correctness/data-loss bugs but did observe liveness/performance bugs; no duration or exposure denominator establishes a general failure rate. §7.1: Hash-table proof LOC 1678→1063 (about 37% lower), search-tree 2220→1531 (about 31% lower), with search-tree executable LOC increasing 289→373. This compares two author implementations and supports reduced annotation burden for those components, not a universal productivity effect. RocksDB test/production ratio 0.99 and BerkeleyDB 0.45 are not directly comparable correctness guarantees. §7.1 Figures 4–5: 98.3% final proof units under 10 seconds, 99.0% under 20; 37 exceed 20 seconds and 54% of those involve dynamic frames. Hide 308 definitions (18%), reveal 1403 times; 52 never revealed and 74% revealed at most five times. Visibility counts both overestimate and underestimate manual effort. Whole verification takes 1.8 CPU-hours, 11 minutes on 32 cloud machines, not 32 CPU cores in one machine. §7.1 reader qualification: Printed sentence says maximum verification time dropped 10s to 32s and 99th percentile dropped 1.3s to 4.8s. The endpoints increase in that order and conflict with dropped; the PDF visibly contains this wording. Do not silently reverse endpoints or claim a quantified speedup from this sentence without correction from source authors. §7.2: HDD/subcomponent runs use AWS d2.xlarge with four hardware hyperthreads, 2.4 GHz Xeon E5-2676 v3; SSD i2.xlarge has four hyperthreads, 2.5 GHz E5-2670 v2 and SATA SSD. All YCSB systems limited to one core and 2 GiB RAM; load 10 million operations/about 5 GiB, run phases 10000 operations. Reported medians and min/max over six runs, not confidence intervals. §7.2.1 Figure 6 reader calculation: Printed HDD Load medians are BerkeleyDB 233, VeriBetrKV-DF 5649, VeriBetrKV 5579 and RocksDB 44670 ops/s. Ratios from linear VeriBetrKV are about 23.9× faster than BerkeleyDB and 8.0× slower than RocksDB, matching abstract 24×/8× but not body/caption over 25× or caption about 9×. Preserve numerical evidence rather than reconcile by guessing different runs. §7.2.1: VeriBetrKV is slower than both other stores on most queries and roughly 4× below RocksDB, but HDD uniform U has RocksDB 35 versus VeriBetrKV 57 ops/s. SSD Load has VeriBetrKV 8986 versus BerkeleyDB 6664 and RocksDB 72645, about 1.35× faster/8.1× slower; body says 4× slower on SSD, inconsistent with printed load values. Workload-specific outcomes matter more than a universal performance claim. §7.2.1: Authors attribute slowdown to effective-cache reduction from fragmentation/conservative cgroup allocation, lack of overlap between CPU and flush I/O, and 1.5× more I/O than RocksDB, about half extra from checkpoint policy. Simulated 1.8 GiB cache exceeds 13K HDD insertions/s and matches 300K I/Os for 1M queries. These simulations/profiling attributions are not a measured optimized implementation. Query I/O usually 1.5 MiB versus RocksDB 4–8 KiB; future partial-node marshaling is unimplemented. §7.2.2: Microbenchmarks compare dynamic frames/linear on 64M random 64-bit-key hash operations and 8M search-tree pairs. Linear hash inserts and queries are faster but deletes slower; search inserts slower and queries faster. Authors suspect reference-count and reconstruction overheads; no isolated causal experiment is supplied. Dafny type extension is 1900 LOC (3%) and C++ backend 3100 (7.5%). Appendix A: Artifact Docker images include source, modified compiler, verification/performance scripts and graph generation. Any x86/x64 CPU with HDD/SSD, 20 GiB disk and several hours expected; source YCSB/config included. External proof bodies, compiler changes, durability implementation and benchmark scripts were not audited or replayed by this reader. No LLM-agent evaluation is present.

- ByMC Blockchain BFT Verification (2019)
    - title: Formal Verification of Blockchain Byzantine Fault Tolerance
    - what: Surveys blockchain-consensus vulnerabilities and formally verifies two Red Belly Blockchain components with the ByMC threshold-automata model checker: binary-value broadcast and a DBFT-style Byzantine consensus variant. The authors provide executable threshold-automaton specifications and temporal properties for safety, validity, agreement, and termination. The paper explains the manual conversion from pseudocode to threshold automata, including shared counters, guards, fairness assumptions, and counterexample debugging. BV-broadcast uses n>3t asynchronous reliable channels and four correctness properties; its 116-line specification verifies in under 40 seconds on a laptop. The larger consensus automaton models two asynchronous rounds and is checked under round-rigidity/fairness with MPI; five properties sum to 1046 seconds. The paper stresses that formal verification covers specified automata, not implementation correctness, and reports six blockchain vulnerabilities including new Casper FFG and HoneyBadger counterexamples.

    - number: ByMC verification of properties over reachable threshold-automaton states

Manual translation can contain errors, and verified properties do not guarantee the blockchain implementation or other execution tools are correct. The large run uses a 64-core AMD Opteron machine and is not a proof of scalability to arbitrary deployments.

- Deconstructing Stellar Consensus (Extended Version) (2019)
    - what: # Reading: Deconstructing Stellar Consensus (Extended Version) (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  García-Pérez and Schett's arXiv 1911.05145 v3 (13 December 2019) gives a mathematical account of Stellar's ballot protocol. Its main construction separates federated voting, an idealized abstract consensus protocol and a concrete protocol that compresses voting statements. Read all 43 pages, eight main sections, Appendices A–E, four algorithms, four figures, all numbered arguments and 16 references. The paper has five theorem statements, four corollaries and 44 lemmas. All 43 page renders inspected through 11 contacts; pages 5, 12, 23, 29, 30, 33, 36 and 37 individually. The paper supplies mathematical proofs and illustrative executions, not a mechanically checked development or an evaluation of deployed Stellar code.  The model fixes a finite universe V. A correct node follows the protocol; an honest node may additionally stop; a malicious node may deviate otherwise. Every pair has an authenticated perfect link. Partial synchrony provides a finite unknown GST after which message delays are bounded; correct timers have bounded disagreement over a given delay after GST. Trust configuration is an input to the proofs. Open membership motivates federated quorums, but this model does not prove dynamic membership changes, trust-list discovery or correctness of a complete blockchain (§§1–2).  A federated Byzantine quorum system (FBQS) assigns each node a nonempty family of slices, each containing that node. A nonempty set is a quorum if every member has a slice wholly within it. Two correct nodes are intertwined when any quorum containing the first intersects any quorum containing the second in a correct node. An intact set I is itself a quorum and its projection has the required intersection between its members. Lemma 3 gives intersection inside I for any two quorums that touch I; Lemma 4 makes intersecting intact sets merge, hence distinct maximal intact sets are disjoint. Agreement is local to an intact set. The four-node Example 2 with faulty v3 has maximal intact sets {v1,v2} and {v4}; the two sets can disagree. In contrast, the classical 3f+1 construction with 2f+1 slices and at most f faults has all correct nodes in one maximal intact set (§2; Appendix A).  The specification requires output once, agreement within each maximal intact set, and weak validity conditional on every node being honest. Under that condition, unanimous proposals force their value and any decided value must have some proposer. It does not require a correct proposer when malicious nodes exist. Its liveness condition is non-blocking: from any prefix, an undecided intact node eventually decides in every admissible continuation where all malicious nodes stop. The authors explicitly say that continuing malicious traffic with good timing can delay termination permanently for their chosen timeout strategy. Thus this is not unconditional Byzantine termination. Section 3's subsequent broad sentence about entailing usual weak consensus in 3f+1 systems, and its phrase “if every correct node is honest,” need that earlier condition retained: correctness already entails honesty, and cardinality quorum intersection alone does not remove the continuing-malicious-traffic case (§3, pp4–5).  Federated voting (FV) is a tagged Bracha-style primitive. A node first sends VOTE(a); a quorum containing the recipient enables READY(a); a set intersecting every one of that recipient's slices also enables READY(a); a quorum containing the recipient enables delivery. Those slice-intersecting sets are called v-blocking. Each intact node's blocking set touches I. Therefore its first READY for a value is justified by a quorum, preventing conflicting READY values inside I. Theorem 6 proves no duplicate deliveries, totality inside I, consistency for intertwined nodes and delivery when all nodes in I vote alike. A finite propagation argument yields an unknown bounded post-GST delivery delay δ_I once one node in I has delivered. FV alone does not guarantee a decision from arbitrary split votes (§4; Appendix B).  The condition that the recipient belong to the supporting quorum is essential. In Example 2, {v3} is a quorum. If v3 is malicious and the recipient-membership condition is removed, it can send different READY values to v1 and v2 and make these intact peers deliver differently. Figure 1 gives a separate benign voting pattern over four classical nodes: two correct nodes vote false, one votes true and the malicious node helps false. The last correct node changes from its VOTE(true) to READY(false) after a blocking set persuades it. Changing a readiness value relative to one's vote is intended behavior, rather than equivocation (§4, Figure 1, Example 8).  The abstract SCP (ASCP) uses ballots (n,x), ordered first by positive round n and then totally ordered value x, plus a null ballot. It runs a Boolean FV instance for each ballot. Voting false aborts that ballot; voting true tries to commit it. To prepare b, a node delivers false for every lower ballot carrying a different value. It may then raise its candidate to the prepared ballot and vote true. Committing b decides x and stops the node. Safety combines Lemma 9, that a committed ballot had an intact preparer, with Lemma 10, that preparing a higher incompatible ballot excludes an intact READY-to-commit for a lower one. Lemma 11 tracks a largest prepared value back to some proposal when all nodes are honest. Ready-trees, whose leaves are voting quorums, support this validity argument (Algorithm 2; §§5 and Appendix C).  ASCP is an idealization: it allocates infinitely many FV states and can process infinitely many ballot messages in one step. Batched network semantics sends possibly infinite sequences atomically and processes an entire received batch atomically in ballot order. These semantics do substantive proof work; they are not a measured implementation optimization. A formalization must specify admissible ordered sequences and existence of the maxima used by later definitions; a bare totally ordered value domain does not by itself provide every enumeration or maximum. The paper abstracts from Stellar's nomination protocol and assumes arbitrary consensus proposals, so its result covers the ballot protocol, not nomination, transaction validation or a ledger implementation (§5, including footnote 1).  A quorum of messages endorsing higher rounds starts or restarts a timer with delay F(round). A timeout advances to the next round, retaining the largest prepared value if any, otherwise the candidate's value. F doubles with the round. The liveness proof defines a round's prepare-footprint as contributing voting-quorum leaves, its abort interval as their sending interval, and its window as δ_I after the first intact preparation. Lemma 12 claims that sufficiently late windows stop overlapping when faulty nodes stop. Corollary 29 then synchronizes the largest prepared ballot across I; Theorem 13 concludes the peers either commit it or advance together with its value. Figure 2 depicts the required inequality F(n) > δ_A(n)+δ_P+δ_I. A machine proof must bound the round-dependent abort interval and justify eventual domination and persistence of this inequality; doubling F alone does not establish those steps. No numerical termination bound is supplied (§5; Appendix C, pp22–24).  Concrete SCP (CSCP) replaces the infinite FV array with one bunched-voting (BV) process. A prepare statement for b summarizes aborting all lower incompatible ballots; a commit statement names b. BV stores the highest ballots voted, readied and delivered for a prepare statement, plus sets of ballots voted, readied and delivered for commit. A node votes prepare only as its maximum rises; commit voting additionally requires b to equal its maximum voted prepare. Quorum and blocking conditions combine prepare statements by inclusion of the lower-incompatible sets. Algorithm 4 keeps the abstract candidate/prepared/round logic and replaces Boolean FV calls with prepare/commit indications. State is finite at each finite execution prefix; the growing ballot numbers and commit sets are not a fixed finite-state bound (§6, Algorithms 3–4).  The printed Algorithm 3 has a material guard mismatch. Line 8 checks max-voted-prep < b, whereas the surrounding explanation and the corresponding Appendix D proof use max-readied-prep < b. The statement in the prose describing lower-incompatible inclusion also reverses its direction relative to the displayed formula. A hand case exposes the guard consequence: use four correct nodes with classical three-node quorums, values {0,1}, and unanimous proposals 0. After all invoke prepare((1,0)), their maximum voted prepare is (1,0); the greatest ballot justified by those matching VOTE messages is also (1,0), so the printed strict guard rejects it. With no READY messages, the blocking rule cannot start. In a lockstep execution, timeouts make every node prepare((r,0)) before that round's votes arrive, repeating the same rejected equality each round despite timely delivery. This identifies behavior of the literal displayed guard; it does not refute the intended max-readied-prep version. The paper's own Figure 4 readiness steps also require the intended version (Algorithm 3, p12; §6, p13; Appendix D, p33).  Theorem 14 claims observational refinement: every CSCP execution has an ASCP execution with the same proposal/decision history projected to I. Appendix D defines a simulation σ that expands prepare statements into abort batches, commit messages into true-voting batches, and preserves external events. It proves candidate/prepared/round agreement and several voting-state invariants, then checks all concrete event cases. Lemma 37 handles finite prefixes; the theorem extends to complete traces. These are paper proofs, not a simulation run or proof-tool output (§6; Appendix D).  Several simulation details need explicit repair or justification before mechanization. Definition 31's φ is a maximum over ballots whose lower incompatible ballots have delivered false, and its commit expansion uses φ(σ(τ)) < b′ ≤ b. One must fix the implicit node parameter, establish that the maximum exists, and explain which pre- or post-preparation cutoff includes the true votes generated during preparation. Lemma 33 states a readied commit ballot is at most max-delivered-prep, but Lemma 35 invokes it as a bound by max-readied-prep; the statement and use need reconciliation. The READY(cmt) cases in Lemma 37 assume b > max-readied-prep, a guard not present in Algorithm 3's commit-readiness rules. Receiving a message at a correct recipient also does not by itself rule out contradictory earlier messages from a malicious sender. These are identified proof/encoding obligations, not completed disagreement executions (Appendix D, pp29–35).  The step from finite prefixes to complete traces and the transfer of conditional properties require more than proposal/decision equality. A fair infinite concrete run must map to an admissible fair abstract run with the relevant timing and stopped-malicious-node conditions. Otherwise every finite prefix could be legal while the infinite execution starves an enabled action. Likewise, weak validity's all-honest premise must survive the construction of abstract behavior outside I, whose external proposals are absent from I's history. Corollary 15 cites history equality to transfer all four properties without separately spelling out these premise-preservation arguments. This reading records the claimed refinement and non-blocking result while leaving those obligations open; it supplies no general refutation of SCP (§6; Appendix D, p36).  The authors illustrate matching abstract/concrete histories in Figures 3 and 4 with correct proposals 3, 3 and 1. Nodes first prepare (1,1), then prepare (1,2) with malicious support, fail to gather a commit quorum, timeout, then commit (2,2) and decide 2. The displayed concrete trace is inconsistent with Algorithm 3: besides the already identified READY-prep guard mismatch, v4 votes commit((1,2)) without ever voting prepare((1,2)). Its max-voted-prep remains (1,1), so the line-18 commit guard rejects that vote even after fixing line 8. This is an author illustration, not a certified literal execution of the printed pseudocode; the local mismatch does not by itself establish disagreement or refute the repaired protocol. No correct node proposed 2, which is permitted by the stated validity under malicious behavior. The abstract execution expands an infinite abort batch when entering round two; the concrete one represents it with one prepare statement. These are worked executions, not empirical benchmark results (Appendices C–D, Examples 30 and 38).  Subjective FBQS permits each correct node to have its own view of malicious nodes' slices, while all correct-node slices agree across views. The intact sets coincide across these views (Lemma 16); any two known quorums touching I intersect inside I (Lemma 17). These facts lift FV, ASCP and the refinement to that setting, yielding Theorems 18–19 and Corollary 20. Appendix E gives the lifted lemmas and analogous proofs. It does not allow correct nodes to disagree arbitrarily about each other's slices, nor does it prove that a real trust configuration contains an intact set. The self-reference in Lemma 50's proof is a visible citation error; its intended non-subjective precursor is Lemma 11 (§7; Appendix E).  There are no measured performance results, production-code equivalence claim backed by an artifact, test statistics, TLA+ model, verified Rust/Verus development or LLM-agent experiment in this edition. Its contribution to distributed verification is the explicit guarantee boundary around intact sets, reusable voting lemmas and a concrete-to-abstract proof structure. The guard mismatch, assumption-preserving refinement and finite-prefix/fairness distinctions are concrete cases for evaluating specification/proof agents; that use is a reader inference. No author code, compiler, checker, solver, prover, model checker or scientific-result artifact was executed or loaded. Hand cases above are source reasoning only.

- IBFT 2.0 (2019)
    - title: IBFT 2.0: A Safe and Live Variation of the IBFT Blockchain Consensus Protocol for Eventually Synchronous Networks
    - what: Defines the IBFT 2.0 proof-of-authority BFT protocol with immediate finality, dynamic validator sets, and eventual-synchrony robustness. It gives pseudocode and a high-level lemma-based proof of optimal safety, persistence, weak liveness, and (with a fair-network probability assumption) liveness for up to floor((n−1)/3) Byzantine validators. The protocol removes IBFT locking and relies on prepared certificates, round-change certificates, and quorum commit seals. Height-tagged messages make lagging nodes request missing finalized blocks, addressing persistence. Safety derives from quorum intersections and induction over rounds (Lemmas 1–7); weak liveness derives from exponential round timers and eventual synchrony (Lemmas 8–17). Full liveness additionally assumes every sent message has positive probability of delivery within one-third of the initial timeout, then uses round-robin proposer selection and stable validator windows (Lemmas 18–23). The paper proposes ten concrete improvements, including fair proposer selection to remove the probabilistic assumption, two-phase fast path, quorum-triggered round advancement, hash certificates, reduced signatures, and aggregate signatures.

Liveness (as distinct from weak liveness) depends on Fair Network Behaviour Assumption unless FairProposer is adopted. Formal specification and implementation details are explicitly future work; validator voting pseudocode is omitted.

- On the parallels between Paxos and Raft, and how to port optimizations (2019)
    - what: # Reading: porting Paxos optimizations to Raft* (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Wang, Zhao, Mu, Chen and Li study a modified Raft called Raft*, not a proof that original Raft and Paxos are interchangeable. The whole 61-page extended paper, Appendices A–C, six printed TLA+ modules, 15 figures and 67 references were read. Exact supporting passages are staged in quotes.json with section and PDF-page anchors.  The central construction starts with already-correct specifications A, its optimization AΔ, and B refining A. A non-mutating optimization introduces state/actions or conjuncts without changing A's existing state updates. Given state and action-parameter mappings, translating the added clauses produces BΔ. Section 4.3 sketches BΔ refining both AΔ and B, preserving their invariants. This is a conditional specification transformation, not a general liveness or performance theorem. Added guards can prevent progress; added actions must explicitly preserve old state, and initialization and new-variable frames must be compatible. The paper leaves implementation to developers and states in §1 that correctness is guaranteed only at specification level.  Raft* prevents deletion of accepted entries by returning extra log entries during elections, selecting safe suffix values, and rejecting shorter appends. A separate accepted-ballot field distinguishes an entry's original Raft term from its latest Paxos acceptance ballot. Figure 3 maps leader self-acceptance as well as explicit messages. One Raft* action can cover multiple Paxos operations, so porting must account for every relevant action, including implicit self-acceptance.  The two implemented cases are quorum leases (PQL) and Mencius. PQL allows a leaseholder to read locally only after relevant pending writes are applied; writes notify the leaseholders of an intersecting quorum. Translating f+1 Paxos acknowledgments to f remote Raft acknowledgments must include the leader's implicit acknowledgment and its own leaseholders. The authors describe a handwritten version that omitted those leaseholders. Mencius assigns default log-slot proposers round-robin. Nondefault recovery proposes no-ops; skipTags allow default no-ops to become executable early. The optimization must be applied to every action corresponding to Paxos acceptance, including BecomeLeader and AppendEntries, not only ReceiveAppend. Appendix B abstracts leases with one global timer; distributed-clock and real lease-expiry correctness require additional implementation assumptions.  Evaluation uses five AWS regions (Oregon, Ohio, Ireland, Canada, Seoul), two m4.xlarge instances per region for client/server, 4 vCPUs, 16 GB RAM, SSD and stated 750 Mbps network. WAN latency spans 25–292 ms. Closed-loop YCSB-like workloads use 100,000 keys, a configurable shared hot-key probability and otherwise region-partitioned uniform keys. Each trial lasts 50 seconds with a stated 10 seconds for warm-up and cool-down; whether 10 seconds applies jointly or separately is unclear. Results are medians of five trials. Implementation builds on etcd c4fc8c09, retaining custom networking/batched forwarding that the authors say improves throughput 2.4×. Oregon is the favorable Raft leader placement.  PQL uses 2-second leases, renewal every 0.5 seconds, default 90% reads and 5% conflict, and 50 clients per region for latency. About 90% of reads take 1 ms; leader-lease leader reads take 1.6 ms, while a 1% follower tail reaches approximately 137 ms from conflicting writes. PQL reports 1.6× throughput at 90% reads and 1.9× at 99%; write latency rises because leaseholders must acknowledge. Figure 9(d)'s x-axis runs from 50% conflict on the left to 0% on the right. Its rising speedup therefore agrees with the prose that lower conflict helps. An earlier apparent contradiction came from overlooking that reversed axis and is withdrawn. Figure 9 captions/legends use Raft where parts of the prose discuss Raft*; keep that baseline wording distinction.  Mencius uses 100% puts, 0% or 100% conflict, and 8-byte or 4-KB payloads. The CPU case reaches about 55,000 versus 41,000 operations/s (about 1.34×); the network case reports 70% higher throughput than Raft-Oregon. Oregon itself beats Seoul by 30% in the stated comparison. Execution can still wait for remote earlier slots: the paper reports roughly 360 ms versus 110 ms for Mencius conflict/no-conflict cases, and about 79 ms at the Raft-Oregon leader. These are workload/deployment measurements, not a verification result, fault campaign, or universal optimization guarantee.  The paper claims a machine-checkable TLAPS refinement proof in §6. Appendix C is a manual prose proof outline, and Appendix B supplies specifications and invariant statements, not executable TLAPS proof bodies. The referenced GitHub master-tree request and, after waiting over five minutes, repository metadata endpoint returned HTTP 404 on 19 October 2026 UTC. No external proof source was recovered; artifact-acquisition.json records the later response. A missing retrieval does not disprove that a historical proof existed. Appendix C calls a batch-to-sequence correspondence 'stuttering', although true stuttering leaves the mapped abstract state unchanged. Its one-step simulation statement needs an abstract batch action or a justified intermediate-state argument. Its BecomeLeader safe-prefix/suffix claims are asserted without derivation in this outline. Its mapping table also uses lastLogEntry.Term where the main text distinguishes the accepted ballot.  Several printed specifications require repair before treating them as checker inputs. These omissions can affect semantics, so they are not classified as merely cosmetic syntax errors. Figure 4 leaves unrelated state unconstrained, uses logs'[i]=v in B versus singleton {v} in A/BΔ, and BΔ initialization quantifies k while referring to i. Appendix B's quorum assumption prints Q⊆Quorum rather than Q⊆Acceptor (Raft variants print both). UpdateLog prints logBallot'[i] without the acceptor index. ProposeEntries prints IF i1=0 THEN raftlogs[a][i1−1][1] ELSE −1, reversing the expected initial-index test. Some modules omit introduced variables from vars/init and omit frames for new variables; PQL Apply, GrantLease and UpdateTimer preserve MP!vars but leave other PQL variables unconstrained. These are findings about the printed artifact, not executed counterexamples against the authors' implementation or an independently obtained historical proof.  For distributed-system verification, this contributes a concrete specification-transformation method and useful cases of implicit acknowledgments, state mappings and safe optimization transfer. It also separates invariant preservation from progress and measured speed, and highlights the need to check exact proof artifacts, initialization, frame conditions and batch simulation. It contains no Verus, verified-Rust or LLM proof-agent experiment. No author code, prover or model checker was run.
    - limits: already-correct input specifications; valid state and parameter mappings; added actions preserve old state with explicit frame conditions; compatible initialization; quorum intersection, crash faults and persistent state; real lease-clock implementation; invariant preservation does not imply general liveness; manually written implementation is not verified; reported TLAPS proof source not recovered; printed listing concerns are not executed counterexamples

- SecCSL (2019)
    - title: SecCSL: Security Concurrent Separation Logic.
    - what: SecCSL extends concurrent separation logic with relational assertions for data-dependent value sensitivity and location sensitivity. Assertions can express pointers, arrays, shared invariants, and security labels; lock invariants combine functional heap facts with policies for attacker-observable sinks. The logic's compositional noninterference property is timing-sensitive, so branches on secret values are forbidden. The authors mechanize soundness in Isabelle/HOL and implement SecC, a symbolic-execution verifier for a C subset. The central case study is a lock-protected record whose classified flag controls data sensitivity and whose public OUTPUT_REG sink must never receive secret data; a variant of Covern's CDDC case study and known benchmark problems are reported as verified within milliseconds in the abstract/overview.
    - number: SecC targets a subset of C
    - number: well-known benchmark problems reported within a few milliseconds
    - number: one concurrent lock/record/OUTPUT_REG case study plus CDDC variant
    - number: soundness mechanized in Isabelle/HOL
    - limits: The logic targets shared-memory, lock-based synchronization and passive attackers; security includes timing of observable updates. Secret-dependent branching is prohibited to preserve compositionality, though the authors suggest future relaxation. The extracted paper contains the design/overview and reports but limited detailed benchmark tables. Verification assurance depends on SecCSL soundness and the modeled C subset; it does not cover arbitrary concurrency primitives or active attackers.

- SPECTRL (2019)
    - title: A Composable Specification Language for Reinforcement Learning Tasks
    - what: Introduces SPECTRL, a language with achieve, ensuring, sequencing (;), and disjunction (or) over state predicates. A compiler builds a task monitor (an automaton with registers), augments state and action spaces, and generates quantitative plus subtask-progress reward shaping. Any RL optimizer can then learn a policy for the compiled MDP; the projected policy carries monitor memory. The monitor resolves non-Markovian sequencing by recording completed subtasks and exposes nondeterministic transition choices as actions. Quantitative semantics use max/min over trajectory segments; shaped rewards preserve rollout ordering under explicit lower/upper bounds on final and progress rewards. Theorem 3.1 links Boolean satisfaction to existence of a positive-reward augmented run; Theorem 3.2 proves ordering properties of the shaped reward. SPECTRL is implemented with ARS and modular neural networks, and is tested on seven waypoint/obstacle/fuel tasks plus continuous-action CartPole.
    - number: Seven specifications φ1–φ7; SPECTRL success curves near 100% for φ1–φ5 and in 4/5 runs for φ6–φ7
    - number: Compared with TLTL, CCE and unshaped SPECTRL; CCE degrades when obstacle+fuel constraints are added, while TLTL/CCE fail on memory-heavy φ5–φ7
    - number: SPECTRL success rate at least 97% for each benchmark task (abstract)
    - number: CartPole: SPECTRL converges faster than TLTL; CCE cannot learn a satisfying policy
    - limits: Policy guarantees are probabilistic optimization objectives, not formal synthesis guarantees for stochastic dynamics. Reward shaping’s policy-order preservation assumes deterministic MDPs for the stated guarantee and requires known bounds C_l and C_u. The language has finite-horizon rollout semantics and user-supplied quantitative predicate functions whose sign must match Boolean truth. Experiments use a 2-D noisy point robot, horizon 40, hand-designed tasks and ARS; scalability beyond the tested nested sequencing depth and other RL algorithms is not established.

- DPA/MCMT (2018)
    - title: Formal Specification and Verification of Dynamic Parametrized Architectures
    - what: Defines Dynamic Parametrized Architectures (DPAs), where indexed component sets, connections, initial configurations, invariants and reconfiguration transitions are first-order formulas over unbounded index sets. It translates information-flow reachability into array-based transition systems and checks the resulting safety problem with MCMT/Yices. The formal language separates parametrized architecture guards from dynamic formulas, restricts transition syntax so index sets and parameter arrays can be rewritten into array updates, and translates component presence plus taint propagation into an array-based safety system. The paper proves that the translation stays in the array-based fragment and that information-flow witnesses correspond exactly to unsafe traces. A C/GNU-Bison extended-AADL translator and five scalable benchmark families expose transition-count, quantifier and solver-call bottlenecks.

Component and connection guards are quantifier-free; initial, invariant and transition formulas obey restrictive quantifier/update/frame syntaxes. Verification targets reachability-style information flow, not noninterference or liveness; the paper explicitly leaves noninterference open. MCMT imposes at most two universal variables in Init, two existential variables per transition, 50 transition disjuncts, four existential variables in unsafe formulas and ten index variables per term. General dynamic information flow is undecidable (Minsky 2-counter encoding), and experiments time out or hit these limits.

- Formal Verification of Usage Control Models: A Case Study of UseCON Using TLA+ (2018)
    - what: # Formal Verification of Usage Control Models: A Case Study of UseCON Using TLA+ (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Antonios Gouglidis, Christos Grompanopoulos and Anastasia Mavridou, MeTRiD 2018, EPTCS 272, pp.52–64, DOI 10.4204/EPTCS.272.5. The paper specifies concurrent usage-control lifecycles and reports finite TLC checks for pre-authorisation and ongoing-authorisation models. The authors claim correctness for “≤ 12 uses”; Table 2 gives measurements at 2, 8 and 12. These are bounded model results, not a proof for arbitrary populations or an implementation. Printed temporal formulas also have substantial specification issues that cannot be reconciled with the reported checks without the executable models (Q0,Q7–Q10).  ## Reading and evidence  Read all 13 pages, sections 1–5 and all eleven references. Inspected four figures, two tables and all displayed definitions/actions/formulas. Viewed eight individual renders, pages 5–12, including enlarged printed safety/liveness formulas and Table 2. The inventory, official arXiv and official EPTCS PDFs are byte-identical. Formula symbols lost in extracted text were checked against the renders.  No executable TLA+ module, TLC configuration or run log was acquired. Section 2 says “see TLA+ specification in [1]”, but reference [1] is Microsoft's TLA+ Tools page, not an identified UseCON model archive (Q1,Q11). The saved PDF's active links point to tools, cited publications and license information. This is a bounded artifact-location finding, not a claim that no source can exist elsewhere. No author code/model executed or imported, no TLC/compiler/checker/solver run and no serialized scientific results loaded.  ## Model and concrete cases  UseCON adds a use entity to subjects, objects and actions. A use records a request's subject/action/object identifiers, lifecycle status and mutable use attributes. U, the set of use records, is the only declared state variable. Subject/object/action records and their identifiers are treated as constants; administrative changes to their attributes are outside the modeled operation. Initially U is empty. Policy-related history can be read through existing uses (Q1,Q2).  Direct policies use attributes of the requesting subject, object and action. Indirect policies select a related entity using CHOOSE; the example checks whether a child's father has premium membership. Complex indirect policies aggregate selected entities; the example sums a customer's account balances. Correct interpretation of CHOOSE requires an appropriate selected entity; existence/uniqueness conditions are not developed as part of these examples. The paper illustrates expressiveness but leaves verification of complex and interacting ongoing policies as future work (Q3,Q10).  Induced use attributes come from participating entities, such as a service price. Observed attributes require an external observation, such as the system time when access is activated. Update procedures change status and other use attributes together. SystemTime is illustrative rather than a concrete bounded clock model. Administrators must supply policy-specific procedures and finite domains for any TLC instance (Q2–Q4; reader finite-model qualification).  The pre-authorisation lifecycle is initial→requested→activated→completed or requested→denied. Request and Complete are subject actions; preEvaluate is a system action. Next is their disjunction. Request nondeterministically chooses a subject/action/object tuple absent from existing records. Because terminal records remain in U and the uniqueness test uses only that tuple, the printed transition rules do not permit requesting the same tuple again in that model lifetime. This restricts the workload compared with repeated real-world accesses (Q5; reader inference).  For ongoing authorisation, Request is followed by Activate without a prior policy test. onEvaluate keeps the use active and updates attributes if the policy holds, or changes it to stopped otherwise; Complete changes active to completed. Next includes all four actions. The evaluation interval is explicitly left to implementation. Thus the model does not establish a maximum revocation delay or that a policy is continuously true between evaluations (Q6).  All update actions are assumed atomic. The authors justify this with a central policy decision point and the absence of updates to subject/object attributes. Concurrent operations are represented by interleaving these whole actions; message loss, replication, distributed transactions, crashes and partial updates are not modeled here. There is no implementation-refinement proof showing that a deployed access-control service realizes these atomic steps (Q4–Q6).  ## Intended properties and printed formulas  TypeCorrectness is U⊆Uses, where Uses is a finite-domain record type. Table 1 lists forbidden status changes and intended progress. Pre-authorisation forbids leaving completed or denied and returning from activated to requested/denied; it expects requested uses eventually to activate or be denied, and activated uses eventually to complete. Ongoing authorisation similarly forbids leaving completed/stopped or returning from activated to requested; it expects requested uses to activate and eventually complete or stop (Q7).  The printed safety formula is □(∃u∈U: u.st="completed" ⇒ □(u.st≠"requested")), where □ means always. This is existential, although the prose says the condition applies to all uses. With the natural quantifier scope shown by the parentheses, the existential is false when U is empty; Init explicitly sets U={} so the formula fails in the initial state. Further, u denotes a bound record value. The inner u.st does not look up that use's changing record in future U states. A completed bound record retains its completed field even if U later contains a replacement record with the same identifiers and a different status. These are semantic issues in the printed formula, not an executed TLC counterexample (Q4,Q7; reader interpretation).  The liveness formula is ∀u∈U: u.st="activated" ↝ u.st="completed", where ↝ means that whenever its left condition holds, the right condition eventually holds. U is quantified at the point where this formula is evaluated. As a top-level property evaluated initially, it is vacuously true because initial U is empty. If evaluated at a later state with an active record, that bound record value does not evolve into the completed record. Adding an outer □ alone would not repair this identity/update issue; the property needs stable identifiers and a state-dependent lookup (Q2,Q4,Q8; reader interpretation).  The paper supplies Init and Next but no complete temporal specification or fairness assumptions. A disjunction of enabled actions alone does not require an enabled completion or policy evaluation eventually to be selected. In the ongoing model, an active use can remain active through onEvaluate repeatedly while completion is postponed. If stuttering is allowed by the full specification, it gives another way to postpone progress. Scheduling/fairness and policy assumptions must therefore be explicit for an eventual-completion theorem. No such theorem can be recovered from the displayed transitions alone (Q5–Q8; reader separating behavior).  There are also printed notation inconsistencies: action guards and createUse use state while other definitions and updates use st; record definitions and extra braces in the ongoing actions are not a complete executable module. These discrepancies could differ from the authors' actual checked models. They prevent treating the printed listings as independently validated source. The reported no-error checks and the initial-state safety failure cannot both refer literally to the same displayed property under standard quantifier interpretation; the missing modules/configurations leave the correspondence unresolved (Q2,Q5–Q9).  ## Measurements and their scope  The authors use TLA+ Tool 1.5.6 and TLC 2.12 on a mid-2014 MacBook Pro, OS X El Capitan, 2.5GHz Intel Core i7 and 16GB 1600MHz DDR3. Table 2 contains six configurations. Each tuple below gives uses; reported diameter; generated states; distinct states; deadlock-check seconds; safety/liveness seconds (Q9).  - Pre: 2;6;21;12;1;3 - Pre: 8;23;277,969;16,832;2;5 - Pre: 12;31;45,533,665;560,128;53;138 - Ongoing: 2;7;33;16;1;3 - Ongoing: 8;25;367,873;23,808;2;7 - Ongoing: 12;37;79,112,449;1,224,704;118;241  All 36 numeric cells were transcribed from the rendered table. Diameter is retained as the paper's reported label; no TLC statistics were independently regenerated. State-count growth and interrupted larger runs demonstrate an exploration limit for this setup. Table 2 does not document every integer population below 12, all policy choices or all attribute domains. The exact subject/object/action split, policy instances, bounds and fairness configuration are not supplied in enough detail to reproduce these counts (Q9).  The paper calls the first experiment deadlock-freedom checking, then describes TLC stopping at a deadlock after every pre-authorisation use is denied or completed. Its conclusion says “The trace of the deadlock errors verifies that the defined specifications of the system operate correctly.” A permitted terminal state and an unexpected stuck state need separate acceptance conditions. These traces can support an intended-termination inspection, but their existence does not itself establish deadlock freedom, exhaustive termination of every behavior or the temporal formulas above (Q9,Q10).  The authors report no safety/liveness errors for the bounded configurations. No model files, logs, certificates or reproduced checks establish which executable formulas produced that result. The appropriate record is therefore an author-reported bounded check, accompanied by the printed-formula issues. Larger populations were stopped for time; no cutoff theorem extends the small checks to arbitrary numbers of uses (Q9,Q10).  ## Contribution to the topic  This is a concrete TLA+/TLC case study of security-related lifecycle modeling, finite-state exploration cost and intended safety/progress distinctions. Its central-state atomic model is relevant to concurrent systems but does not verify a distributed protocol or a Rust/Verus implementation. No LLM specification/proof agent is evaluated. Complex interacting policy verification, composition with secBIP and arbitrary-population guarantees are future work (Q10).  For evaluating generated specifications, the paper provides useful failure modes: temporal quantification over an initially empty set, confusing a fixed record with a changing entity, omitted fairness, treating a terminal deadlock as a general correctness argument, and incomplete correspondence between printed formulas and reported checker success. These are reader lessons from this paper, not author-demonstrated LLM failures.
    - limits: boundedatomiccentralmodel;constantentityrecords;policydomains/fairnessmissing;printedformulasnotverifiedexecutablemodels

- Layered Concurrent Programs (2018)
    - what: Formalizes layered concurrent programs, a single imperative concurrent notation containing a sequence of concrete-to-abstract programs with common code written once. A type system extracts each layer and checker programs; refinement is reduced to cooperative safety of checkers plus safety of the top abstraction. Defines gated atomic actions, parallel calls, preemptive and cooperative semantics, and mover-type commutativity. The projection and reduction theorems show that cooperative checker executions cover preemptive behaviors; instrumentation tracks snapshots and exactly one abstract action across preemption-free fragments. The lock example refines CAS/RESET spinlock to an ownership-aware abstract lock and then skip; Civl uses Boogie and has verified a garbage collector and race detector, with about fifty smaller examples including ticket lock and lock-free stack.
    - number: three-layer spinlock example
    - number: h checker programs for h refinement steps
    - number: around fifty smaller Civl programs
    - number: Section 6: around fifty smaller programs; large cases are a concurrent garbage collector and a data-race detector
    - limits: Soundness requires pairwise commutativity and mover annotations, disjoint outputs/linearity conditions, safe checker programs under cooperative semantics, and a safe top abstract program. The result is safety/refinement only, not liveness. The paper reports no new quantitative benchmark table; executable code generation, scalability, and IDE integration remain open, and concrete atomic-action gates remain trusted specification points.
