- Modular Paxos verification (2018)
    - title: Paxos Consensus, Deconstructed and Abstracted (Extended Version)
    - what: # Paxos Consensus, Deconstructed and Abstracted: whole paper reading (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Álvaro García-Pérez, Alexey Gotsman, Yuri Meshman and Ilya Sergey's extended Paxos Consensus, Deconstructed and Abstracted is arXiv:1802.05969v1,16 February 2018. All 44 pages were read: eight sections, Appendices A–E, all 17 figures, four theorems, five lemmata, four definitions, examples 1–6, all printed proof outlines and 32 references. All 11 contact sheets and individual15,18,21,22,28,32,33,44 were inspected. The original, local and official PDFs match. No author code, scientific-result artifact or formal checking tool was executed or loaded. [Evidence: Q0, frontmatter/v1 identity, PDF pp.1–1; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The paper separates two verification problems: proving single-decree Paxos through composable shared-memory object specifications, and deriving Multi-Paxos by transformations of message handling. It adapts Boichat et al.'s Paxos→Round-BasedConsensus→Round-BasedRegister decomposition. The lower specifications deliberately admit unsuccessful operations that nevertheless expose values to other operations. The aim is to preserve each unchanged module's proof when changing another module or the network implementation. Claims of being the first such modular verification belong to these authors; this reading does not establish historical priority. [Evidence: Q1, §1, two contributions, PDF pp.2–3; Q28, §7, complete comparisons/claimed novelty, PDF pp.24–25; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The setting is crash-stop, majority-quorum consensus, not Byzantine consensus or crash-recovery with durable storage. The basic network neither invents, changes, drops nor duplicates messages, delivers with unbounded delay, and has nonblocking send/blocking receive. Later transformations explicitly introduce selected read-request duplication. Leader election is omitted under an assumption that one correct process trusted by all other correct processes always exists. No failure-detector implementation, storage-recovery proof or general termination proof is supplied. Finite trace refinement and linearizability establish safety properties, not eventual completion: lower specifications may repeatedly choose failure. [Evidence: Q2, §3, decomposition/leader assumption, PDF pp.4–5; Q3, §3, network/imperative language, PDF pp.5–5; Q4, §1, safety scope, PDF pp.1–1; Q19, Definition1, full configuration traces, PDF pp.18–18; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Acceptors retain promised round r, accepted round w and value v. A read(k) gathers distinct-sender acknowledgments from ceil((n+1)/2) acceptors and chooses the value with highest accepted round, or undef. A write(k, v) gathers an equal majority; either operation can fail after a nack. Acceptors accept requests at k≥r and atomically update state with their acknowledgment send. A proposer uses rounds pid, pid+n, pid+2n,…; this guarantees disjoint rounds between processes under one continuing invocation per process. Reinvoking the printed entry operation resets k to pid, so repeated independent calls need fresh-instance state or persistent counters. Fixed process identities and trustworthy message origins under the assumed transport model, plus matching request rounds, are essential. [Evidence: Q6, Figure3/§3, complete read/write implementation, PDF pp.6–6; Q7, Figures4–5, acceptor and entry implementation, PDF pp.7–7; Q8, §3, entry and unique rounds, PDF pp.8–8; Q3, §3, network/imperative language, PDF pp.5–5; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The top sequential specification stores one value, choosing the first proposal and returning that value thereafter. Its concurrent implementation can decide another process's previously exposed proposal; ghost pending-thread entries let one invocation linearize that proposing operation first and then itself. This is helping: a thread's logical effect may occur during another thread's step. The proof assumes unique proposal values, one pending slot per process and single-assignment result cells. A concrete invocation identity or a justification that unique tagging preserves client-visible equality would be needed to generalize beyond that stated setting. Abstract results are declared Boolean in Figure 7 although proposal values have type val; this is a printed typing mismatch. [Evidence: Q5, Figure2, atomic entry specification, PDF pp.4–4; Q10, §4/Figure 7/Theorem 1, complete top refinement, PDF pp.9–10; Q30, Appendix A, complete top-level proof outline, PDF pp.27–29; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Figure 6 gives the reason for weakening the intermediate abstractions. A low-round write can fail overall yet reach one acceptor, after a higher-round quorum has already decided another value. An overlapping read may see that isolated value and complete successfully, although its subsequent write will fail. Real-time order and this information flow can require the failed write to linearize before the read. The abstract specifications therefore retain sets of exposed values rather than only the globally decided value, allow reads at stale rounds and allow a write at an otherwise eligible round to fail because a concurrent operation overtakes it. [Evidence: Q9, §3/Figure 6, complete histories and rationale, PDF pp.8–9; Q11, §4/Figure8, round consensus contract, PDF pp.10–12; Q12, Figure9/§4, complete register contract, PDF pp.12–12; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Round-BasedConsensus's atomic proposeRC(k, v0) nondeterministically selects vD and a success flag. If vD is already exposed or equals v0, it adds vD to valsRC even on failure; success additionally needs k≥roundRC and retains the first decided vRC. Round-BasedRegister is weaker: every write exposes its input, a successful eligible write updates roundRR and vRR, and a read may return any exposed value when its round is stale. Its vRR is not itself an immutable consensus decision. The enclosing read-before-write discipline and unique increasing rounds are needed to recover the deterministic top specification. Theorem 2 places logical effects at failed read or completed write. [Evidence: Q11, §4/Figure8, round consensus contract, PDF pp.10–12; Q12, Figure9/§4, complete register contract, PDF pp.12–12; Q13, Theorem 2, round consensus linearization, PDF pp.12–12; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The register proof uses prophecy variables: ghost values selected according to how an operation eventually returns. Successful reads are intended to linearize when the quorum-th read acknowledgment is sent, successful writes at the quorum-th write acknowledgment, failed reads at their nack return, and failed writes at the first accepting response or at the failure return if none accepted. These are proof annotations, not an implementable ability to predict network schedules. Ghost counters count acknowledgments sent, whereas concrete quorums count acknowledgments received. The basic no-duplication premise is used in relating those quantities. [Evidence: Q14, §4, register prophecy/linearization explanation, PDF pp.12–14; Q15, Figure10, complete instrumented read/write, PDF pp.14–14; Q16, Figure 11/Theorem3, complete annotated acceptor, PDF pp.14–15; Q32, Appendix C, complete register proof outline/invariants, PDF pp.31–41; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Reader source-level objection to the printed read annotation: Figure 11 requires both count_r[k]=quorum−1 and proph_r[k]=(true, v), where v is that particular acceptor's local value. The eventual read result is instead the maximum accepted-round value among received acknowledgments. With n=3, let A1 hold (vA, w=1) from a failed write and A2 hold undef. A read at k=3 returns vA after receiving A1's then A2's replies. When A1 sends, count=0 rather than 1; when A2 sends, count=1 but its undef differs from the prophecy vA. A third sender sees count=2. No successful-read annotation fires. This ordinary schedule substitution identifies a proof-instrumentation gap, not a conflicting-decision attack or an executed trace. The proof needs an annotation tied to the relevant quorum maximum, not equality with an arbitrary quorum-th sender's value. [Evidence: Q15, Figure10, complete instrumented read/write, PDF pp.14–14; Q16, Figure 11/Theorem3, complete annotated acceptor, PDF pp.14–15; Q32, Appendix C, complete register proof outline/invariants, PDF pp.31–41; Q9, §3/Figure 6, complete histories and rationale, PDF pp.8–9; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The incomplete-operation case also needs care. The paper leaves a never-returning write's prophecy undef and never linearizes it, although it may already change an acceptor and supply a value read by another operation. A standard linearizability proof can complete selected pending operations; this annotation needs to support those completions rather than erase every nonreturning writer. Separately, after correcting the acknowledgment predicate notation, Appendix C's intended Val(j, v) invariant equates the current value with existence of a historical write acknowledgment for v. After accepting v1 then v2, the retained message history still contains the acknowledgment for v1 but the current value is v2, contradicting the reverse implication. The intended invariant needs a latest-round qualification. These are reader proof obligations, not a rejection of Paxos's ordinary quorum argument. [Evidence: Q14, §4, register prophecy/linearization explanation, PDF pp.12–14; Q16, Figure 11/Theorem3, complete annotated acceptor, PDF pp.14–15; Q32, Appendix C, complete register proof outline/invariants, PDF pp.31–41; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The appendices contain additional transcription problems material to mechanization. Appendix A's loop annotations use pid=k mod n despite initializing k=pid and maintaining the owner formula ((k−1) mod n)+1; n=3, pid=3, k=3 gives3≠0. They also assert a false pending flag after an unsuccessful iteration, although that branch does not clear it. Appendix B's rely relation prints an index condition (k mod n)≠(k mod n), with no distinct index, which would exclude all interference literally. Appendix C's req/ack predicates reuse the same message identity for a request and its reverse-direction acknowledgment; for different endpoints those fields cannot both hold. These details must be corrected before treating the outlines as machine-checked proofs. [Evidence: Q30, Appendix A, complete top-level proof outline, PDF pp.27–29; Q31, Appendix B, complete round consensus proof outline, PDF pp.29–31; Q32, Appendix C, complete register proof outline/invariants, PDF pp.31–41; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The network layer models local states, protocol transition relations and a multiset of uniquely identified messages retained after reception with active=false. A simple execution chooses an internal step, send or receive at one node. Appendix D flattens proposer code, carries the residual program text in each local state and gives send/receive rules; it leaves ordinary WHILE-language semantics assumed. Thus program counters and auxiliary loop variables are part of this encoding, not invisible implementation details. Receive rules in Figures13–14 also print an unbound n where the destination-node update is intended. [Evidence: Q17, §5.1, local states/messages/transition relations, PDF pp.16–17; Q18, Figure13/§5.1, network steps, PDF pp.17–18; Q33, Appendix D, complete code-aware encoding, PDF pp.41–44; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Out-of-thin-air semantics adds selected read requests without changing local state, justified by predicate P when the proposer has reached at least that round. Lemma 1 claims its complete configuration traces are included in the simple semantics. Appendix E's entire argument says a read request does not change proposer state and has no nontrivial precondition. That does not directly match Appendix D's code-aware encoding, where executing send advances the residual program and loop, and a particular round/destination request is sent once. Extra duplicate requests with unchanged program state therefore need an explicit observation/projection or a different reusable-send protocol. The source's exact-trace inclusion is stronger than client-visible equivalence and its printed one-sentence proof does not bridge them. [Evidence: Q20, §5.2, complete OTA rule/predicate/Lemma 1, PDF pp.18–18; Q19, Definition1, full configuration traces, PDF pp.18–18; Q33, Appendix D, complete code-aware encoding, PDF pp.41–44; Q34, Appendix E, complete transformation/composition proofs, PDF pp.44–44; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Slot replication runs an independent single-decree instance per slot; projection to any slot recovers its base behavior up to stuttering. Widening then copies a triggering read request to all slots at the same recipient. Its refinement requires OTA compliance and P-monotonicity: a request justified at one sender slot must be justified at every other sender slot. Example 4 correctly excludes independently advancing rounds and supplies fixed-round instances as a positive case. Example 5 immediately invokes the unrestricted Section 3 implementation, whose rounds retry independently; the required synchronization/restriction is not established there. An independent-slot source-level case uses n=3, pid=1, with sender slots at rounds 4 and 1: request k=4 satisfies P in the first slot but not the second. The refinement must state a shared-round discipline rather than infer it from ordinary slot independence. [Evidence: Q21, §5.3/Figure14/Definition2/Lemma2, independent slots, PDF pp.18–20; Q22, §5.4/Definition 3, widening rule/OTA compliance, PDF pp.20–21; Q23, §5.4, Definition4/Examples4–5/Lemma3, PDF pp.21–21; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Two further printed conditions need explicit scope. Definition 3 introduces a triggering subset T but then quantifies all messages m∈M instead of m∈M∩T; its read-only P would reject ordinary write/ack messages. Optimized widening's receiveAndAct applies a received message to every slot without an explicit m∈T branch, despite T appearing in the rule's name. Applying a slot-specific write everywhere would not preserve independent slots. Restrict the optimized rule to triggering read requests and retain ordinary per-slot reception for other messages. These are literal-rule qualifications, not evidence about the linked Scala implementation. [Evidence: Q22, §5.4/Definition 3, widening rule/OTA compliance, PDF pp.20–21; Q24, §5.5, all-slot optimized rule/Lemma 4, PDF pp.21–22; Q23, §5.4, Definition4/Examples4–5/Lemma3, PDF pp.21–21; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Reader refinement qualification also applies to Lemmata 3–4. Widening consumes one active request and inserts replicas in the same transition; separate slot-replicating receive and OTA send transitions introduce intermediate configurations. Appendix E explicitly says, “This allows us to emulate this step by a per-slot execution of the OTASend.” That establishes a multistep simulation argument, while Lemma 3 states inclusion of complete configuration traces. Lemma 4 permits compression of intermediate configurations, but expanding receives also leaves inactive replica requests in message history; the optimized rule does not insert that same history. A specified observation/refinement mapping must account for grouped steps and internal request histories, not just remove intermediate configurations. These are source-level proof obligations, not executed counterexamples or consensus-safety violations. [Evidence: FR_FQ19, §5.1, observation definition, PDF pp.17–17; FR_FQ25, §5.4, WStepReceiveT adds replicas while consuming the original, PDF pp.20–20; FR_FQ26, §5.5, optimized widening has no triggering-set guard, PDF pp.21–21; FR_FQ27, §5.5, Lemma 4 configuration compression, PDF pp.22–22; FR_FQ40, Appendix E, Lemma 3 simulation uses multiple steps, PDF pp.44–44; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Optimized widening batches one recipient's per-slot receive, local and reply steps; Lemma 4 relates it to expanding those local sequences. Bunching packages the resulting replies in a message, caches/unpacks them on demand and maps them back to separate messages in Lemma 5. The intended benefit is amortized Phase 1 across slots while reusing single-decree proofs. Because the model permits infinitely many slots, its all-slot batch needs a finite or lazy representation before implementation. The register-provider interface exposes independent write-once slots; it does not itself prove client request deduplication, gap-free log execution, application linearizability or leader-lease correctness. [Evidence: Q24, §5.5, all-slot optimized rule/Lemma 4, PDF pp.21–22; Q25, §5.6/Figure15/Lemma 5, bunching/unpacking, PDF pp.22–23; Q26, §5.7–6, composition/interface/stability, PDF pp.23–24; Q27, §6, Scala/Akka prototype/Theorem 4, PDF pp.24–24; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Theorem 4 composes Theorem 1 with the transformation lemmata to claim refinement of an array of atomic write-once registers. Appendix E supplies short proofs of Lemmata 1–3 and Theorem 4; Lemmata 4–5 have statements/explanations in the body but no separate extended proofs. Stable rely/guarantee assertions are the stated condition for transporting program reasoning across network transformations. The paper's conclusion proposes Raft, PBFT, Mencius, master leases and epoch numbering as future applications, without establishing those results here. [Evidence: Q26, §5.7–6, composition/interface/stability, PDF pp.23–24; Q34, Appendix E, complete transformation/composition proofs, PDF pp.44–44; Q29, §8, contributions/proposed extensions, PDF pp.25–26; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Validation is a Scala/Akka proof-of-concept linked at github.com/certichain/protocol-combinators, plus handwritten specifications, annotations and proof outlines. The paper reports no benchmark configurations, performance measurements, experiment counts or machine-checked artifact for its own argument. “Executable” and “provably correct” are the authors' descriptions; source-code refinement and runtime-library correctness were not inspected in this reading. TLA+, PVS, Isabelle, EventML, PSync, Ivy, IronFleet, Verdi and Disel appear as related work, not tools used to check these particular proofs. [Evidence: Q27, §6, Scala/Akka prototype/Theorem 4, PDF pp.24–24; Q28, §7, complete comparisons/claimed novelty, PDF pp.24–25; Q34, Appendix E, complete transformation/composition proofs, PDF pp.44–44; literal spans in quotes.json.]  For this database the main contribution is proof reuse across both object abstraction and protocol-aware network optimization, with failed-write information flow as a concrete test of specification adequacy. Reader follow-ups are a TLA+ model of ghost-free concrete histories and slot/round synchronization; Verus/Rust ownership and typed sender/instance proofs for a finite lazy network adapter; and LLM proof-agent tasks checking prophecy coverage, pending-operation completions, stable interference assertions and exact refinement observations. No Verus, verified Rust or LLM evaluation is reported. These proposed checks should distinguish repairing printed proof outlines from discovering an actual consensus safety violation. [Evidence: Q1, §1, two contributions, PDF pp.2–3; Q14, §4, register prophecy/linearization explanation, PDF pp.12–14; Q23, §5.4, Definition4/Examples4–5/Lemma3, PDF pp.21–21; Q27, §6, Scala/Akka prototype/Theorem 4, PDF pp.24–24; Q29, §8, contributions/proposed extensions, PDF pp.25–26; literal spans in quotes.json.]
    - number: Module proofs can be reused across implementations
    - number: Crash-failure Paxos setting
    - limits: Crash-stop failures; majority quorums; reliable transport with no invented, modified, lost or duplicated base messages; fixed process identifiers; unique proposals and disjoint increasing rounds under one continuing invocation per process; correct trusted leader assumed. Handwritten proof annotations require repairs. Transformations need stable assertions, cross-slot P-monotonicity, triggering-message guards, and an observation/refinement mapping.

- Nickel: A Framework for Design and Verification of Information Flow Control Systems (2018)
    - what: Nickel verifies that deterministic sequential system interfaces and their C implementations satisfy information-flow policies. A state-dependent action-domain function and possibly intransitive flow relation define permitted influence. Noninterference compares observations after original and optionally purged action histories. Coq-proved unwinding and restricted-refinement theorems reduce system proofs to Z3 checks over individual operations. Demonstrations cover the NiStar DIFC kernel, NiKOS process isolation and a repaired ARINC 653 interface specification. Records exact purge and seven unwinding obligations, lockstep data/policy refinement, trusted policy/checker/initialization boundaries, deterministic/uniprocessor/static-policy restrictions, manual observational equivalence, all object/label/quota/scheduler mechanisms, concrete covert channels and interface redesigns, complete LOC cells, verification/effort times and the logical-time experiment. Separates mechanized metatheory from trusted SMT implementation and ARINC specification analysis from kernel implementation verification.
    - limits: §§1,3.1,3.5,6.3: Only sequential deterministic state machines, with system-call/trap handlers atomic on a uniprocessor. NiStar disables timer interrupts in kernel space and all other interrupts, using polling drivers. This does not establish security under multicore/shared-memory concurrency, weak memory or interleaved kernel handlers. §3.1 Definition1: Policy P=(D,flow,dom); domains represent exercised authority, flow is a fixed relation on domain pairs, and dom(action,state) may depend on the current thread/labels. Intransitive flow can allow p1→scheduler→p2 while forbidding direct p1→p2. A state-dependent dom does not make the can-flow-to relation dynamic; changing that relation is unsupported. §3.2 Definition2/Figures4–5: sources recursively tracks permitted direct or intermediate influence along a trace. purge is a SET of subtraces: it always permits retaining an action and permits dropping it only when its domain is outside sources, recomputing later state on the removed branch. Noninterference quantifies over every action, original trace and permitted purged trace and requires equal next-action output from the two resulting states. This is a relational security property over histories, not only memory isolation or functional refinement. §3.2: Authors establish reduction to classical noninterference for state-independent dom and monotonicity when flow is relaxed while domains/dom stay fixed. The PDF states these sanity checks but does not print their detailed proofs. Do not generalize monotonicity to changes of all policy components. §3.3 Theorem1/Figure6: Developers supply an inductive state invariant and per-domain observational equivalence. Seven universally quantified obligations cover invariant initialization/preservation, equivalence, consistency with dom, consistency with flow, output consistency, local respect and weak step consistency. Accepted invariants/equivalences are checked proof inputs, not trusted axioms; inventing an unsuitable relation can fail the checks. They remain substantial human work. §3.4 Theorem2: General refinement may introduce leaks. Supported data refinement requires same actions/outputs, related initial states, one-step preservation and equal output for related states satisfying the implementation invariant. Policy refinement additionally preserves dom for the same action/history and uses the same domain set/flow relation. No arbitrary stuttering, action splitting or nondeterministic refinement is claimed. §§3.5,6.2: Guarantees cover modeled interface observations, including logical scheduling time. Physical timing, sound, energy and microarchitectural channels outside the model are excluded. No distributed network fault, crash-recovery, availability, termination or fairness guarantee is supplied. The scheduler description assumes an infinite stream of timer interrupts rather than proving hardware progress. §4: Coq mechanizes the two general theorems. Python specifications/proof inputs and symbolic execution of C-derived LLVM IR produce Z3 obligations. Paper explicitly trusts information-flow policy, unwinding checker and Z3; implementation verification adds refinement checker and unverified initialization/glue. This is not an independently checked proof certificate for each SMT result or fully verified checker/compiler/hardware chain. §4: The intended policy is the trusted top-level requirement and needs auditing. Automated verification cannot show that an overly permissive policy matches the user’s security intention. A failed condition may reflect a genuine interface/implementation channel or insufficient invariant/observational equivalence; every solver counterexample is not automatically an exploit. §5: Determinism is obtained by exposing allocation choices as arguments, specifying allocation behavior precisely (NiStar), or hiding choices below the interface (NiKOS virtual addresses). Encrypted sequential names technically violate exact noninterference despite a claimed negligible practical leak; Nickel avoids them rather than proving probabilistic security. §§6.1–6.2: DIFC safe-flow relation combines sender and receiver ownership for controlled declassification/endorsement. Alice’s tty daemon is trusted to release secrets only to her terminal; a label proof does not verify that daemon’s declassification logic. Only threads/gates may have nonempty ownership. Fixed-size pages and finite interfaces aid SMT automation. §6.3: NiStar uses a kernel identity mapping and separate user/kernel page tables. The incomplete POSIX emulation, musl, lwIP, file-system wrapper and applications demonstrate usability; their existence does not extend the kernel proof to full application correctness, network security or durable filesystem semantics. §7: NiKOS mirrors a particular mCertiKOS interface after changing executable loading to make quotas agree. It is not a proof of every mCertiKOS variant. ARINC 653 result verifies a Python-modeled 14-operation communication interface after fixing three previously known channels; no ARINC kernel implementation/refinement proof or new bug discovery is reported. §8: Counterexamples for injected §2 examples and the round-robin workload demonstrate detection within these models. The logical-time plot supports its stated scenario, not absence of unmodeled physical channels. No Linux performance comparison, end-to-end application throughput benchmark, repeated-run confidence interval, or quantitative covert-channel capacity study is provided. §8 Figure11: LOC for interface implementation excludes shared C/x86 infrastructure; user-space LOC excludes third-party musl/lwIP. Effort figures are authors’ experience, not a controlled productivity comparison: one author six/two/one weeks for specification+verification, several researchers about six months implementing NiStar, and two months exploring framework definitions. §§9–10: Related work includes seL4/CertiKOS/Ironclad/Komodo and Covern, plus DIFC policy-synthesis tools; those citations do not mean Nickel supports concurrency or automatically synthesizes the complete policy/observational equivalence. No direct Verus, Rust, TLA+ or LLM-agent experiment. Public source pointer saved; artifact/proofs not run.

- TCS (2018)
    - title: Multi-Shot Distributed Transaction Commit
    - what: Defines Transaction Certification Service (TCS), a repeated transaction commit interface whose committed transactions must have a legal real-time-respecting sequential order for a configurable conflict-check function. States correctness of multi-shot 2PC under algebraic conditions on shard-local conflict checks, then a simulation from a crash-tolerant protocol that weaves certification into Paxos-style replication. Provides key invariants and an agreement proof; main theorem proofs are external. Gives analytic failure-free latency reductions from seven to four message delays, or three with all-to-all acknowledgements. The correctness definition linearizes the committed projection, so it imposes safety on commits without forcing every abort to be justified by the global conflict function. It separates checks against decided-commit transactions (f_s) from stronger checks against prepared-commit transactions (g_s); conditions (6)–(8) require global/local consistency, strengthening, and a cross-order commutativity property. Abstract 2PC deliberately allows forgetting and recalling final decisions: votes/order survive recovery while a leader can temporarily lose knowledge of an abort, affecting later certifications. The concrete protocol persists transaction-vote pairs at quorums, computes votes only at leaders, synchronizes recovered follower state, and allows multiple unreplicated coordinators. Invariant 7 proves one accepted vote and slot per transaction, using preservation across ballots and transaction uniqueness.
    - number: {'shard_replicas': '2f+1; quorum f+1; at most f crash-stop faults', 'failure_free_message_delays': {'vanilla_2PC_over_Paxos': 7, 'integrated_protocol': 4, 'all_to_all_variant': 3}, 'timing_scope': 'includes communication with client, analytical message delays, not measured wall-clock latency'}
    - number: TCS supports serializability and snapshot-isolation certification functions
    - number: Failure-free optimized protocol can expose a transaction decision in four message delays including the client, or three with all-to-all communication
    - number: Correctness is established through refinement theorems and invariants
    - limits: Optimistic concurrency control; each submitted transaction has completed its speculative execution and carries read/write/version information. Example writes are also reads, commit versions exceed read versions, and serializability additionally assumes read versions already exist in committed history. Global and local certification functions distribute over union via conjunction; f_s/g_s must satisfy (6)–(8). Serializability and snapshot-isolation instances are supplied, with different prepared-transaction checks. Asynchronous reliable FIFO channels; crash-stop faults. Each shard has 2f+1 processes with at most f crashes; shard groups disjoint for simplicity. FIFO is used to preserve prefix order at followers. Only safety established. Liveness is left to eventual consistent leader election or sufficiently long synchrony; retransmission details omitted. Theorem 1 proof is reference [7] Appendix A; Theorem 2 simulation proof is [7] Appendix B. Supplied 18 pages include no appendices, mechanization or executable implementation evaluation. Pessimistic/mixed concurrency control and Byzantine adaptation are proposed future work, not results.

- Auxiliary Variables in TLA+ (2017)
    - what: # Auxiliary Variables in TLA+ (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Leslie Lamport and Stephan Merz, arXiv:1703.05121v2, paper dated 27 May 2017. This is a practical account of extending TLA+ specifications with history, prophecy and stuttering variables so that state expressions can connect different representations of a concurrent system. Its central requirement is equivalence after hiding the added variables, not just that an augmented specification has some behaviors of the original. The worked snapshot result concerns safety of a simplified algorithm whose reads may never terminate. General language-independent soundness and completeness proofs are explicitly deferred to future work (Q1,Q2,Q16,Q21).  ## Reading and evidence scope  Read the complete 71-page PDF: cover, abstract, contents, printed pages 1–69, all sections 1–6.6, two numbered theorems, displayed definitions/equations, all 27 figure listings and all nine bibliography entries. The introduction says 25 figures, but numbering continues through Figure 27. There are no numbered tables, appendix or checklist. References were read as bibliography entries, not recursively followed. All 71 page images were generated; only PDF pages 19,20,27,39,40,41,44,45,46,48,49,54,57,62,66,67,68,70 were individually inspected. Complete extracted text was read for every page; this does not claim visual inspection of every page.  Read the complete official project webpage, downloaded its 317-member ZIP, and read nine selected plaintext assets whole: Prophecy.tla, Stuttering.tla, AfekSimplified.tla, AfekSimplifiedH.tla, NewLinearSnapshotPS.tla, and both MC.cfg/MC.tla pairs under AfekSimplifiedH Model_1/Model_2. Other ZIP modules, prettyprints, model copies and result/log files were inventoried but not read. The retained files are exact archive bytes; each has a SHA256 and explicit scope. No author code, TLA+ module, TLC model, TLAPS proof or checker was executed/imported; no model/checkpoint or serialized experiment data was loaded. Static symbolic traces below are reader reasoning, not rerun results. The official webpage calls these “small TLC models”; their existence does not establish universal verification (W1,C1–C9).  ## What an auxiliary variable must preserve  A TLA+ specification describes behaviors, sequences of states allowing steps that leave the observed variables unchanged. Hiding a variable uses a temporal existential quantifier: it chooses an entire value sequence and includes the logic's treatment of stuttering. It is not ordinary quantification over one state value. The paper first gives an approximate behavior intuition, then explains why stuttering makes that intuition incomplete. Definitions must be expanded with bound-name renaming; its NotUnique substitution example demonstrates how naive textual replacement can turn a valid expression into a contradiction. Recursive definitions are treated as fixed-point abbreviations, not arbitrary source-text rewriting (Q3,Q4).  A refinement mapping substitutes state expressions for abstract variables; the implementation must imply that substituted specification. Forgetting auxiliary variable a must recover the original specification: ∃a:Spec_a is equivalent to Spec. Merely proving the projection implies Spec would permit the extension to remove original behaviors, even all of them. The examples teach how to avoid this loss, rather than offering an unbounded mechanized proof for every stated transformation (Q2,Q3,Q5).  ## History: remembering information the implementation discarded  MinMax1 stores all integer inputs in a set; MinMax2 stores only extrema. Both classify each input as Hi, Lo, Both or None. The set maps readily to its minimum and maximum, but an extrema-only state cannot reconstruct all forgotten intermediate inputs. MinMax2H adds h, initially empty, recording the inputs on response steps, which supplies the missing refinement state. Infinity and MinusInfinity are distinguished noninteger values; assumptions on these sentinels matter to empty-history handling (Q5,Q6).  Theorem 1 gives a syntactic history-variable rule: augment Init with h=exp_Init; choose a disjunctive representation of Next; replace each subaction A by A conjoined with h'=exp_A; include h in the variable tuple. Expressions must obey the stated variable/context restrictions after definition expansion. Earlier discussion permits using the old history value in updates. The point of a disjunctive representation is to expose each enabled choice and its bound identifiers without changing conjunction/disjunction scope (Q6,Q7).  Theorem 2 permits replacing a subaction A by B when they are equivalent under an invariant and for every identifier in the subaction's context. Equation 3.2 is an action formula, which the paper says TLAPS can prove but TLC cannot directly check; TLC can instead compare the full temporal specifications on chosen finite models. A reported successful small comparison is not a proof for arbitrary state domains. Adding history to a specification with liveness retains the original liveness conjunct. Fairness can be expressed through the augmented actions when the required correspondence and enablement hold (Q7,Q8).  ## Prophecy: exposing a later choice earlier in a proof  SendInt examples choose a sent integer at different stages. A history variable cannot manufacture a choice still in the future. A prophecy variable p predicts it, constrains the action consuming that prediction, and can then choose a fresh prediction. Validity requires that each reachable original action has an admissible good prediction, rather than demanding a prediction for every syntactically describable state pair. Predictions that are not consumed must keep their intended relationship across steps (Q9).  SendSetUndo lets an implementation remove pending elements; its prophecy array predicts send versus undo, and the mapping retains only predicted sends. SendSeqUndo adds ordering and shifting indices. A partial injection maps surviving old prediction identities to new identities. Injectivity prevents identifying distinct predictions and accidentally excluding behaviors. The prose in §4.5 calls DomInj a “partial function”, but both the formal condition and retained Prophecy module require membership in PartialInjections. The formal requirement is the one used here (Q10,Q11,C1).  The three conditions combined in ProphCondition are: an available good prediction, a partial injection with the correct old/new domains, and PredDom containing every index whose prediction can affect the action predicate. PredDom may overapproximate those dependencies; consuming extra predictions safely allows extra refreshes. NewPSet preserves predictions only along unconsumed mapped identities and leaves consumed/new ones free. These obligations are properties of reachable actions of the original specification. The paper explains why a TLC checking module may need to end before declaring p: the original Spec does not constrain that variable. It recommends checking these conditions before the final refinement, which gives useful intermediate debugging obligations (Q11,C1).  Liveness is retained from the original specification, not blindly replaced with fairness of the newly constrained NextP. In §4.6, allowing the prophecy value infinity would permit an endless stall in the safety formula; original weak fairness excludes that prophecy choice. Thus the combined formula can fail to be machine closed: its liveness condition rules out an otherwise allowed finite prefix, rather than only requiring later progress. The authors permit this as an intermediate proof instrument and caution against using it to describe an implementation. This is a scope condition on the method, not evidence that prophecy is unsound (Q12).  ## Stuttering: matching different internal step counts  The clock example connects a 24-hour counter to an hour/minute clock by inserting 59 steps that leave the original hour unchanged. A stuttering variable stores which action/context is being expanded and a value that advances toward a bottom value. Despite the name decr, the example's order can use j+1, initial 1 and bottom 59. A refinement expression recovers the minute from the auxiliary state. PreStutter requires a predicate equivalent to the chosen action's actual enablement; otherwise it could start an unfinished expansion of an action that cannot run (Q13).  PostStutter and PreStutter insert at least one step; MayPostStutter and MayPreStutter permit zero. Correctness needs a countdown closed in its domain and reaching bottom from every domain value, a valid initial countdown on reachable original actions, and correct enablement for the pre form. StutterConstantCondition expresses finite reachability of bottom through a recursive closure over Nat. The alternative version is justified for finite Sigma. Replacing Nat and Sigma with finite TLC domains checks those instances only (Q14,C2).  There is an actual paper/archive difference. Figure 16 sets MayPreStutter's first countdown to decr(initVal), whereas the retained root Stuttering.tla sets it to initVal. A static trace with bot=0, initVal=1, decr(j)=j−1 therefore adds one pre-action step in the printed version and two in the archived version. This concerns the exact extra-step count when initVal differs from bot; it does not by itself refute auxiliary preservation or the snapshot example, which uses MayPostStutter. The archive comment also says “one fewer” while giving 42 as both the original and May operator example count. Do not assume all embedded Stuttering copies are identical to this root file (Q13,C2,D1).  Section 5.4 treats a different problem: an implementation whose original variables halt may need to represent an abstract behavior with infinitely many internal steps. A history counter on an added unchanged-vars action, plus weak fairness for that auxiliary action, supplies infinitely many hidden steps. This is distinct from accidentally making an ordinary countdown infinite. Section 5.5 retains the original liveness conjunct and states preservation of machine closure for the described stuttering construction (Q15).  ## Snapshot case: assumptions and proof structure  The generic linearizable-object specification uses BeginOp, DoOp and EndOp with visible operation interfaces and hidden object/internal state. Type and closure assumptions establish the legal operations and results; weak fairness describes eventual internal execution and return. The snapshot specialization has disjoint logical Readers and Writers, a nonempty value set with an initial value, and a register per writer. One physical process may act in both logical roles. These are atomic shared-memory specifications, with no network failure, crash recovery, weak-memory or Rust implementation model (Q16).  The example simplifies the unbounded-storage Afek snapshot algorithm. Each atomic register contains a value paired with an unbounded write number, so repeated writes never look identical. A reader collects registers twice, in arbitrary order, and returns only when the tagged collections agree. The omitted helping mechanism in the full algorithm is what would ensure termination despite interference; this simplified reader can retry forever. The paper explicitly states that only safety is satisfied (Q17,C3).  A direct state mapping to the usual linearizable snapshot fails. A reader can collect an old register value, a writer can install a new value, and the reader can still finish with the old snapshot. Its abstract read must occur before the write, but the original state then lacks the future information needed to choose that point. The authors move the hard prophecy construction to a more permissive abstract specification, NewLinearSnapshot. It records for each active reader the sequence of memory values during that read and chooses the returned member at completion. This reformulation gives the implementation a simpler history-only connection to the intermediate specification (Q18).  NewLinearSnapshotPS predicts which positive sequence index each reader will return. It adds an auxiliary step after BeginRd if index 1 is predicted, and after a write for each reader whose newly appended memory value is the predicted one. A countdown set removes one chosen reader at a time; its refinement expression assigns the corresponding atomic abstract read to those added steps. The displayed module asserts separate safety and full-spec refinement theorems. Its assumptions include StutterConstantCondition over SUBSET Readers with one-element removal; that condition must hold, so it cannot silently be extended to an infinite reader set containing an infinite countdown subset (Q19,Q20,C5).  AfekSimplifiedH instead records the memory sequence h for each active reader. BeginRd starts it, writes append to active histories, successful read completion clears it, and failed retries retain it. The mapping uses the first component of each tagged register for abstract memory, h for rstate, and wrNum/interface/tag relationships for abstract writer state. Figure 27's final statement is exactly “theorem SpecH ⇒ NLS ! SafeSpec”. This is a safety target, even though some surrounding prose and a retained source comment loosely refer to the full Spec. No general liveness result for the simplified algorithm follows from that prose (Q21,C4).  ## Evaluation and released model boundaries  The paper reports the final theorem checked by TLC in about ten hours on a circa-2012 laptop, using two readers, two writers, two register values, reader/writer symmetry, a state constraint of at most three writes per writer and two workers. This is a finite, constrained example check. No state count, memory use, scaling sweep, repeated-runtime distribution or unbounded mechanized final proof is supplied. Some local algebraic obligations have displayed TLAPS proofs, such as Next/Nxt equivalence; the final refinement theorems in the selected source files have no accompanying TLAPS proof (Q22,C4,C5).  The two retained shipped AfekSimplifiedH models use the same 2/2/2 reader/writer/value cardinalities, SpecH, TypeOKH and NLS!SafeSpec. Model_1 replaces Nat with 0..2 and constrains wrNum[i]<2; Model_2 uses 0..3 and wrNum[i]<3. Each defines symmetry as permutations of writers only. These retained settings differ from the prose's reader-and-writer symmetry and at-most-three-writes description. Strict wrNum<3 permits completed initial-state transitions only while the counter is below 3; it is not an exact encoding of allowing the third increment while remaining inside the state constraint. Without the unread historical logs/configuration, neither file can be identified as the reported ten-hour run. The observations qualify source correspondence; no rerun discrepancy is claimed (C6–C9,D2).  ## Contribution to the requested topics  This is directly relevant to proving concurrent/distributed systems correct in TLA+: its concrete value is choosing additional proof state without restricting the system's original behaviors, checking intermediate prophecy/countdown conditions, and separating safety from progress. It supplies substantial worked specifications and a concurrent snapshot refinement, rather than an empirical comparison of tools. It contains no Verus, verified Rust or LLM experiment.  Possible applications to specification/proof agents are reader proposals: require the hiding-equivalence obligation, preserve fairness and parameter assumptions, distinguish finite model checks from general proofs, and record paper/source differences. An agent that merely obtains a passing refinement implication could have strengthened away required behaviors or proved only safety. This paper explains the mathematical obligations such an evaluation should expose; it does not measure agent performance.
    - limits: Temporal hiding equivalence;prophecy reachability/injection/dependency obligations;terminating countdown/correctenablement;originalfairness;atomicsharedmemory/unboundedtags;simplifiedreadmaynotterminate;finiteconstrainedTLCnotunboundedproof;paper/archiveandmodelcorrespondencedifferences.

- Dijkstra Monads for Free (2017)
    - what: The paper derives a weakest-precondition generator automatically from a simple definition of an effect such as state or exceptions. A weakest precondition is the least that must hold before code runs for its result to be correct. With this, F* can generate proof obligations for Z3.
    - number: no experiments, runtimes or proof sizes reported
    - number: No success rate, runtime, proof size or person-hours reported

- Girault et al. MPC (2017)
    - title: Monotonic Prefix Consistency in Distributed Systems
    - what: Defines Monotonic Prefix Consistency (MPC) for replicated lists, gives a centralized toy implementation whose traces satisfy MPC, and proves that no convergent distributed implementation under asynchronous message delivery, availability, and the paper’s observability can be strictly stronger than MPC. Formalizes implementations as total update/query/message handlers, asynchronous broadcast/receive executions with causal delivery and fairness, and traces that record per-site query order/results plus an unordered set of writes. MPC requires per-site monotone reads, pairwise prefix-compatible reads across sites, and read values drawn from writes. Lemmas establish update/query availability, invisible-read deletion, fair execution limits, and convergence extension; induction then realizes every finite MPC trace and a limit argument realizes infinite traces. The paper compares MPC with causal and strong consistency and notes its observability makes MPC and strong-consistency traces indistinguishable.
    - number: MPC monotonicity/prefix/consistency conditions
    - number: finite and infinite trace construction
    - number: eventual-consistency convergence definition
    - number: all messages eventually delivered
    - limits: Assumes broadcast communication (peer-to-peer is encoded), all messages eventually delivered in infinite executions, total handlers (availability), list updates/queries with updates split from queries, unique write values (timestamps can enforce this), and a trace abstraction that hides write origin and write/query real-time order. Convergence means every write is absent from only finitely many queries. The impossibility does not directly cover operations that are both reads and writes, origin-sensitive traces, or probabilistic settings; the MPC implementation is centralized for simplicity.

- Gomes et al. SEC Isabelle (2017)
    - title: Verifying Strong Eventual Consistency in Distributed Systems
    - what: Builds a reusable Isabelle/HOL framework that formalizes strong eventual consistency (SEC), an asynchronous unreliable causal-broadcast network, and three operation-based CRDTs: Replicated Growable Array, Observed-Remove Set, and increment-decrement Counter. The abstract convergence theorem proves that distinct happens-before-consistent operation lists with the same set of operations yield the same state when concurrent operations commute, while a separate no-failure condition gives progress. The network locale has six axioms covering unique node histories, delivery cause, local delivery, globally unique message IDs, causal delivery, and valid broadcast operations; messages may be delayed, reordered, dropped, or nodes may crash permanently. RGA proof models immutable element IDs, tombstones, insertion ordering, causal validity, and proves concurrent operations commute and application never fails. ORSet uses add IDs and remove sets of observed IDs; Add/Remove commute only under a proved independence condition. Counter operations commute unconditionally. The framework is executable via Isabelle code generation; one counter implementation ran over TCP, but detailed performance/fault-tolerance evaluation was left for future work.
    - number: 3 CRDTs verified
    - number: 6 network axioms
    - number: ~620 lines network, ~380 convergence, ~775 RGA, ~270 ORSet, ~55 Counter
    - number: ORSet proof about four hours; Counter minutes
    - limits: Network assumptions are axiomatic: finite totally ordered per-node histories, unique events/IDs, causal delivery for dependencies, and valid-msg predicates supplied by the algorithm. The model uses crash-stop permanent node failure, not recovery, and does not model overlay implementation, timing, or resource limits. RGA retains tombstones; garbage collection is explicitly out of scope. The correctness claims are for operation-based CRDTs satisfying the five SEC locale obligations, not arbitrary distributed data types. Executable extraction demonstrates computability rather than performance.

- Alloy meets TLA+: An exploratory study (2016)
    - what: # Alloy meets TLA+: An exploratory study (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Macedo and Cunha compare a manually embedded hotel locking model in Alloy and TLA+, then measure checking costs with Alloy Analyzer 4.2/MiniSat and TLC 2.08. This is a one-example exploratory study. Their scope is explicit: “using a concrete example” (§1, PDF p. 2, Q6). No general translator, equivalence theorem, implementation proof or named theorem is presented. The source is arXiv1603.03599v1, 11 March2016, 14pages; a further publication venue was not established.  ## What the model assumes and checks (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The front desk is disconnected from room locks. A lock accepts its current key or the next key in its room’s ordered, disjoint keypool; accepting a newer key invalidates the old one. Guests keep keys after checkout. Initially rooms are vacant, guests hold no keys and the front desk’s last-issued key equals the lock’s current key (§§2–2.1, PDF pp. 3–5, Q7–Q11). The authors describe the retained-key behavior directly: “allows the guest to keep the room key” (§2.1, PDF p. 5, Q11). The finite model has no fairness constraint (§3.1, PDF p. 9, Q22).  The prose says a guest should enter only its own room. The printed assertion is narrower: “(Entry[t, t’, g, r, k] and some FD.occupant.t[r]) implies g in FD.occupant.t[r]” (Figure 1, PDF p. 4, Q10). It tests entry into an occupied room and allows entry into an empty room. The TLA+ property has the same nonempty-occupant guard (Figure 3, PDF p. 7, Q17). The experiment checks safety; discussion of finite-trace liveness pitfalls is not a liveness evaluation.  The counterexample has five states: initialization; g0 checks into r1 with k1; g0 checks out; g1 checks into r1 with k2; g0 enters using k1 before g1 first uses k2 (Figure 2, PDF p. 6, Q15; §2.2, PDF p. 5, Q14). It demonstrates the delay between issuing a fresh key and updating a disconnected lock. The proposed restriction is to “assume that guests enter the room immediately” after check-in (§2.2, PDF p. 5, Q14). NoIntervening therefore changes allowed environment behavior. The authors report no counterexample after adding it in the checked models; this is not a verified implementation repair or an unbounded Alloy proof.  The printed models and prose contain a reproducibility mismatch. Figure 1 includes NoIntervening and prints “check NoBadEntry for 3 but 30 Time” (Q10), while §2.2 describes 3 elements/5 Time and a counterexample (Q13), and §4 calls Figure 1 an n = 4/t = 30 model (Q30). Figure 3 also includes NoIntervening in Spec, while Figure 4 shows a counterexample (Q17–Q18). The evaluation explicitly ran with and without the restriction (Q29); the printed restricted listings alone should not be described as producing those unrestricted traces.  ## What the embedding exposes (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The study distinguishes TLA+ expressiveness from TLC’s executable subset (§3, PDF p. 6, Q16). Alloy’s static structures range over possible assignments, while a TLC constant assignment fixes one structure. To preserve the intended search, Room, Guest and keypools are initialized nondeterministically as variables and then held unchanged. Fixing exactly 3 rooms and 3 keys misses this bug because a room needs two spare keys for the successive check-ins (§3.1, PDF p. 8, Q19). This is a concrete example of a checker returning no counterexample after the search universe has been narrowed incorrectly.  Alloy’s explicit Time ordering creates exact trace scopes. The authors warn that “there is no guarantee that counter-examples occurring” in smaller traces recur at larger exact scopes (§2.2, PDF p. 5, Q13). They therefore check every scope through t and accumulate the cost. A bound below 5 misses this bug. These are finite trace checks over finite key/room/guest universes, not a parameterized proof.  The TLA+ version encodes Alloy facts through TypeInv in Next. The authors separately report checking TypeInv as an invariant and observing preservation by the actions (footnote 2, PDF p. 9, Q23); this is a reported finite-model check. TLC’s enumeration/order/next-state requirements and relational encoding issues constrain the manual embedding (§3.1, PDF pp. 8–10, Q20–Q24). No general derivation algorithm is supplied.  Stuttering affects what counts as an observed event. When a registered key is used again, Entry changes no variable: “TLC will not recognize such” steps, “identifying instead a stuttering step” (§3.2, PDF p. 10, Q25). The box-action NoBadEntry accepts stuttering independently of its inner action condition. The paper leaves general action-semantic correspondence unresolved (§5, PDF p. 14, Q37). This record identifies that limit; it does not construct a reachable unauthorized stuttering entry or refute the restricted model. NoIntervening is rewritten from a constraint on three Time instants to a state predicate Post plus the next Entry; stuttering remains allowed and no fairness is added (Figures 1/3, Q10/Q17;Q22/Q27). Deadlock reporting was disabled for finite key exhaustion and state-preserving Entry (§3.2, Q26). No progress or deadlock-freedom result follows.  ## What was measured (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The study used a 1.8 GHz Intel Core i5, 4 GB, OS X 10.10, Alloy Analyzer 4.2 with MiniSat and TLC 2.08. Tests ran “multiple times”; no repetition count, variance or raw trials are supplied (§4, PDF p. 11, Q29). TLC breadth-first and depth-first searches were tested, with depth-first limited to 100. Thus the paper’s general description of unlimited TLC trace length must not be applied to every plotted curve.  Figure 5 contains six panels and five series: TLA BF, TLA DF, Alloy, TLA En, Alloy En (PDF p. 12, Q31 and figure-evidence.json). Panels a/b vary n with Alloy t = 30; c/d use the different exact universe; e/f vary t with n = 4. The vertical axis is logarithmic seconds. All panels were inspected; no exact point timings were inferred from raster curves. No error bars or raw trials are provided.  Here n denotes exact ordered-key count and maximum room/guest counts. Alloy’s time at t aggregates checks for all scopes 1 through t; BF finds a shortest witness directly (§4, Q30). The authors report that the Analyzer finds the n = 4 counterexample at t = 5 “in less than a second in contrast to TLC’s 415 seconds” (§4, PDF p. 11, Q30). The sentence does not identify which TLC search mode the 415 seconds denotes. This is verification runtime on this model and historical setup, not application latency or a general tool speedup.  Generating static configurations is expensive. The original model has 776 initial states at n = 3 and 18,960 at n = 4. A second model fixes exactly n rooms and n guests and uses n + 2 keys, giving 108 and 960 states. The paper explicitly says these universes “are not directly comparable” at the same n (§4, PDF pp. 12–13, Q32–Q33). Do not treat the change as a semantics-preserving reduction of the original universe.  A preliminary hybrid uses Alloy to enumerate initial structures and TLC to check each fixed structure. Alloy’s symmetry breaking reduces 18,960 to 520 representatives for n = 4, generated in “less than 2 seconds” (§4, PDF p. 13, Q34). Disabling symmetry recovers 18,960. The partition loses the global shortest-counterexample guarantee and state sharing. For counterexample timings the authors “considered a” portion of the initial states after measuring the fraction that produce a counterexample; neither fraction nor portion/order is supplied (§4, Q35). With NoIntervening they consider all initial states. The hybrid improves some TLC curves; its gains over pure Alloy “are not so evident” (Q35). No proven general translator or symmetry-preservation result accompanies this preliminary comparison.  ## What it adds to the requested topic (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The direct contribution is a detailed historical Alloy/TLA+ modeling comparison with a complete small example and measured checking costs. Distributed algorithms motivate systems with rich structures and dynamics (§1, Q3), but hotel locking is the sole evaluated case. No Verus, verified Rust or LLM experiment appears.  For evaluating LLM-written specifications, I infer concrete checks from this example: compare exact versus maximum cardinalities; read the property’s guard; separate an implementation repair from an environment restriction; check event/stutter correspondence; distinguish checked invariants from imposed constraints; compare trace coverage and cumulative timing; retain sampled-state caveats. These are reader-derived evaluation lessons, not measured LLM results. The authors’ own conclusion keeps the main performance limit explicit: “the obvious caveat of the bounded search space” (§5, PDF p. 14, Q37).  ## Evidence and limits of this read (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Read all 649 layout-text lines, all 14 pages, every printed Alloy/TLA+ definition/configuration, five figures including six Figure 5 panels, Table 1 and all 11 references. Inspected seven contact sheets and the fullsize plot page. The bundle retains 38 literal line/character/page-anchored excerpts, 17 reader diagnostics, numeric evidence, visual evidence and hashes. combined.quote-source.txt is the staging source; source_files contains path strings and raw hashes are separate. No separate model/benchmark archive URL was identified in the paper or obtained. No author code, checker, solver or prover was executed, and no serialized scientific/model/results data were loaded. Results are author-reported and statically inspected, not independently reproduced. Historical language/tool claims should not be projected onto current versions.
    - limits: Disconnected front desk/locks; finite ordered disjoint disposable-key pools; guests retain keys after checkout; no fairness. Printed property checks only occupied-room entry. NoIntervening restricts environment behavior. Bounded exact Alloy trace scopes; finite TLC universes and depth-first limit 100. General translation and stutter/event correspondence unresolved. Printed scope/variant mismatch retained.
