- Multi-Paxos TLAPS (2016)
    - title: Formal Verification of Multi-Paxos for Distributed Consensus
    - what: # Formal Verification of Multi-Paxos for Distributed Consensus (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Saksham Chand, Yanhong A. Liu and Scott D. Stoller. The original paper appeared at FM 2016, pp 119–136, DOI 10.1007/978-3-319-48989-6_8. This record reads the 37-page corrected and extended arXiv:1606.01387v4, dated 11 November 2019, classified cs.DC on its first page. The inventory's 2016 year identifies the original work; it does not date the corrected version read here (Q1,Q45; arxiv.html).  The contribution is a small TLA+ specification of Multi-Paxos and its preemption extension, an author-reported TLAPS proof of per-slot agreement, and concrete strategies for managing the much larger proof. Two qualifications matter. The authors explicitly retract the earlier completeness claim: “The claim of a complete proof in [7] was incorrect” (§1 p2, Q3), explaining a PTL backend bug and their replacement type-invariant proof. Separately, this read finds inconsistent maximum axioms in the printed appendix and pinned proof files as written (Appendix C p29, Q49,Q59,Q63). Thus the record preserves the corrected checking report but does not treat checker acceptance under those retained assumptions as independent certification of the assumption-free theorem. This is a mathematical assumption problem, not an observed execution failure or a counterexample to Multi-Paxos agreement.  ## Reading and evidence scope  Read all 37 PDF pages in extracted text and rendered images: sections 1–7, all six figures, both numeric tables, all 26 numbered equations, acknowledgments, all 63 reference entries, and Appendices A–C containing the specification, invariants and hierarchical proof. The figures are algorithm/proof listings and comparisons, not statistical plots. The two PDFs, one from the local inventory and one freshly fetched from arXiv, are byte-identical. The local PDF is the corrected v4, not the 2016 proceedings text.  Fetched the author-linked DistAlgo/proofs repository's metadata, commit and recursive tree. The pin is fb4a727ed8fab39197d9a20ed066d21abbb83573, last commit 4 November 2019. Read its full README and selected plaintext specification, assumption, type-invariant and final-theorem regions of the improved Multi-Paxos/preemption modules, plus the historical FM16 TypeOK regions. The README distinguishes these versions and says improved proofs were checked with TLAPS 1.5.6 (Q56). Downloaded Basic Paxos baseline and historical modules provide retained source context; this is not a whole-repository or whole-module audit. The explicit excerpt/range inventory is in source-scope.json. Also read the complete official issue 40 body describing the coalescing bug (Q66); metadata says opened 1 April 2017 and closed 17 May 2018. Closing an issue does not identify the fix commit or certify every installed tool version.  No author code, model, TLAPS, TLC, SMT solver or proof assistant was executed or imported. No serialized data/model/checkpoint was loaded. Reader checks use file hashes, literal source slices, arithmetic and symbolic reasoning. Raw Git files retain their bytes; .lf.txt files are identified newline-normalized derivatives for character and LF-line quote pointers. The PDF extraction renders □ as 2, primes as 0 and some negated existential/membership symbols unusually; rendered pages were used to interpret the formulas. quotes.json retains extraction bytes, including such artifacts.  ## The model and the exact theorem  The system has proposer, acceptor and learner roles, which may be colocated. Constants describe proposers P, acceptors A, quorum family Q and possible values V. Ballots B and slots S are natural numbers. Membership and the quorum family are fixed: dynamic reconfiguration is excluded. The sole stated quorum condition is Q ⊆ SUBSET A and pairwise nonempty intersection. Majority, tree or matrix quorums can instantiate it once that condition is established; the paper does not construct or evaluate those alternatives (Q6,Q13,Q19).  The state contains the set msgs of every message ever sent, proposer ballots pBal, each acceptor's highest seen ballot aBal, and aVoted, a set of ballot-slot-value records retaining the highest voted ballot for each slot. Init uses no messages/votes, proposer ballot 0 and acceptor ballot −1. Next chooses one proposer or acceptor phase. Spec = Init ∧ □[Next]_vars permits an action or stuttering at each step (Q13–Q19).  The messages-as-history abstraction lets processes select any previously sent message, repeatedly and in any order. An unhandled message models arbitrary delay or loss; repeated handling models duplication. Crashes are modeled by not scheduling the crashed process, and recovery resumes its retained state. The authors require “all state (vars) is stored in stable storage” (§3.3 p11, Q20). The model has no Byzantine message fabrication, volatile-state reset, implementation of durable storage, or explicit network delivery queue. This is a safety abstraction of benign failures.  There are no fairness or eventual-delivery clauses in the displayed Spec. Repeating Init forever is a valid behavior through UNCHANGED vars and never chooses a value. This reader witness demonstrates the absence of decision progress, without challenging agreement. A quorum of functioning processes and delivery assumptions would matter for liveness; no such liveness theorem is established here (Q9,Q15,Q19; math-witness.json).  The target is Spec ⇒ □Safe, where Safe means any two chosen values for one slot are equal. The paper identifies C1 proposed-value validity, C2 agreement and C3 learner correctness, then says C1/C3 are excluded from the formal proof; §3.3 and §4.8 discuss them informally (Q5,Q22,Q33). There are no explicit learner variables/actions. Validity means a chosen slot/value occurred in a protocol 2a proposal, not that a value came from an external client or satisfied an application invariant. Equations 24/26 use sent although the state variable is msgs; the record treats this as the printed informal derivation, not a checked new theorem.  The precise Chosen definition used in the proof is ∃b ∈ B: ∃Q ∈ Q: ∀a ∈ Q: VotedForIn(a,b,s,v). VotedForIn witnesses a sent 2b message with that ballot and slot/value. The introductory equations 3/4 place ∃b inside ∀a, allowing different ballots for different acceptors. Those predicates differ; use equations 10–12 and Appendix B for the actual theorem (Q6,Q23,Q48). A separating predicate valuation in math-witness.json makes the difference explicit, without claiming a reachable protocol counterexample.  ## Minimal algorithm changes  Basic Paxos carries one latest accepted ballot/value pair. Multi-Paxos carries a set of ballot-slot-value records in each promise. Phase1a sends a ballot. Phase1b responds only to a strictly higher ballot, returning its voted-record set and recording the promise. Phase2a waits for same-ballot promise replies covering a quorum and sends at most one 2a message per ballot. It selects the maximum accepted ballot separately per slot, retaining that slot's value, and adds new slot/value pairs. Phase2b accepts a ballot at least its promise, sends a 2b reply, and replaces voted records only for the slots in that proposal (Figures 2–5, Q14–Q18).  MaxBSV filters the voted records to maximal ballots per slot; MaxSV drops ballots; UnusedS finds absent slots; NewSV chooses a set of unused-slot/value pairs with no duplicate slot carrying different values; PropSV combines old and new pairs. Ballots are shared across slots, and old unfinished slots can be reused. The authors explain that this is more involved than separately running independent Basic Paxos instances (Q15,Q17).  NewSV has no explicit nonemptiness, finiteness or client-input requirement. The empty set satisfies its uniqueness predicate. CHOOSE denotes a fixed mathematical choice for the same argument, not a newly sampled nondeterministic result each time. The formulas are useful abstractions for agreement, but do not by themselves ensure an executable finite batch or useful new work. Finite msgs does not imply finite message payloads (Q17,Q47; math-witness.json).  Preemption adds an addressed reply containing an acceptor's highest ballot when it rejects a proposer ballot. A proposer receiving a strictly higher preempt ballot updates pBal above it. Phase1a then sends pBal, and the two acceptor actions have extra rejection branches (Figure 6/Appendix A, Q34,Q35,Q47). In Phase1b equality also enters the else branch, although the prose often says lower ballot. The proposer's strict-higher guard prevents equality from raising pBal.  Two source differences constrain exact correspondence. The printed NewBal requires only b>b2; the pinned NewBallot also excludes ballots already used by a 1a message, matching §5.1's fresh-ballot explanation. Conversely, the pinned non-preemption Phase1a omits the no-prior-1a guard displayed in Figure 2. Both retain the global no-prior-2a-per-ballot guard. These differences are documented, not silently normalized or labeled agreement violations (Q14,Q35,Q36,Q47,Q57,Q62).  Actual software must allocate ballots, for example ordered counter/process-identifier pairs, rather than inspect a global history oracle. Reconfiguration, message/state reduction and failure detection are omitted. A 1b reply can contain votes for every previously used slot. There is no verified Rust implementation, code refinement or operational throughput experiment in this paper (Q21).  ## Invariants, decomposition and final argument  Inv conjoins TypeOK, AccInv and MsgInv. TypeOK constrains every message/function/record field; the corrected preemption version additionally requires finite msgs. AccInv relates each acceptor's promise and current voted records to the historical 2b votes: stored records were actually voted, every historical vote is covered by a same-slot record at least as high, and no vote lies above the current same-slot maximum (Q25,Q26,Q48).  MsgInv1b records the sender's promise, actual historical votes in its returned set, and absence of votes between that slot's stored maximum and the promised ballot. MsgInv2a says every proposal is SafeAt its ballot, a message has at most one value per slot, and there is only one 2a message per ballot. MsgInv2b ties each vote to a matching 2a message and bounds its ballot by the acceptor's highest seen ballot (Q27,Q28).  SafeAt(b,s,v) says that for every lower ballot, some quorum consists of acceptors that voted v or have promised above that ballot and never voted there. WontVoteIn expresses the latter alternative. VotedInv carries SafeAt from proposals into votes; VotedOnce states same-ballot/same-slot votes have the same value, even from different acceptors. Their role is concrete: at equal chosen ballots use VotedOnce; at unequal ballots intersect the chosen quorum with the SafeAt quorum. The common acceptor's old vote excludes WontVoteIn, leaving equal values (Q24,Q50,Q55).  The proof first establishes Init ⇒ Inv and Inv ∧ [Next]_vars ⇒ Inv′, with separate action cases and an explicit unchanged-state case. PTL then derives Spec ⇒ □Inv and transfers Inv ⇒ Safe to the temporal Safety theorem (Q29,Q52–Q55). The final temporal QED is present; its presence still does not prove the assumed mathematics consistent.  The reusable method separates unaffected old elements from each action's new message or voted-record increment. Invariance lemmas preserve old VotedForIn/SafeAt facts; the new element is proved from the sending action and local invariants. Conjuncts and actions form a mechanical decomposition, while difficult bounds and witnesses require manual facts. The paper illustrates this fully for Phase1b and MsgInv and shows the longer Phase2a proof in Appendix C (Q30,Q31,Q51,Q54).  Proof syntax matters to performance. A common acceptor voting both values in the equal-ballot case is supplied explicitly; the authors report about 90 seconds reduced to a few milliseconds, calling it a 10,000× local speedup. Replacing direct record membership by an existential record with field equalities made another proof proceed after the original stalled for one or two minutes. These are local development observations, with no repeated-run distribution or uniform ablation (Q32). Tuple access is a concrete proof-engineering choice, not evidence of an algorithm runtime speedup.  ## The disclosed checker bug and the remaining assumption problem  The authors report that historical PTL coalescing could “prove” a primed state formula from its unprimed assumption outside a valid temporal context. It incorrectly accepted TypeOK′ under TypeOK ∧ [Next]_vars. The official issue's example falsely preserves xyz=0 while Next permits increasing xyz. Choosing increment 1 from xyz=0 gives xyz′=1, refuting that preservation claim symbolically (Q36,Q66). Historical FM16 modules retain PTL at primed message-type leaves; improved excerpts instead give explicit action proofs (Q60,Q64,Q65).  The repair proves type preservation for all phases. Preemption needs a fresh larger natural ballot to exist. With finite msgs, the used 1a ballots form a finite set T; max(T∪{received ballot})+1 supplies a larger unused witness. The authors added IsFiniteSet(msgs), proved preservation under message addition and supplied this witness. They report initial TypeOK proof growth 54→93 lines and 75→138 lines for the two variants; subsequent improvements reduce those to 54 and 77 (Q36,Q37,Q52).  A separate issue remains in the retained text. Appendix C calls two axioms “straightforward consequences” but writes MaxInSet as ∀S∈SUBSET N: Max(S)∈S (Q49). Substitute S=∅: it asserts Max(∅)∈∅, false for every CHOOSE result. The pinned source writes the domain as (SUBSET Nat) \\ {}, which subtracts no elements and still includes ∅ (Q59,Q63). Removing the empty set as an element would require {{}}. That change alone would still leave unbounded infinite subsets: applying MaxInSet and MaxOnNat to Nat gives a natural maximum k, but k+1 is a larger natural. A sound maximum lemma needs nonempty finite or bounded domains and proof that each use meets the restriction.  These are static mathematical witnesses to inconsistent assumptions as written, not TLAPS runs, discovered new protocol executions, or an assertion that every leaf exploited inconsistency. The retained files cannot alone support an assumption-free machine-certification claim. Agreement may be true and the intended invariant argument may be repairable, but repairing and replaying it is outside this literature read. The disclosed PTL repair does not address these maximum axioms. This distinction is particularly useful when evaluating automated proof agents: green obligations and completed syntax must be accompanied by an audit of explicit axioms and the actual target.  ## Evaluation and quantitative evidence  The evaluation compares Basic Paxos, Multi-Paxos and Multi-Paxos with Preemption, plus older incomplete and corrected/improved proof variants. It measures text size, proof-tree structure, lemma reuse, obligations and proof-checking cost, not network performance or application throughput. Table 2 specifies a four-core Intel i7-4720HQ at 2.6 GHz, 16 GB RAM, 64-bit Ubuntu 17.10 and TLAPS 1.5.6. Earlier elapsed times from 1.5.3 are marked separately. No raw timing logs, repeat counts, variance or matched cross-tool benchmark are provided (Q40–Q42).  The corrected new rows report specification lines excluding comments of 52, 55 and 74; proof lines 310, 787 and 831; obligations 239, 779 and 825; total CPU seconds 40, 175 and 180; total elapsed seconds 14, 51 and 90. CPU time and elapsed time are distinct; the total CPU values are close to the headline “about 3 minutes,” whereas the elapsed values are 51/90 seconds. Adding Multi-Paxos slots costs only three specification lines but 477 proof lines and 540 obligations; adding preemption costs 19 specification lines, 44 proof lines and 46 obligations. The per-slot data structure creates most of the proof expansion (Q40,Q41).  The new proof trees have maximum level 10 and degree 17, against 7 and 3 for Basic Paxos. Multi-Paxos/preemption use five/six invariance lemmas 32/35 times and three proofs using set increments each. §4.6's five lemmas used 27 times describes an earlier count; Table 2 provides the updated reuse counts (Q31,Q41). Reflowed appendix counts, including 528 proof lines excluding comments, are layout alternatives, not another method or independently smaller proof.  The three improvements factor message predicates, merge similar unchanged-state cases and remove leaf scaffolding TLAPS can establish automatically. Reported reductions for preemption include 75 lines from predicate factoring, 27 from merged cases, 110 from about 25 unnecessary-obligation instances and 61 from 22 TypeOK instances. The full repaired proofs shrink 1049→787 and 1117→831 lines; those are 25%/26% reductions from the repaired versions. Old incomplete rows marked ^^ are not valid proof-performance baselines (Q3,Q37–Q41).  Table 1 gives improved TypeOK elapsed times 8/25 seconds; Table 2 gives 7/24. Their old preemption TypeOK elapsed entries also differ, 21 versus 22. Preserve these printed differences rather than inventing a run-variance explanation. numeric_tables.json retains every metric, alternate count and footnote marker. The report that adding preemption took less than an hour explicitly excludes TypeOK; it cannot be used as total authoring time for the corrected proof (Q34,Q37,Q41).  ## Relationship to the requested research topics  This is directly useful for TLA+ and formal verification of distributed protocols: concrete fault abstraction, per-slot agreement, quorum reasoning, stronger invariants, hierarchical proof feedback, old/new-set decomposition and sensitivity to formula representation. Its strongest evaluation lesson is the explicit correction history, reinforced by the separate maximum-assumption findings in this read.  The work contains no Verus, verified Rust or LLM-agent experiment. Transfer to those topics is a reader proposal: preserve the exact property and fault model; measure changes in executable specification separately from proof scaffolding; give agents small action/conjunct obligations with explicit contexts; distinguish finite-instance testing from parameterized proof; and check axioms, omitted obligations, tool versions and source correspondence before reporting success. No agent success rate or Rust implementation result follows from this paper.  Related-work proof-size comparisons with IronFleet, Verdi, EventML/Nuprl, EPR and other systems are author descriptions of differently scoped work, not controlled evidence that TLAPS is uniformly faster or requires fewer lines. The separate executable-program work in reference 45 reportedly found a safety violation missed by testing/model checking, but that example is cited rather than reproduced and verified here (Q43,Q44). Those leads can be followed as separate full reads.

- Practical verification framework for preemptive OS kernels (2016)
    - title: A Practical Verification Framework for Preemptive OS Kernels
    - what: Presents a Coq-mechanized framework that models preemptive-kernel API correctness as contextual refinement from a practical C subset, with abstract specifications, concrete interrupt-aware semantics, a relational concurrent separation logic, and proof tactics. It verifies scheduler, timer interrupt, synchronization APIs and priority-inversion freedom in commercial μC/OS-II. The framework separates application state from kernel state, gives small-step sequentially consistent semantics for task continuations and x86-8259A interrupt state, and uses a high-level language with nondeterministic/configurable scheduler relation. Relational assertions connect concrete memory, abstract state and remaining abstract command; ownership-transfer invariants account for nested interrupt levels. A soundness theorem is proved by compositional simulation. PIF is stated using original task priorities and proved for a simplified μC/OS-II mutex under no nested mutex use; verification required minor defensive changes because contextual refinement quantifies over arbitrary clients.
    - number: about 1,300 C lines verified, 63% of frequently used APIs
    - number: 216,000 lines of Coq implementation/proofs; 5.5 person-years
    - number: Coq framework package about 216,000 lines and compilation about 16 hours on 3.6 GHz/32 GB machine
    - number: proof-script to verified-C ratio about 26:1
    - limits: The machine model is tailored to Intel 8259A-style interrupts and sequential consistency; adapting other processors requires new interrupt rules. The C subset excludes function pointers and virtual memory. Contextual refinement assumes arbitrary clients, unlike original μC/OS-II precondition assumptions, motivating code checks. PIF theorem assumes no nested mutexes, no deadlock and scheduler consistency. Task creation/deletion was unfinished. Proof effort and data-structure abstraction (bitmap waiting queues) are substantial.

- Alloy MCA Verification (2015)
    - title: An Alloy Verification Model for Consensus-Based Auction Protocols
    - what: Builds a machine-readable Alloy model of Max-Consensus Auction (MCA) protocols, separating bidding and agreement mechanisms from policy choices, and checks convergence to conflict-free allocations. The model represents asynchronous bid and allocation vectors, timestamps, network topology, physical/virtual network mapping, policy predicates, and bounded message-processing traces. The authors optimize Alloy encoding by replacing ternary relations/integers with binary relations and custom value signatures. Assertions check eventual consensus across scopes; counterexamples show oscillation when bids are non-submodular and agents release/rebid subsequent items, plus denial-of-service via rebidding lost items.
    - number: 259K SAT clauses reduced to about 190K by abstraction changes
    - number: representative assertion runtime reduced from about one day to less than two hours
    - number: Result 1: consensus holds for checked policies except non-submodular utility plus release/rebid policy
    - number: Result 2 finds no consensus when rebidding on outbid items is allowed
    - limits: Finite Alloy scopes and SAT bounded analysis cannot establish unbounded correctness; checking SAT is generally exponential. Agents are intended to cooperate, and convergence requires no rebidding on already-lost items and generally submodular utilities. The attack analysis is model-level; preventing malicious rebids and larger instances are left open.

- Automated and Modular Refinement Reasoning for Concurrent Programs (2015)
    - what: Introduces civl, a Boogie extension that proves multi-layer refinement of concurrent shared-memory programs. Cooperative yield semantics, mover-type automata, location invariants, linear permissions and modular ownership reduce preemptive concurrency reasoning to automated per-procedure obligations. A high-level atomic action is refined by a procedure whose paths contain exactly one implementing atomic block; other blocks preserve visible state. Mover types and the yield sufficiency automaton justify cooperative reasoning for sequentially consistent preemptive execution. Linear variables guarantee distinct thread identifiers or disjoint permission sets across threads. Location invariants are checked for sequential correctness and non-interference; modules own disjoint globals so quadratic checks are per module. A soundness theorem chains layers from safe high-level program to low-level implementation. Implementation infers verification conditions and atomic blocks, computes graph simulations in O(n·m), and supports variable hiding/data refinement.
    - number: 32 benchmarks, 17–539 LOC, total 20 s
    - number: GC ~2,100 Boogie LOC, six refinement layers, 60 s total
    - number: GC 54 s sequential/non-interference; remaining checks insignificant
    - number: yield-simulation algorithm O(n·m) in control-flow nodes/edges
    - limits: The paper focuses on sequential consistency and leaves weak memory models future work. Safety guarantee requires high-level safety, valid refinement, sequential/non-interference invariants, linear typing, pairwise commutativity, sufficient yields, and infinitely frequent yields on infinite executions. The full formalization is in a technical report. Modules require strict global-variable ownership. Location invariants cannot refer to other threads and can be incomplete. The approach verifies safety/refinement, not liveness or arbitrary functional properties.

    - what: Checks a set of temporal-logic requirements before any model exists: finds every smallest group of requirements that contradict each other, finds requirements implied by others, and suggests new requirements to cover behavior the set leaves open. Tested on random formulas, a plane-landing example, and four Honeywell avionics models.

Completeness needs a human to separate assumptions, required and forbidden behavior, and was not tested on Honeywell data. Honeywell requirements are confidential.

    - title: Use of Formal Methods at Amazon Web Services
    - what: Engineers describe using TLA+ and the TLC model checker on ten AWS designs. TLC is a tool that tries every reachable state of a design to look for bugs. Before checking design changes, they wrote down the failure modes and the environment.

- Distributed Protocols and Heterogeneous Trust: Technical Report (2014)
    - what: # Distributed Protocols and Heterogeneous Trust: Technical Report (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Sheff, van Renesse and Myers use information-flow labels to reason about distributed protocols whose participants disagree about which machines may crash or lie. The central guarantee is participant-specific: a failure that violates one participant’s assumptions must not damage participants whose assumptions still hold (§1.1, Q2). They generalize Bosco Fast Consensus and the OARcast/Nysiad construction. Evidence consists of mathematical/prose proofs, an offline SMT threshold search, and small consensus simulations.  I read arXiv:1412.3136v1, stamped 9 December 2014 (Q1). The local PDF and the official v1 PDF retrieved on 19 October 2026 are byte-identical. Both have 2018/10/9 footers; those footers do not establish a new arXiv version or publication date. The report has 26 pages, six figures, Algorithm 1, Appendices A–B and 40 references, all read. There are no tables.  ## Model and assumptions (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The network is asynchronous and permits all participants to communicate. Resending is assumed to make delivery reliable unless sender or receiver fails. A faulty participant cannot forge a correct participant’s source identity; signatures are suggested (§2.1, Q3). Almost-sure termination adds the assumption that repeatedly sent concurrent messages eventually arrive in every possible order. Section 5 phrases this as random ordering. Appendix B.5 additionally needs a selection function with nonzero probability for each input value (Q36). These assumptions matter: ordinary asynchronous message delivery alone does not establish the termination result.  A **guru** functions correctly and has true trust assumptions. A **chump** functions correctly but has false trust assumptions. The authors state that “chumps may receive ‘wrong’ results” (§2.2, Q4). Most guarantees concern gurus, rather than every participant that happens to execute correctly. The requirements also exclude some trust configurations: arbitrary mutual distrust is not automatically supportable (Q2).  The labels adapt the Decentralized Label Model. Each integrity or availability policy names an owner and a principal trusted to influence content or availability. Principals may be machines, people or compound authorities. Conjunctive principals combine authority; disjunctive principals describe alternative authority. Label ordering and join/meet track how message combinations trade integrity against availability (§§3–4, Appendix A, Q5–Q8, Q32, Q41). “More restrictive” labels correspond to weaker usable assurances. The paper describes static reasoning before execution; it does not report a compiler enforcing these policies or a complete implementation proof.  The simple synthesizers make the tradeoff concrete: accepting the first of three messages gives high availability but permits any sender to influence the value; requiring three identical messages increases integrity but makes each sender necessary. Contradiction can produce the detectable unavailable value `none` (§4.1, Q7, Q42). Survivor sets and failure-prone sets can be represented as labels separately for each principal’s crash and Byzantine expectations. For four participants tolerating any single failure, the system principal is the disjunction of the four triples, while the attacker principal is the disjunction of the six pairs (§4.2, Q8).  ## Consensus construction and proofs (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Algorithm 1 broadcasts a proposal, waits for a sufficiently available received set, and examines sets of identical messages. A high threshold D permits decision; a lower threshold C forces a value into the underlying consensus. Otherwise a selection function chooses from received values. The underlying protocol can recursively be another fast-consensus round. A deciding participant then broadcasts its decision once more so that the remaining participants can proceed consistently (§5, Q10–Q11). A deterministic selector may let an attacker prevent convergence, so randomness is advised (Q12).  The stated properties have different scopes (§5, Q9):  - Agreement: any two deciding gurus choose the same value, and repeated decisions by a guru agree - Unanimity: if **all correct participants** initially propose v, a deciding guru chooses v - Validity: if a participant decides and **all participants are correct**, some participant proposed the value - Progress: gurus eventually decide if the underlying consensus terminates - Termination: recursive fast consensus eventually decides for gurus with probability 1 under the extra random-network assumptions  Validity is deliberately weak: it does not guarantee a nonfaulty participant proposed the value in an execution containing Byzantine faults. Probabilistic termination is not a bounded worst-case or expected-round theorem.  For homogeneous assumptions, c is the total number permitted to be unavailable and b is the number permitted Byzantine, with c ≥ b. The received-set threshold is |R| ≥ n−c, decision requires |S| > (n+c)/2+b, and change requires |S| > (n−c)/2. The requirement is n > 3c+2b, reducing to n > 3c for crashes only and n > 5b when c=b. Tightness is attributed to earlier Bosco work, not newly proved here (§5.1, Q12).  For heterogeneous assumptions, each participant supplies its own availability threshold and change/decision labels. Constraints (1)–(6) ensure that sender failures bound message availability, tolerated failures cannot block a sufficient set, progressing parties can continue without blocked parties, decision thresholds imply change, liar/wrong sets cannot redirect other votes, and one guru’s decision forces other correct parties to enter the underlying consensus with that value (§§5.2–5.3, Q13–Q18). This is an explicit set of conditions to satisfy, rather than a theorem for every label assignment.  Appendix B proves agreement separately for decisions in the same round and different rounds. It uses constraints (4)–(6), unanimity/agreement of the underlying protocol, and continued participation by deciding parties. Unanimity propagates the common correct proposal through the underlying protocol. Fault-free validity follows from selecting received values and authentic message sources. Progress uses constraints (1)–(3) to ensure sufficient messages arrive (Q33–Q35).  The termination argument deserves a precise limit. Appendix B.5 concludes: “in any pair of consecutive rounds, there is a non-zero probability a guru will decide. Therefore, each guru, with probability 1, eventually decides” (Q36). Nonzero probabilities that can decrease across rounds do not alone justify almost-sure termination. The appendix does not explicitly supply a uniform conditional lower bound, independence, or a complete stochastic fairness argument. Its intended strong network/selection assumptions may provide that missing step. This read flags a proof-detail dependency; it does not supply a counterexample to the protocol.  Two notation concerns are visible in the PDF, not extraction artifacts. Equation (3) quantifies R ⊆ P but uses S in its body (p.12, Q16). Figure 3’s A_sys row for Bob/Carol/Dave/Eve prints an integrity-policy superscript I where the text describes an availability threshold (p.13, Q17). The record preserves both; it does not silently repair the formulas or claim a checked encoding.  ## Five-party case and measured results (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Alice tolerates arbitrary failure by Bob and no other failures. Bob, Carol, Dave and Eve tolerate Byzantine Alice plus at most one crash among themselves (§§1, 2.2, 5.4, Q19). The searched thresholds let these five reach consensus. A homogeneous envelope must allow one Byzantine failure and one additional crash, hence b=1, c=2 and n>8: nine participants (Q21). Five versus nine exploits different trust structure; it does not violate a lower bound for an identical failure model.  Alice sometimes must listen to Bob despite distrusting him. If Bob is Byzantine, the others’ trust assumptions fail and Alice alone may remain a guru. But when Bob is merely crash-prone, Carol and Dave might change their votes after hearing Bob and Eve. Alice therefore waits for Bob or Eve to preserve agreement in executions where those other participants are gurus (§5.4, Q20). This illustrates why individually “ignoring everyone distrusted” is not sufficient.  The authors simulated 1000 instances for homogeneous nine-party Bosco and the corresponding heterogeneous five-party protocol. Each round’s message ordering and the selector’s choice are uniform random. All parties begin with different values. They compute the mean probability of a participant deciding after each round and show standard-error bars (§5.4, Figs.4–5, Q21–Q23). Exact curve ordinates and error magnitudes are not tabulated. The captions say “in each round,” while the prose says “after each round” and the curves increase toward one; this record retains reported round quantiles without reconstructing the raw event distribution.  The explicit reported outcomes are (§5.4, Q21, Q24):  - no failures: heterogeneous median 3 rounds and 95th percentile 5; homogeneous median 5 and 95th percentile 8 - Alice Byzantine and Bob crashed: heterogeneous 95th percentile 4; homogeneous median 6 and 95th percentile 12  The heterogeneous median in the fault scenario is not explicitly stated in the prose, so none is invented. Byzantine Alice’s behavior is specific: she proposes a fresh never-before-seen value every round. This is a chosen attack, not comprehensive Byzantine exploration. The paper attributes the faster convergence partly to reduced contention among fewer active participants. It evaluates the whole customized configuration, without isolating label adaptation from the participant-count change. It reports no deployed throughput, wall-clock communication latency, hardware, random seed or raw per-run result file in the paper.  For threshold synthesis, the requirements are encoded in quantifier-free bitvector logic and Z3 is used offline. Four- to six-party protocols reportedly take “a few minutes for each protocol” (§5.4, Q25). There are no exact timings, hardware or Z3 version. Checking given labels is described as polynomial, placing the search problem in NP; precise hardness remains future work. This is not a claim of NP-completeness or polynomial-time synthesis.  Section 5.5 adds a distrusted client as a participant so the best case is client→servers→client, without waiting for inter-server communication. The construction is subject to threshold requirements and explicitly trades bandwidth for latency; there is no implementation timing experiment (Q26).  ## OARcast, Nysiad and artifact evidence (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  OARcast has a designated sender and echoers. Messages carry author and relay signatures plus sequence numbers. Echoers rebroadcast unseen sender messages, cease on conflicting sender-signed values for a sequence number, and deliver only after lower sequence numbers and a sufficient identical-echo threshold. Constraint (7) prevents two gurus from delivering different values for the same position (§6.1, Q27–Q28). If the sender is correct, no correct participant (even a chump) can simulate a different ordering. Liveness further requires a correct sender and enough uncompromised echoers. The report says its correctness proof “proceeds exactly as in [13, 14]”; it does not give a separate full OARcast appendix proof (Q28–Q29).  Nysiad simulates a crash-tolerant system of deterministic state machines. Each simulated machine gets an OARcast sender. A host accepts a simulated message only when its own simulation independently computes an identical message and the matching OARcast delivers it. Gurus thus simulate the same execution and ordering. A faulty designated sender can block availability without independently changing the derived message value/order; each host’s own integrity and availability still constrain its simulation (§6.2, Q30). Footnote 2 limits OARcast integrity to uniform guaranteed delivery and ordering; application content can have further constraints. A 3f+1 crash-Bosco conversion using a deterministic selection function is described as 3f+1 Byzantine consensus with best-case two sends, compared with Fast Byzantine Paxos “by this very simple metric” (Q30). Nysiad has no empirical evaluation here.  The linked Dropbox ZIP was recovered successfully. It contains only `pysmt/pylisp2.py` (9444 bytes) and `pysmt/pysmt.py` (40332 bytes). Both were read in full, 1151 LF-delimited lines including terminal empty lines. They are a Lisp parser and Python-2-style SMT converter/solver wrapper. The converter eliminates supported finite datatypes and Boolean interfaces, turns functions into bitvector lookup tables, unrolls quantifiers, emits QF_BV headers, and defaults to invoking `z3`. It includes local threshold optimization and HTML analysis modes (Q37–Q40). Its help explicitly notes that compactly declared bitvector lengths can entail exponential file expansion (Q37).  No README, protocol constraint input, simulator, dependency pin, historical commit identifier or experiment output is present in this particular archive. The source supports the existence of a QF_BV preparation and solver workflow, but cannot alone reproduce the five-party thresholds or plotted simulations. No author code was imported or executed; no solver, prover, verifier or compiler was run. Only reader extraction, rendering, hashing, arithmetic and validation scripts were used. Simulation and synthesis outcomes remain author-reported.  ## Relevance to the requested topic (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  This paper adds a useful formal-specification question for distributed systems: whose assumptions must hold for each guarantee? Its concrete contributions are a label-based description of integrity/availability, explicit threshold constraints, conditional consensus proofs, and a worked heterogeneous deployment comparison. It separates agreement from delivery availability and shows how Byzantine behavior can deny progress without corrupting the eventual ordering.  There is no Verus, verified Rust, TLA+ or LLM-agent experiment. Transfer to agents writing specifications/proofs is reader inference: state trust and scheduling assumptions per participant, retain the weak validity quantifier, check threshold side conditions, and distinguish prose proof, SMT synthesis, simulation and implementation verification. A useful evaluation would test multiple trust graphs and adversarial schedules while pinning constraint encodings and the simulator. Confidentiality, self-interested behavior and general automatic protocol transformation remain future work (§7, Q31).  The quotation identifiers used above are defined in quotes.json and mirrored in the JSONL record; each definition includes its exact source span and section. Reading Figure 3’s vote combinations as distinct participants, its intended change threshold is two matching votes and its decision threshold three among Bob, Carol, Dave and Eve; Alice’s own vote does not contribute to those counts (Q17). This helps explain why she can need Bob’s message despite distrusting him.
    - limits: Asynchronous reliable source-authenticated messages and participant-specific constraints (1)–(6); guarantees mainly for gurus. Recursive almost-sure termination adds random/fair message ordering and full-support selection. Appendix B.5 leaves its stochastic justification terse. Validity applies only when all participants are correct. Nysiad assumes deterministic state machines. No mechanized full protocol or implementation proof is reported.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/1412.3136v1)
    - record id: `distributed-protocols-and-heterogeneous-2014`
- Dialog Model Checking Fault-Tolerant Algorithms (2012)
    - title: Starting a Dialog between Model Checking and Fault-tolerant Distributed Algorithms?
    - what: Introduces a parameterized modeling framework for threshold-guarded fault-tolerant message-passing algorithms. Processes are extended control-flow automata with symbolic parameters, threshold guards, asynchronous interleavings, message buffers and faulty-process nondeterminism; instances become Kripke structures checked in LTL without next. The paper makes the formalization problem explicit (pseudocode semantics and hidden environmental assumptions), defines parameterized process skeletons and CFA operational semantics, and translates a family of broadcast algorithms under Byzantine, symmetric, omission and crash faults. Spin/Promela experiments systematically vary resilience and specifications: expected resilience boundaries are recovered, and impossible configurations produce violations.
    - number: Byzantine n>3t; symmetric/omission n>2t; clean crash n>t
    - number: valid cases Spin-verified; n=7,t=3 Byzantine relay specification violates as expected
    - number: n=5 omission with three faulty processes violates C and R
    - number: n=11 experiments exhaust memory
    - limits: Experiments are bounded finite instances and do not establish parameterized correctness; n=11 runs out of memory. The framework initially targets asynchronous threshold algorithms, with partial synchrony and round models left for future work. Correctness depends on explicit resilience, delay, fault and scheduling assumptions.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `dialog-model-checking-fault-tolerant-algorithms-2012`
- TLAPS (2012)
    - title: TLA+ Proof System (TLAPS) and its temporal support
    - what: # TLA+ Proofs (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Denis Cousineau, Damien Doligez, Leslie Lamport, Stephan Merz, Daniel Ricketts and Hernán Vanzetto, 2012. This record reads the 16-page extended article, arXiv:1208.5933v1, dated 29 August 2012. Its first-page footnote says a shorter version appears at FM 2012; reference [7] gives LNCS 7436, pp 147–154. The official TLAPS publications page links both a longer article and a Peterson model (Q1,Q3,Q30,Q37). The shorter proceedings paper was not separately read. The inventory's cs.LO label is not the PDF's classification: the PDF says cs.SE (Q1).  The article explains how to write, decompose, check and maintain TLA+ proofs through Peterson's two-process mutual exclusion algorithm. Its central result is an illustrated invariant proof, backed by reported TLC and theorem-prover checks. The most important qualification is explicit: the displayed final temporal QED is “proof omitted” (Figure 6, Q19), because the then-current TLAPS could not check temporal reasoning (Q11). The state-level proof obligations are reported checked; the complete temporal theorem is justified by standard induction rules in the explanation, rather than entirely checked by the presented tool. Liveness is outside the demonstrated verification.  ## Reading and evidence scope  Read all 16 PDF pages in text and rendered images, including sections 1–6, all six figures, every displayed formula/proof and all 24 bibliography entries on pp 14–16. There is no appendix, checklist, numbered equation, numerical plot or printed table. The six figures contain the PlusCal algorithm, its TLA+ translation, high-level proof, inductive invariant, preservation-proof outline and hierarchical proof. This is one mutual-exclusion theorem target shown at successive levels of detail, not six independently evaluated algorithms.  The local inventory PDF and freshly fetched official arXiv PDF have identical bytes. Read the official TLAPS publications page and two complete plaintext Peterson modules from the official tlaplus/tlapm repository, pinned to commit 901ae51ed5224f88d43a1a02969cd9be4ed1b48d, dated 2026-10-16. One module is the publications-page artifact path; its bytes match the separately fetched live official model. The second is under doc/fm2012. These are retained example artifacts, not an audit of the current TLAPS implementation. The repository tree was inventoried to locate them. Other examples, proof-manager/backend implementation, modern tutorials, the shorter article and recursively cited case-study papers were not read. The modern repository pin does not establish the exact executable versions used in 2012.  No author code, PlusCal translator, TLC, TLAPS, Isabelle, Zenon or SMT solver was executed or imported. No model, serialized dataset or checkpoint was loaded. The reader checks are symbolic reasoning, file comparisons and metadata/arithmetic checks. quotes.json contains exact passages with section/page, LF lines and character slices; figure-evidence.json preserves every figure listing. numeric_tables.json explicitly records zero tables and the limited quantitative claims. The extracted listings often render □ as 2 and a prime as 0; quotes retain those literal extraction bytes, while the analysis uses the symbols confirmed in the rendered PDF. source-scope.json, source-files.json and hashes.json document the reading boundary and bytes.  ## Specification and assumptions  TLA+ combines linear-time temporal logic, Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory with choice, and modules. The proof language adds hierarchical natural-deduction structure. The Toolbox is an Eclipse-based interface to both TLC model checking and TLAPS proof checking, so the same formulas can be tested and used in deductive proofs (Q2). These descriptions concern the paper's 2012 tools; they are not a current feature inventory.  The example has exactly two process identifiers, 0 and 1, with Not exchanging them. Shared variables are a Boolean-valued flag function, a turn value initially 0, and the generated program-counter function pc. Initially both flags are false and both counters are at a0. Each process repeatedly sets its flag, gives the other process the turn, checks the other's flag and turn through two separate labeled tests, executes the critical-section skip, then clears its flag (Q5–Q8).  Atomicity is specified by PlusCal labels: an action runs from one label to the next. Figure 2 exposes seven action formulas per process, and Next existentially selects one process's action. Reads/writes and control changes have exactly the interleaving semantics of these formulas. The example supplies no weak-memory, compiler-reordering, machine-instruction, crash, recovery or network-message model. Correctness of a compiled Rust or C implementation would need a separate correspondence argument. The critical section itself is represented by an atomic skip; the paper's property is that the two program counters never simultaneously equal cs (Q6–Q9).  The full specification is Spec = Init ∧ □[Next]_vars, with vars collecting flag, turn and pc. Brackets admit either a Next transition or a step leaving all listed variables unchanged. A behavior is an infinite state sequence. Stuttering is included to simplify refinement, not to assert progress (Q7,Q8,Q10).  There is no fairness clause in the displayed Spec. A reader witness repeats the initial state forever: Init holds, every step satisfies UNCHANGED vars, and neither process ever reaches cs. Thus that exact Spec does not imply eventual entry into a critical section. This is compatible with its mutual-exclusion safety property. The paper says liveness can be expressed and proved in TLA+, but does not give the extra progress assumptions or a liveness proof here (Q4; math-witness.json). It would be wrong to infer starvation freedom merely from the safety theorem.  ## What is proved, and the checking boundary  The target is Spec ⇒ □MutualExclusion. Its three state-level obligations are Init ⇒ Inv, Inv ∧ [Next]_vars ⇒ Inv′, and Inv ⇒ MutualExclusion. Figure 3 and §3.1 explain how propositional reasoning plus temporal induction and monotonicity yield the temporal theorem from these premises. They then state: “TLAPS does not yet handle temporal reasoning, so we omit the proof of the qed step” (Q11). Figure 6 calls itself the “complete hierarchical proof” while visibly retaining that omission (Q19). This is a disclosed tool limitation, not evidence that the mutual-exclusion theorem is false.  Inv is TypeOK ∧ I. TypeOK constrains pc to seven labels per process, turn to {0,1}, and flag to Boolean-valued functions on {0,1}. I says a process from a2 through a4 has its flag set. A process at cs or a4 excludes the other from both cs and a4; if the other is still in either waiting-test location, turn favors the process already in that region (Q12). The stronger control-state conditions explain why the simple target predicate is preserved even across the entry/exit protocol.  Two small reader checks clarify the formula. Init satisfies TypeOK, while all control-state antecedents in I are false at a0. If pc[0]=cs, I for process 0 excludes pc[1]=cs; if pc[0]≠cs, MutualExclusion already holds. These checks cover initialization and implication to the target. They do not replace the preservation proof or independently certify the tool. TypeOK describes 7²×2×2²=392 possible valuations; that is a domain-size calculation, not the paper's reported 58 reachable states (math-witness.json).  The paper first reports TLC checking execution errors and MutualExclusion over all 58 reachable states of this particular finite model. It also checks inductiveness by replacing Init with Inv, model checking Inv ∧ □[Next]_vars. Starting from every state satisfying the candidate invariant tests preservation beyond the original reachable-state set. For more complicated or infinite-state specifications, the authors recommend small-instance model checking as a way to find errors, and explicitly limit its ability to establish general inductiveness (Q9,Q12). A successful finite instance is not a universal proof for arbitrary process counts or data domains.  The illustrated checks also reveal the human work. A leaf proof citing only Init and Inv fails because TypeOK and I remain uninterpreted. Supplying their definitions lets TLAPS prove initialization. Expanding definitions for the entire preservation obligation still fails with the default provers and produces a 65-line obligation. CVC3 through the SMT backend proves it “in a few seconds” (Q13–Q15). No hardware, precise solver versions, timeout, repeat count or full timing log accompanies that statement.  To demonstrate hierarchy rather than rely on this easy SMT success, the authors deliberately decompose the preservation proof using Zenon and Isabelle. The SUFFICES step replaces [Next]_vars with Next only after separately proving the unchanged-variable case. It then splits Inv′ into TypeOK′ and I′. Zenon proves the SUFFICES reduction; Zenon fails on the stated TypeOK′ proof but Isabelle succeeds. Both fail on I′ without further decomposition (Q16–Q17).  The next level introduces an arbitrary process j, names its invariant subexpression as I!(j), and picks the process i that performs the Next action. Instead of seven action-by-action cases, two cases, i=j and i≠j, suffice for the displayed example. Explicit facts and definition expansions then discharge the leaves. A case step's own name denotes its case assumption, so citing that name within its proof is not circular citation of its conclusion. NEW-bound assumptions and the PICK membership fact are automatically available within scope; ordinary facts still need the stated context discipline (Q18–Q19).  ## Proof management, reuse and trust  The Proof Manager translates proof steps into sequents with a goal and context of symbols, assumptions and previously established facts. It tracks context changes and sends leaf obligations to backends. It records success, failure, cancellation and omission. The interface can check one step, theorem or file, show the exact failing obligation, navigate back to the producing proof, and hide irrelevant portions of the hierarchy (Q21–Q22).  “Proof obligations are independent of one another” describes the ability to check conditional obligations in any order (Q21). It does not make a theorem valid while required assumptions or earlier steps remain unproved. The distinction matters in this very example: the state-level work can be developed independently while the final temporal QED is omitted. Global completion requires all needed obligations and the correct bridge to the stated property.  Fingerprinting stores an obligation's status under a canonical representation. Bound variables become de Bruijn indices, so renaming them does not change the representation; unused symbols and hypotheses are removed from the context. This supports reuse after proof reorganization, moving a step or removing and later reinserting it. Status can also be queried without launching proofs (Q23). The paper describes the mechanism but gives no hash-collision theorem, implementation-level soundness proof or controlled cache benchmark. Correct reuse depends on the representation preserving all relevant logical dependencies. This record did not inspect the fingerprint implementation.  The backends described are Isabelle/TLA+, Zenon and SMT solvers. Default attempts use Zenon, then Isabelle; the SMT route translates obligations into SMT-LIB for CVC3, with native Yices/Z3 alternatives. Isabelle is the most trusted backend. Zenon can emit Isar proofs that Isabelle checks. The paper says certification of SMT proof traces is future work, so a successful SMT result should not be silently counted as Isabelle-certified evidence in this article (Q3,Q15,Q27).  The trusted components also include translations and critical Proof Manager transformations such as distributing prime through addition. Users can disable fingerprints and reprove obligations, which addresses cache reuse concerns without removing the other trust dependencies. The authors further warn that an incorrect target formula can make a proof meaningless and that incorrect assumed mathematical facts can invalidate an apparent result. TLC can test those exact assumed formulas on instances; that supplies error-finding evidence, not a proof of every assumed mathematical statement (Q27–Q28).  ## Evaluation and concrete larger cases  The evaluation is a worked proof tutorial plus development experience, not a randomized user study or comparative prover benchmark. Its concrete local evidence comprises the 58-state TLC result, the failed/repaired leaf proofs, the 65-line preservation obligation, backend-specific outcomes and the qualitative CVC3 timing. There are no statistical estimates, repeated trials, task success rates, hardware budgets or uniform comparison across proof assistants (Q9,Q13–Q19).  Larger uses are named: Byzantine Paxos, the Memoir security architecture, and Pastry lookup/join protocols. These are cited case studies, not complete proofs reproduced in this article (Q20). A cited 1100-line invariance proof has 100 non-leaf steps, illustrating the scale at which the hierarchy acts like nested lemmas (Q22). The claim that 100 conventional linear lemmas would be unreadable is the authors' judgment, not a measured readability result.  The strongest maintenance anecdote concerns Lamport's Byzantine Paxos proof. He initially spent several weeks over several months on its safety proof while debugging TLAPS. Later he discovered the algorithm lacked the desired liveness property, changed it, repaired small bugs found by TLC, and re-established safety in a day and a half, about 12 hours of actual work. About three quarters of the new proof obligations had already been proved (Q24). This shows reported reuse during a real revision and separately shows why safety does not imply liveness. It does not report a newly checked liveness proof, an exact obligation count, or an isolated speedup attributable solely to fingerprinting.  The related-work discussion compares declarative proofs and interface features with Mizar/Isar, reuse with KIV, set-theoretic/refinement approaches with Rodin/Event-B, and high-level specifications with VCC/Why source-code verification. These are architectural comparisons and historical author assessments, not present-day benchmark rankings (Q25). The conclusion also lists proof constructs not exercised in the Peterson example, including existential witnesses, local definitions and facts supplied implicitly to backends. Temporal reasoning and improved sequence support are planned work in the article (Q26,Q29).  ## Static artifact findings  Both pinned Peterson modules contain the full PlusCal comment, generated actions, candidate invariant, ISpec for inductiveness testing, and two variants of the same safety proof. One variant uses SMT for preservation; the other expands the hierarchy. Both end their top-level theorem with PROOF OMITTED (Q31–Q35). This independently preserves the article's disclosed checking boundary in a usable example artifact. The source exists; no dynamic proof certificate or successful replay is supplied by this read.  The artifact retains ProcSet and uses USE DEF ProcSet, matching the paper's footnote about a definition inlined in Figure 2. MutualExclusion uses negated conjunction, logically equivalent to the paper's disjunction. Source leaf proofs often cite Inv, Next and proc(i) directly where Figure 6 cites numbered steps; the explanatory text explicitly permits such fact references (Q7,Q17,Q32–Q35). The FM directory copy differs only by an unused NeverCS predicate and DEF versus DEFS spelling in one leaf proof (Q36 and retained file comparison). Neither difference establishes an algorithm change or runtime failure. The two files remain exact downloaded bytes.  ## Contribution to the requested research topics  This is directly relevant to formal verification of distributed systems and TLA+: it gives a concrete path from a labeled algorithm to a temporal specification, tested invariant, structured proof obligations, backend checks and maintained proof status. It makes both the specification assumptions and proof-completion boundary visible. Its larger-system anecdotes link those mechanisms to distributed protocols while leaving those cited proofs outside this article's detailed evidence.  It contains no Verus or verified-Rust development, LLM agent, generated-specification benchmark or automatic invariant-synthesis evaluation. Its transferable design ideas are therefore reader proposals: give proof agents explicit sequents and scoped context; use checker feedback to distinguish missing definitions from difficult obligations; split proofs without dropping stuttering or other cases; cache normalized obligations only with valid dependencies; and report omitted steps and certification routes alongside success counts. The article does not measure those proposals with LLMs. Its most useful evaluation lesson is to keep a correct formalization, state-level proof success, temporal completion, liveness, certificate checking and proof-reuse evidence separate.
    - number: Peterson mutual-exclusion example; proof obligations are split among back-end provers
    - limits: Exactly two processes and labeled atomic shared-state interleavings with arbitrary stuttering; no fairness/weakmemory/compiledimplementation correspondence. Reported state-level proof checks leave temporalQED explicitly omitted in2012tools. No liveness proof or fullTLAPSsoundness proof. Larger case studies cited; maintenance/timing evidence is anecdotal.2026modulepin does not identify2012executables.

- Effectively Nonblocking Consensus Procedures Can Execute Forever - a Constructive Version of FLP (2011)
    - what: # Effectively Nonblocking Consensus Procedures Can Execute Forever: whole paper reading (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Robert L. Constable's Effectively Nonblocking Consensus Procedures Can Execute Forever – a Constructive Version of FLP is arXiv:1109.3370v1,15 September 2011. Its frontmatter also gives July 17, 2008, a footnote describing changes through August 2011, and a May 20, 2021 print date. These identify the saved version rather than three independent research results. All 17 pages were read: Sections 1–2, their eight subsections, all lemma/theorem/corollary statements and proofs, Figures 1–7, the One Step Program, acknowledgments and ten references. All five contact sheets and individual4,10,14,16 were inspected. Original, local and official PDFs match. No author code, scientific-result artifact or formal checking tool was executed or loaded. [Evidence: Q0, frontmatter/abstract/dates, PDF pp.1–1; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The contribution is a constructive variant of the Fischer–Lynch–Paterson impossibility argument. Instead of only proving that some indecisive run exists, the paper assumes a computable nonblocking witness and explains how to calculate every finite prefix of an infinite indecisive execution. Its distinction is between an existence proof and an effective construction, not a new consensus protocol. Classical nonblocking alone is handled using excluded middle/choice; the stronger effective premise supplies the computational content. Historical claims that earlier proofs lack this construction are Constable's assessment of the cited work, not a literature-wide priority finding established here. [Evidence: Q1, §1.1, constructive versus classical problem, PDF pp.2–2; Q6, §1.4, full theorem/lemma/corollary summary, PDF pp.5–6; Q12, §2.2, full constructive/classical corollaries, PDF pp.10–11; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The model is deterministic binary consensus with crash failures, reliable FIFO message channels and unbounded asynchronous delay. Processes have private state and messages in queues; executions are serialized into individual actions. Fairness requires every message to a nonfailed process eventually be read and every enabled action at such a process eventually be taken. Uniform inputs require the corresponding decision, and all deciding processes agree. The main construction concerns tolerating one failure, although no process actually crashes in its resulting fair run. Randomized termination, known-delay synchrony and Byzantine fault behavior are outside the stated theorem. [Evidence: Q3, §1.2, complete computing/fairness model, PDF pp.3–4; Q4, §1.3, consensus/nonblocking/witness, PDF pp.4–4; Q11, §2.2, CFLP theorem/full proof, PDF pp.10–10; Q12, §2.2, full constructive/classical corollaries, PDF pp.10–11; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Constable defines the premise in §1.3: “P is called effectively nonblocking if from any reachable global state s of an execution of P and any subset Q of n − t nonfailed processes, we can find an execution α from s using Q and a process Pα in Q which decides a value v ǫ B.” Effective nonblocking is a total computable function wt(s, Q): for every reachable state s and every chosen set Q of n−t nonfailed processes, it returns a finite execution using Q, its deciding state and a process deciding a bit. This is an existential progress witness, not a promise that every continuation progresses. It is stronger information than an abstract claim that some extension exists. A constructive proof of nonblocking can supply such a witness; the paper cites Nuprl/Coq's constructive setting and Mark Bickford's nonblocking formalizations as motivation. It does not present a checked Nuprl or Coq development of this complete CFLP proof. [Evidence: Q4, §1.3, consensus/nonblocking/witness, PDF pp.4–4; Q2, §1.1, Nuprl/Coq and blocking motivation, PDF pp.3–3; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The paper's v-possible predicate asks whether one of finitely many wt(s, Q) results decides v. For one-failure tolerance this can be decided by n witness evaluations, one for each Q_i excluding P_i. That does not decide all possible future decisions. Effective bivalence instead includes explicit finite executions deciding 0 and 1; arbitrary bivalence remains undecidable according to the source. In particular, a witness may choose only 0-valued continuations even when another execution decides 1, so failure to find a 1 in those n selected witnesses does not establish univalence. The construction retains a known opposite-valued path rather than replacing that information by a finite sample of witness answers. [Evidence: Q5, §1.3, v-possible/effective bivalence, PDF pp.5–5; Q8, §2.1, finite witness-decision test, PDF pp.6–6; Q11, §2.2, CFLP theorem/full proof, PDF pp.10–10; Q13, §2.3, initialization/replay and witness independence, PDF pp.11–12; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Initialization starts at all-zero inputs and flips one process at a time until all-one inputs. It chooses the first initialization s_k for which some witness decides 1. At s_(k−1), the witness excluding P_k decides 0. Replay that finite execution from s_k: only P_k's initial state changed and it takes no step, so the other processes still decide0. Together with the 1-witness this constructs a bivalent initial state. With three processes the chain is 000→100→110→111; the first transition revealing 1 supplies the distinguished excluded process. This is ordinary explanatory substitution, not an executed trace. The witness itself need not return identical paths on the two inputs; the proof explicitly uses replay rather than assuming witness extensionality in unseen state. [Evidence: Q9, §2.1, full initialization proof, PDF pp.7–7; Q13, §2.3, initialization/replay and witness independence, PDF pp.11–12; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The One Step Lemma takes explicit bivalent paths and a process P_i, and constructs a reachable state b′ with two opposite-valued deciding paths using only Q_i. Its first proof walks backward along a finite 1-path, queries wt at predecessor states and uses agreement to transfer decisions across an isolated P_i action. The commutativity lemma permits executions of disjoint process sets to be replayed in either order while delaying cross-set messages. This depends on the asynchronous message model and on the selected actions remaining executable; it is not a general claim that arbitrary shared-memory operations commute. Figures 1–4 show the two decision branches and commuting diagrams. [Evidence: Q10, §2.1, full One Step proof/Figures 1–4, PDF pp.7–10; Q14, §2.3, both commutativity lemmata/proofs, PDF pp.12–12; literal spans in quotes.json.]  David Guaspari's alternative proof counts P_i actions on one branch of a fork. A fork is a pair of finite paths ending in different decisions; an i-fork has one branch avoiding P_i, and a full i-fork has both branches avoiding it. At the last P_i action, query the witness on Q_i. If its decision matches the branch already avoiding P_i, commutation produces a full i-fork after that action. If it matches the other branch, replace the suffix by the witness path and strictly decrease the number of P_i actions. Finite descent yields the One Step Lemma. The returned path length and witness-running time are unbounded by this paper; the finite descent measures distinguished-process actions, not total execution cost. [Evidence: Q15, §2.3, fork definitions/modification lemma, PDF pp.13–14; Q16, §2.3, alternative One Step proof, PDF pp.15–15; literal spans in quotes.json.]  CFLP then cycles through processes and selected enabled actions. Before executing P_i's action, it obtains a state b′ bivalent via Q_i; replaying the two Q_i decision paths after that action preserves bivalence by commutativity and agreement. If the action was already executed while reaching b′, it is not repeated. Repeating this procedure supplies longer indecisive prefixes while giving each process turns. The source asserts the resulting execution is fair and failure-free. A formal extraction should explicitly enumerate pending messages/enabled actions so process-level round robin also discharges its stronger message/action fairness definition. The printed proof does not give that queue-selection policy or an explicit total function for the n-th output action. This is an extraction/detail obligation, not a counterexample to classical FLP. [Evidence: Q11, §2.2, CFLP theorem/full proof, PDF pp.10–10; Q3, §1.2, complete computing/fairness model, PDF pp.3–4; Q12, §2.2, full constructive/classical corollaries, PDF pp.10–11; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The source separates three consequences. A purported deterministic single-failure terminating consensus algorithm supplies the needed nonblocking witness and contradicts the constructed run; §2.2 calls this FLP corollary constructive because its result is a contradiction. Strong FLP instead assumes nonblocking without a computable witness and invokes choice/excluded middle to obtain an infinite run. The Blocking corollary states that a procedure terminating on all failure-free executions must have a reachable state where one process's failure prevents any decision. That last existence result is explicitly classical; it does not give an automatic algorithm locating the blocking state. Nontermination and a blocked state are different: the constructed run retains deciding continuations throughout. [Evidence: Q12, §2.2, full constructive/classical corollaries, PDF pp.10–11; Q2, §1.1, Nuprl/Coq and blocking motivation, PDF pp.3–3; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Printed details need correction before turning this presentation into executable or checked code. The summary's CFLP statement requires more than two processes, whereas its initialization lemma and FLP corollary use two or more, and the main theorem omits a count restriction; the record preserves that statement mismatch instead of claiming the displayed CFLP statement alone proves the two-process case. The fork proof's final inequality prints i−len(φ′)<i−len(φ′), a false self-comparison; its preceding lemma correctly compares the new fork with the old one. Figure 7's One Step Program is informal pseudocode with inconsistent s/S and path/process notation and unstated initialization details. It describes computational content, not a ready-to-run verified adversarial scheduler. [Evidence: Q6, §1.4, full theorem/lemma/corollary summary, PDF pp.5–6; Q11, §2.2, CFLP theorem/full proof, PDF pp.10–10; Q15, §2.3, fork definitions/modification lemma, PDF pp.13–14; Q16, §2.3, alternative One Step proof, PDF pp.15–15; Q17, §2.3/Figure 7, full program/conditions/invariants, PDF pp.15–16; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The abstract's “undefeatable attacker” is an in-principle consequence under the theorem's premises. The construction needs access to the relevant global state, a correct total witness and control of an asynchronous schedule with arbitrarily long finite delays. The paper reports no network deployment, attack implementation, performance measurement, experimental protocol instance or complexity bound for this suggestion. It does not establish a practical denial-of-service method for protocols whose liveness depends on eventual synchrony or randomness. The full proof is a written mathematical proof; related Nuprl nonblocking work is reported but no complete CFLP proof artifact is provided or audited here. [Evidence: Q0, frontmatter/abstract/dates, PDF pp.1–1; Q2, §1.1, Nuprl/Coq and blocking motivation, PDF pp.3–3; Q11, §2.2, CFLP theorem/full proof, PDF pp.10–10; Q17, §2.3/Figure 7, full program/conditions/invariants, PDF pp.15–16; literal spans in quotes.json.]  For this database the paper makes progress assumptions explicit and turns a proof witness into a scheduler construction. Reader follow-ups are a TLA+ statement separating fairness, eventual decision and mere existence of deciding extensions; a constructive Coq/Nuprl realization of the witness and finite-descent transformation; Verus/Rust proofs of finite path replay, FIFO state updates and witness-interface contracts; and LLM proof-agent tests that distinguish selected-witness decidability from bivalence, constructive output from classical existence, and unbounded computation from a measured attack. No Verus, verified Rust, TLA+ model or LLM evaluation is reported in this source. [Evidence: Q4, §1.3, consensus/nonblocking/witness, PDF pp.4–4; Q5, §1.3, v-possible/effective bivalence, PDF pp.5–5; Q11, §2.2, CFLP theorem/full proof, PDF pp.10–10; Q16, §2.3, alternative One Step proof, PDF pp.15–15; Q12, §2.2, full constructive/classical corollaries, PDF pp.10–11; literal spans in quotes.json.]
    - limits: Deterministic Boolean consensus; reliable FIFO asynchronous message passing; one-crash tolerance; total computable progress witness for each reachable state and permitted surviving subset; explicit finite opposite-decision paths. The construction assumes global-state and scheduling access. Fair action/message enumeration and productivity require explicit extraction details; printed process-count and pseudocode discrepancies remain qualified.

- AOGG distributed graph systems (2010)
    - title: Modeling and Reasoning over Distributed Systems using Aspect-Oriented Graph Grammars
    - what: Defines aspect-oriented graph grammars (AOGGs) for modular system-wide policies over graph-rewriting distributed systems, gives an encoding into a single typed graph grammar, and uses AGG critical-pair analysis to detect conflicts and dependencies in aspect weaving. The base model uses DPO graph rules for a client-server system; aspects are rule-level spans with pointcuts, interfaces and effects. Log and security aspects respectively add execution tracing and read/write permissions. Weaving is non-reentrant and ordered, yielding a concrete graph grammar. Encoding operations R1–R3 represent rule spans as typed nodes/edges, and S1–S3 encode concrete rules/advices; the resulting typed graph lets AGG analyze both execution and weaving. In the case study, ExecuteSET/ExecuteGET conflict on shared data, while log/security advices are conflict-free, implying aspect-order confluence for that model.
    - number: four node kinds in base type graph
    - number: two aspects in encoded example
    - number: R1–R3 and S1–S3 encoding stages
    - number: one ExecuteSET/ExecuteGET conflict pair reported
    - limits: The semantics uses nondeterministic rule/match choice and DPO/SPO graph-transformation conditions; aspect order may matter when advices conflict. The encoding’s behavioral relation to the original base system plus aspects is identified as future work. Results are a small illustrative client-server grammar, not a quantitative distributed-system benchmark or proof of general confluence.
