- Verifying Safety Properties With the TLA+ Proof System (2010)
    - what: FirstpublicTLAPSrelease:hierarchical safety-proof management,backendtranslation/trust and Peterson/Bakery/incompletePaxoscases. # Verifying Safety Properties With the TLA+ Proof System (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Kaustuv Chaudhuri, Damien Doligez, Leslie Lamport, and Stephan Merz, IJCAR 2010, LNCS 6173, pp142–148; arXiv:1011.2560v1, 11 November 2010. The venue/pages are confirmed by Stephan Merz's author bibliography: “booktitle = {5th Intl. Joint Conf. Automated Reasoning (IJCAR 2010)}” and “{142-148}” (Q17; complete bibliography block in quotes.json). Read all seven PDF pages, §§1–5.1, acknowledgements and all 15 references; all seven page renders were inspected individually. The paper has no figures, tables, numbered theorems, benchmark suite, or appendix. This is the saved outside-index arxiv_all PDF, whose hash matches the reconstructed 352-path inventory. No author implementation or proof files were executed/imported, and no checkpoint or serialized data was loaded. Consultation and independent review are complete; the IJCAR bibliographic correction was applied. REVIEW.md records scope and root-only checks. The fetched author-hosted PDF has the same extracted body after removing the arXiv margin and declared glyph/spacing differences; author-copy-comparison.json records the comparison. No additional scientific content was inferred from that normalization.  The paper describes the first public TLAPS release, its proof-obligation processing, and three example developments. It directly concerns TLA+ verification of concurrent and distributed systems. Its reported support covers invariance and step simulation but excludes liveness proofs. TLA+ the language can specify liveness; this release's prover cannot establish those properties. These are historical 2010 capabilities, not a statement about today's TLAPS. The authors state: “It handles almost all the non-temporal part of TLA+ as well as the temporal reasoning needed to prove standard safety properties, in particular invariance and step simulation, but not liveness properties” (§1 p1, Q1).  ## Proof representation and what must be trusted  Specifications and proofs share the TLA+ language. A hierarchical proof decomposes a theorem into steps and ultimately leaf obligations, each citing facts and definitions. TLAPM flattens imported/instantiated modules and sends processed leaves to backends. A user can work with incomplete proofs and choose which steps to check; the release uses pragmas to exclude proofs from checking. Thus a successful check of selected leaves does not itself establish completion of every claimed theorem. This completion check is a practical evaluation requirement inferred from the workflow. Q2–Q4 preserve the architecture and selection semantics.  The backend called Isabelle/TLA+ implements the constant fragment as a dedicated Isabelle object logic. The paper explains that embedding into existing HOL/ZF logics was inconvenient because TLA+ is untyped, with no proposition/term distinction. Its single value type, classical first-order logic, equality, conditionals, Hilbert choice, Zermelo–Fränkel set axioms, and primitive function objects supply the mathematical basis. Natural numbers are introduced using a Peano structure whose existence follows from ZF; arithmetic is developed recursively. Tuples/sequences and records are functions, strings are character sequences. Ordinary “typing” facts become proof hypotheses: the paper's n+0=n example needs a numeric n. Boolean conjunction is described as Boolean-valued even on arbitrary arguments. These are semantics/proof-library facts reported by the authors, not a reader audit of the object logic. Q9/Q10 give the details.  Zenon searches for first-order proofs and emits Isabelle/Isar scripts checked against Isabelle/TLA+. The paper explicitly says “Zenon is therefore not part of the trusted code base of TLAPS” (§4.2 p5, Q11). That exclusion applies to this certificate-checking route; it does not make the entire TLAPS pipeline untrusted. TLAPM's priming rewrite engine is expressly trusted. The generic SMT-LIB backend and Cooper Presburger arithmetic backend are expressly non-certifying in this release. “Certifying veriT” is a planned integration even though veriT is already among solvers tried through the generic non-certifying interface. Distinguishing solver brand from integration mode prevents a false claim that all SMT results were certified. Q3/Q5/Q11/Q12 preserve these boundaries.  Backends reason over constant expressions. TLAPM moves primes through ordinary mathematical operators and leaves constants unchanged: (u=v+2c)' becomes u'=v'+2c when u,v are state variables and c is constant. Double priming is rejected by the syntactic analyzer. Defined operators are hidden by default and expand when made usable. Modal operators may fail substitution of equals: knowing u=42 now does not imply u'=42. Reader illustration: a transition from u=42 to u'=43 satisfies the former but violates the latter. Usable non-substitutive definitions are expanded; hidden applications become opaque hashes, conservatively losing some valid equivalences such as O(e∧f)=O(f∧e). This is a stated completeness tradeoff, not a collision/security guarantee or a proof of implementation soundness. Q5/Q6 give the transformation and example.  Positional and labelled references resolve subexpressions before later processing. In O(x,y)=x=20y+2, O(3,4)!2!1 selects 20·4. A label l on y·20 can keep referring to that term after unrelated surrounding syntax changes. The feature supports concise proof reuse, but the paper supplies no maintenance experiment. Default discharge tries Zenon, then Isabelle automation; pragmas can request tactics or other backends, often indirectly via a named standard theorem such as SimpleArithmetic. Q7/Q8 give these cases.  The generic arithmetic route rewrites obligations into SMT-LIB AUFLIRA, often dropping assumptions outside that theory, with conservative sort detection for bound variables. Reader inference: proving a conclusion from fewer assumptions is logically stronger, so omission alone is not a soundness defect; translation and sort handling must still preserve meanings. The paper reports successful use of Yices, CVC3, Z3, veriT, and Alt-Ergo, without solver-specific counts, timings or comparative accuracy. It does not prove the soundness/completeness of these translations in this note. Q12 records the exact scope.  ## Development practice and concrete evidence  The authors first check finite instances with TLC and then attempt proofs. On leaf failure, TLAPM prints usable hypotheses and the goal, expanding usable definitions. Their reported common failure is omission of a needed fact/definition. They split difficult obligations into lower-level assertions, verify the local qed, then prove those assertions in any order. The example case split proves (P1∨…∨Pn)⇒Q by each Pi⇒Q. This is a concrete incremental authoring/debugging workflow, not evidence that partial obligations are sufficient. The Toolbox then only hid proof portions; broader integration was future work, while an Emacs mode provided hierarchical viewing and proof selection. Q13/Q14 preserve this historical distinction.  The authors warn that unchecked “obviously true” assumptions can be wrong and recommend checking such data-structure facts with TLC. Finite model checking can expose counterexamples; it does not universally prove an unbounded assumption. That latter qualification is a reader inference, not a claim that the authors measured assumption-error rates. The claim that specification errors are likelier than TLAPS soundness bugs is also the authors' engineering judgment, with no quantified comparison in this paper. Q3/Q14 carry both source statements.  Section 5.1 gives these author-reported developments, without the actual full proof scripts:  - two-process Peterson mutual exclusion: about 12 PlusCal lines and a proof of about 130 lines - N-process Bakery with atomic reads/writes: an 800-line proof - high-level Paxos: 100 TLA+ specification lines covering a consensus abstraction and two refinements; first refinement completed with a 550-line proof, second mostly proved and estimated to exceed 1,000 lines  The Paxos case is explicitly incomplete, including the acknowledgement to Jean-Baptiste Tristan. The estimated second-proof length is not a measured completed artifact. Neither case length establishes checking time, human time, bug counts, proof coverage across implementations, liveness, or executable-code correctness. The N-process claim concerns the mathematical example as reported, not a finite-N experiment independently rerun here. Q15/Q16 preserve the complete cases.  The library is reported to contain more than 1,400 lemmas/theorems, about 200 pages of pretty-printed Isar. These size measures and the three case descriptions support feasibility of the release; no theorem about the verifier itself, controlled user study, performance comparison, memory result, automated success rate or systematic experiment is supplied. The full development files and checker outputs were not inspected/reproduced in this reading. “It has not turned out to be difficult once the proof idea has been understood” is an author experience report, rather than a measured proof-automation guarantee (§5.1 p7, Q16).  For the database, this is direct foundational evidence for high-level safety proofs and TLA+ proof tooling. It contributes explicit trust distinctions, partial-proof management, priming/substitution pitfalls, finite-model-checking practice, and a deliberately incomplete Paxos case. It has no Verus, verified Rust, or LLM experiment. A proposed use as an evaluation reference for proof agents is reader inference: record the precise theorem and unchecked assumptions, require every obligation, distinguish certificate-checked from trusted solver routes, and report whether the intended specification captures the requirement.
    - limits: Historical2010safetyfragment;Isabelleobjectlogic,TLAPMtrustedrewrites and noncertifyingarithmeticpaths. Selected/incompleteproofspossible;uncheckedassumptionsandspecificationvalidityremainhumanresponsibilities. Fullproofscripts/checkerresultsnotread/rerun;noquantitativetime/memory/successbenchmark.

- Woodcock survey (2009)
    - title: Formal Methods: Practice and Experience
    - what: Survey of industrial formal-methods practice combining a new questionnaire covering 62 projects, earlier surveys, eight highlighted industrial project families, and proposals for reusable verification experiments in the Verified Software Repository. Explains formal methods across requirements, specification, architecture, design, implementation, testing and maintenance. The new survey collected structured questionnaires November 2007–December 2008; 56 project reports came from involved individuals and six from literature. Reports application areas, size bands, techniques, expertise/training, perceived time/cost/quality effects and intentions to reuse. Compares model-checking uptake across decades using Fisher exact tests. Eight highlighted families cover Transputer floating-point microcode, railway control, Mondex, AAMP processors, Airbus SCADE, the Maeslant barrier, Tokeneer and Mobile FeliCa. They expose learning/reuse costs, translation and optimization bugs outside proof boundaries, requirements clarification, selective formalization, and tool limitations. Discussion favors choosing rigor by component/property, robust supported tools, familiar design-language interfaces, and integration with existing development processes. Repository proposal requires explicit proof assumptions and limits and reusable theory/artifacts; pilots include file stores, FreeRTOS, auctions, pacemakers and hypervisors alongside Mondex/Tokeneer.
    - number: Model checking in surveyed projects: 13% in the 1990s versus 51% in the 2000s; two-tailed Fisher p=0.003.
    - number: Survey: 62 projects; 56 participant reports and six literature reports; about half supplied code size.
    - number: Figure 6 quality improvement: 92% of cases reported an increase, none a decrease; this is a perception measure.
    - number: Tokeneer: 9,939 core Ada lines plus 16,564 SPARK annotation/comment lines; 260 reported person-days, excluding independent testing contribution.
    - limits: Historical 2009 synthesis, not a current adoption census or controlled causal evaluation. Open-invitation sampling favors the authors' contacts, includes few model-checking contributions and underrepresents disappointed stakeholders; Europe dominates. Outcomes mostly report participant beliefs, with no comparison population or reliable universal cost model. Most projects lack relative time/cost evidence; reported quality improvement is not a measured 92% defect reduction. Significance tests concern this selected sample and do not remove selection/confounding bias. Industrial project claims summarize cited reports and were not independently reproduced. Some formal work covers models, selected code, selected properties, or parts of certification rather than a complete implemented system. Tokeneer's tabulated 4% system-testing effort excludes independent SPRE testing; the text estimates 25% as more representative. The new survey's individual responses and underlying dataset were not inspected. Recovered STFC author copy is 40 substantive pages with placeholder journal metadata, versus DOI/Crossref 36 pages; repository record explicitly links this title/file to DOI 10.1145/1592434.1592436, whose short metadata title is Formal methods. Database keyword/researcher estimates are explicitly rough historical estimates, not reliable counts of the field.

    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1145/298595.298598); [notes: Requirements methods predating LLMs](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_6_intent_to_spec.md); [notes: papers](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_agents_20261007.md)
    - record id: `property-specification-patterns-1998`
- Four Dark Corners of Requirements Engineering (1997)
    - what: The paper separates three things: facts about the environment that are wanted, facts about it that are assumed, and behavior the machine can enforce at its interface. The spec plus domain knowledge must imply the requirements.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1145/237432.237434); [notes: Requirements methods predating LLMs](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_6_intent_to_spec.md)
    - record id: `four-dark-corners-of-1997`
- Formal Methods: State of the Art and Future Directions (1996)
    - what: Surveys formal specification, finite-state model checking and machine-assisted theorem proving, using industrial case reports to explain their benefits and limits. It then proposes research, tool, education and transfer priorities. Formal methods alone do not guarantee correctness [Q1]. Contrasts rich sequential specification languages (Z, VDM, Larch) with concurrent notations; explains exhaustive checking and counterexamples versus proof by axioms and inference [Q4,Q7]. Discusses composition, decomposition, abstraction, reuse, hybrid systems and integration of methods. Tool criteria include early benefits, incremental gains, reuse, integration, ease of use and focused analysis [Q8–Q10]. Industrial examples include CICS, CDIS, C130J, TCAS II, SCI, NewCoRe/ISDN, HDLC, arithmetic circuits, processor instructions and microcode.
    - number: CICS: estimated 9% development-cost reduction [Q2]
    - number: CDIS: about 0.75 faults per thousand lines, described as two to ten times better than selected comparison projects [Q3]
    - number: NewCoRe: 145 requirements, about 7,500 SDL lines, 112 errors; approximately 55% inconsistent requirements [Q6]
    - limits: A 1996 survey and research agenda, not a new controlled comparative experiment. Industrial costs, fault rates and successes are cited case reports; they do not establish a general causal effect. Model checking faces state explosion [Q5]; theorem proving can cover infinite domains but may require substantial human guidance. Tool strengths and modeling assumptions must be stated [Q9]. No modern Verus, TLA+ or LLM evaluation.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://www.cs.columbia.edu/~wing/publications/ClarkeWing96.pdf)
    - record id: `formal-methods-state-of-1996`
- The Semantics of TLA on the PVS Theorem Prover (1996)
    - what: Defines Lamport's Temporal Logic of Actions (TLA, a logic where a system is a set of allowed state changes and properties are 'always' and 'eventually' statements) inside the PVS proof assistant. The hard part is hiding a variable (the existential quantifier over program variables), which needs a choice operator.
    - number: 17 pages
    - number: one worked example: two counters x, y with weak fairness on both increment actions
    - limits: Definitions only. It proves one lemma (stut equals the stuttering-removal function, Theorem 1) and gives no verified system, proof rules, or tooling. Report is marked unpublished and not peer reviewed.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.82444/warw.33641848); [notes: ## The semantics of TLA on the PVS theorem prover (1996)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `the-semantics-of-tla-1996`
- Linearizability (1990)
    - title: Linearizability: A Correctness Condition for Concurrent Objects
    - what: Defines linearizability: each operation takes effect at a single instant between its call and its reply, and the history must match some allowed sequential history.
    - limits: By itself it does not promise that requests finish, that data survives crashes, or that two retries count as one operation. That is the notes' inference.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- 3SF model checking (2025)
    - title: Technical Report: Exploring Automatic Model-Checking of the Ethereum specification
    - what: The authors translate the Python spec of Ethereum's 3-slot-finality (3SF) consensus protocol into TLA+ and use the Apalache model checker to test Accountable Safety: if two conflicting chains are both finalized, at least one third of validators can be shown to have broken a voting rule. They then add hand-made abstractions and re-encode the problem in SMT (CVC5) and Alloy to check larger instances.
    - number: Direct translation (Spec2): checking Accountable Safety timed out after more than 40 hours (Table 1)
    - number: Alloy with Kissat: Accountable Safety holds for up to 6 blocks; e.g. 5 blocks, 25 checkpoints, 12 votes in 234 s (Table 5)
    - number: CVC5: verified up to 5 blocks with 5 checkpoints (1h 40m) or 3 blocks with 6 checkpoints (74h) (Table 4)
    - number: A 7-block Alloy run went over 16 days without finishing
    - limits: Only the finality gadget and only Accountable Safety, on small bounded instances (at most 7 checkpoints and 24 votes); every validator has stake 1. The hand-made abstractions are not proved equal to the Python spec. Appendix A (translation rules) and B (soundness proofs) were skimmed, not read closely.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.07958); [notes: ## Exploring Automatic Model-Checking of the Ethereum specification (2](claude_readings.md)

- Anvil thesis (2025)
    - title: Building and Verifying Cloud Infrastructure Systems with State-Centric Reasoning (PhD dissertation)
    - what: The thesis proves liveness of Kubernetes controllers in Verus, using the Anvil framework. The human writes one general liveness formula, "eventually stable reconciliation": if the desired state stops changing, the cluster eventually matches it and stays that way. The proof splits into two parts: the environment becomes stable, and the controller reaches the desired state.
    - number: about two person-months to verify the ZooKeeper controller, about two person-weeks for each of the other two
    - number: each controller takes around 2.5 person-months to implement and verify; proof-to-code ratio 4.5 to 7.4
    - number: ZooKeeper: executable 1,134 lines, proof 8,352 (ESR 7,245), trusted 950; RabbitMQ: executable 1,598, proof 7,228, trusted 548
    - number: framework: 5353 lines of reusable lemmas, 7817 lines of trusted code; 70+ temporal-rule lemmas, 60 Kubernetes-environment lemmas
    - limits: The controller must be a state machine whose actions are atomic (they cannot be interrupted). The proof trusts the environment model, the shim layer, external APIs, the verifier, the compiler and the OS. After verification, testing still found one bug. It came from an incomplete spec of a trusted ZooKeeper API. About 40% of the 111 pages were read.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://tianyin.github.io/thesis/SUN-DISSERTATION-2025.pdf)

    - title: Formal modelling and analysis of Clock-SI with TLA+
    - what: A master's thesis that models the Clock-SI protocol (snapshot isolation for a partitioned database using loosely synchronized clocks) in TLA+ in two versions, a detailed one and a simpler one, and uses the TLC model checker to test that the protocol gives Snapshot Isolation, stated with a client-centric specification.
    - number: Abstract model, Snapshot Isolation check: 2 keys, 2 partitions, 2 txns 3 s; 3 txns 3 min 28 s; 3 keys, 2 partitions, 3 txns 11 h 23 min 20 s; 3 keys, 3 partitions, 3 txns timed out (Table 10)
    - number: Detailed model: 3 keys, 2 partitions, 2 txns 21 min; most larger cases timed out (Table 9)
    - number: Symmetry and view features cut one 2-key, 2-partition, 3-txn state-space run from 2 h 54 min to 2 min 2 s (Table 8)
    - number: Machine: i7-7700HQ, 16 GB RAM; each time is the average of 3 runs; maximum runtime 12 hours
    - limits: Bounded to at most 3 transactions, keys, partitions; the target of 3/3/3 was not reached. The abstract model removes the message system, so it does not cover the real messaging. Only the safety property (Snapshot Isolation) is checked; no liveness. Chapters 2-3 (background) and the model listings in 4.1-4.2 were skimmed.

- Korg thesis (2025)
    - title: Verification and Attack Synthesis for Network Protocols
    - what: Proves properties of three network-protocol pieces with theorem provers (Karn's RTT algorithm and TCP's timeout rule in Ivy and ACL2s, and Go-Back-N efficiency in ACL2s), model-checks TCP, DCCP and SCTP handshakes in SPIN, then builds Korg, a tool that searches for attacker behaviour that breaks a given property.
    - number: Handshake properties: TCP 4 (phi1-phi4), SCTP 10 (gamma1-gamma10), DCCP has its own set
    - number: Korg asked for at most 10 attacks per property; default search depth 600,000, maximum 2,400,000
    - number: TCP phi3 (peers do not get stuck) took 72 min to 223 min per attacker model; other TCP properties took seconds
    - number: SCTP: one patched-vs-unpatched check; with the CVE-2021-3772 patch, Korg finds no off-path attack, which proves the patch closes it for the model
    - limits: Models are small by design to avoid state-space explosion; the handshake models are not linked to the Ivy/ACL2s models. Korg handles one centralized attacker, not coordinated multi-process attackers, and is exact only for the model, not the real code. Models took long to write by hand.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01124); [notes: ## Von Hippel thesis on protocol verification and attack synthesis (20](claude_readings.md)

- PolySemist (2025)
    - title: Accelerating Protocol Synthesis and Detecting Unrealizability with Interpretation Reduction.
    - what: A tool that fills in the missing guards and updates of a half-written TLA+ distributed protocol (a sketch) so the protocol meets a safety or liveness property. It loops: the TLC model checker finds a counterexample, the tool rules out every candidate that shares it, and it skips candidates that behave the same on the states seen so far.
    - number: 7 benchmark protocols: 2PC, consensus, decentralized lock, lock server, sharded KV store, two reconfigurable Raft variants
    - number: faster than the previous tool Scythe in 160 of 171 realizable problems; total time about 9,000 s vs 32,000 s on those both solve
    - number: whole distributed-lock protocol (6 expressions over 2 actions) synthesized in 125 s; Scythe timed out at 1 hour
    - number: detected 'no protocol exists' in 80 of 123 unrealizable problems vs 16 for Scythe (easy 46/50, medium 21/35, hard 13/38)
    - limits: A human supplies the sketch (state variables, actions, hole grammars) and the property; results hold only for the chosen finite parameters (e.g. number of nodes), and the all-parameters step is left to TLAPS proofs from prior work. Termination is guaranteed only when each hole grammar has finitely many expressions up to interpretation equivalence.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.14585); [notes: ## PolySemist: protocol synthesis by sketching (2025)](claude_readings.md)

- Verification and synthesis of distributed systems with weak synchronisation (2024)
    - what: A theory thesis on deciding, for processes that talk only by broadcast or only by taking and releasing locks, whether a system meets a property, and whether processes can be given local strategies that guarantee it. The trick is to pick a small description of what each process may do alone (an invariant or a set of patterns) and check whether any combination of such local runs can go wrong.
    - number: 254 pages, three models: broadcast networks of register automata, lock-sharing systems, dynamic lock-sharing systems
    - number: coverability for broadcast networks with data is complete for the class F_omega^omega, so needs non-primitive-recursive time
    - number: verification of lock-sharing systems with 2 locks per process or nested locks is NP-complete; synthesis is Sigma_2^P-complete
    - number: synthesis of local controllers is undecidable for general lock-sharing systems
    - limits: Pure decidability and complexity results with no tool or benchmark; the models are heavily restricted (only broadcast or only locks), and the author notes adding a shared variable makes the lock model undecidable. Read from the introduction, summaries and conclusions only, not the proofs.
    - how much was read: parts read

- Elle (2020)
    - title: Elle: Inferring Isolation Anomalies from Experimental Observations
    - what: A test-time checker that runs random transactions against a database, records what clients saw, and rebuilds a dependency graph between transactions. A cycle in that graph is a proof that the database broke its claimed isolation level.
    - number: Found anomalies in all 4 databases tested: TiDB, YugaByte DB, FaunaDB, Dgraph
    - number: Checks histories of hundreds of thousands of transactions in tens of seconds; Knossos often fails after a few hundred
    - number: Linear time in history length
    - limits: Needs generated workloads where reads reveal the full write history (such as list append). Ignores predicates, so it cannot separate repeatable read from serializability. It finds bugs, not proof that a database is correct.

- Pretend Synchrony / Goolong (2019)
    - title: Pretend synchrony: synchronous verification of asynchronous distributed programs
    - what: Verifies Go distributed programs by automatically rewriting them into an equivalent synchronous program (messages become assignments), then checking ordinary verification conditions with the Z3 solver. Four case studies: two-phase commit, Raft leader election, single-decree Paxos, Multi-Paxos key-value store.
    - number: manual invariant annotations reduced by 6x versus Dafny
    - number: check time 1.73 s total vs 1455.84 s in Dafny for the three protocols
    - number: Paxos 504 lines of Go, 14 loop-invariant lines, 1.51 s; Dafny 72 invariant lines, 1141.35 s
    - number: Multi-Paxos KV: 847 Go lines, 1.64 s check, 118.5 req/ms, within 1.5-3x of unverified EPaxos code
    - limits: Safety only, no liveness. Needs structured loops, symmetric process ids, typed channels, pairwise communication and round non-interference; rules out Multi-Paxos stable-leader optimization, Stoppable Paxos and ring topologies such as Chord.

    - title: Iris from the ground up: A modular foundation for higher-order concurrent separation logic
    - what: This paper explains a checked framework for reasoning about concurrent programs. Ghost resources (resources that exist only in the proof) are tied to program state through invariants.
    - limits: The paper covers Iris 3.1. The current project has newer references.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://www.mpi-sws.org/~dreyer/papers/iris-ground-up/paper.pdf); [notes: S7](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_9_formulation_methods.md)

- CertiKOS (2016)
    - title: CertiKOS: An Extensible Architecture for Building Certified Concurrent OS Kernels
    - what: CertiKOS specifies an interface and the visible events at each kernel layer. It proves that a module can stand in for its specification in the contexts it allows (this is called contextual refinement). The mC2 kernel is proved in Coq.
    - number: Under 2 person-years for supporting concurrency
    - number: 6500 lines of C and x86 assembly
    - limits: The 2 person-years is the cost of adding concurrency support, not the cost of a whole new kernel.

    - [source](https://www.usenix.org/system/files/conference/osdi16/osdi16-gu.pdf); [notes: S9](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_9_formulation_methods.md)

- Chapar (2016)
    - title: Chapar: Certified Causally Consistent Distributed Key-Value Stores
    - what: Chapar states the public causal-visibility rule separately from the store internals. The rule says that if an update is visible, its dependencies are visible. The authors prove two store algorithms and extract OCaml. They verify client examples, such as publishing a photo before a post that refers to it.
    - limits: It trusts serialization and the runtime infrastructure.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://adam.chlipala.net/papers/ChaparPOPL16/ChaparPOPL16.pdf); [notes: papers](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_systems_20261007.md); [notes: Chapar: Certified Causally Consistent Distributed Key-Value Stores](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md)

- Verdi Raft (2016)
    - title: Planning for Change in a Formal Verification of the Raft Consensus Protocol
    - what: The authors complete the proof of Raft state-machine safety in Verdi. They manage changes across interdependent invariants by hiding definitions behind interface lemmas.
    - number: 90 system invariants discovered and proved
    - number: about 45000 additional lines on top of an earlier roughly 5,000-line proof
    - number: 530 lines of code and 50,000 lines of proof
    - number: 530 lines of code and 50,000 lines of proof (Raft development only, §5, PDF p.5)

- AWS formal methods (2015)
    - title: Use of Formal Methods at Amazon Web Services (How Amazon Web Services Uses Formal Methods)
    - what: AWS engineers used TLA+ and TLC to explore distributed designs and find subtle bugs, including in replication and recovery designs. It is the clearest industrial statement of the gap between model and code.
    - limits: It checks designs only and does not prove the deployed code correct. The linked manuscript is dated 29 September 2014 and differs from the final publication.

    - title: Dafny: An Automatic Program Verifier for Functional Correctness

- Hyperproperties (2010)
    - what: Defines hyperproperties, which are specifications over sets of executions. Shows that refinement can break them, for example by leaking a secret. Shows that k-safety hyperproperties reduce to safety on k copies of the system.
    - limits: A mathematical classification only. No runnable artifact. k-safety does not cover all hypersafety, and the paper does not solve hyperliveness (the progress counterpart).

- seL4 (2009)
    - title: seL4: Formal Verification of an OS Kernel
    - what: This is a proof in Isabelle that a microkernel's implementation refines (correctly implements) its spec.
    - number: 8,700 lines of C
    - number: 200,000 lines of Isabelle script
    - number: Abstract spec took about 4 person months
    - number: Refinement statements at most 20% of effort
    - limits: It is a historical baseline. The 2014 paper has the expanded stack and the effort analysis.

    - record id: `sel4-2009`
- Environment Assumptions for Synthesis (2008)
    - what: This work computes environment assumptions from a spec alone, so that a conditional spec becomes implementable. It first forbids unsafe environment moves. It then adds strong fairness (an action that is enabled infinitely often must eventually run) to some of the remaining moves.
    - number: smallest set of fair edges is NP-hard (Theorem 11)
    - number: safety sufficiency check polynomial; fairness minimization yields a set with no removable subset, not the smallest (Theorem 12)
    - limits: No unique maximal sufficient assumption exists. The results are for finite reactive games, and there is no distributed benchmark. It does not certify an implementation that was already chosen.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/pdf/0805.4167)
    - record id: `environment-assumptions-for-synthesis-2008`
- Separation logic (2002)
    - title: Separation Logic: A Logic for Shared Mutable Data Structures
    - what: Adds a separating conjunction P * Q for assertions about disjoint parts of memory. Adds a frame rule, so the proof of a small component can be reused inside a larger heap.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~jcr/seplogic.pdf); [notes: S6](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_9_formulation_methods.md)
    - record id: `separation-logic-2002`
- Specifying Systems (2002)
    - what: This book builds TLA+ specifications from state, initial conditions and allowed steps. It then adds requirements that something eventually happens, and fairness (an action that stays possible eventually runs).
    - limits: The notes read only chapter 8 and the passages on refinement. A spec that covers only safety permits a system to do nothing. Fairness on a combined action can miss the starvation of one request.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://lamport.azurewebsites.net/tla/book-02-08-08.pdf)
    - record id: `specifying-systems-2002`
- Specifying Systems (2002)
    - title: Specifying Systems: The TLA+ Language and Tools for Hardware and Software Engineers
    - what: A book on TLA+. Chapters 2-5 develop initial states and allowed next steps. Chapters 8 and 14 cover progress conditions and model checking.
    - limits: Translation does not decide which failures or observations the human cares about (the notes' inference).
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://lamport.azurewebsites.net/tla/book.html); [notes: human specification practice](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_foundations_20261007.md); [notes: P03. Specifying Systems](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_2_liveness.md)
    - record id: `specifying-systems-2002-2`
- Property Specification Patterns (1999)
    - title: Patterns in Property Specifications for Finite-state Verification
    - what: This is a catalog of reusable requirement patterns, about what occurs and in what order. It also lists scopes: global, before, after, between, and after-until. Each pattern is mapped to formal languages for describing behavior over time. The authors also surveyed real specifications.
    - limits: The notes read the authors' pattern repository, not the ICSE PDF. The repository labels a formula about all branching futures (AG/AF) as LTL.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://matthewbdwyer.github.io/psp/papers.html)

- Forward/backward simulations (1995)
    - title: Forward and Backward Simulations, Part I: Untimed Systems
    - what: It defines relations between steps of two systems (forward and backward simulations). They prove that one system's visible behaviors are included in the other's. They are for cases where a plain abstraction function cannot match the choices the systems make.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://groups.csail.mit.edu/tds/papers/Lynch/TM-486.pdf); [notes: S3](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_9_formulation_methods.md)

- TLA (1994)
    - title: The Temporal Logic of Actions
    - what: The paper describes a system as states and allowed steps (behaviors). It gives a logic for safety and for temporal properties such as progress that rests on fairness.
    - limits: The hard work sits in the invariants for the premises of WF1 (the proof rule for weak fairness). Page numbers refer to the manuscript on the author's site.

The Temporal Logic of Actions](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_2_liveness.md); [notes: connecting different descriptions of the same behavior](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_foundations_20261007.md)
    - record id: `tla-1994`
- Refinement mappings (1991)
    - title: The Existence of Refinement Mappings
    - what: It shows that suitable refinement mappings (functions from implementation states to spec states) exist once auxiliary variables are added under stated hypotheses. It covers three obstacles: history, prophecy (needing to know the future) and step granularity.
    - limits: It is a completeness result. It is not an algorithm that finds the variables or the mapping. The notes' author did not fully read the proofs.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://lamport.azurewebsites.net/pubs/abadi-existence.pdf); [notes: S2](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_9_formulation_methods.md); [notes: connecting different descriptions of the same behavior](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_foundations_20261007.md)
    - record id: `refinement-mappings-1991`
- DLS partial synchrony (1988)
    - title: Consensus in the Presence of Partial Synchrony
    - what: It defines two ways to weaken synchrony. In one, the timing bounds are fixed but unknown. In the other, the bounds are known but hold only after an unknown global stabilization time. It gives consensus algorithms whose agreement always holds and whose termination holds after stabilization.
    - number: crash/omission consensus needs N >= 2t+1 and Byzantine needs N >= 3t+1 in the partially synchronous columns of Table I
    - number: Algorithm 1: all correct processors decide by round GST + 4(N+1) in the basic round model
    - number: Theorem 4.1(b): N >= 3t+1
    - limits: The proofs are on paper, with no machine-checked artifact. The bound is counted in rounds of the basic round model, not in seconds. A timeout is not evidence of a crash.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1145/42282.42283); [notes: Dwork–Lynch–Stockmeyer partial synchrony](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_consensus_20261007.md); [notes: Consensus: where the assumptions enter the proof](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_2_liveness.md)
    - record id: `dls-partial-synchrony-1988`
- FLP (1985)
    - title: Impossibility of Distributed Consensus with One Faulty Process
    - what: Shows that no deterministic asynchronous consensus protocol can guarantee a decision on every allowed execution if one process may stop, even when messages are always eventually delivered.
    - limits: This is an impossibility result under its stated assumptions only. Randomization or eventual synchrony changes the problem.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://groups.csail.mit.edu/tds/papers/Lynch/jacm85.pdf); [notes: S26](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_9_formulation_methods.md); [notes: Consensus: where the assumptions enter the proof](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_2_liveness.md)
    - record id: `flp-1985`

    - limits: The claimed thresholds depend on the paper's assumptions about messages and synchrony.

- Hoare logic (1969)
    - title: An Axiomatic Basis for Computer Programming
    - what: Introduces reasoning about programs with assertions: if P holds before the program runs and the program finishes, then Q holds after.

    - title: Crash-Stop Failures in Asynchronous Multiparty Session Types
    - what: Extends asynchronous multiparty session types with crash-stop failures, crash handling branches, and optional reliability assumptions. It proves sound and complete correspondence between global/local semantics and derives deadlock freedom, protocol conformance, and liveness for well-typed processes under crashes.
    - number: Covers reliability from no failures to arbitrary process crashes
    - number: Theorems establish subject reduction, session fidelity, deadlock freedom, and liveness
    - number: Formal development spans 71-page LMCS article
